<image-desc>The multicolored Google logo.</image-desc>

This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project to make the world’s books discoverable online.

It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and the book to enter the public domain. A public domain book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary from country to country. Public domain books are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture, and knowledge that is often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations, and other marginalia <note>original: "marginalia"; notes written in the margins of a book</note> present in the original volume will appear in this file—a reminder of this book’s long journey from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

## Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying <note>the use of software to automatically search or download data</note>.

We also ask that you:

+ *Make non-commercial use of the files* We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.

+ *Refrain from automated querying* Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google’s system. If you are conducting research on machine translation, optical character recognition <note>OCR; the process of converting images of text into machine-readable text</note>, or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.

+ *Maintain attribution* The Google “watermark” you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.

+ *Keep it legal* Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we cannot offer guidance on whether any specific use of any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book’s appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liability can be quite severe.

## About Google Book Search

Google’s mission is to organize the world’s information and to make it universally accessible and useful. Google Book Search helps readers discover the world’s books while helping authors and publishers reach new audiences. You can search through the full text of this book on the web at <insert>http://books.google.com/</insert>

<image-desc>
An intricate engraving serving as a reproduction of a 10th-century Byzantine illumination from the Paris Psalter. It depicts the biblical King David as a young shepherd boy seated on a rock, playing a lyre. Leaning on his shoulder is a female figure personifying Melody <note>labeled in the original Greek as ΜΕΛΟΔΙΑ (Melodia)</note>. The landscape includes a dog sitting at David's feet, grazing sheep and goats, and a reclining male figure in the lower right representing Mount Bethlehem <note>labeled in the original Greek as ΟΡΟΣ ΒΗΘΛΕΕΜ (Oros Bethleem)</note>. In the upper left background, a walled city is labeled Bethlehem <note>labeled in the original Greek as ΒΗΘ ΛΕ (Beth Le)</note>. A figure peers out from behind a draped column on the right. The entire composition is enclosed within a wide, decorative geometric border featuring Greek key patterns and corner squares with X-motifs.
</image-desc>

# *Illuminated manuscripts in classical and medieval times*

## John Henry Middleton

<term>Illuminated manuscripts</term>: Handwritten books decorated with gold, silver, or brilliant colors, often featuring intricate illustrations and borders.

<summary>This frontispiece features a reproduction of a 10th-century Byzantine illumination from the Paris Psalter, depicting young David playing the lyre accompanied by a personification of Melody. It serves as the title page for John Henry Middleton's study on the history of manuscript decoration.</summary>

<keywords>Illuminated manuscripts, Byzantine art, Paris Psalter, King David, Melody, Bethlehem, John Henry Middleton, medieval art history</keywords>

## CHAPTER I. Page 1 to 10.

->CLASSICAL MANUSCRIPTS WRITTEN WITH A STILUS.<-

Survival of classical methods in medieval times; epigraphy and paleography; manuscripts on metal plates; lead rolls; tin rolls; gold amulets; Petelia tablet; waxed tablets and diptychs; tablets shown on gems and coins; tablets found in tombs; tablets from Pompeii; Consular diptychs; many-leaved tablets; the form of the waxed tablets; whitened boards used by the Greeks; late survival of tablets; “bidding the beads;” lists of members of guilds; wooden book in Norway; ivory tablets and diptychs; inscribed Anglo-Saxon lead tablet; “horn-books.”

### BYZANTINE MANUSCRIPTS.

### CHAPTER V. Page 62 to 79.
### MANUSCRIPTS OF THE CAROLINGIAN PERIOD.

The age of Charles the Great; the school of Alcuin of York; the *Gospels* of Alcuin; the *golden Gospels* of Henry VIII; the *Gospels* of the scribe Godesscalc; Persian influence; technical methods; the later Carolingian manuscripts; continuance of the Northumbrian influence; beginning of life-study; the *Gospels* of Otho II; period of decadence in the 11th century.

## CHAPTER IX. Page 126 to 146.

### **FRENCH MANUSCRIPTS.**

The age of Saint Louis; archaism of costume in miniatures; French manuscripts of the fourteenth and fifteenth centuries; historiated Bibles <note>Bibles with decorative paintings within the initial letters</note>; the ivy-pattern; the *Horae* of the Duc de Berri; the *treasure-book* of Origny Abbey; the Anjou *Horae*; costly and magnificent French *Horae*; their beautiful decorations; their numerous miniatures; the Bedford *Breviary*; the Bedford *Missal*; various styles in the same manuscript; manuscripts in *Grisaille* <note>painting executed entirely in shades of gray</note>; manuscripts of secular works; Cristina of Pisa <note>Christine de Pizan</note>; *Chronicles* and *Travels*; *Romances* and *Poems*; Italian influence in the south of France; the growth of secular illuminators; the inferiority of their work; cheap and coarsely illuminated *Horae*; manuscripts of the finest style; use of flowers and fruit in borders and initials; influence of the Italian Renaissance; the *Horae* of Jehan Fouquet of Tours.

## CHAPTER X. Page 147 to 153.

### **PRINTED BOOKS WITH PAINTED ILLUMINATIONS.**

*Horae* printed on vellum in Paris; their woodcut decorations; the productions of the earliest printers; the Mazarine Bible <note>The Gutenberg Bible</note>; the Mentz *Psalter* <note>The Mainz Psalter</note>; illuminators becoming printers; Italian printed books with rich illuminations; the colophons of the early printers; the books of Aldus Manutius; invention of Italic type; manuscripts illustrated with woodcuts; block-books; the long union of the illuminators' and the printers' art.

### CHAPTER XI. Page 154 to 182.
#### ILLUMINATED MANUSCRIPTS OF THE TEUTONIC SCHOOL AFTER THE TENTH CENTURY.

The revival of art in Germany in the eleventh century; the *Missal* of the Emperor Henry II; the designs used for stained glass; the progress of manuscript art under Frederick Barbarossa; grotesque monsters; examples of fine German illuminations from the twelfth century; their resemblance to mural paintings; the school of the Van Eycks; the Grimani *Breviary*; Gerard David of Bruges; examples of Flemish miniatures; the use of gold; grotesque figures; the influence of manuscript art on the painters of altar-pieces; the school of Cologne; a triptych by the elder Holbein; a book illuminated by Albert Dürer <note>Albrecht Dürer</note>; Dutch fifteenth-century manuscripts; their decorative beauty and realistic details; illumination using pen outlines in blue and red.

### CHAPTER XII. Page 183 to 205.
#### THE ILLUMINATED MANUSCRIPTS OF ITALY AND SPAIN.

Italian art was slow to advance; its degraded state in the twelfth century; illuminators mentioned by Dante; a *Missal* in the Chapter library of Saint Peter's; the monk Don Silvestro in the middle of the fourteenth century; his style of illumination; the monk Don Lorenzo; Fra Angelico as an illuminator; an Italian *Pontifical* in the Fitzwilliam library; manuscripts of the works of Dante and Petrarch; motifs of decoration; Italian manuscripts after 1453; the introduction of the "Roman" hand; the great perfection of writing and the finest quality of vellum; the illuminators Attavante, Girolamo dai Libri, and Liberale of Verona; manuscripts of northern Italy and their influence on painting in general; Italian manuscripts of the sixteenth century, a period of rapid decline; Giulio Clovio as a typical miniaturist of his time; the library of the Vatican and its records of the cost of illuminating manuscripts. The manuscripts of Spain and Portugal; the manuscripts of Muslim countries, especially Persia.

## PREFACE.

The object of this book is to give a general account of the various methods of writing, the different forms of manuscripts, and the styles and systems of decoration that were used from the earliest times down to the sixteenth century A.D., when the invention of printing gradually put an end to the ancient and beautiful art of manuscript illumination.

I have attempted to give a historical sketch of the growth and development of the various styles of manuscript illumination, and also of the chief technical processes which were employed in the preparation of pigments, the application of gold leaf, and other details, to which the most unsparing amount of time and labor was devoted by the scribes and illuminators of many different countries and periods.

An important point with regard to this subject is the remarkable way in which technical processes lasted, in many cases, almost without alteration from classical times down to the latest medieval period, partly owing to the existence of an unbroken chain of

<vocab>manuscripts, illumination, printing, technical processes, pigments, gold leaf, scribes, medieval</vocab>

<summary>The author introduces the book's purpose: to chronicle the history, styles, and technical methods of manuscript illumination from antiquity until the rise of printing. The preface emphasizes the continuity of artistic techniques and the immense labor required by historical scribes.</summary>
<keywords>manuscript illumination, printing history, pigments, gold leaf, medieval art, scribal techniques, history of writing</keywords>

## PREFACE.

traditional practice, and partly on account of the medieval custom of studying and obeying the precepts of such classical writers as Vitruvius and Pliny the Elder.

To an English student, the art history of illuminated manuscripts should be especially interesting, as there were two distinct periods when the productions of English illuminators were of unrivaled beauty and importance throughout the world¹.

In the latter part of this volume, I have tried to describe the conditions under which the illuminators of manuscripts did their work, whether they were monks who labored in the *scriptorium* of a monastery, or members of some secular guild, such as the great painters’ guilds of Bruges or Paris.

The extraordinary beauty and marvelous technical perfection of certain classes of manuscripts make it a matter of interest to learn who the illuminators were, and under what daily conditions and for what reward they labored with such astonishing patience and skill.

The intense pleasure and refreshment that can be gained by the study of a fine medieval illuminated manuscript depend largely on the fact that the exquisite miniatures, borders, and initial letters were the product of an age which in almost every respect differed widely from the unhappy, machine-driven nineteenth century in which we now live.

### BOOKS ON ILLUMINATED MANUSCRIPTS

The following are some of the most important works on this subject, and the most useful for the purposes of a student. Many others, which deal with smaller branches of the subject, are referred to in the following text.

Bastard, *Peintures et Ornemens des Manuscrits, classés dans un ordre Chronologique*, Imperial folio, Paris, 1835, etc.; a very magnificent book, with 163 plates, mostly colored.

Birch and Jenner, *Early drawings and illuminations*, London, 1879; this is a useful index of subjects which occur in manuscript miniatures.

Bradley, J. W., *Dictionary of Miniaturists and Illuminators*, 3 volumes 8vo. London, 1887–1890.

Chassant, *Paléographie des Chartes et des Manuscrits du XIme au XVIIIme Siècle*, 12mo.; a useful little handbook, together with the companion volume, *Dictionnaire des Abbréviations Latines et Françaises*, Paris, 1876.

Denis, F., *Histoire de l’Ornementation des manuscrits*; 8vo. Paris, 1879.

Fleury, E., *Les Manuscrits de la Bibliothèque de Laon étudiés au point de vue de leur illustration*, 2 volumes, Laon, 1863. With 50 plates.

Humphreys, Noel, *Illuminated Books of the Middle Ages*, folio, London, 1849; a handsome, well-illustrated book.

# CHAPTER I.

## CLASSICAL MANUSCRIPTS WRITTEN WITH A STYLUS.

Before entering into any discussion of the styles and methods of decoration that are to be found in medieval manuscripts and of the various processes, pigments, and other materials that were employed by the medieval illuminators, it will be necessary to give some account of the shapes and kinds of books that were produced among various races during the classical period.

<margin>Survival of methods.</margin>
The reason for this is that classical styles of decoration and technical methods—in the preparation of paper, parchment, pigments, and the like—both survived to a greater extent and until a much later period than is usually supposed. Indeed, they continued to influence both the artistic qualities and the mechanical processes of the medieval illuminator almost until the time when the production of illuminated manuscripts was gradually ended by the invention of printing.

<margin>The pen and the stylus.</margin>
The word *manuscript* is usually understood to imply writing with a pen, brush, or <term>stylus</term>, to the exclusion of inscriptions cut with a chisel or a graver in stone, marble, bronze, or other hard substances. The science of <term>paleography</term> deals with the former, while <term>epigraphy</term> is concerned with the latter. The inscribed clay tablets of Assyria and Babylon might be considered a sort of link between the two, because the <term>cuneiform</term> writing on them was executed with a stylus in soft, plastic clay, which was subsequently hardened by

<vocab>stylus, paleography, epigraphy, cuneiform, Assyria, Babylon, medieval manuscripts, illuminated manuscripts</vocab>

<summary>This chapter introduces the history of classical manuscripts, explaining that medieval illumination techniques were deeply rooted in ancient traditions. It defines the differences between paleography and epigraphy, using cuneiform tablets as an example of an early writing method using a stylus.</summary>
<keywords>classical manuscripts, stylus, paleography, epigraphy, medieval illumination, cuneiform, Assyria, Babylon, book history</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of early writing materials from Chapter I, moving from clay tablets to the use of soft metal plates as a medium for manuscripts.</meta>

<page-num>2</page-num>
<header>WRITING ON METAL PLATES [CHAP. I.</header>

baking in the potter’s kiln, but it will be unnecessary to describe them here.

<margin>Writing on metal.</margin> *Manuscripts on metal plates.* Another form of writing used especially by the ancient Greeks, which falls more definitely under the category of manuscripts, consists of characters scratched with a sharp iron or bronze <term>stylus</term> on plates of soft tin, lead, or pewter. When not in use, these could be rolled up into a compact and conveniently portable cylinder.

A considerable number of these inscribed lead rolls have been found in the tombs of Cyprus; unfortunately, none of them have yet been found to contain matter of any great interest.

<margin>Lead rolls.</margin> For the most part, they consist of either monetary accounts or formulas for imprecations—curses condemning some enemy to punishment at the hands of the gods. We know, however, from the evidence of classical writers that famous poems and other important literary works were occasionally preserved in the form of these inscribed tin or lead rolls. Pausanias, for example, tells us that during his visit to Helicon in Boeotia, he was shown the original manuscript of Hesiod’s *Works and Days* written on plates of lead; see Paus. IX. 31. Again, at IV. 26, Pausanias records the discovery at Ithome in Messenia of a bronze urn (<term>hydria</term>) which contained a manuscript of the “Mysteries of the Great

<margin>Tin rolls.</margin> Deities” written on “a thinly beaten plate of tin, which was rolled up like a book,” κασσίτερον ἐληλασμένον ἐς τὸ λεπτότατον, ἐπείλικτο δὲ ὥσπερ τὰ βιβλία. This method of writing would be quite different from the laborious method of cutting inscriptions on bronze plates with a chisel and hammer, or with a graver.

A scribe could write on the soft white metal with a sharp <term>stylus</term> almost as easily and rapidly as if he were using pen and ink on paper, and the manuscript produced in this way would have the advantage of extreme durability.

Indeed, we may hope that even now some priceless lost work of early Greece might be recovered through the discovery of lead rolls similar to those which Cesnola found in Cyprus.

<summary>This page describes the ancient Greek practice of writing manuscripts on soft metal plates, such as lead or tin, which were rolled into cylinders for portability. It cites historical examples from Pausanias, including a lead manuscript of Hesiod, and notes the durability and ease of writing on such materials.</summary>
<keywords>metal plates, lead rolls, tin rolls, stylus, Cyprus, Hesiod, Works and Days, Pausanias, Ithome, Messenia, hydria, Cesnola, ancient Greek manuscripts</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of ancient writing materials, moving from lead and tin rolls to gold manuscript plates used as amulets, and then to the use of waxed wooden tablets used in Greece and Rome.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
# CHAP. I.] AND WAXED TABLETS.
<sig>I—2</sig>

<margin>Gold amulets.</margin>
Some very beautiful small Greek manuscripts, written on thin plates of gold, have also been discovered in various places. The most remarkable of these were intended to be amulets; they were rolled up in small gold or silver cylinders and worn around the neck during life. After death, they were placed with the body in the tomb. Several of these, discovered in tombs in the district of Sybaris in Magna Graecia, are inscribed with fragments from the mystic Orphic hymns and give directions to the soul regarding what it will find and what it must do in the spirit world.

<margin>Petelia tablet.</margin>
The most complete of these small gold manuscripts, usually known as the Petelia tablet, is preserved in the gem room in the British Museum. The manuscript consists of thirteen hexameter lines written on a thin plate of pure gold measuring 1 1/2 inches long by 2 1/8 inches wide; it dates from the third century B.C.<note>See *Jour. Hell. Stud.* Vol. III. p. 112.</note>

In classical times, manuscripts were of two different forms: first, the *book form*, <term>πίναξ</term> (<term>pinax</term>), <term>πινάκιον</term> (<term>pinakion</term>), or <term>δελτίον</term> (<term>deltion</term>), in Latin <term>codex</term> (older spelling <term>caudex</term>); and secondly the roll, <term>κύλινδρος</term> (<term>kylindros</term>), <term>βίβλος</term> (<term>biblos</term>), or <term>βιβλίον</term> (<term>biblion</term>), in Latin <term>volumen</term>.<note>It was not until quite a late period that the word <term>βίβλος</term> (<term>biblos</term>) was used to mean a form of book other than the roll. The word <term>σανὶς</term> (<term>sanis</term>) is also used for a tablet; see p. 30.</note>

<margin>Waxed tablets.</margin>
*Manuscripts on tablets.* Both the Greeks and the Romans used tablets (<term>πίνακες</term> [<term>pinakes</term>], Latin <term>tabulae</term> or <term>cerae</term>) of wood very extensively. These were covered with a thin coating of colored wax, on which the writing was formed with a sharp-pointed <term>stilus</term> (<term>γραφὶς</term> [<term>graphis</term>]) made of wood, ivory, or bronze. The wax was colored either black or red so that the writing scratched upon it would be clearly visible. The reverse end of the <term>stilus</term> was made flat or in the shape of a small ball so that it could be used to make corrections by smoothing out words or letters that had been erroneously scratched into the soft wax.

These tablets were commonly about ten to fourteen inches in length and about half that in width. The main surface of each tablet was recessed from 1/8 to 1/10 of an inch

<header>WAXED TABLETS [CHAP. I.</header>

in depth to receive the wax layer, leaving a rim all around about the size of that around a modern schoolboy's slate.
<margin>Waxed diptychs.</margin>
The purpose of this was so that two of these tablets might be placed together face to face without the danger of rubbing or erasing the writing on the wax, which was applied in a very thin coat, no more than 1/16 of an inch in thickness. As a rule, these tablets were fastened together in pairs by sturdy loops of leather or cord. These double tablets were called by the Greeks <term>pinakes ptyktoi</term> or <term>diptycha</term> (from <term>dis</term> and <term>ptysso</term>) and by the Romans <term>pugillares</term> or <term>codicilli</term>. Homer (*Il.* VI. 168) mentions a letter written on folding tablets—
> ->πόρεν δ' ὅ γε σήματα λυγρά<-
> ->Γράψας ἐν πίνακι πτυκτῷ.<-

<margin>Tablets on coins and gems.</margin>
Representations of these folding tablets occur frequently in both Greek and Roman art; for example, on various Sicilian coins, the artist’s name is placed in tiny letters on a double tablet, which in some cases—as on a <term>tetradrachm</term> of Himera—is held open by a flying figure of Victory.

A gem from about 400 B.C., a large scarabaeoid in chalcedony recently acquired by the British Museum, is engraved with a seated figure of a lady holding a book consisting of four leaves; she is writing lengthwise on one leaf, while the other three hang down from their hinge.

Some of the beautiful terracotta statuettes from the tombs of the Boeotian Tanagra represent a girl reading from a somewhat similar double folding tablet.

On Greek vases and in Roman mural paintings, <term>pugillares</term> are frequently shown, though the roll form of manuscript is on the whole more common.

<margin>Tablets from tombs.</margin>
Some examples of these tablets have been found in a good state of preservation in Greco-Egyptian tombs and during recent excavations in Pompeii.

Part of a poem in Greek written in large <term>uncial</term> characters is still legible on the single leaf of a pair of tablets from Memphis in Egypt, which is now in the British Museum. Although the coating of wax has nearly all perished, the

# CHAP. I. OR PUGILLARES.

A sharp stylus has marked through onto the wood behind the wax, so that the writing is still legible. Its date appears to be shortly before the Christian era. <note>A fine set of five tablets is preserved in the coin room in the Paris National Library; see *Archaeological Review* Vol. 8, p. 461.</note>

<margin>Pompeian tablets.</margin>
Some well-preserved *pugillares* found in Pompeii are now in the Museum in Naples; the writing on them is of less interest, consisting merely of accounts of expenditure. Though the wood is blackened and the wax destroyed, the writing is still perfectly visible on the charred surface.

A more costly form of *pugillares* was made of bone or ivory; <note>A well-preserved example of Roman *pugillares* formed of two leaves of ivory, now in the Capitoline museum in Rome, is illustrated by Baumeister, *Monuments*, Vol. 1, p. 355.</note> in some cases the back of each ivory leaf was decorated with carving in low relief.

<margin>Consular diptychs.</margin>
A good many examples of these tablets, dating from the third to the sixth century AD, still exist. These late, highly decorated *pugillares* are usually known as *Consular diptychs* because, as a rule, they have the name of a Consul on the carved back, and very frequently a representation of the Consul in his *pulvinar* (state box) presiding over the Games in the Circus. It is supposed that these ivory diptychs were inscribed with complimentary addresses and were sent as presents to newly appointed officials during the time of the later Empire.

<margin>Many-leaved tablets.</margin>
In some cases, the ancient writing tablets consisted of three or more leaves hinged together (<term>triptychs</term>, <term>pentaptychs</term>, etc.); this was the earliest form of the *codex* or *book* in the modern sense of the word. The inner leaves of these *codices* had recessed areas to receive the wax on both sides; only the backs of the two outer leaves were left plain or carved in relief to form the covers.

When the written matter on these tablets was no longer wanted, a fresh surface for writing was prepared either by smoothing down the wax with the handle of the stylus, or else by scraping it off and pouring in a fresh supply. This is mentioned by Ovid (*The Art of Love* I. 437); "cera......rasis infusa" <note>wax... poured onto the erased surfaces</note>

<summary>This page describes various archaeological examples of ancient writing tablets, from functional wooden ones found in Pompeii to ornate ivory Consular diptychs. It also explains how multi-leaved tablets formed the early structure of the modern book (codex) and how the wax surfaces were recycled for new writing.</summary>

# GREEK METHODS OF WRITING [CHAP. I.

<margin>Waxed tablets.</margin>
tablets¹." These tablets were sometimes called briefly *cerae*; the phrases *prima cera* and *altera cera* meant the first page and the second page. The best types of wooden writing tablets were made of boxwood, and therefore they are sometimes called <term>πυξίον</term>. In addition to the holes along one edge of each tablet, through which the cord or wire was passed to hold the leaves together and form the hinge, additional holes were often made along the opposite edge. These allowed a letter or other writing on the *tabulae* to be kept private by tying a thread through the holes and then impressing a seal on the knot. Plautus (*Bacch.* IV. iv. 64) alludes to this when mentioning the various items required to write a letter:

> *Effer cito stilum, ceram, et tabellas et linum.*

In some cases, wooden tablets of this kind were used without a coating of wax, having instead a simple, smooth surface for writing with ink and a reed pen. Many examples of these have been found in Egypt. The writing could be erased and a new surface prepared by sponging the wood and rubbing it with pumice stone.

<margin>Whitened boards.</margin>
Among the Greeks, wooden boards whitened with chalk or gypsum were often used for writing that was intended for temporary use only. Charcoal was used to write on these boards, which were called <term>λευκώματα</term> or <term>γραμματεῖα λελευκωμένα</term>². Public advertisements and official announcements were frequently written in this way and then hung in a conspicuous place in the *agora*, or market-place, of the city.

Thus, some inscriptions from the fourth century B.C. found at Delos mention that every month a <term>λεύκωμα</term> was suspended in the *agora*, on which was written a statement of the financial management and all the expenses of the Temple.

<note>² Charcoal or crayon-holders made of bronze with a spring clip and sliding ring, exactly like those used today, have been found in Pompeii. These and other writing materials are illustrated by Baumeister, *Denkmäler*, Vol. III, p. 1585.</note>

# CHAP. I.] AND MEDIEVAL SURVIVALS. 7

<margin>Sacred accounts.</margin>

This was also the custom regarding the financial records of the Athenian Parthenon, and probably most of the important Greek temples. In connection with the sacred records, the Delian inscriptions mention—in addition to the <term>λευκώματα</term>—other forms of tablets: the <term>δέλτος</term> and the <term>πίναξ</term>, as well as <term>χάρται</term> or writings on *papyrus*. Manuscripts of this last kind will be discussed in a later section¹.

<margin>Late survivals.</margin>
*Late survivals of writing on tablets.* Before moving on to describe other forms of classical manuscripts, it may be interesting to note that the ancient waxed tablets, or *pugillares*, continued to be used for certain purposes throughout the entire medieval period, down to the 16th century or even later. Many of the major churches, especially in Italy but also in other countries, possessed one or more diptychs on which were inscribed the names of all those who had in any way been benefactors either to the religious foundation or to the building. In early times, during the daily celebration of Mass, the list of names was read out from the *diptych* by the deacon standing in the gospel ambon; and the congregation was requested or "bidden" to pray for the souls of those whose names they had just heard.

<margin>“Bidding the beads.”</margin>
The "bidding prayer" before university sermons at Oxford and Cambridge is a survival of this custom, which in the 15th century was called "bidding the beads"—that is, "praying for the prayers" of the congregation. In some cases, fine specimens of the old ivory *consular diptychs* were used for this purpose in Italian churches until relatively late times, but as a rule they fell out of use before the 11th or 12th century, as the list of names became too long for the waxed leaves of a diptych. Therefore, by degrees, vellum rolls or else *codices*, often beautifully written in gold and silver letters, were substituted. One of the most splendid of these lists, the

MEDIEVAL WAXED TABLETS [CHAP. I.

The *Liber Vitae* of Durham is now preserved in the British Museum; *Cotton manuscripts*, Domit. 7. 2.

For many other purposes, both ecclesiastical and secular, classical waxed tablets were used in England and on the Continent, especially for lists of names. For example, in great cathedral or abbey churches, they were used for the weekly list of the various priests who were appointed to celebrate mass at each of the numerous altars.

<margin>List of guild members.</margin>The British Museum possesses a very interesting late example of a waxed tablet which, in shape, size, and general appearance, is exactly like the Roman *pugillares*. This is an oak tablet, about 20 inches long by 10 inches wide, covered with a thin layer of wax protected by the usual slightly raised margin about half an inch wide. Along one edge are three holes with leather loops to form the hinges; the other leaf is lost. On the wax is inscribed a list of the names of the members of a Flemish guild; each name is still as sharp and legible as the day it was written. The form of the writing shows that it belongs to the end of the fifteenth century. Such tablets were used both by the trade guilds of the Middle Ages and by the religious guilds formed for the cult of some special saint.

<margin>Wooden book.</margin>The most interesting medieval example of the classical form of manuscript made up of several leaves of waxed tablets was found a few years ago in a blocked-up recess in the old wooden church at Hopperstad in Norway. It was enclosed in a casket of wood covered with leather, and thus it still remains in a very perfect state of preservation; it is now in the University Museum at Christiania <note>Now known as Oslo</note>. The book consists of six tablets of boxwood, coated with wax within the usual raised margin, and hinged with leather thongs. The outer leaves are decorated on the back with carving mixed with inlay of different colored woods.

<margin>Bestiary.</margin>The manuscript itself, which is written on the wax, is a *Bestiary*, dating—as its style shows—from the latter part of the thirteenth century, though the book itself is probably older. It contains lists of animals in Latin with a Norwegian translation, and it is copiously illustrated with drawings of

# CHAP. I.] AND IVORY TABLETS.

In every detail, except of course in the character of the writing and drawings, this book exactly resembles an ancient Greek or Roman many-leaved wooden book, *πολύπτυχον*, a very striking example of the unaltered survival of ancient methods for an extraordinarily long period.

<margin>Ivory tablets.</margin>
During the medieval period, sets of ivory tablets hinged together were frequently made for devotional purposes. This form of manuscript has no layer of wax, but the writing is executed with a pen on the thin smooth leaf of ivory. Each leaf has its margin raised, like the ancient *pugillares*, to prevent the two adjacent surfaces from rubbing together.

These ivory tablets usually contain a set of short prayers, and they are frequently illustrated with painted miniatures of sacred subjects exactly like those in the vellum manuscripts of the same date.

<margin>Tablet with eight leaves.</margin>
The South Kensington Museum possesses a very beautiful example of these ivory books; it is of Northern French workmanship dating from about the middle of the fourteenth century. It consists of eight leaves of ivory, measuring 4⅛ inches by 2⅝ inches in width. The six inner pages are extremely thin, no thicker than stout paper, and have paintings on both sides; the two covers are of thicker substance, about a quarter of an inch, and are decorated on the outside with beautiful carved reliefs.

This remarkable work of art has on the inner leaves fourteen very delicately executed miniatures of sacred subjects, single figures of saints and scenes from Christ's Passion, painted in gold and colors in the finest style of French fourteenth-century art, evidently executed by some very skillful illuminator.

<margin>Ivory diptychs.</margin>
Tablets like this with as many as eight ivory leaves are rare, but a very large number of beautiful ivory diptychs still exist, with carved reliefs on the outside of very graceful style and delicate execution. Most of these diptychs date from the fourteenth century and are of French workmanship, but

They were also produced in England at the same time and were of quite equal merit in design and execution.

<margin>Inscribed lead tablet.</margin>
*Manuscripts on lead plates*, like those of the ancient Greeks, were occasionally used in medieval times.

A single lead leaf of an Anglo-Saxon manuscript from Lord Londesborough's collection is illustrated in *Archaeologia*, Vol. XXXIV, Plate 36, page 438. This leaf measures 6½ inches by 5 inches in width. On it is incised with a stylus in fine, bold semi-uncial writing the beginning of Aelfric's preface to his first collection of *Homilies*, which in modern English runs thus:
> “I, Aelfric, monk and mass-priest, was sent in King Aethelred’s time by Bishop Aelfeage, the successor of Aethelwold, to a certain monastery which is called Cerne, etc.”

At the top of the page, there is a heading in large Runic characters. Aelfric was sent by Aelfeage, Bishop of Winchester, to be Abbot of Cerne in 988 or 989, and this interesting page appears to be of contemporary date. It was found by a laborer while digging in the precincts of the Abbey of Bury St Edmunds. Along one edge of the leaden page, there are three holes to receive the loops which hinged the plates together, but the other leaves were not found.

<margin>Horn-books.</margin>
*Horn-books.* One form of wooden tablet continued in use, especially in boys’ schools, until the sixteenth century. This was a wooden board, rather smaller than an ordinary schoolboy’s slate, with a long handle at the bottom; on it was fixed a sheet of vellum or paper on which was written or (in the latest examples) printed *the Alphabet, the Creed, the Lord’s Prayer*, or similar texts. Over this, a thin sheet of transparent horn was nailed, from which these tablets were often called “horn-books.” A good example dating from the sixteenth century is now preserved in the Bodleian Library at Oxford.

## CHAPTER II.

### CLASSICAL MANUSCRIPTS WRITTEN WITH PEN AND INK.

To return now to classical forms of manuscripts, it appears to have been a long time before the *book* or *codex* form of manuscript was adapted from wood and ivory tablets to writings on parchment or paper.

<margin>The roll form of manuscript.</margin>
<margin>The codex form.</margin>
It seems probable that throughout the Greek period, manuscripts on paper or vellum were usually, if not always, in the shape of a long roll; and that it was not until about the beginning of the Roman Empire that leaves of parchment or paper were sometimes cut into pages and bound together in the form of the older tablets. During the first two or three centuries of the Empire, manuscripts were produced in both of these forms—the *codex* and the *volumen*—but the *roll* form was by far the more common, almost until the transfer of the seat of government to Byzantium.

The roll form of book is the one shown in many of the wall paintings of Pompeii; but on some sarcophagus reliefs of the second century A.D., books of both the *roll* and the *codex* shape are represented¹.

<margin>Writing with a pen.</margin>
Having given some account of the various classical forms of manuscript in which the writing is incised with a sharp *stylus*, we will now move on to the other chief forms of manuscript which were written with a pen and with ink or other pigment.

---

¹ <note>See, for example, a relief on the sarcophagus of a *scriba librarius* (library curator) which is illustrated by Daremberg and Saglio, *Dict. Ant.* 1. p. 708. The scribe is represented seated by his bookcase or *armarium*, on the shelves of which both *volumina* and *codices* are shown.</note>

<header>THE RITUAL OF THE DEAD. [CHAP. II.</header>

<margin>Books of the dead.</margin>
Manuscripts on *papyrus*; the oldest existing examples of this category are the so-called *Rituals of the Dead* found in the tombs of Egypt, especially in those of the Theban dynasties. The oldest of these date as far back as the sixteenth or fifteenth century B.C.<sup>1</sup>

They are created with a reed pen in hieroglyphic writing on long rolls of papyrus and are abundantly illuminated with painted miniatures illustrating the subject of the text, drawn with great spirit and colored in a very fine, decorative style. Immense numbers of these Egyptian illuminated manuscripts still exist in a more or less fragmentary condition. One of the most perfect of these is the *Book of the Dead of Ani*, a royal scribe, dating from the fourteenth century B.C., which is now in the British Museum. An excellent facsimile of the whole of this fine illuminated manuscript was edited by Dr. Budge and published by the Trustees of the British Museum in 1890.

<margin>Egyptian psalter.</margin>
Manuscripts of this important category are not very accurately described as *Rituals of the Dead*; as Dr. Budge points out, they actually consist of collections of *psalms* or *sacred hymns* which vary considerably in different manuscripts.

They appear to have been written in large numbers and kept in stock by Egyptian undertakers, ready for purchasers. Blank spaces were left for the name and titles of the deceased person for whom they were bought.

Thus, we find that the names are often filled in carelessly by a different hand than that of the original writer of the manuscript, and some examples exist in which the spaces for the name are still left blank.

Another of the finest and most complete of the funerary *papyri* is preserved in the Museum in Turin; see Pierret, *Le livre des Morts des anciens Egyptiens*, Paris, 1882.

<margin>Use of papyrus.</margin>
Papyrus seems to have been used for manuscripts more than any other substance, both by the Greeks from the sixth century B.C. and by the Romans down to the time of the later Empire. Some very valuable Greek manuscripts on papyrus are preserved in the British Museum; among them, the most

---

<meta>This page continues the discussion of early papyrus manuscripts, transitioning from the Egyptian "Book of the Dead" to early Greek texts and significant archaeological discoveries in Egypt and Herculaneum.</meta>

<header>CHAP. II.] EARLY GREEK MANUSCRIPTS.</header>

<margin>Existing Greek manuscripts.</margin>
Some fragments of Homer's *Iliad* from the third or second century B.C. are important for their early date. Another papyrus manuscript in the same collection, dating from the first century B.C., contains four *Orations* by the Athenian orator Hyperides, a contemporary and rival of Demosthenes. In the last few years, an important discovery has been made: in certain late Egyptian tombs dating from the Roman period, the mummified bodies were packed in their coffins with large quantities of what was then considered waste paper. In some cases, this packing has been found to consist of papyrus manuscripts, some of which are of great importance. In this way, the newly discovered treatise by Aristotle on the *Political Constitution of Athens* and the *Mimes* of Herondas were saved from destruction by being used as inner wrappings for a coffin from about the year 100 A.D.<note>Some very interesting fragments of the *Antiope* by Euripides were brought to England by Mr. Flinders Petrie and have been edited by Dr. Mahaffy in a collection titled *The Flinders Petrie Papyri*, Dublin, 1892.</note>

Other important manuscripts may yet be found, now that careful searches are being conducted in this direction.

<margin>Herculaneum library.</margin>
Unfortunately, the large library of manuscripts—consisting of nearly 1,800 papyrus rolls discovered about the middle of the eighteenth century in the lava-buried town of Herculaneum—has not yet been found to contain any works of much value or interest. These rolls are all charred by the heat of the lava that overwhelmed the town, and the work of unrolling and deciphering the brittle, carbonized paper necessarily proceeds very slowly. The owner of this library appears to have been an enthusiastic student of Epicurean philosophy in its later development, and his books are mainly dull, pedantic treatises on various sciences, such as mathematics, music, and the like, treated from the Epicurean point of view—or rather, from that of the Graeco-Roman followers of Epicurus.

All these manuscripts appear to be from about the same period, which is not many years older than the year 79 A.D., when the eruption of Vesuvius overwhelmed Herculaneum and Pompeii in the same catastrophe. They are written in fine, bold <term>uncial</term>

<meta>This page continues the description of the Herculaneum papyrus rolls, which are charred and difficult to unroll. It transitions into a historical overview of writing materials, specifically the shift from papyrus to parchment, citing Herodotus and the rivalry between the libraries of Pergamus and Alexandria.</meta>

# MANUSCRIPTS ON PAPYRUS AND PARCHMENT. [CHAP. II.]

<margin>Papyrus rolls.</margin> characters without illumination or ornament of any kind on rolls of papyrus nine or ten inches in width. In their current burnt and shrunken condition, the rolls average about two inches in diameter, but they were probably larger than that in their original state; see *Palaeographical Society* Plates 151, 152; the other published 'facsimiles' of the Herculaneum manuscripts are not perfectly trustworthy.

<margin>Herodotus on manuscripts.</margin> In the time of Herodotus (about 460 B.C.), *papyrus paper* (<term>βιβλία</term> or <term>χάρται</term>)<sup>1</sup> appears to have been used by the Greeks almost to the exclusion of parchment or other kinds of skin. In his interesting section on the introduction of the art of writing into Greece by the Phoenicians, Herodotus (v. 58) remarks that the Ionians in ancient times used to call *papyrus rolls* <term>διφθέραι</term> or "**parchment**," because they had once been in the habit of using the skins of sheep or goats for manuscripts at a time when *papyrus paper* was not available; and, Herodotus goes on to say, "Barbarians even now are accustomed to write their manuscripts on parchment."

<margin>Use of parchment.</margin> *Manuscripts on parchment*; this ancient use of parchment for manuscripts was again introduced among the Greeks by Eumenes II, king of Pergamus from 197 to 159 B.C. At this time, people had forgotten that parchment had ever been used for books, and so Varro, quoted by Pliny (*Natural History* XIII. 70), tells us that Eumenes *invented* this use of parchment. The reality is that he reintroduced an ancient custom and encouraged the careful preparation of parchment for the sake of the great library which he was anxious to make the most important collection of manuscripts in the world.

<margin>Pergamena.</margin> Varro tells us that he was driven to this use of parchment by the jealousy of the Egyptian King Ptolemy Epiphanes, whose enormous library at Alexandria was the only existing rival to the Pergamene collection. One of the Greek names for parchment, *Pergamena*, was derived from the fact of its being so extensively made for the Pergamene Kings Eumenes and Attalus, both of whom were not only great patrons of—

---

<sup>1</sup> <note>The book market in Athens was called <term>τὰ βιβλία</term>, that is, <term>οὗ τὰ βιβλία ὤνια</term> (the place where books are for sale); see Pollux IX. 47. Lucian, in his treatise *Against the Ignorant Book-Collector*, gives an interesting account of the Greek book buyers and book sellers in his time; see § 1 and § 4.</note>

<summary>This page discusses the physical state of the Herculaneum papyrus rolls and the historical transition from papyrus to parchment. It details how King Eumenes II of Pergamus promoted parchment to compete with the library of Alexandria after papyrus supplies were restricted.</summary>
<keywords>papyrus, parchment, Herodotus, Ionians, Eumenes II, Pergamus, Varro, Pliny, Ptolemy Epiphanes, Alexandria, Pergamena, Attalus, writing materials</keywords>

# CHAP. II.] MANUSCRIPTS ON LINEN AND POTSHERDS. 15

literature and collectors of ancient manuscripts, but were also enthusiastic buyers of pictures, statues, rich textiles and works of art of every class. The other word for parchment used for manuscripts is *membrana*.

<margin>Linen manuscripts</margin>
*Manuscripts on linen*; in ancient Egypt hieroglyphic manuscripts with sacred hymns and portions of the so-called *Ritual of the Dead* were frequently written with a reed pen on fine linen. These manuscripts, which are often found among the mummy wrappings of burials under the Theban Dynasties, are usually illustrated with pen drawings in outline, not painted miniatures like those on the papyrus rolls. These drawings are executed with much spirit and with a beautiful, clean, certain touch.

<margin>Early manuscripts in Italy</margin>
The early Italian races, Latins, Samnites and others, appear to have used linen very frequently for their manuscript records and sacred books. Among the public records mentioned by Livy as having once been preserved with the Archives in the Capitoline Temple of Juno Moneta were some of these early linen manuscripts (<term>libri lintei</term>); see Livy iv. 7, 13, 20. Livy also (x. 38) describes an ancient manuscript, containing an account of the ritual customs of the Samnites, as a <term>liber vetus linteus</term>. In historic times, however, *papyrus* and *parchment* appear to have superseded *linen* in ancient Rome.

<margin>Inscribed potsherds</margin>
*Ostraka Manuscripts.* For ephemeral purposes, such as tradesmen's accounts and other business matters, writing was often done with a pen and ink on broken fragments of pottery (<term>ὄστρακα</term>). An enormous number of these inscribed potsherds, mostly dating from the Ptolemaic period, have been found in Egypt, and especially on the little island of Elephantine in the Nile a short distance below the first cataract.

Among the Greeks too, writing on potsherds was very common; especially when the Athenian tribes met in the Agora to record their votes for the exile of some unpopular citizen, from which is derived the term *ostracism* (<term>ὀστρακισμός</term>).

The word *liber* as meaning a *book* is supposed to be derived from a primitive custom of writing on the smooth inner bark

<header>MANUSCRIPTS ON LEAVES AND BARK. [CHAP. II.</header>

<margin>*MSS. on leaves.*</margin>
The large broad leaves of some varieties of the palm tree have also been used for manuscript purposes, more especially among the inhabitants of India and Ceylon. In early times, the questions asked of the Oracle of the Pythian Apollo at Delphi were said to have been written on leaves of the laurel plant. Pali manuscripts in Ceylon are even now frequently written on palm leaves; and we have the evidence of Pliny that this custom once existed among some of the ancient classical cultures: see *Hist. Nat.* XIII. 69,

> "Ante non fuisse chartarum usum, in palmarum foliis primo scriptitatum ; deinde quarundam arborum libris. Postea publica monumenta plumbeis voluminibus, mox et privata linteis confici coepta aut ceris. Pugillarium enim usum fuisse etiam ante Trojana tempora invenimus apud Homerum."

In this passage, Pliny gives a list of all the primary materials that had been used for manuscripts in ancient times: the *leaves* and *bark of trees*, *plates of lead*, *linen cloth*, and *waxed tablets*. He then goes on to describe at considerable length the methods of making paper from the pith of the papyrus plant; see page 22.

<margin>*Greek libraries.*</margin>
*Ancient libraries*; among the Greeks and Romans of the historical period, books do not appear to have been either as rare or as costly as they were during the greater part of the medieval period.

In the time of Alexander, during the latter part of the fourth century B.C., large libraries had already been formed by wealthy lovers of literature. In the second century B.C., the rival libraries of Ptolemy Epiphanes at Alexandria and of King Eumenes II. at Pergamus were said to have contained between them nearly a million volumes.

<margin>*Roman libraries.*</margin>
Among the Romans of the Empire, books were no less common. The owner of the library mentioned above at Herculaneum, consisting of nearly 1,800 rolls or volumes, does not appear to have been a man of exceptional wealth; his house was small and his surroundings simple in character.

As early as the reign of Augustus, Rome possessed several large public libraries (<term>bibliothecae</term>). The first of these

<meta>This page continues the history of ancient libraries, specifically detailing the major public libraries established in Rome from the 1st century B.C. through the reign of Trajan, as well as their internal organization, fittings, and decorations.</meta>

<page-num>17</page-num>
<header>CHAP. II.] ROMAN LIBRARIES. 17</header>

<margin>The great libraries of Rome.</margin>
The first public library in Rome was established in 37 B.C. by Asinius Pollio for both Greek and Latin manuscripts. The second was the *Bibliotheca Octaviae*, founded by Augustus in the Campus Martius in honor of his sister. The third was the magnificent double library of *Apollo Palatinus*, which Augustus built on the Palatine Hill. The fourth, also on the Palatine, was the *Bibliotheca Tiberiana*, founded by Tiberius. The fifth was built by Vespasian as part of the group of buildings in his new *Forum Pacis*. The sixth and largest of all was the double library for Greek and Latin books built by Trajan in his Forum close to the *Basilica Ulpia*. To some extent, a classification of subjects was adopted in these great public libraries: one was mainly legal, another for ancient history, and a third for state papers and modern records; however, this classification appears to have been only partially followed.

<margin>Parish libraries.</margin>
In addition to these state libraries, Rome also possessed a large number of smaller “parish libraries” in the separate *vici* <note>neighborhoods or districts</note>, and the total number, given in the *Regionary catalogues* as existing in the time of Constantine, is enormous; see Séraud, *Les livres dans l’antiquité*.

<margin>Library fittings.</margin>
Regarding the arrangement and fittings of Roman libraries, the usual method appears to have been this. Cupboards (*armaria*), fitted with shelves to receive the rolls or *codices* and closed by doors, were placed against the walls all around the room. These *armaria* were usually rather low, no more than four to five feet high, and busts of famous authors were placed on them; meanwhile, the wall space above the bookcases was decorated with similar portrait reliefs or paintings designed to fill panels or circular medallions.

<margin>Library decorations.</margin>
Pliny (*Hist. Nat.* xxxv. 9) speaks of it being a new fashion in his time to adorn the walls of libraries with ideal portraits of ancient writers, such as Homer, executed in gold, silver, or bronze relief.

The public library of Asinius Pollio was, Pliny says, decorated with portraits; but whether the great libraries of Pergamus and Alexandria were ornamented in this way, Pliny is unable to say. Magnificent medallion portraits in gold and silver were fixed around the walls of the two great

<page-num>18</page-num>
<header>ROMAN LIBRARIES AND [CHAP. II.</header>

libraries of Apollo on the Palatine Hill, and probably in the other still larger public libraries which were founded by subsequent Emperors.

<margin>Recent discovery.</margin>
The ordinary private libraries of Rome were decorated in a similar way, but with reliefs of less costly materials. A very interesting example of this has recently been discovered and then destroyed on the Esquiline Hill in Rome. The house in which this library was discovered was of no very exceptional size or splendor. The *bibliotheca* itself consisted of a handsome room; the lower part of its walls, against which the *armaria* <note>bookcases or cupboards</note> fitted, was left quite plain. <margin>Authors’ busts.</margin> Above that, the walls were divided into square panels by small fluted pilasters, and in the center of each space there was, or had been, a medallion relief-portrait about two feet in diameter enclosed in a molded frame. All this was executed in fine, hard marble-dust stucco (*opus albarium* or *marmoreum*).

The names of the authors whose portraits had filled the medallions were written in red upon the frames. Only one was legible—APOLLONIVS THYAN... No doubt the works of Apollonius of Tyana were kept in the *armarium* below the bust.

The library at Herculaneum, which contained the famous papyrus rolls, was a much smaller room. Besides the bookcases all around the walls, it had also an isolated *armarium* in the center of the room; and this, no doubt, was a usual arrangement.

The room at Herculaneum was so small that there can only have been just enough space to walk between the central bookcase and the *armaria* ranged all around against the wall.

<margin>Closed bookcases.</margin>
As Commendatore Lanciani has pointed out (*Ancient Rome*, p. 195), it is interesting to note that the ancient Roman method of arranging books in low, closed cupboards is still preserved in the great library of the Vatican in Rome; which is unlike most existing libraries in the fact that on first entering no one would guess that it was a library, as not a single book is visible.

Of the ancient *armaria* themselves no example now

<header>CHAP. II.] ROMAN BOOKSELLERS.</header>

exists. They were made of wood and were therefore, of course, perishable. But we may, I think, argue by analogy that the doors of the cupboards were richly ornamented with painted decorations, thus forming an elaborate dado or <term>podium</term> below the row of portrait reliefs that occupied the upper part of the walls.

<margin>Booksellers' quarter.</margin>
The principal quarter in Rome for the shops of booksellers (<term>bibliopolae</term> or <term>librarii</term>) appears to have been the *Argiletum*, which (in Imperial times) was an important street running into the Forum Romanum between the Curia and the Basilica Aemilia; see Martial I. 3, 117 <note>The end of the Argiletum is shown in the plan of the Forum Romanum in Middleton, *Ancient Rome*, 1892, Vol. I.</note>.

For ancient manuscripts or original works by famous authors, large prices were often paid. Aristotle is said to have given three talents (about £750) for an original manuscript by Speusippus, and a manuscript of Virgil's second book of the *Aeneid*, thought to be the author's own copy, sold for twenty <term>aurei</term>—more than £20 in modern value; see Aulus Gellius III. 17, and II. 3.

<margin>Cost of new books.</margin>
But ordinary copies of newly published works, even by popular authors, appear to have been only slightly more expensive than books of this class are at the present day. The publisher and bookseller Tryphon could sell Martial's first book of *Epigrams* at a profit for two <term>denarii</term>—barely two shillings in modern value; see Martial XIII. 3. It may seem strange that written manuscripts were not much more costly than printed books, but when one considers how they were produced, the reason is evident. <margin>Slave scribes.</margin> Atticus, the Sosii, and other major publishers of Rome owned a large number of slaves who were trained to be neat and rapid scribes. Fifty or a hundred of these slaves could write by dictation from one reader, and thus a small edition of a new volume of Horace's *Odes* or Martial's *Epigrams* could be produced with great speed and at very small cost <note>One reason for this was that even the most popular authors did not receive large sums for the copyright of their works.</note>.

Little capital would be required for the education of the

Cicero (*Att. II. 4*) speaks of the publisher Atticus selling manuscripts produced in this way by slave labor on a large scale.

<margin>Librarii.</margin>
The name *librarius* was given not only to the booksellers, but also to slave librarians and to scribes, the latter being sometimes distinguished by the name *scriptores librarii*. *Librarii antiquarii* were writers who were specially skilled in copying ancient manuscripts. The word *scriba* commonly denotes a *secretary* rather than what we would now call a scribe.

In Athens a class of booksellers, βιβλιογράφοι, appears to have existed as early as the fifth century B.C.; see Poll. VII. 211. The name βιβλιοπῶλαι was subsequently used, and adopted by the Romans.

## THE TECHNIQUE OF ANCIENT MANUSCRIPTS¹.

<margin>Parchment and vellum.</margin>
***Parchment.*** With regard to the preparation of parchment and other kinds of skin for writing on (*Pergamena* and *Membrana*), there is little to be said. The skins of many different animals have been used for this purpose both in classical and medieval times, especially the skins of calves, sheep, goats, and pigs. Unlike manuscripts on papyrus, parchment or vellum² manuscripts were usually covered with writing on both sides, since the ink does not show through from one side to the other, as it is liable to do on the more absorbent and spongy

---

¹ Much of what is said in this section with regard to the technique of classical manuscripts will apply also to manuscripts of the medieval period. Many of the processes had been inherited in an unbroken tradition from ancient times, and others were revived in the Middle Ages through a study of various classical writers on pigments and the like, especially Pliny and Vitruvius.

² The words *parchment* and *vellum* are used vaguely to imply many different kinds of skins. Strictly speaking, *vellum* implies calf-skin, but the word is commonly used to denote the finer and smoother qualities of skin; the name *parchment* being given to the coarse varieties; see Peignot, *L’histoire du parchemin*, Paris, 1812.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on ancient writing materials, moving from the use of parchment and vellum to the processes of erasing and reusing materials, and the characteristics of papyrus.</meta>

<page-num>21</page-num>
<header>CHAP. II.] OF CLASSICAL MANUSCRIPTS.</header>

<margin>Erasures.</margin>
papyrus paper. For this reason, complete or partial erasures were much easier to perform on vellum than on papyrus. The writing was first sponged to remove the surface ink, and the traces that still remained were removed by rubbing the surface of the vellum with pumice stone. In some cases, the writing was erased from the whole of a vellum codex or roll, and the cleaned surface was then used to receive new writing.

<margin>Palimpsests.</margin>
*Palimpsests*; manuscripts of this class, on twice-used vellum, were called *palimpsests* (παλίμψηστος); see Cicero, *Epistulae ad Familiares* vii. 18. Several important texts, such as the legal work of Gaius, have been recovered by laboriously deciphering the writing on these palimpsests that was not entirely obliterated. During the early medieval period, when classical learning was little valued, many a dull treatise of the schoolmen or other theological work of small interest was written over the erased text of some much earlier and more valuable classical author.

<margin>Papyrus MSS.</margin>
In some cases, it appears that papyrus manuscripts were made into palimpsests, but probably not very often, as it would be difficult to erase the ink on a roll of papyrus without seriously damaging the surface of the paper.

Moreover, as papyrus manuscripts were only written on one side of the paper, the back was free to receive new writing without any necessity to rub out the original text. The recently discovered treatise by Aristotle on the *Political Constitution of Athens* has some monetary accounts written on the back of the papyrus by some unphilosophical businessman not many years after the date of the original treatise.

<margin>Papyrus paper.</margin>
*Papyrus paper.* The ancient methods employed in the preparation of papyrus paper (*charta*) can be clearly understood by the evidence of existing examples, aided by the detailed but not entirely accurate description given by Pliny, *Natural History* XIII. 71 to 83.

The papyrus plant, the *Cyperus Papyrus* of Linnaeus (Greek βύβλος), is a very tall, handsome variety of reed which grows in marshes and shallows along the sides of streams of

<meta>This page continues the physical description of the papyrus plant and details the traditional methods used to transform its pith into writing material.</meta>

<page-num>22</page-num>
<header>THE MANUFACTURE OF [CHAP. II.</header>

<margin>Papyrus plant.</margin>
water. The plant has a very graceful tufted bunch of foliage at the top; its stem averages three to four inches in diameter, and the total height of the plant is ten to twelve feet.

It grows in many places in Syria, in the Euphrates valley, and in Nubia. In Egypt itself it is now extinct, but it was abundant there in ancient times, especially in the Nile Delta.

The only spot in Europe where the papyrus plant grows wild is near Syracuse in the small river Anapus, where it was probably introduced by Arab conquerors in the eighth or ninth century A.D.

It grows there in great abundance and sometimes nearly blocks the stream, so that a boat can scarcely pass through.

The stem of the papyrus consists of a soft, white, spongy, or cellular pith surrounded by a thin, smooth, green rind. Papyrus paper (βιβλία or χάρτης) was made entirely from the cellular pith. The manufacturing process was as follows.

<margin>Process of manufacture.</margin>
The long stem of the plant was first cut into convenient pieces of a foot or more in length; the pith in each piece was then very carefully and evenly cut with a sharp knife into thin slices. These slices were then laid side by side, their edges touching but not overlapping, on the smooth surface of a wooden table. This table was slightly tilted to let the excess sap run off as it was squeezed out of the pith slices by gentle blows from a smooth wooden mallet. When repeated beating had hammered the layer of pith into a thinner substance and much of the sap had drained off, a fine paste made of wheat flour was carefully brushed over the entire surface of the pith. A second layer of pith slices, previously prepared by beating, was then laid crosswise on the first layer—which had been made sticky by the paste—so that the slices in the second layer were at right angles to those of the first. The beating process was then repeated, with the workmen being careful to remove all lumps or irregularities. The beating continued until the various slices of pith in the two layers were thoroughly united and fused together.

For the best quality of papyrus, these processes were repeated

<meta>This page continues the technical description of papyrus production, focusing on the layering process, the dimensions of the sheets, how they were joined into rolls, and the historical longevity of the material.</meta>

<page-num>23</page-num>

## CHAP. II. PAPYRUS PAPER.

<margin>*Use of multiple layers.*</margin>
...a third and sometimes even a fourth time, the separate slices in each layer being cut much thinner than in the coarser sorts of paper which consisted of two layers only. The next process was to dry and press the paper; after which its surface was carefully smoothed and polished with an ivory burnisher <note>In some cases, the paper was *sized* (treated with a glaze or glue) before the final smoothing; but as a rule, sufficient *size* was supplied by the flour used to paste the layers together.</note>; its rough edges were trimmed, and it was then ready to be made into sheets or rolls. <margin>*Sizes of papyrus.*</margin> There was nothing in the manufacturing method to strictly limit the size of the papyrus sheets (<term>σελίδες</term>, <term>paginae</term>) in either width or length; the workman could lay side by side as many slices of the pith as he liked, and slices of great length might have been cut out of the long stem of the *papyrus*. In practice, however, it was found convenient to make the paper in rather small sheets; twelve to sixteen inches are the typical widths of papyrus manuscripts.

<margin>*Joining the sheets.*</margin>
The reason for this, obviously, was that it would have been impossible to cut slices of great length to the necessary thinness and evenness of substance; therefore, papyrus manuscripts are always composed of a large number of separate sheets carefully pasted together. This was very skillfully done by workmen who (in Pliny's time) were called <term>glutinatores</term>; see Cicero, *Letters to Atticus*, IV. 4. The two adjacent edges of the sheets, which were to be joined together by overlapping, were thinned down by careful rubbing to about half their original thickness. The two overlapping edges were then brushed over with paste, accurately joined together, and the union was completed by beating with a wooden mallet. When the pasted joint was dry, it was rubbed and polished with the ivory burnisher until scarcely any mark of the join remained. <margin>*Long rolls.*</margin> In this way, long rolls were formed, often fifty feet or more in length; as a rule, however, excessive length for a single roll was inconvenient. Pliny mentions 20 sheets as being an ordinary limit. Thus, for example, in works such as Homer's *Iliad* or Virgil's *Aeneid*, each *book* would form a separate <term>volumen</term> or roll (Greek <term>κύλινδρος</term> or <term>τόμος</term>).

The invention of papyrus paper dates from an early period in the history of Egypt. Examples still exist which

<page-num>24</page-num>
<header>PAPYRUS IN GREECE AND ROME. [CHAP. II.</header>

date as far back as 2300 B.C., and its manufacture was probably known long before that.

<margin>Papyrus used in Greece.</margin>
In later times, Egyptian papyrus was an important export to many countries. An Attic inscription from the year 407 B.C. tells us what the cost of paper was in Athens at that time; two sheets (χάρται δύο) cost two drachmae and four obols, equal in value at the time of this book's writing to about four shillings; see *C. I. A.* I. 324. In this case, the word χάρται probably refers not to a single page, but to several sheets pasted together to form a roll.

<margin>Papyrus made in Rome.</margin>
In Pliny's time, paper was made not only in Egypt but also in Rome and at other places in Italy¹. The best kind was formerly called *Hieratica*, because it was used in Egypt for sacred hieroglyphic writing only. In later times, this finest quality—in Rome, at least—was called *Augusta*, and the second quality was called *Liviana*, named after Livia, the wife of Augustus. A coarse variety used for wrapping parcels and the like was called "shop-paper," or *emporetica*. Pliny also tells us that paper was manufactured in many different widths, varying from about four to eighteen inches. The most common width was about twelve inches; see Pliny, *Hist. Nat.* XIII. 71 to 83.

<margin>Old manuscripts on papyrus.</margin>
In the last of these paragraphs, Pliny mentions examples of old papyrus manuscripts existing in his time, such as manuscripts in the handwriting of Tiberius and Gaius Gracchus, which were nearly two centuries old. He says that manuscripts written by Cicero, Augustus, and Virgil are still frequently to be seen.

Regarding the antiquity of paper, Pliny's views are far from correct. He thinks paper was first made in Egypt in the time of Alexander the Great (*Hist. Nat.* XIII. 79), whereas, as is mentioned above, fine quality papyrus paper was certainly made in Egypt nearly 2,000 years before the time of Alexander, and probably much earlier.

The best kinds of papyrus paper have a close texture, with

¹ <note>Some of the enormous ranges of storehouses for goods imported into Rome and landed on the Tiber quay were specially devoted to paper warehouses, *horrea chartaria*; extensive remains of these have recently been discovered near Monte Testaccio; see Middleton, *Remains of Ancient Rome*, 1892, Vol. II. pp. 260--262.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the material properties and historical usage of papyrus, specifically focusing on its surface quality, fibrous texture, and representation in ancient Greek art and artifacts.</meta>

<page-num>25</page-num>
<header>CHAP. II.] PAPER OF GOOD QUALITY. 25</header>

<margin>*Paper of fine quality.*</margin>
...a smooth surface, very pleasant to write upon with a reed pen, and adapted to receive miniature paintings of great refinement and delicacy of touch. To prevent the ink from spreading or soaking into the paper, as a final process, it was sometimes soaked in size made of fish-bones or gum and water, exactly as modern linen paper is sized. The color of the papyrus is a pale brown, very pleasant to the eye, and excellent as a background for painted decorations.

<margin>*Fibrous texture.*</margin>
When it was first made, papyrus paper must have been extremely durable and tough owing to its compound structure with two or more fibrous layers placed cross-wise. The parallel fibrous lines of the pith are very visible on the surface of papyrus paper; and these regular lines served as a guide to the scribe when writing, so that when papyrus was used it was not necessary to cover the page with ruled lines to keep the writing even, as had to be done when the manuscript was on vellum.

In a papyrus manuscript the pages of writing are set side by side, across the roll, with a small margin between each page or column.

<margin>*Greek examples of papyrus rolls.*</margin>
A small terracotta statuette¹ of about the fifth or fourth century B.C. found at Salamis in Cyprus in 1890, shows a Greek scribe writing on a long papyrus roll placed on a low table before which he is sitting.

Among Greek vase paintings of the same date a common subject is the poetess Sappho reading from a papyrus roll. A fourth-century vase with this subject in the Central Museum in Athens shows Sappho holding a manuscript on which the following words are inscribed (supplying missing letters and correcting blunders):

->**ΘΕΟΙ ΗΕΡΙΩΝ ΕΠΕΩΝ ΕΡΧΟΜΑΙ**<-
->**ΑΓΓΕΛΟΣ ΝΕΩΝ ΥΜΝΩΝ.**<-

By the figure of Sappho is inscribed the beginning of her name, **$ΑΠ** in letters of an archaic form.

A very similar design occurs on a beautiful gem in the British Museum (B.M. *Cat. of gems*, No. 556), which appears to date from the latter part of the fifth century B.C. A very

<note>¹ Now in the Fitzwilliam Museum.</note>

<page-num>26</page-num>
# PAPYRUS MANUSCRIPTS AND [CHAP. II.

<margin>
*Sappho reading.*
</margin>
A graceful female figure, probably intended to be Sappho, is depicted sitting on a chair with a high curved back. She is reading from a manuscript roll which she holds by the two rolled-up ends, with one in each hand.

This method of holding a papyrus manuscript is shown very clearly on a vase in the British Museum on which the same motif is painted. The lady (Sappho) holds the two rolled-up portions of the manuscript, stretching tight the middle section that displays the column of writing she is reading.

<margin>
*Umbilicus or roller.*
</margin>
As the reader progressed, the paper was unrolled from the roll held in the right hand, and the part just read was rolled up in the left-hand roll. These Greek representations do not usually show any stick or roller for the manuscript to be rolled around; however, in Roman times a wooden or ivory roller (<term>ὀμφαλός</term> or <term>umbilicus</term>) was used as the core of the roll, and the end of the long strip of papyrus at the last page or column of text was pasted onto it. The ends of the <term>umbilicus</term> were often fitted with a round knob or boss, which was decorated with gilding or color. The edges of the papyrus roll were smoothed with pumice stone (<term>pumice mundus</term>), and the entire manuscript was often provided with a vellum case, which was stained a bright color, such as red, purple, or yellow. Tibullus (*El.* III. i. 9) alludes to these decorative methods:

> *Lutea sed niveum involvat membrana libellum.*
> *Pumex et canas tondeat ante comas ;*
> · · · · · · · · · · · · · · · ·
> *Atque inter geminas pingantur cornua frontes.*

The <term>frontes</term> are the edges of the roll, and the <term>cornua</term> are the projecting portions of the two wooden rollers.

<margin>
*Inscribed titles.*
</margin>
The title of the manuscript was written on a ticket or slip of vellum, which hung down from the closed roll like the hanging seal of a medieval document. Thus, when a number of manuscripts were piled on the shelf of an <term>armarium</term> <note>a storage cabinet or chest for books</note>, the tags hanging down from the ends of the rolls clearly indicated what the books were without the necessity of pulling them from their place.

Small numbers of rolls, especially manuscripts which had

It references artistic depictions of Sappho and Latin poetry by Tibullus to explain how these ancient books were handled and organized.</summary>

<page-num>27</page-num>
<header>->CHAP. II.] METHODS OF ORNAMENT.<-</header>

to be carried about, were often kept in round drum-like boxes (*capsae* or *scrinia*), with loop handles to carry them by.

<margin>Colored inks.</margin>
Much of the beauty of an ancient manuscript depended on the use of red or purple ink for *headings*, *indexes*, and *marginal glosses*. As Pliny says (*Hist. Nat.* XXXIII. 122), *minium in voluminum quoque scriptura usurpatur* <note>red lead is also used for the writing of books</note>.

The use of purple ink for the *index* is mentioned by Martial in his epigram *Ad librum suum* (III. 2), where he sums up the various methods of decoration which in his time were applied to manuscripts:

> *Cedro nunc licet ambules perunctus,*
> *Et frontis gemino decens honore*
> *Pictis luxurieris umbilicis;*
> *Et te purpura delicata velet,*
> *Et cocco rubeat superbus index.*

<margin>Use of oil.</margin>
The oil of cedarwood, mentioned in the first of these lines, was smeared over the back of papyrus manuscripts to preserve them from bookworms.

The act of unrolling a manuscript to read it was called *explicare*, and when the reader had come to the end, it was *opus explicitum* <note>the work is unrolled</note>. In medieval times, from the false analogy of the word (*hic*) *incipit* <note>here it begins</note>, a verb *explicit* was invented and was often written at the end of *codices* to show that the manuscript was complete to the end, though, strictly speaking, the word is only applicable to a *roll*.

<margin>Medieval use of papyrus.</margin>
The use of papyrus paper for manuscripts to some extent continued until medieval times. Papyrus manuscripts of the sixth and seventh century A.D. are not uncommon, and long after vellum had superseded papyrus paper for the writing of books, short documents, such as letters, Papal deeds, and the like, were still frequently written on papyrus. Papal *Briefs* on papyrus still exist which were written as late as the eleventh century.

<margin>Black ink.</margin>
The *black ink* which was used for classical manuscripts was of the kind now known as "Indian" or, more correctly, "Chinese ink," which cannot be kept in a fluid state but has to be rubbed up with water from day to day as it is required. One of the menial duties which Aeschines, when a boy, had to

It also notes the continued use of papyrus for official documents like Papal Briefs well into the medieval period.</summary>
