<meta>This page continues the discussion of manuscript production, transitioning from the physical storage and general decoration of papyrus to the specific materials and preparation of black and red inks used in antiquity.</meta>

<page-num>28</page-num>
<header>BLACK AND RED INK [CHAP. II.</header>

<margin>Carbon ink.</margin>
The task he had to perform in his father’s school was “rubbing the ink,” **τὸ μέλαν τρίβων**; see Demosthenes, *De Corona*, p. 313. This kind of ink (**μέλαν** or **μελάνιον**, <term>atramentum librarium</term>) simply consists of finely divided particles of carbon, mixed with gum or with size made by boiling down shreds of parchment. It was obtained by burning a resinous substance and collecting the soot on a cold flat surface, from which it could afterwards be scraped off. The soot then had to be very finely ground, mixed with a gummy medium, and then molded into shape and dried. The process is described by Pliny, *Natural History* XXXV. 41; and even better by Vitruvius, VII. 10.

<margin>Black pigment.</margin>
A variety of this carbon pigment used for pictures on stucco by wall-painters was called <term>atramentum tectorium</term>, modern “lamp-black”; the only difference between this and writing ink was in the kind of glutinous medium used with it. Careful scribes probably prepared their own ink, as the writers of medieval manuscripts usually did. The common commercial black ink of about 300 A.D. was sold at a very cheap rate, as is recorded in an inscription containing part of Diocletian’s famous edict which was found at Megalopolis and published by Mr. Loring (*Journal of Hellenic Studies* Vol. XI., 1890, p. 318, line 46). Under the heading “Pens and ink,” **Περὶ καλάμων καὶ μελανίου**, the price of ink, **μελάνιον**, is fixed at 12 small copper coins per pound.

Very great skill is required to prepare carbon ink of the finest quality. Although it is now largely manufactured in Europe, none but the Chinese can make ink of the best sort.

In some places, sepia ink from the cuttlefish was used in ancient times; see Persius, *Satires* III. 12; and compare Pliny, *Natural History* XI. 8, and XXXII. 141.

<margin>Red inks.</margin>
The *red ink* used for ancient manuscripts was of three different kinds, namely red lead, vermilion or sulfide of mercury, and red ochre. The ancient names for these red pigments were used very indiscriminately: **μίλτος**, <term>minium</term>, <term>cinnabaris</term>, and <term>rubrica</term>. In some cases, **μίλτος** certainly means the costly vermilion; and yet the word is also used both for red lead and for the much cheaper red ochre. The latter appears to be always meant by the name **μίλτος Σινώπις**; see

<meta>Digitized by Google watermark at the bottom of the page.</meta>

<page-num>29</page-num>
<header>CHAP. II.] AND INKSTANDS.</header>

Choisy, *Inscriptions of Lebadeia*, p. 197. The Latin words *minium* and *rubrica* are used in the same vague way; see Vitruvius VII. 9; and Pliny, *Natural History* XXXV. 31 to 35.

In medieval manuscripts red ink (*rubrica*) was largely used not only for headings and glosses, but also in service books for the ritual directions, which have since taken the name of *rubrics*.

<margin>Purple ink.</margin>The purple ink (*coccus*), which Martial mentions in the passage quoted above on page 27, was made from the *kermes* beetle, which lives on the ilex trees of Greece and Asia Minor. This was one of the most important of the ancient dyes for woven fabrics, and it was also used as a pigment by painters; see below, page 246.

<margin>Double inkstands.</margin>The inkstands of ancient scribes were commonly made double to hold both black and red ink. Many examples of these from Egypt and elsewhere still exist, and they are depicted in many of the Pompeian wall paintings. They are usually in the form of two bronze cylinders linked together, each with a lid attached by a small chain. Other inkstands are single, small round bronze boxes shaped like a large pillbox. Another method, especially common in ancient Egypt, was for the scribe to carry their ink—both black and red—in solid form; they then mixed it with water as needed. The box and palette mentioned below were made for this use of solid inks, except that the entire object, handle and all, is made from a single piece of metal.

<margin>Reed pens.</margin>The pens used by ancient writers of manuscripts were mainly some variety of reed (κάλαμος, *calamus* or *canna*), cut diagonally to a point like a modern quill pen. Great numbers of reed pens have been found in Egyptian tombs and also in Pompeii; they exactly resemble those still used in Egypt and in Eastern countries generally.

<margin>Metal pens.</margin>Metal pens were also used by Greek and Roman scribes. Examples in both silver and bronze have been found in Greece and in Italy, shaped very much like a modern steel pen¹.

<note>¹ A silver pen was found by Dr. Waldstein in 1891 in the tomb of the Aristotle family at Chalcis.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of ancient writing tools, moving from the history of inks to the physical implements used for writing, such as brushes, pen-cases, and reed pens used in Egypt and classical Greece.</meta>

<page-num>30</page-num>
<header>PENS AND PEN-CASES. [CHAP. II.]</header>

<margin>Scribes' palettes.</margin>
In some cases, manuscripts were written with a fine brush instead of a pen, especially the hieroglyphic manuscripts of ancient Egypt. Many combined scribes' palettes and brush cases have been found in Egyptian tombs. These are long slips of wood, partly hollowed to hold the brushes, and with two cup-like sinkings at one end for the writer to prepare his cakes of black and red ink.

In Egyptian manuscripts, red ink is used much more copiously than in either Greek or Latin manuscripts. Very often the scribe writes his columns alternately in black and red for the sake of the decorative appearance of the page.

<margin>Pen-cases.</margin>
Egyptian pen-cases in the form of a bronze tube about 3/4 inch in diameter and 10 inches long, with a tightly fitting cap, have frequently been found. The British Museum possesses good examples of these and the other writing implements described here.

<margin>Reed pens.</margin>
The above-mentioned passage in the *Edict of Diocletian* (see page 28) gives the prices of reed pens (<term>κάλαμοι</term>) of various qualities. The difference is very great between the best and the inferior kinds of pens; the best quality appears to have been made from the long single joint of a reed.

There is no evidence that quill pens were used in classical times, but it is difficult to believe that so natural a solution never occurred to any ancient scribe, especially when the use of vellum for manuscripts became common. For papyrus paper, the softer reed pen would be more convenient than a quill, and indeed for all the earlier sorts of Greek and Latin writing in large *uncial* characters. It is only for the smaller *cursive* writing that a quill would be as suitable as a reed pen.

The inscription mentioned on page 24 as giving the cost of paper in Athens in 407 B.C. is part of a record of the expenses for building the Erechtheum. It also mentions the purchase for 4 drachmae of 4 wooden writing-tablets: 
<term>χάρται ἐωνήθησαν δύο, ἐς ἃς τὰ ἀντίγραφα ἐνεγράψαμεν</term> .................... **┣┣IIII**
<term>Σανίδες τέτταρες</term>........................................................... **┣┣┣┣**

<page-num>31</page-num>

## CHAPTER III.

### CLASSICAL ILLUMINATED MANUSCRIPTS.

<margin>Illumination.</margin>
The medieval phrase *illuminated manuscript* means a manuscript which is "lighted up" with colored decoration in the form of ornamental initial letters or painted miniatures. Dante speaks of "The art which in Paris is called illuminating,"

->......*quell' arte*<-
->*Che alluminare è chiamata in Parisi; Purg.* XI. 80.<-

<margin>Use of minium.</margin>
The important use of red paint (*minium*) in the decoration of manuscripts led to the painter being called a *miniator*, from which the pictures he executed in manuscripts were called *miniature* or *miniatures*. Finally, the word *miniature* was extended in meaning to imply any painting on a *minute scale* <note>There is, of course, no etymological connection between the words *miniature* and *minute*; the latter is derived from the Latin *minutus*, *minus*.</note>. Originally, however, it was only applied to the painted decorations of manuscripts.

<margin>Egyptian miniatures.</margin>
The Egyptian manuscript "Books of the Dead" are very copiously illuminated with painted miniatures, both in the form of ornamental borders along the edge of the papyrus, and also with larger compositions which occupy the whole depth of the roll.

It is difficult to say to what extent illuminated manuscripts were known to the ancient Greeks, but they were certainly not uncommon in Rome toward the close of the Republic; and it may fairly be assumed that it was from the Greeks that the very inartistic Romans derived the custom of decorating manuscripts with painted miniatures.

<summary>This page defines the terms "illuminated manuscript" and "miniature," explaining their etymological origins in red lead paint (minium). It also notes the early use of illumination in Egyptian "Books of the Dead" and its eventual adoption by the Romans from the Greeks.</summary>
<keywords>illumination, miniature, minium, miniator, Dante, Books of the Dead, papyrus, ancient Rome, Greek art</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the history of illuminated manuscripts, moving from the definitions of "illumination" and "miniature" to specific historical examples of illustrated works in the Roman and Greek worlds.</meta>

<page-num>32</page-num>
# ILLUMINATIONS IN CLASSICAL MANUSCRIPTS. [CHAP. III.

<margin>Illustrations in Roman manuscripts.</margin>
Pliny tells us (*Hist. Nat.* XXXV. 11) that a number of manuscripts in the library of M. Varro in the first century B.C. contained no fewer than 700 portraits of illustrious figures.

That the original manuscript of Vitruvius' work on *Architecture* was illustrated with explanatory pictures is shown by the frequent reference in the text to these lost illustrations which are mentioned as being at the end of the work; *e.g.* see III., *Preface*, 4.

A manuscript written in letters of gold is mentioned by Suetonius (*Nero*, 10); this was a copy of Nero's own poem which was publicly read aloud to an audience on the Capitol, and was then deposited in the Temple of Jupiter Capitolinus.

<margin>Writing in gold.</margin>
Again, two centuries later the mother of Maximus, who was titular Caesar from 235 to 238 A.D., is said to have given him a manuscript of Homer's poems written in gold letters on purple vellum; see Jul. Capit., *Max. Vita*.

There is, in short, abundant evidence to show that illuminated manuscripts were common among the Romans of the Imperial period; and there is a very strong probability that manuscripts decorated with miniatures were no less frequent in the great libraries of the Ptolemies and of the Attalid kings, in fact throughout the Greek world from the time of Alexander the Great onwards, if not earlier still.

<margin>Greek miniatures.</margin>
Some notion of the great beauty of the illustrations in Greek manuscripts may perhaps be gathered from an examination of the masterly and delicately graceful drawings incised in outline which decorate the finest of the Greek bronze *cistae*. Nothing could surpass the perfect beauty of the outline engravings on the so-called *Ficoronian cista*, which is now preserved in the Museo del Collegio Romano in Rome. Part of this series representing scenes from the adventures of the Argonauts is shown on fig. 1.

<margin>Two sources of knowledge.</margin>
With regard to the general scheme of decoration in classical manuscripts, we have the evidence of a few existing examples dating from about the time of Constantine, and also a large number of copies of Roman manuscript-pictures of earlier date than the third century A.D., which are to be

<summary>This page provides historical evidence for illuminated manuscripts in the Roman and Greek worlds, citing examples such as Varro's portraits, Vitruvius' architectural diagrams, and luxury editions written in gold. It also suggests that the style of lost Greek miniatures can be inferred from contemporary bronze engravings like those on the Ficoronian cista.</summary>

<margin>Isolated pictures.</margin>
The evidence derived from these two sources leads to the conclusion that as a rule the illuminations in classical manuscripts were treated as separate pictures, each surrounded with a simple painted frame, and not closely linked to the text in the characteristic medieval fashion. The medieval method, by often introducing miniature paintings within the boundary of large initial letters, and by surrounding the page with borders of foliage which grow out of the

<page-num>34</page-num>
<header>MINIATURES FROM THE ILIAD [CHAP. III.</header>

<margin>
*Medieval method.*
</margin>
chief initials of the text, makes the decoration an essential part of the whole and creates a close union between the literary and the ornamental parts of the book. This is very unlike the usual ancient system of having a plainly written text with isolated miniature paintings introduced at intervals throughout the pages of the book.

<margin>
*Iliad of the 4th century.*
</margin>
*Manuscript of the Iliad at Milan*; of all existing Greek or Latin manuscripts, none gives a better idea of the style of illumination used in manuscripts of the best Greco-Roman period than the fragments of Homer's *Iliad* which are preserved in the Biblioteca Ambrosiana in Milan.

These fragments consist of fifty-eight miniature paintings which have been cut out of a folio manuscript on vellum of Homer's *Iliad*, dating probably from the latter part of the fourth century A.D. The person who mutilated this <term>codex</term> seems only to have cared to preserve the pictures, and the only portion of the text which still exists is about eight hundred non-consecutive lines which happen to be written on the backs of the paintings. Great additional interest is given to this priceless fragment by the fact that the miniatures are much older in style than the date of the manuscript itself, and have evidently been copied from a much earlier Greek original.

<margin>
*Older Greek style.*
</margin>
And more than that; these paintings take one back further still; their rhythmical composition, the dignity of their motifs, the simplicity of the planes, and the general largeness of style which is especially noticeable in some of the miniatures representing fighting armies of gods and heroes, all suggest that we have here a record—weakened and debased though it may be—of some grand series of mural decorations on a large scale, dating possibly from the best period of Greek art.

As is naturally the case with copies of noble designs executed at a period of extreme decadence, these paintings are very unequal in style, combining feebleness of touch and coarseness of detail with great spirit in the action of the figures and great dignity in the compositions. These compositions have numerous figures crowded without confusion of line, thus suggesting a large scale even though the paintings are actually

It contrasts the ancient system of isolated illustrations with the integrated medieval style where decoration and text form a unified whole.</summary>

<page-num>35</page-num>
<header>CHAP. III.] IN THE AMBROSIAN LIBRARY.</header>

<margin>Hellenic models.</margin>
miniatures only five or six inches long. The treatment of gods and heroes, especially Zeus, Apollo, Achilles, and others, has much that recalls fine Hellenic models. And some of the personifications, such as *Night* and the river *Scamander*, possess a gracefulness of pose and beauty of form which was far beyond the conception of any fourth-century artist.

It should, however, be observed that a fine Hellenic origin is not suggested by all the fifty-eight pictures from this *Iliad*. Some of them are obviously of a later and inferior style, with weak, scattered compositions, very unlike the nobility and decorative completeness of the best among the miniatures.

<margin>Scheme of color.</margin>
With regard to the arrangement of these pictures, each is surrounded by a simple frame formed of bands of blue and red; in most cases, the miniatures reach across the whole width of the page. The coloring is heavy, painted in opaque *tempera* pigments with an undue preponderance of *minium* or red lead. White lead, yellow, brown, and red ochres are largely used, together with a variety of vegetable colors and the purple-red of the *kermes* beetle (*coccus*), but no gold is used, a bright yellow ochre being employed as a substitute<note>1. Further details with regard to these pigments are given below; see pages 239 to 249.</note>.

The costumes are partly ancient Greek and partly of later Roman fashion. A nimbus encircles each deity's head, and different colors are used to distinguish them. The nimbus of Zeus is purple, that of Venus is green; those of the other gods are mostly blue. To a large extent, the backgrounds of the pictures are not painted, but the creamy white of the vellum is left exposed<note>2. Reproductions of these miniatures were published by Cardinal Mai, *Picturae antiquissimae bellum Iliacum repraesentantes*, Milan, 1819. Far more accurate copies of some of the miniatures, but without color, are given by the *Palaeographical Society*, Plates 39, 40, 50, and 51.</note>.

<margin>The Vatican Virgil.</margin>
*The Virgil of the Vatican*; next in importance to the Ambrosian *Iliad*, among the existing examples of classical illuminated manuscripts, comes the manuscript of Virgil's poems (*Vat. No. 3225*) which is supposed to have been

# THE COSTUMES AND STYLE [CHAP. III.

written in the third or, more likely, the fourth century A.D. The text is written in large, handsome capitals, well-formed except that all the cross lines are too short; for example, T is written like this: <image-desc>a capital T where the horizontal crossbar is extremely short, appearing as a small hook or tick at the top of the vertical stem</image-desc>.

The entire manuscript, but especially the *Aeneid*, is decorated with fifty pictures in total, each framed by a simple border of colored bands. The style of these miniatures is very different from and artistically much inferior to that of the *Ambrosian Iliad*.

<margin>
Miniatures of the 5th century.
</margin>

The designs as a whole—in composition, drawing, and the costumes of the figures—belong to the fourth century. The details are coarse, the poses lack spirit, and the figures are clumsy. The backgrounds are painted in, and the coloring is dull in tone and heavy in texture, applied with a substantial amount of pigment (*impasto*). Gold, used as a fluid pigment rather than in leaf form, is used extensively for highlights on trees, mountains, building roofs, and the folds of drapery, especially where the fabric is red or purple. The male figures have reddish-brown skin, similar to many Pompeian wall paintings; they wear short tunics with cloaks thrown over their shoulders. Other figures wear a long *dalmatica* or tunic, decorated with two vertical purple stripes, closely resembling the tunics which have recently been found in great numbers in the late Roman tombs of the Fayum in Upper Egypt.

<margin>
Period of decline.
</margin>

Overall, the miniatures are neither graceful nor highly decorative; they were produced near the lowest point of classical artistic decline, shortly before the Byzantine revival under Justinian. Much of what has been written in their praise must be attributed to antiquarian enthusiasm rather than to fair criticism.<note>1. Some fairly accurate reproductions of these miniatures were published by Bartoli, *Antiquissimi Virgiliani Codicis fragmenta Bibl. Vat.*, Rome, 1741 and 1782. Examples from this and two other ancient but non-illuminated codices of Virgil in the Vatican library are provided by the *Palaeographical Society*, Plates 113 to 117.</note>

Before moving on to another category of manuscripts, it should be noted that a manuscript exists from the fourth or fifth century A.D. which is of special interest because of—

# CLASSICAL PAINTINGS. [CHAP. III.

conventional character. This is a Roman *Calendar*, which forms part of a manuscript in the Imperial library in Vienna. The ornaments have but little decorative merit, but they are of interest as showing that the illuminations in classical manuscripts were not always confined to subject pictures.

<margin>Copies of lost originals.</margin>
As a rule, it has not been sufficiently noticed that the style of miniature paintings in manuscripts of a considerably earlier date than either the Ambrosian *Iliad* or the Vatican Virgil is very fairly represented in various manuscripts of the tenth to the twelfth century, the illuminators of which have evidently copied, as accurately as they were able, miniatures in manuscripts of the first or second century AD.

The originals of these early Roman manuscripts do not now exist, and therefore the information as to their style and composition, which is given in the medieval copies, is of great interest.

<margin>Classical design.</margin>
<margin>Greco-Roman design.</margin>
A Greek twelfth-century *Psalter* in the Vatican library (No. 381) has one special picture which is obviously a careful copy of a miniature painting of the first century AD or even earlier: see figure 2. The subject is Orpheus seated on a rock playing to a circle of listening beasts together with two nymphs and a youthful Faun or shepherd. These figures are arranged so as to form a very graceful composition in a landscape with hills and trees. The figures are extremely graceful both in outline and in pose, showing a considerable trace of Greek influence. The whole design closely resembles in style some of the wall paintings in the so-called “House of Livia” on the Palatine Hill in Rome, of which figure 3 shows the scene of Io watched by Argus, and those in the now-destroyed villa which was discovered by the Tiber bank in the Farnesina Gardens<note>The most important of these paintings were removed from the walls of the villa and are now placed in the Museo delle Terme in Rome. The painting shown in figure 3 is still in its original location; that given in figure 4 is now in the Museum at Naples.</note>, and many of the better class of paintings on the walls of the houses of Pompeii. Of the latter, a good example is shown in figure 4, a painting the design of which has much fine Hellenic feeling in the grace of its form and the simplicity of the composition.

It highlights a twelfth-century Greek Psalter as a primary example of this Greco-Roman influence, comparing its design to wall paintings found in Pompeii and the House of Livia.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how classical artistic designs were preserved in later medieval manuscripts, transitioning from the discussion of copies of lost originals to specific examples of adapted iconography.</meta>

# CLASSICAL PAINTINGS. [CHAP. III.

<margin>Orpheus transformed into David.</margin>
Returning now to the previously mentioned *Psalter* in the Vatican, the scribe—likely a Greek monk who painted this miniature in the twelfth century¹—transformed it into a completely different subject: David playing the harp. He achieved this through the simple method of labeling each figure with a new name. Orpheus is called "David," while one of the nymphs who sits affectionately close to Orpheus (likely intended to be his wife, Eurydice) is labeled "Sophia" (Wisdom). Meanwhile, the other two figures are converted into local personifications to indicate the setting of the scene.

It is rare for a medieval copyist to have preserved the entire composition of a classical and pre-Christian subject in this way. However, it is common to find individual figures or parts of pictorial designs of similarly early dates among the manuscript illuminations of the ninth through twelfth centuries.

As an example of this, we may mention a painting in a tenth-century Greek *Psalter* in the Paris library (*Bibl. Nat.* No. 139). This represents the Prophet Isaiah standing and gazing up toward heaven in a very beautiful landscape, with trees growing from a lush, flower-speckled meadow. The somewhat stiff figure of the Prophet is Byzantine² rather than classical in style, but the other two figures introduced are
<margin>Graeco-Roman personifications.</margin>
purely Graeco-Roman in design. On one side is a personification of Night (ΝΥΞ), a very graceful standing female figure with part of her drapery floating in the wind. This forms a sort of curved canopy over her head, such as is so often represented above the heads of goddesses or nymphs on the reliefs of fine Graeco-Roman sarcophagi.

On the other side of the Prophet is a winged boy, like a youthful Eros, carrying a torch to symbolize the dawn.

The bold and very decorative, yet almost realistic, treatment of the foliage of the trees and of the flowers which are

<margin>Classical style.</margin>
sprinkled among the grass is purely classical in style, and the whole miniature shows that the tenth-century illuminator had before him some very fine manuscript from the early Imperial period. From this, he selected a picture which could be adapted to his subject through omissions and modifications; for the figure of the Prophet, he relied on another less ancient original, which still must have been several centuries older than his own time.

This explains what at first seems to be such a strange union in the same painting: very graceful single figures alongside others that are rigid and awkward. Likewise, great skill is shown in the drawing of individual figures, yet this is combined with a feeble and clumsy arrangement of the entire composition.

<margin>Byzantine style.</margin>
Fig. 5 shows a miniature of a very similar style representing the Prophet Ezekiel in the Valley of Dry Bones. It is taken from a manuscript of the *Sermons* of Saint Gregory Nazianzen, which was written for the Byzantine Emperor Basil, who reigned from 867 to 886. This figure primarily illustrates the Byzantine, rather than the Classical, element in the miniatures of this mixed style of art, though there is also a clear trace of Graeco-Roman influence in the Prophet’s finely designed drapery.

The curious union of two utterly different styles is well-exemplified in another of the miniatures in the previously mentioned *Psalter*. Here, David is represented like a Byzantine Emperor, crowned and wearing the richly embroidered *toga picta*, while holding an open book. The figure could easily pass for a representation of the Emperor Justinian, and the original painting was probably from that period, in the early part of the sixth century.

<margin>Graeco-Roman figures.</margin>
On each side of the Byzantine David is a female figure draped in most gracefully designed folds of pure Graeco-Roman style, providing a most striking contrast to the central figure. It is impossible to say whom these ladies represented in the original manuscript, but the tenth-century painter who illuminated the *Psalter* called them *Wisdom* and *Prophecy*, writing the names *Sophia* and *Prophetia* beside them.

## LATE CLASSICAL SURVIVAL. [CHAP. III.

<margin>*Value of late copies.*</margin>
Many other examples could be given to show that a more accurate understanding of classical illuminated manuscripts in the best Greco-Roman style can be gained from a study of the works of medieval copyists than from manuscripts which, though older, are of a late and degraded style like the famous illuminated Virgil of the Vatican¹.

After Rome had ceased to be the seat of government, Constantinople became the chief center for the production of illuminated manuscripts², but nevertheless the older classical style of drawing did survive in Italy to some extent, though in a very degraded form, until the thirteenth century, when Cimabue and his pupil Giotto inaugurated the brilliant Renaissance of Italian painting.

<margin>*Classical survival.*</margin>
The *Gospels*, for example, which St. Augustine is said to have brought with him to Britain in 597 AD, have paintings—enthroned figures of the Evangelists—which in design and color are purely in the late Roman style, unchanged by the widespread influence of Byzantine art at the time.

---

<note>¹ Several manuscripts of this class are described by H. Bordier, *Manuscrits Grecs de la Bibliothèque Nationale*, Paris, 1883.</note>
<note>² A great public library was founded by Constantine in New Rome and partially stocked with manuscripts transferred from the old capital. This library was rapidly enlarged by his sons and successors, and it was rebuilt on a grander scale by the Emperor Zeno after the building had been damaged by fire around the year 488 AD.</note>

<summary>This page explains how medieval copies often preserve high-quality classical artistic styles better than later original manuscripts and traces the survival of Roman artistic traditions in Italy and Britain until the Renaissance.</summary>
<keywords>illuminated manuscripts, Greco-Roman style, medieval copyists, Vatican Virgil, Constantinople, Cimabue, Giotto, St. Augustine, Byzantine art, Emperor Zeno</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of classical survivals in Western manuscripts to the specific history and origins of the Byzantine style, which emerged as Constantinople became the new center of the Roman world.</meta>

# CHAPTER IV.

## BYZANTINE MANUSCRIPTS.

<margin>Byzantine style.</margin>
The history of the origin, development, and decay of the Byzantine style in manuscripts, as in other branches of art, is a long and strange one¹. The origin of the Byzantine style dates from the time when Christianity became the state religion and when Constantine transferred the capital of the world from Rome to Byzantium.

In Russia and other eastern parts of Europe, the Byzantine style still exists, though in a sad state of decay. It exists not as an antiquarian revival, but as the latest link in a chain of unbroken tradition, going back without interruption to the age of Constantine in the early part of the fourth century after Christ.

<margin>Many strains of influence.</margin>
During the early years of the Eastern Empire, Constantinople, or "New Rome" as it was commonly called, became the world's chief center for the practice of all kinds of arts and handicrafts. Due to its central position midway between the East and the West, the styles and techniques of both regions met and were fused into a new stylistic development of a most remarkable kind. Western Europe, Asia Minor, Persia, and Egypt all contributed elements of both design and technical skill, which combined to create the new and, for a while, vigorously flourishing school of Byzantine art. The dull, lifeless forms of Roman art in its extreme degradation

---

<note>¹ For a valuable account of Byzantine manuscripts, see Kondakoff, *Histoire de l'Art Byzantin*, Paris, 1886—1891.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the Byzantine style of manuscript illumination, tracing its origins to Constantine’s establishment of the Eastern Empire and its unique fusion of Eastern and Western artistic influences. It also notes the style’s continued, though declining, presence in Eastern Europe as an unbroken historical tradition.</summary>
<keywords>Byzantine art, Constantinople, Constantine, Eastern Empire, manuscript illumination, New Rome, artistic fusion, Roman art degradation, Kondakoff</keywords>

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Byzantine style, focusing on the technical expertise of its craftsmen and the luxurious materials used in Imperial manuscripts.</meta>

<header>THE CHIEF CHARACTERISTICS [CHAP. IV.</header>

were again brought back to new life and beauty in the hands of these Byzantine craftsmen, who became, as it were, the heirs and inheritors of the art and technique of all the major countries of antiquity.

<margin>Technical skill.</margin>
In architecture, mosaic work, metalwork of all kinds, and textile weaving, the craftsmen of New Rome reached the highest level of technical skill and decorative beauty. Similarly, a new and brilliant school of manuscript illumination was soon formed, and Constantinople became the chief center for the production of all kinds of manuscripts for several centuries.

The Oriental element in Byzantine art shows itself in a love of extreme splendor and the most abundant use of gold, silver, and the brightest colors.

<margin>Murex purple.</margin>
Manuscripts written in burnished gold on vellum stained with the brilliant purple from the *murex* shell were produced in large numbers, especially for the private use of the Byzantine Emperors. This *murex* purple, produced with an immense expenditure of labor, came to be considered the special mark of imperial rank¹. A golden inkstand containing purple ink was kept by a special official in waiting, and no one but the Emperor himself might, under heavy penalties, use the purple ink for any purpose; these sumptuous gold and purple manuscripts were for a long time written only for imperial use.

<margin>Gold and purple gospels.</margin>
The principal class of manuscripts written either partly or entirely in this costly fashion was *Books of the Gospels*; many magnificent examples of these still exist, dating not only from the early Byzantine period but down to the ninth or tenth century. In these manuscripts, the burnished gold and the brilliantly colored pigments used for the illuminations are still as bright and fresh in appearance as ever, but the *murex* purple with which the vellum leaves were dyed—rather than painted—has usually lost much of its original splendor of color.

---

¹ The title *Porphyrogenitus*, “Born in the purple,” referred to the fact that Byzantine Empresses gave birth to their children in a magnificent room lined with slabs of polished porphyry.

<summary>This page describes the technical mastery of Byzantine craftsmen and the development of a prestigious school of manuscript illumination in Constantinople. It highlights the use of gold ink and rare murex purple dye as exclusive symbols of imperial status in luxury Gospel books.</summary>
<keywords>Byzantine art, technical skill, manuscript illumination, Constantinople, murex purple, imperial rank, Books of the Gospels, Porphyrogenitus, vellum, gold ink</keywords>

--- Page 78 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Byzantine style, shifting from the luxurious materials used in imperial manuscripts to the rigid, unchanging nature of the style itself and its governance by ecclesiastical rules.</meta>

<header>CHAP. IV.] ON THE BYZANTINE STYLE. 47</header>

<margin>Monotony of style.</margin>
Before describing the characteristics of Byzantine illuminated manuscripts, it is worth noting that the Byzantine style is unique in the artistic history of the world because of the way it rapidly crystallized into rigidly fixed forms. It then continued for century after century with marvelously little modification or development in color, drawing, or composition.

This absence of any real living development was due to the fact that paintings of all kinds in the Eastern Church—from a colossal mural picture down to a manuscript miniature—were produced by ecclesiastics and for the Church, under a strictly applied series of <term>hieratic</term> rules.

<margin>Hieratic rules.</margin>
The drawing, the pose, the colors of the drapery of every Saint, and the scheme of composition of all sacred figure subjects came gradually to be defined by ecclesiastical rules, which each painter was bound to obey. Thus it happens that during the many centuries covered by the Byzantine style of art, though there are periods of decay and revival of artistic skill, there is a most remarkable monotony in style. This makes it especially difficult to judge the date of a Byzantine painting from internal evidence. In manuscripts, the <term>paleographic</term> evidence, not the artistic, is the best guide, aided of course by various small technical peculiarities, and also by the amount of skill and power of drawing displayed in the paintings.

<margin>Absence of change.</margin>
Long after the capture of Constantinople by the Ottoman Turks in 1453, the Byzantine style of painting survived; and even at the present day, the monks of Mt. Athos execute large wall paintings which, as far as their style is concerned, might appear to be the work of many centuries ago. M. Didron found the monastic painters in one of the Mount Athos monasteries using a treatise called the <term>'Ερμηνεία τῆς ζωγραφικῆς</term> <note>The Hermeneia, or "Explanation of Painting."</note>, in which directions are given on how every figure and subject is to be treated, and which describes the old traditional forms without any perceptible modification¹. The proportions of the human form are laid down after the characteristic

---

¹ A translation of this curious treatise was published by Didron and Durand, in their *Manuel d'iconographie chrétienne* <note>Manual of Christian Iconography</note>; Paris, 1845.

<summary>This page explains why Byzantine art remained remarkably consistent for centuries, attributing its lack of stylistic change to strict ecclesiastical and hieratic rules. Because of this monotony, scholars must rely more on paleographic evidence than artistic style to date Byzantine manuscripts.</summary>

<keywords>Byzantine style, manuscript illumination, Eastern Church, hieratic rules, paleography, Constantinople, Mt. Athos, M. Didron, Hermeneia, iconography</keywords>

# THE VIENNA "GENESIS." [CHAP. IV.]

...slender Byzantine models—the complete body, for example, being nine heads in height.

<margin>5th-century manuscript of Genesis.</margin>
The earliest Byzantine manuscript known to exist today is a fragment of the *Book of Genesis*, now in the Imperial Library of Vienna, which dates from the latter part of the fifth century. This fragment consists of twenty-four leaves of purple-dyed vellum, illuminated with miniatures on both sides. In the main, the designs are feeble in composition and weak in drawing, belonging more to the final decadence of Roman classical art than to the still-undeveloped Byzantine style. This style was soon to grow with great artistic spirit and strong decorative power—a completely new birth of aesthetic concepts, the brilliance of which is all the more striking because it followed so closely upon the degraded, lifeless, and worn-out art of the Western Empire. In this manuscript of *Genesis*, there is very little promise of the Renaissance that was so close <margin>Weak drawing.</margin> at hand. The drawing of each figure, though sometimes graceful in pose, is quite weak, and the painter hardly aimed for anything like true composition; his figures merely stand in long rows, with little or nothing to group them together. Figure 6 shows examples of two of the best miniatures, representing the story of the accusation of Joseph by Potiphar's wife. In every way, this *Genesis* manuscript forms a striking contrast to the delicate beauty and strongly decorative feeling seen in a work from only a few years later: the famous *Dioscorides* of the Princess Juliana.

<margin>The Dioscorides of circa 500 A.D.</margin>
Among all existing Byzantine manuscripts, perhaps the most important for its remarkable beauty as well as its early date is this Greek *codex*¹ of Dioscorides' work on *Botany*, which is now in the Imperial Library in Vienna², listed as No. 5 in the catalog. The date of this manuscript can be fixed to about the year 500 A.D. by the record it contains, stating that it was written and illuminated for the—

---

<note>¹ All manuscripts described in this book, from the Byzantine school onwards, may be understood to be in *codex* form and written on *vellum*, unless they are otherwise described.</note>

<note>² Published by Lambecius, *Comment. sur la Bibl. de Vienne*, 1776, Vol. III.</note>

It highlights the transition toward the vibrant decorative power of the emerging Byzantine style.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of early Byzantine manuscripts, transitioning from the Vienna Genesis to a detailed description of the "Dioscorides of Juliana" (the Vienna Dioscurides), focusing on its history, artistic styles, and specific miniatures.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>50</page-num>
<header>50 THE DIOSCORIDES OF [CHAP. IV.</header>

<margin>Juliana's Dioscorides.</margin> Princess Juliana Anicia was the daughter of Flavius Anicius Olybrius, who was Emperor for part of the year 472, and his wife Galla Placidia. Juliana Anicia died in 527.

This beautiful manuscript, produced in Constantinople, contains five large and elaborate miniatures and a great number of vignettes representing various plants. The fifth of the large miniatures consists of a central group framed by two squares interlaced within a circle. The plait pattern on the bands forming the framework and the overall design closely resemble a fine mosaic pavement from the second century AD. The resemblance is far too close to be accidental; indeed, this manuscript is not the only example we have of miniature painters copying patterns and motifs from earlier mosaic floors.

<margin>Portrait figure.</margin> The central group in this beautiful full-page painting represents Juliana Anicia, for whom the manuscript was written, enthroned between standing allegorical female figures. Minutely painted figures of Cupids, engaged in a variety of handicrafts and arts, fill the small spaces in the framework.

<margin>Inferior paintings.</margin> In these paintings, we find a curious combination of different styles; the enthroned figure of the Princess is in the stiff Byzantine style, while the attendant figures and the little Cupids are drawn in an almost purely classical manner. This manuscript forms a link between the classical or Greco-Roman style and the Christian or Byzantine style. Other paintings in the same manuscript are of much lower quality in design, sharing the characteristics of the late Roman decline rather than the superior, earlier art of the picture mentioned above. Fig. 7 shows one of these. It represents Dioscorides seated on a sort of throne; in front of him is a female figure, *Euresis* (*Discovery*), presenting to him the magic plant *mandragora* (mandrake). The dying dog refers to the popular belief, recorded by Josephus, regarding how the mandrake was gathered. When pulled from the ground, the mandrake uttered a scream that caused the death of any living creature that heard it. It was therefore customary to tie a dog to the plant and move to a safe distance before calling it, thereby causing the dog to drag the plant out

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Byzantine artistic techniques, specifically how gold-line work in manuscripts imitated the appearance of cloisonné enamel.</meta>

<page-num>52</page-num>
<header>ENAMELS IMITATED IN MINIATURES. [CHAP. IV.</header>

is very widely used in Byzantine art, both in the colossal pictures of the wall mosaics and also in most of the finest class of illuminated manuscripts.

<margin>*Cloisonné enamel.*</margin>
In this use of gold, in thin delicate lines which strengthen the drawing, we have a very distinct copying of another quite different art: that of the worker in enamelled gold, an art which was practiced in Constantinople with wonderful taste and skill. The kind of enamel which was so often imitated by the manuscript illuminator is now called cloisonné enamel from the thin slips of gold or *cloisons* which separate one color from another and mark out the chief lines of the design. So closely did many of the illuminators copy designs in this cloisonné that very often one sees manuscript miniatures which look at first sight as if they were actual pieces of enamel. In other ways, too, the art of the goldsmith had considerable influence on Byzantine illuminations; and the designs of the mosaic worker and the miniaturist acted and reacted upon each other, so that we sometimes see an elaborate painting in a book which looks like a design for a wall mosaic; or again, the gorgeous glass mosaics with gold grounds on the vaults and walls of Byzantine churches frequently look like magnified leaves cut out of some gorgeously illuminated manuscript.

<margin>*The pure Byzantine style.*</margin>
It was only for a short period that manuscripts were executed at Constantinople which, in their miniatures, were links between the classical and the Byzantine style. Thus we find that the famous Greek manuscript of *Cosmas Indicopleustes* <note>The OCR reads "Indopleustes," a variant or misspelling of Indicopleustes.</note> in the Vatican library (No. 699) is of the pure and fully developed Byzantine style, with its formal attitudes, its rigid drapery, its elongated proportions of the figures, and stiff, monotonous schemes of composition, such as grew to be accepted as the one sacred style; as such, it has been preserved by the Eastern Church down to the present century.

This manuscript of Cosmas is certainly a work of Justinian’s time—the first half of the sixth century A.D.—though it has usually been attributed to the ninth century. It really is but little later than the Dioscorides of Juliana, and yet it has but

<summary>This page explains how Byzantine manuscript illumination imitated cloisonné enamel work and wall mosaics, leading to the rigid, "sacred" Byzantine style exemplified by the Cosmas Indicopleustes manuscript.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Byzantine artistic styles, moving from the imitation of enamels to the specific characteristics of early Gospel manuscripts and the first known artistic depictions of the Crucifixion.</meta>

<page-num>53</page-num>
<header>CHAP. IV.] EARLY PAINTING OF THE CRUCIFIXION.</header>

...little trace of the older classical style, either in drawing, composition, or color¹.

The Laurentian library in Florence possesses a manuscript of the *Gospels* which, though poor as a work of art, has several points of special interest. A contemporary note in the *codex* records that it was written in the year 586 by the Priest Rabula in the Monastery of St John at Zagba in Mesopotamia.

<margin>Early crucifixion.</margin>
Its illuminations are weak in drawing, coarse in execution, and harsh in coloring, but one of them, representing the Crucifixion of our Lord between the two thieves, is noticeable as being the earliest known example of this subject. The primitive Christian Church avoided scenes representing Christ’s Death and Passion, preferring to suggest them only by means of types and symbols taken from Old Testament History.

This and other subsequent paintings of the Crucifixion treat the subject in a very conventional way, and it is not until about the thirteenth century that we find the Death of Christ represented with anything like realism.

In the *Gospels* of the Priest Rabula, Christ is represented crowned with gold, not with thorns; He wears a long tunic of Imperial purple reaching to the feet. The arms are stretched out horizontally, an impossible attitude for a crucified person, and four nails are represented piercing the hands and both feet separately.

<margin>Oriental influence.</margin>
It appears to have been the gloomy Oriental influence that gradually introduced scenes of martyrdom, with horrors of every description, into Christian art, which originally had been imbued with a far healthier and more cheerful spirit—a survival from the wholesome classical treatment of death and the grave. Hell with its revolting horrors and hideous demons was an invention of a still later and intellectually more degraded period.

<margin>MSS. of the Gospels.</margin>
<term>Evangeliaria</term> or *manuscripts of the Gospels*. One of the most important classes of Byzantine manuscripts, and the—

---

¹ Copies of some of the miniatures in the Vatican *Cosmas* are given by N. Kondakoff, *Histoire de l’Art Byzantin*, Paris, 1886, Vol. I. pp. 142 to 152.

# MAGNIFICENT EVANGELIARIA [CHAP. IV.

<page-num>54</page-num>

<margin>Manuscripts of the Gospels.</margin>
Some of the most magnificent examples of these currently in existence are the *Books of the Gospels*—already mentioned on page 46—which were occasionally written, either entirely or partially, in gold letters on leaves of purple-dyed vellum.

<margin>The four Evangelists.</margin>
These imperially magnificent manuscripts are usually decorated with five full-page paintings placed at the beginning of the <term>codex</term>. These five pictures represent the four Evangelists, each enthroned like a Byzantine emperor under an arched canopy supported by Corinthian columns of marble or <term>porphyry</term>. Each Evangelist sits holding the manuscript of his *Gospel* in his hand, or, in some cases, he is shown writing it. In the earlier manuscripts, St. John is correctly represented as an aged man with a white beard; however, in later times, St. John was always depicted as a beardless youth, even in illuminations that show him writing his Gospel on the Island of Patmos, as seen in early fifteenth-century *Books of Hours*. Next comes the fifth miniature representing "Christ in Majesty," usually enthroned within an oval or <term>vesica-shaped</term> aureole. He sits on a rainbow, and at His feet is a globe representing the earth, or in some cases a small figure of <term>Tellus</term> or <term>Atlas</term> with the same symbolic meaning.

<margin>The Canons of Eusebius.</margin>
Other highly decorated pages in these Byzantine *Gospels* are those containing the "Canons" of Bishop Eusebius, a set of ten tables providing lists of parallel passages in the four Gospels. These tables are usually framed by columns supporting a semicircular arch, richly decorated with architectural and floral ornaments in gold and colors. Birds, especially doves and peacocks, are frequently introduced in the spandrels over the arches; they are often arranged in pairs drinking out of a central vase or chalice—a motif that occurs very often among the reliefs on the sarcophagi and marble screens of early Byzantine churches in both Italy and the East <note>St. Mark's in Venice and the churches of Ravenna and Constantinople are full of examples of this design.</note>. These birds appear to be purely ornamental, despite the many attempts that have been made to discover symbolic meanings in them. Other birds, such as cocks, quails, and partridges, are commonly used in these

<summary>This page details the opulent decoration of Byzantine Gospel books, describing the standardized depictions of the four Evangelists, "Christ in Majesty," and the ornate "Canons of Eusebius." It highlights recurring artistic motifs, such as the use of gold letters on purple vellum and the inclusion of symbolic or decorative bird imagery.</summary>

<header>CHAP. IV.] OR TEXTUS MANUSCRIPTS.</header>
<page-num>55</page-num>

<margin>Sasanian style.</margin>
...decorative illuminations, and this class of ornament was probably derived from Persia under the Sasanian Dynasty, when decorative art and skillful handicrafts flourished to a very remarkable extent¹.

Among the most sumptuous and beautiful illuminations found in these Byzantine *Gospels* are the headings and beginnings of books written in very large golden capitals, so that six or seven letters frequently occupy the entire page. These letters are painted over a richly decorated background covered with floreated ornament, and the whole is framed in an elaborate border, all glowing with the most brilliant colors and brightened by burnished gold of the highest decorative beauty².

<margin>Textus for the High Altar.</margin>
These sumptuous *Evangeliaria*, or <term>*Textus*</term> as they were often called, soon became something more than merely a magnificent book. They developed into one of the most important pieces of furniture belonging to the High Altar in all significant Cathedral and Abbey churches³. Throughout the entire medieval period, every wealthy church possessed one of these magnificently written <term>*Textus*</term> or *Books of the Gospels* bound in costly covers of gold or silver, thickly studded with jewels. This <term>*Textus*</term> was placed on the High Altar before the celebration of Mass, during which it was used for the reading of the Gospel.

The jewel-studded covers had on one side a representation

---

<note>¹ This Sasanian art was an inheritance from ancient Babylon and Assyria and was the ancestor of what in later times has been called Arab art, though the quite inartistic Arabs appear to have derived it from the Persians whom they conquered and forcibly converted to the Muslim Faith.</note>

<note>² The gold in even the finest Byzantine manuscripts is never as sumptuous or as highly burnished as that in manuscripts of the fourteenth century, because it was usually applied as a fluid pigment, or at least not over the best kind of highly raised ground or <term>mordant</term>, which is described below on page 234.</note>

<note>³ In early times, and indeed throughout the whole medieval period, very few objects of any kind were placed upon the High Altar even in the most magnificently furnished churches. In addition to the chalice and paten and the <term>Textus</term>, the only ornaments usually allowed were a small crucifix and two candlesticks. The modern system of crowding the <term>mensa</term> of the altar with many candles and flowers did not begin until after the Reformation.</note>

<header>GOLD ENCASED TEXTUS. [CHAP. IV.]</header>
<page-num>56</page-num>

<margin>Textus used as a Pax.</margin>
...of Christ's crucifixion, executed in enamel or else in gold relief, and the book was used to serve the purpose of a <term>Pax</term>, being handed around among the ministers of the Altar for the ceremonial kiss of peace, which in primitive times had been exchanged among the members of the congregation themselves. One of the most magnificent examples of these <term>Textus</term> is the one now in the possession of Lord Ashburnham, the covers of which are among the most important and beautiful examples of the early English goldsmith's and jeweler's art which now exist <note>¹ Fine colored plates of this wonderful Textus-cover were published in 1888 by the Society of Antiquaries in their *Vetusta Monumenta*.</note>.

<margin>The Textus at Durham.</margin>
An interesting description of the <term>Textus</term> which, until the Reformation, belonged to the High Altar of Durham Cathedral, is given in the *Rites and Monuments of Durham*, written in 1593 by a survivor from the suppressed and plundered Abbey <note>² Published in 1844 by the Surtees Society of Durham.</note>, who in his old age wrote down his recollections of the former glories of the Church. He writes, "the <term>Gospeller</term> <note>³ The "Gospeller" was the officiating Deacon; the Sub-deacon being called the "Epistoller."</note> carried a marvelous **FAIR BOOK**, which had the Epistles and Gospels in it, and laid it on the Altar; this book had on the outside of the covering the picture of our Savior Christ, all of silver, of goldsmith's work, and all partially gilded, very fine to behold; which book served for the **PAX** in the Mass."

These <term>Textus</term> were not infrequently written wholly in gold on purple-stained vellum, not only during the earliest and best period of Byzantine art but also occasionally by the illuminators of the age of Charles the Great.

<margin>Weak drawing of the figure.</margin>
Returning now to the general question of the style of Byzantine art, it should be observed that, though little knowledge of the human form is shown by the miniaturists, they were nonetheless able to produce highly dignified compositions, very strong in decorative effect. Study of the nude form was strictly prohibited by the Church; and the beauty of the human figure was regarded as a snare and a danger to minds

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the artistic style found in religious manuscripts and mosaics, focusing on the stylized nature of figure painting intended to direct the viewer's mind toward spiritual rather than worldly matters.</meta>

<header>CHAP. IV.] UNREAL FIGURE PAINTING.</header>

which should be fixed upon the imaginary glories of another world. What grace and dignity there is in Byzantine figure painting depends chiefly on the skillful treatment of the drapery with simple folds modeled in gracefully curving lines.

<margin>Livid flesh color.</margin>
The utmost splendor of gold and color is lavished on this drapery, and on the backgrounds, border-frames, and other accessories, while the coloring of the flesh, in faces, hands, and feet, is commonly unpleasant; with, in many cases, an excessive use of green in the shadows, which gives an unhealthy look to the faces. This copious use of green in flesh tints is especially apparent in the later Byzantine paintings, and again in the Italian imitations of Byzantine art. Even paintings by Cimabue and some of his followers, in the second half of the thirteenth century, are disfigured by the flesh in shadow being largely painted with <term>terra verde</term> <note>¹ The remarkable artistic advance which was made by Giotto is to be seen not only in his improved and more realistic drawing, but also in his freedom from the long-established abuse of green in his flesh painting, for which he substituted a warmer and healthier tint.</note>.

<margin>Monastic bigotry.</margin>
The monastic bigotry, which prohibited study either of the living model or of the beauties of classical sculpture, tended to foster a strongly conventional element in Art, which for certain decorative purposes was of the highest possible value. Anything like realism is quite unsuited both for colossal mural frescoes or mosaics and for miniature paintings in an illuminated manuscript.

<margin>Fine early mosaics.</margin>
Thus, for example, the existing mosaics on the west front of St. Mark’s Basilica in Venice <note>² Of the original mosaics on the west facade of St. Mark’s, only one remains of the original, highly decorative twelfth-century mosaics. The rest, shown in Gentile Bellini’s picture of St. Mark’s, have all been replaced by later mosaics. Inside the church, happily, the old mosaics still, in most places, exist; see p. 61.</note>, which were copied from noble paintings by Titian and Tintoretto, are immeasurably inferior to the earlier mosaics with stiff, hieratic forms designed after Byzantine models—as for example the mosaics in the Apse of SS. Cosmas and Damian in Rome, executed for Pope Felix IV from 526 to 530; see fig. 8.

So, again, the skillfully drawn and modeled figures in a manuscript executed by Giulio Clovio in the sixteenth

It argues that monastic restrictions on studying realism actually benefited decorative arts like mosaics and manuscript miniatures, which are better suited to conventional, non-realistic forms.</summary>

simple, rhythmical beauty which we find in any Byzantine manuscript of a good period <note>¹ See page 202 for an account of Giulio Clovio.</note>.

<margin>
Limitations of Byzantine Art.
</margin>
It should, however, be noted that in Byzantine art this conventional treatment of the human form is carried too far. Therefore, as splendid as a fine Byzantine manuscript usually is, it falls far short of the almost perfect beauty that may be seen in Anglo-Norman and French illuminated manuscripts of the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries. These include such marvels of beauty, for example, as French manuscripts of the *Apocalypse* produced in the first half of the fourteenth century in Northern France; see below, page 118.

Until the eighth century, Byzantine art, both in manuscripts

<summary>This page argues that while Byzantine art possesses a unique decorative dignity, its rigid conventions eventually limited its beauty compared to the later Anglo-Norman and French illuminated manuscripts of the 13th and 14th centuries.</summary>
<keywords>Byzantine style, SS. Cosmas and Damian, mosaic, Iordanes, Anglo-Norman, French illuminated manuscripts, Apocalypse, Giulio Clovio, art history</keywords>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Byzantine art's history, moving from its earlier technical progress to the artistic decline caused by the Iconoclast schism and the subsequent shift of manuscript production to the Frankish Empire.</meta>

# CHAP. IV. ICONOCLAST SCHISM.

<margin>*Edict against statues.*</margin>
and in other branches of art, continued to advance in technical skill, though little change or development of style took place. In the eighth century, the Iconoclast schism—fostered by the Emperor Leo III the Isaurian, an uncultured and ignorant soldier who began by issuing an edict against image-worship in the year 726 A.D.—dealt a blow to Byzantine art that caused a very serious decline during the ninth and tenth centuries, especially in Constantinople, which up to that time had been one of the primary literary and artistic centers of the Christian world.

Pictures of all kinds, as well as statues, were destroyed by iconoclast fanatics, and the cause of learning suffered almost as much as the arts of painting and sculpture.

<margin>*Frankish manuscripts.*</margin>
One result of this schismatic outbreak was that Constantinople ceased to be one of the chief centers for the production of beautiful illuminated manuscripts. Various Frankish cities, such as Aix-la-Chapelle and Tours, took its place under the enlightened patronage of Charles the Great, Emperor of the West. In the second half of the ninth century, with the help of the famous Northumbrian scholar and scribe Alcuin of York, he brought about a wonderful revival of literature and the illuminator's art in various cities and monasteries within the Western Empire.

<margin>*Byzantine decline.*</margin>
By the end of the eleventh century, Byzantine art practiced in its original home had reached its lowest possible level. For example, a manuscript of some of the works of St. Chrysostom (Paris, *Bibl. Nat. Coislin.*, 79) contains miniatures whose figures are mere sack-like bundles with little or no suggestion of the human form. The artist's entire skill was spent on painting the elaborate patterns on the clothing; he did not even attempt drawing or composition.

Fig. 9 shows a miniature from this manuscript, representing the Greek Emperor enthroned between four courtiers and two allegorical figures of *Truth* and *Justice*. The Emperor is Nicephoros Botaniates, who reigned from 1078 to 1081. An equally striking example of the degradation of Byzantine art in Germany is illustrated on page 78.

<summary>The Iconoclast schism under Emperor Leo III caused a significant decline in Byzantine art and learning, prompting a shift in manuscript production toward Frankish centers under the patronage of Charlemagne and Alcuin of York. By the late eleventh century, Byzantine artistic quality had deteriorated to the point where human figures were rendered as shapeless forms focused only on decorative patterns.</summary>

<keywords>Iconoclast Schism, Leo III, Byzantine art, Constantinople, Frankish manuscripts, Charlemagne, Alcuin of York, St. Chrysostom, Nicephoros Botaniates, manuscript illumination, Aix-la-Chapelle, Tours</keywords>

### CHAP. IV. MONOTONY OF BYZANTINE ART.

<margin>
Lack of life in Byzantine Art.
</margin>
After this period of decay during the tenth and eleventh centuries, Byzantine art began to revive, largely under the influence of the West. The original life and spirit had, however, passed away, and the subsequent history of Byzantine art is one of dull monotony and growing weakness—the inevitable result of a continual copying and recopying of older models.

It is rather as a modifying influence on the art of the West that Byzantine painting continued to possess real importance. As a distinct and isolated school, Constantinople fell into the background at the time of the iconoclasts and never again came to the front as an artistic center of real importance<note>1</note>.

---

<note>1. Mr. M. R. James has pointed out to me an interesting example of similar designs being used by manuscript illuminators and by mosaic workers. The designs of the miniatures in a fifth or sixth-century manuscript of *Genesis* in the British Museum (*Otho, B, vi*) are in many cases identical to those of the twelfth and thirteenth-century mosaics in Saint Mark's at Venice; see Tikkanen, *Genesis-bilder*, Berlin.</note>

It notes that while Constantinople faded as an artistic center, it remained significant for its influence on Western art and manuscript traditions.</summary>

## CHAPTER V.

### MANUSCRIPTS OF THE CAROLINGIAN PERIOD.

<margin>The Age of Charles the Great.</margin>
The age of Charles the Great and his successors. Charles the Great, who was elected King of the Franks in 768 and became Emperor of the West in the year 800, did much to foster all branches of art—architecture, bronze founding, goldsmith’s work, and more especially the art of writing and illuminating manuscripts. The Imperial Capital, Aix-la-Chapelle (Aachen), became a busy center for arts and crafts of all kinds, and various monasteries throughout the Frankish kingdom became schools of manuscript illumination of a very high standard of excellence.

<margin>Alcuin of York.</margin>
It was especially with the aid of a famous English scholar and manuscript writer, Alcuin of York<note>1</note>, that Charles the Great brought about such a remarkable revival of both letters and the illuminator’s art, and created what may be called the Anglo-Carolingian school of manuscripts. From 796 until his death in 804, Alcuin was Abbot of the Benedictine monastery of Saint Martin at Tours; and there he carried out various literary works for Charles the Great and supervised the production of a large number of richly illuminated manuscripts. Alcuin’s most important literary work was the revision of the Latin text of the Bible, the *Vulgate*, which

---

The Gospels of Alcuin.

The British Museum possesses (*Additional Manuscripts, No. 10546*) a magnificently illuminated copy of the *Vulgate* as revised by Alcuin, which there is every reason to believe is the actual manuscript that was prepared for Charles the Great either by Alcuin himself or under his immediate supervision. This splendid manuscript is a large folio in delicate and beautifully formed *minuscule* characters, with the beginnings of chapters in fine *uncials*; it is written in two columns on the purest vellum. The miniature paintings in this manuscript show the united influence of various schools of manuscript art. The figure subjects are mainly classical in style, with fine architectural backgrounds in the Roman style, drawn with unusual detail and accuracy, and even with fairly correct perspective. The initial letters and all the conventional ornaments show the Northern artistic strain which

# CHAP. V.] CELTIC-CAROLINGIAN STYLE.

<meta>catchword: grown</meta>
<margin>Celtic influence.</margin>
developed from a combination of two very different styles: that of Rome, as introduced by St. Augustine, and the Celtic style, which the monks of Ireland and Lindisfarne had brought to such marvelous perfection in the seventh century.

Figure 10 shows an initial of the Celtic-Carolingian type, with a goldsmith's pattern on the shaft of the *P* and a bird of Oriental type forming the loop; and figure 11 shows a large initial *B* in which the Oriental element is very strong; compare figure 13 on page 68.

The **Carolingian** class of manuscripts in this way combined many different strains of influence—native **Frankish**, **Classical**, **Oriental and English**—all modified by the **Byzantine** love for **gorgeous colors**, shining gold and silver, and **purple-dyed vellum**. A significant number of manuscripts were written during the reign of Charles the Great in letters of gold on purple vellum, like those prepared in earlier times for the Byzantine Emperors. A manuscript *Book of the Gospels* of this magnificent class was given by Pope Leo X to Henry VIII of England in return for the presentation copy of his work against Luther, titled *Assertio Septem Sacramentorum*, which the king had sent to the Pope in 1521 as proof of his allegiance to the Catholic Faith and the Holy See. This magnificent *Textus* later entered the Hamilton collection through Mr. Beckford of Fonthill and was subsequently bought by Mr. Quaritch¹.
<margin>Henry VIII's Gospels.</margin>

As was the case with the earlier Byzantine manuscripts, the most magnificent books produced in the Carolingian period were this kind of *Evangeliaria* or *Books of the Gospels*. Although they differ in the details of their ornamentation, these later *Gospels* are decorated with the same set of miniature subjects that occur in the Byzantine Gospels. The Paris library possesses a fine typical example of this (*Bibl. Nat. Nouv. Acq. Lat. 1993*), a richly decorated and signed *Evangeliarium*.
<margin>Carolingian Gospels.</margin>

# CHAP. V. CELTIC AND ORIENTAL INFLUENCE.
<sig>5—2</sig>

<margin>Gospels of Godesscalc.</margin>
...which was written for Charles the Great in 781 by the scribe and illuminator Godesscalc. Every page is sumptuously ornamented with large initials and a border in brilliant burnished gold, silver, and bright colors; and there are also six full-page miniatures, the first four representing the four Evangelists enthroned in the usual way. The fifth has a painting of *Christ in Majesty* with one hand holding a book, the other raised in blessing; see fig. 12. The sixth miniature represents the Fountain of Life. In all these paintings the backgrounds are very rich and decorative, with a greater variety and more fancifully designed ornament than is to be found in Byzantine manuscripts of a similar class, owing, of course, to the introduction of the many different elements of design which were combined with great taste and skill by the Carolingian illuminators.

<margin>Oriental influence.</margin>
In this and many other manuscripts of the same class a very distinct Semitic or Persian strain of influence can be traced in much of the rich conventional ornament. Very beautiful and highly decorative forms and patterns were derived from Oriental sources <note>See for example the beautiful patterns of the woven hangings behind the enthroned figure of Christ shown on fig. 12; compare also page 84.</note>, owing to the active import into France and Germany of fine Persian carpets and textile fabrics from Muslim looms in Syria, Sicily (especially Palermo), and from other parts of the Arab world; all these textiles were designed with consummate taste and skill both in color and drawing.

<margin>Sicilian silk cope.</margin>
Fig. 13 shows a fine specimen of woven silk from the Arab looms of Syria. It was used as an Imperial cope or mantle by various German Emperors; in the center is a palm tree, and on each side a lion devouring a camel, treated in a very decorative and masterly manner. The form of the conventional foliage on the lions' bodies is imitated in many manuscript illuminations, as, for example, in the ornaments of the initial *B* shown in fig. 11, page 64.

One important characteristic of the Carolingian manuscripts is their extreme splendor. The freely used burnished gold is often made more magnificent by the contrast of no

# CHAP. V.] CAROLINGIAN MINIATURES. 69

<margin>*Splendor of MSS.*</margin>
...less brilliant silver. Purple-stained vellum was widely used, and all the pigments are of the most gorgeous hues that great technical skill could produce. And yet, in spite of all this magnificence of shining metals and bright colors, the effect is never harsh or gaudy, thanks to the taste and judgment shown by the illuminators in the way they broke up their colors—avoiding large, unrelieved masses—and in the arrangement of the colors so as to give a general effect of harmony in spite of the great chromatic force of the separate parts.

<margin>*Technical methods.*</margin>
The somewhat realistic way of representing the Evangelists as aged, white-haired men, which occurs in Byzantine manuscripts, is replaced in the Carolingian *Gospels* by a more conventional treatment; thus, they are as a rule represented as youthful, beardless men of an idealized type. The general treatment of the figure is flat, with little or no light and shade or modeling of any kind. The drapery is represented by strong, dark lines applied over a flatly laid wash of pigment. The painter first drew his outlines with a fine brush dipped in red, and then filled in the intermediate spaces with a wash of color mixed with a large proportion of gummy medium, so that a very glossy, lustrous surface was produced. The folds of the drapery and the rest of the internal drawing of the figures were put in after the application of the flat base color. This method closely resembles the process of the early Greek vase-painters. In order to give a richness of effect by using a thick body of color, the illuminator commonly applied his flat tints in two or even three distinct washes, a method which is recommended by Theophilus<note>Theophilus, *Schedula diversarum Artium*, I. 34; this work is frequently cited in Chapter 15.</note> and other early writers on the technique of illumination.

<margin>*Gospels at Vienna.*</margin>
Another *Book of the Gospels* which belonged to Charles the Great, now preserved in the Imperial Treasury at Vienna, is decidedly inferior as a work of art to the Paris manuscript mentioned above. In it, the influence of the weakened Roman style is much stronger; the detail is far less refined and decorative, in spite of a copious use of burnished gold. This inferiority is due mainly to the absence of that Northumbrian

<header>LATER CAROLINGIAN SCHOOL. [CHAP. V.</header>
<page-num>70</page-num>

influence, to which the best Carolingian manuscripts owe so much of their beauty.

<margin>Successors of Charles.</margin>
*Manuscripts of the later Carolingian school.* Under the successors of Charles the Great, the art of illuminating manuscripts continued to flourish, and in the <gloss>ninth century</gloss>, under his grandsons Lothair and Charles the Bald, it reached the peak of its development. During this century, decorative splendor of a very high order was achieved, despite very little progress in the ability to depict the human form. Gold, silver, ultramarine, and brilliant pigments of all kinds were skillfully used; the subjects for miniatures became more varied, and details were more delicate and highly finished <note>¹ See Janitschek, *Die künstlerische Ausstattung des Ada-Evangeliars und die Karolingische Buchmalerei*; folio, Leipzig, 1889.</note>.

<margin>Portrait figures.</margin>
Portraits of kings were often introduced at the beginning of books from this period, a fashion which in later times was extended to patrons of art and learning other than royalty. A great number of locations, chiefly Benedictine monasteries in France, became active centers for the production of fine illuminated manuscripts. Among the principal locations were Paris, St. Denis, Rheims, Verdun, Fontanelle, and the two Abbeys of St. Martin at Tours and Metz.

Figure 14 shows a miniature from a manuscript of the *Gospels* in the Paris library, representing King Lothair enthroned between two guards. This manuscript was written about the year 845 in the monastery of St. Martin at Metz. In this picture, a strong classical influence is apparent; the illuminator must have been familiar with manuscripts written in Rome or elsewhere in Italy.

<margin>Celtic influence.</margin>
Some of the finest manuscripts of this period show a strongly marked northern influence, imitating the old Celtic illuminations of Ireland and Lindisfarne. Less gold is used in this class of manuscripts, and the intricate interlaced patterns of the Celtic monks are used with great skill and beauty. The figures of Christ and the Evangelists are sometimes hardly human in form, but are instead worked into a kind of conventional scroll pattern, just as they are in the older Celtic illuminations. The Paris library possesses

# CELTIC SCHOOL AT ST. GALLEN. [CHAP. V.

Two manuscripts of the *Gospels* are good examples of this revived Celtic style (*Bibl. Nat. Lat. Nos. 257 and 8849*). The borders and initial letters in these manuscripts are remarkable for their intricate, delicate design and for their rich, tastefully arranged colors; meanwhile, the figure drawing is of the purely ornamental scroll type.

<margin>Classical school of St. Gallen.</margin>
In the ninth century, the Benedictine monastery of St. Gallen in Switzerland, which had previously produced manuscripts of a purely Celtic type, developed a very unusual school of miniature art<note>See Weidmann, *History of the Library of St. Gallen*, 8vo, St. Gallen, 1841.</note>. The pictures in these St. Gallen manuscripts have figure subjects drawn in outline and then faintly colored with transparent washes, very much like the Anglo-Saxon (classical) style of illumination during the ninth and tenth centuries. These rather weak drawings, which have little decorative value, show the influence of the Roman school of illuminators, who still mainly adhered to the old, debased form of classical art, modified by some observation and even careful study of the actual life and movement which the painters saw around them. In this curious class of manuscripts, although the figure subjects lack much vigor and artistic force, the decorative details of the initials and borders are extremely fine, full of invention and delicacy of detail. Figure 15 shows a pen drawing from a St. Gallen manuscript of the ninth century, the magnificent *Psalterium aureum*<note>See J. R. Rahn, *The Golden Psalter of St. Gallen: A Contribution to the History of Carolingian Miniature Painting*, folio, St. Gallen, 1878.</note>; it represents David going out to battle.

<margin>Studies from life.</margin>
Regarding studies from life, whether of men or animals, it should be remembered that an artist is always biased by tradition and association to a degree that is now very difficult to realize. Even when looking at the same object, two painters of different races and educations might receive very different impressions on their retinas. Thus, in the very interesting sketchbook of Villard de Honecourt, a French sculptor and architect of the thirteenth century, there are studies of men, lions, and other animals, which he has noted as being from life; and yet these drawings look to
