# BYZANTINE INFLUENCE. [CHAP. V.

...tive and distorted manner, whereas when he is dealing with fishes, reptiles, plants, and the like, he is able to depict them with the most wonderful grace, accuracy, and realistic spirit.

<margin>Individual perspective.</margin>
For this reason, when examining an illuminated manuscript or other early work of art to discover how much the artist studied nature, one should always take into account the influences that made him see each natural object in a special, personal way. We must not argue that because a drawing now looks very unreal, it may not have been as careful and accurate a study from life as the painter’s eye and hand could produce.

<margin>Byzantine influence.</margin>
During the later Carolingian period, there was a marked revival of Byzantine influence, which did not tend to delay the advancing decline¹. Figures 16 and 17 show a very striking example of this: a two-page miniature from a magnificent purple and gold manuscript of *the Gospels*, which was produced for the Emperor Otho II and is now in the Munich library. On the right-hand page is the Emperor enthroned, holding a long scepter and an orb, with an archbishop and some armed courtiers beside him. On the opposite page, personifications of *Rome*, *Gaul*, *Germany*, and *Slavonia* are doing homage and offering gifts. The entire motif and design are borrowed from much earlier Byzantine works, such as the mosaics of Justinian’s time (circa 530 A.D.) in the churches of Ravenna.

<margin>Classical influence.</margin>
Figure 18, from another fine manuscript of *the Gospels*, is far nobler in style; here, the influence is classical rather than Byzantine. The figure illustrates one of the usual four miniatures of the Evangelists: Saint Mark dipping his pen into the ink. The Saint is robed in an *alb*, a *dalmatic* with two stripes, a *chasuble*, and a *pall*, reflecting his role as the Archbishop of Alexandria. The figure is very dignified and is evidently copied from a much earlier Italian *Textus*, such as the one Saint Augustine received from Pope Gregory or brought from Italy to Canterbury.

¹ See L. Delisle, *L’Évangéliaire d’Arras et la calligraphie Franco-Saxonne du IXme siècle* (The Arras Gospel Book and Franco-Saxon Calligraphy of the 9th Century), 8vo, Paris, 1888.

# THE LATER CAROLINGIAN SCHOOL. [CHAP. V.

<margin>
Later Emperors.
</margin>
Throughout the tenth century, and especially under the patronage of the three Emperors named Otto and Henry the Fowler, fine and richly decorative manuscripts continued to be produced, with little change in the style of ornament used. After a long period of great artistic brilliance and wonderful productivity, the Carolingian style of illumination came to an end when Charles the Great's Empire was (in France) divided among various feudal lords. Then a serious decline in art set in, which lasted until the beginning of a most magnificent artistic revival in the twelfth century.

To a large extent, the illuminations of French manuscripts during the latter part of the eleventh century consisted of

# CHAPTER VI.

## THE CELTIC SCHOOL OF MANUSCRIPTS.

One of the most extraordinary artistic developments that ever took place in the history of the world was the Celtic Monastic School of Art, which in the seventh century reached its highest aesthetic and technical peak, especially in the production of exquisitely minute gold jewelry and no less minute and richly illuminated manuscripts.

<margin>*The Irish Church.*</margin>
The Christian Church in the east of Ireland dates from an earlier period than the establishment of Christianity in England <note>Earlier, that is, than the conversion of the Saxon conquerors; to some extent, a Romano-British Church had been established in Britain during the period of Roman domination, but this native Church appears to have been almost wholly eradicated by the Saxon Conquest.</note>. It was founded about the year 430 A.D., and the monks of Ireland, due to their remote position, were able for a long period to develop peacefully their artistic skill, undisturbed by such successive foreign invasions as those which for so many years kept Britain in a constant tumult of war and massacre.

<margin>*Celtic goldsmiths.*</margin>
Thus it happened that by the middle or latter part of the seventh century, the Celtic monks of Ireland had learned to produce goldsmiths' work and manuscript illuminations with such marvelous taste and skill as has never been surpassed by any age or country in the world <note>Celtic manuscripts of all periods are well illustrated by Westwood, *Miniatures and Ornaments of Anglo-Saxon and Irish Manuscripts*, London, 1868; see also Westwood, *Palaeographia Sacra Pictoria*, 1843—1845, and the companion volume, *Illuminated Illustrations of the Bible*, 1846.</note>. Not even the finest Greek or Etruscan jewelry, enriched with enamels and

<summary>This page introduces the Celtic Monastic School of Art, which reached a pinnacle of technical skill in jewelry and manuscript illumination during the seventh century. It notes that the Irish Church's relative isolation allowed for a peaceful development of these arts, free from the invasions that troubled Britain.</summary>
<keywords>Celtic Monastic School of Art, Irish Church, Celtic goldsmiths, manuscript illumination, Ireland, seventh century, Westwood, jewelry</keywords>

### CHAP. VI.] CELTIC GOLDSMITHS. 81

<margin>*Gold jewelry.*</margin>
...jewelry, studded with gems, can be said to exceed the amazing perfection shown in such a masterpiece of the goldsmith's art as the so-called "Tara brooch" <note>Tara was the ancient inland capital of Ireland before Dublin was founded by Viking pirates.</note> in the Museum of the Royal Irish Academy. As a rule, the skill of these Irish goldsmiths was devoted to the service of the Church in the manufacture of objects such as croziers, morses (or cope-brooches), shrines, chalices, textus-covers, receptacles for Bishops' bells, and other pieces of ecclesiastical furniture.

<margin>*Technical processes.*</margin>
These precious objects are decorated by a variety of technical processes, such as applied filigree, *repoussé* or beaten reliefs, enamels (both *champlevé* and *cloisonné*), and the inlay of precious stones—especially the carbuncle in minute slices—set in delicate gold *cloisons* and backed with shining gold leaf. All these and other decorative processes were employed with unrivaled skill by the monastic goldsmiths of eastern Ireland. This is an important fact to notice, since nearly all the methods and styles of ornament which occur in the Irish illuminated manuscripts of the same period are clearly derived from prototypes in gold jeweled work. It is, in fact, often possible to trace in a fine Irish manuscript of the class we are now concerned with, ornamental patterns of several quite distinct classes: one being derived from the patterns of spiral or plaited form produced by soldering delicate gold wire onto plain surfaces of gold; another being copied from gold *champlevé* enamels; and a third no less clearly derived from the inlaid rectangular bits of carbuncle framed in delicate gold strips or *cloisons*.

<margin>*Influence on illuminations.*</margin>
This strongly marked influence of the technique of one art on the designs of another is due to the fact that the arts of both the goldsmith and the manuscript illuminator were carried on side by side in the same monastery or group of monastic dwellings <note>The Irish monasteries of this era frequently appear to have consisted of a group of a dozen or more separate wooden huts or stone "bee-hive" cells, with one small central chapel of rectangular design; the whole was enclosed within a wooden fence or a stone perimeter wall, in which there was only one entrance; see *Arch. Jour.* xv. p. 1 seq.</note>, and in some cases we have written

# THE WONDERFUL BEAUTY [CHAP. VI.

evidence that the scribe who wrote and illuminated an elaborate manuscript and the goldsmith who crafted and jeweled its gold cover were one and the same person¹.

<margin>The Book of Kells.</margin>
It was in the second half of the seventh century that the Celtic art of Eastern Ireland reached its highest point of perfection. To this period belongs the famous *Book of Kells*, now in the library of Trinity College, Dublin, which was probably written between 680 and 700. For many years, along with its jeweled gold covers, it was the principal treasure of the cathedral church at Kells². This church had been founded by Saint Columba, and so in old times this marvelous manuscript was usually known as "the Great Gospels of Saint Columba."

<margin>Perfect workmanship.</margin>
No words can describe the intricate delicacy of the ornamentation of this book, lavishly decorated as it is with all the different varieties of pattern mentioned above. The most remarkable among them are the ingeniously intricate patterns formed by interlaced and knotted lines of color, plaited in and out, with such amazingly complicated lines of interlacing that one cannot look at the page without astonishment at the combined taste, patience, unfaltering certainty of touch, and imaginative ingenuity of the artist. The wonderful detail of the work, when examined through a microscope, fills one with wonder at the apparently superhuman eyesight of the scribe.

<margin>Complex interlacings.</margin>
With regard to the intricate interlaced ornaments in which (with the aid of a lens) each line can be followed in its windings and is never found to break off or lead to an impossible loop of knotting, it is evident that the artist must have enjoyed, not only an aesthetic pleasure in the invention of his pattern, but must also have had a distinct intellectual

---

<note>¹ For example, in an early Cashel *Calendar*, the monk Dagaeus, who died in 586, is recorded to have been both a goldsmith (*aurifex*) and an illuminator of manuscripts. Westwood, in *Miniatures in Irish Manuscripts*, gives a number of excellent colored reproductions of illuminations of this school and also of the Anglo-Celtic school of Northumbria.</note>
<note>² It was formerly believed that this manuscript had once belonged to Saint Columba, who lived from 521 to 597, but internal evidence of its style shows it to be a century later than Saint Columba's time.</note>

### CHAP. VI.] OF THE BOOK OF KELLS.

enjoyment in his work, such as a skillful mathematician feels in the working out of a complicated geometrical problem.”

<margin>Microscopic intricacy.</margin>
The combined skill of eye and hand shown in the minute plaits of the *Book of Kells* places it among the most wonderful examples of human workmanship that the world has ever produced. By the aid of a microscope, Mr. Westwood counted in the space of one inch no less than 158 interlacements of bands or ribbons, each composed of a strip of white bordered on both sides with a black line.

Giraldus Cambrensis, who visited Ireland in 1185 as secretary to Prince John, writes in the most enthusiastic language of the splendor of a similar manuscript of the *Gospels* which he saw in Kildare Cathedral. It shows, he says, superhuman skill, worthy of angels’ hands, and he was lost in wondering admiration at the sight.

<margin>Copies of jewelry.</margin>
<margin>Primitive spiral patterns.</margin>
One class of ornament in the *Book of Kells* and in other manuscripts of this class consists of bands or diapers formed with step-like lines enclosing small spaces of brilliant color. It is this class of pattern which is derived from the *cloisonné* inlay with bits of transparent carbuncle used in gold jewelry. Other ornaments consist of various spiral forms derived from the application of gold wire to flat surfaces of gold, a class of pattern which appears to have come, as it were, naturally to the gold-workers of many different periods and countries. Many of these spiral designs in the Irish manuscripts are almost identical with forms which occur so frequently among the gold ornaments of the Greek “Mycenean period,” one among many examples in the art history of the world, which show the remarkable similarity of invention in the human mind at a certain stage of development, whatever the time or the place may be. <note>See Westwood, *Irish Manuscripts*, Plate 9.</note>

It should moreover be noticed that this close imitation of metalwork is not limited to the separate details of the manuscripts. The main lines and divisions of the decoration on whole pages are accurately copied from the enameled and jeweled gold or silver covers in which these precious *Gospels* were bound. Thus, the same design might appear in delicate

# ->ORIENTAL INFLUENCE. [CHAP. VI.<-

goldsmiths’ work on the covers of a <term>*Textus*</term>, and could also be seen represented by the illuminator in brilliant colors on a page within.

<margin>Trumpet pattern.</margin>
One form of ornament that occurs very frequently in Irish manuscripts is what is often called the “trumpet pattern,” named for its supposed resemblance to a curved metal trumpet. This kind of spiral ornament is used not only in Celtic manuscripts and goldsmiths’ work, but also on bronze shields and other large-scale pieces of metalwork. This specific ornament is not unique to the Irish but was commonly used by the Celtic tribes of Britain from a very early date.

<margin>Arab influence.</margin>
Combined with these purely native types of ornament, we find in these Celtic manuscripts a curious class of foreign ornament derived from the patterns on imported textiles woven in Arab looms¹. Among many strange forms of serpents, dragons, and other monsters of northern origin, other animals such as lions, eagles, and swans occur. These closely resemble those represented with such perfect conventional skill on the rich silk fabrics and early Oriental carpets woven in Syria, in the Arab towns of Sicily, and in other Muslim centers. These beautiful fabrics were imported extensively into Northern Europe for ecclesiastical purposes, such as for priestly vestments or to wrap some sacred reliquary².

<margin>The human form.</margin>
Although these Celtic manuscripts show such marvelous dexterity of touch and unerring firmness of line in every minute and complicated pattern, the monastic artist appears to have been absolutely incapable of representing the human form.

The figures of Christ and the saints, which do sometimes occur in these manuscripts, are treated in a purely ornamental and (in the stricter sense) conventional way; for example, the hair and beard are worked into scrolls or spiral

---

¹ <note>See fig. 13 on page 67.</note>
² <note>When the grave of Saint Cuthbert in Durham Cathedral was opened in 1827, it was found that the Saint’s body had been wrapped in rich Siculo-Arab silk from the eleventh century at the time his body was moved in 1104 A.D. See Raine, *St. Cuthbert*, Durham, 1828, p. 183 and following.</note>

# ->TECHNICAL DETAILS. [CHAP. VI.<-

ornaments, and the draperies are merely masses of varied color, with little or no resemblance to the folds of a dress.

<margin>*Colors without gold.*</margin>
The pigments used by the Celtic monks are very varied and of the most brilliant tints, prepared with such skill that after more than a thousand years they seem as fresh and bright as ever.

Among these pigments is included the fine *murex* purple which the Irish monks used occasionally to stain sheets of vellum like those in the *Golden Gospels* of the Byzantines. We are told by the Venerable Bede that the Irish monks had learned how to extract this beautiful dye from a variety of the *murex* shellfish which is not uncommon on both shores of the Irish Channel. Splendid as they are in color, there is one curious feature in the early Irish manuscripts of the finest class, such as the *Book of Kells*; that is, that no gold or silver either in the form of leaf or as a fluid pigment is used. This seems specially strange when we remember the close connection there was between the arts of the goldsmith and of the illuminator of manuscripts among the Irish artists.

<margin>*Celtic art in Britain.*</margin>
In later times, when the Celtic style of illumination was transplanted to England, gold was to some extent introduced, but in the finest Irish manuscripts of the best period—the latter half of the seventh century—gold is completely absent. Nevertheless, so great was the decorative genius of these Irish monks that, even without burnished gold and silver, their illuminated pages quite equal, not only in artistic beauty, but even in mere splendor of effect, any illuminations that have ever been produced.

<margin>*The Book of Durrow.*</margin>
In addition to the *Book of Kells*, another manuscript of similar style and date and of almost equal splendor should be mentioned: the *Book of Durrow*<note>1. Library of Trinity College, Dublin, manuscripts A, iv. 5.</note>, which, like the *Book of Kells*, was also known as the “Gospels of Saint Columba.” Columba is said to have left behind him at his death in 597 no less than three hundred manuscripts written with his own hand. It is not impossible that the *Book of Durrow* is one of these, as it bears some signs of being earlier in date than the *Book of Kells*.

<summary>This page describes the pigments used in Celtic illumination, notably murex purple, and highlights the unique absence of gold in early masterpieces like the Book of Kells. It also identifies the Book of Durrow as another significant work potentially authored by Saint Columba.</summary>
<keywords>Celtic monks, illumination, pigments, murex purple, Book of Kells, Book of Durrow, Saint Columba, Venerable Bede, Irish Channel, vellum</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Celtic art, shifting focus from the technical aspects of pigments and gold usage in Irish illumination to the spread of this art form from Ireland to Scotland and Northumbria via the monastic settlement at Iona.</meta>

## CELTIC ART IN SCOTLAND [CHAP. VI.]

<margin>Monks of Iona.</margin>
From Ireland, the art of illuminating manuscripts was carried by monastic colonists to the western coasts of Scotland, and especially to the Island of Iona, where a monastery had been founded by Saint Columba in the late sixth century<note>See Jamieson, *History of the Ancient Culdees of Iona*; Edinburgh, 1811.</note>. Great numbers of manuscripts resembling in style the *Book of Kells* were produced in Iona; and offshoots from the monastery of Iona, established at various places on the mainland, became similar centers for the writing of richly decorated manuscripts. No less than thirteen monasteries in Scotland and twelve in England were founded by Irish monks from the mother settlement in Iona. In fact, the whole of Britain seems to have owed its Christianity, during the Anglo-Saxon period, to the Irish missionaries from Iona, with the important exception of the kingdom of Kent, which was occupied by the Roman mission of Saint Augustine.

<margin>Celtic missionaries.</margin>
In the year 635, at the request of Oswald, King of Northumbria, the Scottish king sent an Irish monk from Iona, named Aidan, to preach Christianity to the Northumbrian worshipers of Thor and Odin. Aidan selected the small island of Lindisfarne as the headquarters of his missionary church, which, at first consisting mainly of a few Irish monks from Iona, rapidly grew in size and importance. In a few years, Saint Aidan, Bishop and Abbot of Lindisfarne, was able to establish a number of monastic houses throughout the Northumbrian Kingdom, and his own Abbey of Lindisfarne became one of the chief centers of Northern Europe for the production of fine illuminated manuscripts of the Celtic type.

After the death of Saint Aidan, other Irish monks succeeded him as Bishop of Lindisfarne, and the school of manuscript illumination continued to flourish.

<margin>Gospels of St Cuthbert.</margin>
One of the most beautiful existing examples of the Lindisfarne branch of the Irish school of miniature work is the famous "Book of the Gospels of Saint Cuthbert<note>Saint Cuthbert was a monk of Irish descent, at first a member of the Celtic monastery of Melrose, and afterwards sixth Bishop of Lindisfarne from 685 to 688. In later times his gold, gem-studded shrine in Durham Cathedral was one of the most magnificent and costly in the world; see *Rites and Monuments of Durham*, Surtees Soc., 1842, pp. 3 and 4.</note>" as it is

<summary>This page describes how the art of manuscript illumination spread from Ireland to Scotland via Iona and then to Northumbria through Saint Aidan's foundation at Lindisfarne. It highlights the significant role of Irish monks in Christianizing Britain and identifies the Gospels of Saint Cuthbert as a primary example of this artistic tradition.</summary>

# CHAP. VI.] AND AT LINDISFARNE.

<margin>*Gospels of Saint Cuthbert.*</margin>
called, now in the British Museum (*Cotton manuscripts, Nero*, D. IV). The history of this manuscript is a very curious one; it was written some years after Saint Cuthbert’s death in 688, not during his lifetime as was formerly believed. Eadfrith, a monk of Lindisfarne during Saint Cuthbert’s time and subsequently the eighth Bishop of Lindisfarne (698 to 721), was the writer of these *Gospels*, “in honor of God and of Saint Cuthbert,” as he records in a note. The illuminations were added by the monk Aethelwold, who later became the ninth Bishop of Lindisfarne, and the elaborate gold, gem-studded cover of this magnificent *textus* was the work of a third monk of the same Abbey named Bilfrith.

<margin>*Viking piracy.*</margin>
<margin>*Travels of the Gospels.*</margin>
In the ninth century, Viking pirates were constantly raiding the shores of Northumbria; more than once the Abbey of Lindisfarne was plundered and many of the monks were killed, until at last, in the year 878, the small group of survivors who had escaped the cruelty of the Northmen decided to leave Lindisfarne and seek a new settlement in the original home of the founders of Lindisfarne, the eastern coast of Ireland. In 878 the survivors set off, carrying with them the body of Saint Cuthbert and the magnificent manuscript of the *Gospels*, which was the chief treasure of their Abbey and which had been successfully hidden in Saint Cuthbert’s grave at the time of the invasion of the Northmen. The monks crossed to the western shore of Northumbria and there took a ship for Ireland. A great storm, however, arose; their boat took on a heavy sea which washed overboard the precious *Gospels* of Saint Cuthbert, which had been carefully packed in a wooden box. Eventually the little ship was driven back and finally was stranded on the Northumbrian shore. Soon after reaching the land, the fleeing monks, wandering sadly along the beach, found to their great joy the lost box with its precious manuscript thrown up by the waves and lying on dry land. According to the chronicle of Symeon <note>The works of Symeon Dunelmensis were published by the Surtees Society in 1868.</note> (chapter xxvii.), the brilliant illuminations were quite uninjured by the sea water; this is not literally the case; some of the pages are a good

THE GOSPELS OF ST CUTHBERT [CHAP. VI.

<margin>
*Durham Minster.*
</margin>
When, after many wanderings, the successors of the exiles from Lindisfarne found, in 995, a final resting place for the body of Saint Cuthbert in the Minster which they founded at Durham, the manuscript of the Gospels was laid on the coffin of the Saint. There it remained until 1104, when Saint Cuthbert's body was exhumed, and soon after it was sent back to Lindisfarne, where a Benedictine monastery had been founded in 1093 by some monks from Durham on the site of Saint Cuthbert's ruined Abbey.

<insert>/</insert> There it was safely preserved until the dissolution of the monasteries under Henry VIII. The gold covers were then stripped off and melted, but the still more precious manuscript escaped destruction; it was subsequently acquired by Sir Robert Cotton, and is now one of the chief manuscript treasures of the British Museum. <insert>(p)</insert>

<margin>
*Anglo-Celtic school.*
</margin>
In point of style, the "Gospels of Saint Cuthbert" are a characteristic example of the Irish school of illumination, modified by transplantation to English soil. The intermediate stage in Iona and other monasteries of western Scotland seems to have introduced no change of style into the primitive Irish method of ornament. Whether produced in eastern Ireland or in western Scotland, the manuscripts were the work of the same Celtic race, the Scots, who, at first inhabiting the northeast of Ireland, passed over to the nearby shores of northern Britain to which these Irish settlers gave the name Scotland.

When, however, the Irish monks passed from Iona to Northumbria, the case was different; they were surrounded with a new set of artistic influences, mainly owing to the introduction into Northumbria of fine Byzantine and Italian manuscripts. The result of this was that though the Lindisfarne manuscripts continued to be decorated with exactly the same class of patterns that had been used in the *Book of Kells* and other Irish manuscripts for initials, borders, and the like, <insert><note>Underlined in original</note> yet in the treatment of the human figure, a very distinct advance was made.</insert> Thus in Saint Cuthbert's *Gospels* the

### CHAP. VI.] AND OTHER CELTIC MANUSCRIPTS.

<margin>Improved drawing.</margin>
The seated figures of the Evangelists are drawn with great dignity of form and with some attempt at accuracy in the pose, the proportions, and the arrangement of the folds of the drapery. The monk Aethelwold, who painted these miniatures, must have had before him some fine manuscripts of the Gospels, probably in both Byzantine and Italian styles.

<margin>Use of metal leaf.</margin>
The overall result is very splendid; in their richness of invention and minute intricacy of pattern, the *Gospels* of Saint Cuthbert almost equal the *Book of Kells*. Meanwhile, the figure subjects, instead of being grotesque masses of ornament, are paintings with great beauty of line as well as extreme splendor of color. Another modification is the introduction of gold and silver leaf, which are completely absent from the *Book of Kells* and the other finest purely Irish manuscripts.

<margin>MS. of Bede.</margin>
<margin>Italian influence.</margin>
Other typical examples of this combined Celtic and English style are the magnificent *Gospels* in the Imperial Library in St. Petersburg, and a manuscript of the Commentary on the Psalms by Cassiodorus, now in the Chapter Library at Durham. This latter manuscript, which dates from the eighth century, is traditionally said to have been written by Bede himself. The illuminations in this manuscript are especially rich with interlaced patterns, dragon monsters, and <term>diapers</term> of the most minute scale, all purely Celtic in style, and all showing with particular clarity their derivation from original works in goldsmithing. Not only are the distinctly metallic motifs of ornament faithfully copied, but even the manner in which gold-workers built up their elaborate manuscript covers—by inserting separate little plates of gold filigree and enamel side-by-side on a large plate or matrix—is exactly reproduced by the illuminator. As in the case of the *Lindisfarne Gospels*, the figures of the Psalmist which are introduced are far superior to any figures that occur in purely Irish manuscripts, showing the clear influence of Italian manuscripts of a debased classical style.

Another very interesting example of the Anglo-Celtic school of illumination, with fine initials and a painting of an eagle of the characteristic Northern type, is in the possession of Corpus Christi College, Cambridge; No. CXCVII.

<summary>This page describes the artistic style of the Lindisfarne Gospels and other Anglo-Celtic manuscripts, noting the influence of Byzantine and Italian styles on figure drawing and the use of gold and silver leaf. It also highlights a Commentary on the Psalms at Durham, attributed to Bede, which mimics the techniques of contemporary goldsmiths.</summary>

### THE CORPUS AND LAMBETH GOSPELS. [CHAP. VI.

<margin>
*The Corpus Gospels.*
</margin>

This is an incomplete manuscript of the *Gospels*, containing only the Gospels of Saint Luke and Saint John. The decorative borders and initials feature the interlaced Irish style of ornament. This interesting manuscript was (in the sixteenth century) in the library of Archbishop Parker, who added a note stating that it was one of the manuscripts sent by Pope Gregory to Saint Augustine. The actual date of the manuscript is probably no earlier than the eighth century, despite the ancient appearance of the painted figures. An earlier copy of the *Gospels* in the same library has full-page miniatures of the two Evangelists in a purely classical style, surrounded by an architectural framework of a degraded Roman form, very little modified from similar Roman miniatures of the fifth century A.D.

<margin>
*Gospels of MacDurnan.*
</margin>

Returning for a moment to the Irish school of Celtic art, it should be observed that richly illuminated manuscripts continued to be produced in Ireland until the ninth and tenth centuries. However, these later manuscripts, fine as they are, do not equal the beauty of the *Book of Kells* and other works of the seventh and eighth centuries. The Book of the *Gospels of MacDurnan*¹, who was Archbishop of Armagh from 885 to 927, is a good example of the later school of Irish art. In this work, the figures of the Evangelists are no less grotesque than those in the earlier manuscripts, while the interlaced and <term>diapered</term> patterns of the borders and initials are inferior in minute delicacy of execution compared to such masterpieces as the *Book of Kells*; see fig. 20.

<margin>
*Book of Deer.*
</margin>

Another even stronger proof of artistic decline among the Celtic illuminators of this period is provided by the *Book of Deer*² in the Cambridge University Library. This is a small octavo copy of the *Latin Gospels* following the Itala version³. In style, it is a mere shadow of the glories of early Irish art,

<note>³ The so-called *Itala* version is the older Latin translation of the Bible, which existed before the revision by Saint Jerome.</note>

# COMBINATION OF THE CELTIC [CHAP. VI.]

<margin>
*Gospels of MacRegol.*
</margin>
One of the finest of the manuscripts of the later Irish type is the Book of the *Gospels of MacRegol* in the Bodleian library (D. 24. No. 3946), produced in the ninth century. The ornaments and the very conventional figures of the Evangelists are of the purely Irish type, unmodified by any imitation of the superior figure drawing found in Byzantine and Italian miniatures <note>1. See Westwood, *Irish Manuscripts*, Plate 16.</note>.

<margin>
*Gospels of St Chad.*
</margin>
The manuscript *Gospels of Saint Chad* in the Chapter library of Lichfield Cathedral is another example of the Irish school and is from the same period as the last-mentioned book. It is named after Ceadda, or Chad, who, in the seventh century, was the first Bishop of Lichfield, nearly two hundred years before the date of this manuscript of the *Gospels* <note>2. This is one of many examples of books being named after an earlier Saint who was connected with the monastery where the manuscript was written; for example, the *Gospels of Saint Augustine* in the Corpus library at Cambridge, the *Gospels of Saint Cuthbert*, and the *Gospels of Saint Columba* are all later than the dates of the Saints they are named after.</note>.

<margin>
*Celtic school on the Continent.*
</margin>
During the most flourishing period of Celtic art in Ireland, its influence was by no means limited to the Northumbrian school of illuminators. The Irish types of ornament were adopted by the scribes of Canterbury and other places in the South of England; and on the Continent of Europe, Celtic art was widely spread by Irish missionaries such as Saint Columbanus, and by the founding of Irish monasteries during the sixth century in various countries—as, for example, at Bobbio in Northern Italy, at St Gallen in Switzerland, at Wurtzburg in Germany, and at Luxeuil in France. In these and in other places, Irish monastic illuminators worked hard at the production of manuscripts and spread the Celtic style of ornament over a large area of Western Europe. The library of St Gallen possesses a number of richly illuminated manuscripts of the later Irish type, exactly similar in style to those which, during the eighth and ninth centuries, were produced in the monasteries of Ireland and Scotland <note>3. See Weidmann, *History of the Library of St Gallen*; St Gall, 1841.</note>.

The result of this spread of Celtic influence was that borders, initial letters, and similar ornaments of pure Irish

### AND THE CLASSICAL STYLES. [CHAP. VI.]

<margin>Psalter of Saint Augustine.</margin>
This style was used in many manuscripts in which the figures of Saints were designed in either an equally pure Italian or a debased classical style. A good example of this is the so-called *Psalter of Saint Augustine* <note>1. This manuscript was formerly believed to have once been in the possession of Saint Augustine, but it is clearly much later than his time.</note> (British Museum *Cotton manuscripts Vesp. A. i*) which belonged to the Cathedral of Canterbury for many centuries. This is a manuscript from the eighth century; one of its primary miniature paintings represents David enthroned, playing a harp with a group of attending musicians and two dancing figures around his throne. These figures are purely Italian in style, belonging to the debased Roman School; however, the arched frame bordering the picture is filled with ornament of the Irish metal type. It is closely similar in style to the *Book of Kells*—except that gold and silver are used extensively—though it is inferior in the delicacy of its execution. It is, of course, very possible that the illuminations in this *Psalter* are the work of two artists: the figures being painted by an Italian illuminator and the borders by an English or Irish monk.

<margin>Scandinavian art.</margin>
In later times, especially during the ninth century, the Celtic art of Ireland appears to have been largely introduced into Scandinavia by the Viking pirates who raided the entire coast of Britain and Ireland. Finally, in the ninth century, they established a Norse Kingdom in eastern Ireland with the newly founded Dublin as its capital <note>2. Eventually, there were three Norse kingdoms in Ireland, with capitals at Dublin, Waterford, and Limerick; the three main ports of Ireland—Dublin, Cork, and Belfast—were all founded by the Viking invaders. See C. F. Keary’s valuable work, *The Vikings in Western Christendom*, London, 1891, pages 165 to 185.</note>. The Norsemen were far from being a literary people, and Irish art was not introduced into Norway and Denmark through manuscript illuminations. Instead, it arrived in the form of the rich gold and silver jewelry with which the Viking chiefs adorned themselves, and also on a larger scale through the magnificent decorative reliefs carved on the wooden planks that formed the door frames or architraves of Scandinavian wooden churches in the eleventh

<summary>This page discusses the hybrid artistic style of the eighth-century Psalter of Saint Augustine and explains how Viking raids spread Celtic artistic influences from Ireland to Scandinavia, primarily through jewelry and architectural carvings.</summary>

<keywords>Psalter of Saint Augustine, Cotton manuscripts, Canterbury Cathedral, Italian style, debased Roman School, Irish metalwork style, Book of Kells, Scandinavian art, Celtic art, Vikings, Dublin, Norse kingdoms, C. F. Keary</keywords>

### THE CODEX AUREUS OF STOCKHOLM. [CHAP. VI.]

and twelfth centuries, after the worship of the Thunderer had been replaced by the Faith of the White Christ.

Lindisfarne, Iona, and the other chief Irish monasteries suffered again and again from the raids of the Vikings. These raiders found rich and easily won plunder in the form of gold and silver chalices, reliquaries, and book covers in the treasuries of the monastic churches, which were undefended except by unarmed and peaceful monks.

<margin>
*The Golden Gospels of Stockholm.*
</margin>
One curious record of Viking plunder is preserved in the Royal Library of Stockholm. This is a very magnificent manuscript book of the *Gospels* from the eighth century, commonly known as the *Codex Aureus* of Stockholm. It is mostly written on alternate leaves of purple vellum, with the text in golden letters. In its general style and the splendor of its ornaments, it closely resembles the Lindisfarne “*Gospels of Saint Cuthbert*,” described above on page 88; most probably, like the latter, it was also written in the monastery of Lindisfarne. The interlaced ornaments of the Irish type are marvels of beauty, while the dignified drawing of the enthroned figures of the four Evangelists shows clearly the influence of Continental manuscript art. In this case, the Celtic or English illuminator must have had before him a copy of the *Gospels* in the Byzantine rather than the Italian style, since the Evangelists and other figures in the book who are represented in the act of blessing do so in the Oriental rather than the Latin fashion <note>1. The blessing in the Greek Church is given by raising three fingers; in the Western Church, two fingers and the thumb are used.</note>.

<margin>
*Viking robbers.*
</margin>
On the margin of the first page of Saint Matthew’s Gospel, an interesting note was written around the year 850 by the owner of the Gospels, an English <term>Ealdorman</term> named Aelfred. This note records that the manuscript had been stolen by Norse robbers and that Aelfred had purchased it from them for a sum in pure gold so that the sacred book might be rescued from heathen hands. Aelfred then presented it to the Cathedral Church of Canterbury, and new gold covers appear then to have been made for this <term>Textus</term>, as there is another note in a ninth-century hand—

---

<note>1. The blessing in the Greek Church is given by raising three fingers; in the Western Church, two fingers and the thumb are used.</note>

### [CHAP. VI.] CELTIC AND PAPAL PARTIES.

requesting the prayers of the Church for three goldsmiths, probably those who replaced the original gold covers that the Viking pirates had torn off<note>1. See Westwood, *Miniatures of Irish Manuscripts*, London, 1868, Pl. I and II.</note>.

<margin>*The two Churches in Britain.*</margin>
Returning now to the manuscripts of the Celtic Church in Northumbria, in order to understand the gradual introduction into Northern England of the Italian or classical style of painting, it is necessary to remember the struggle that took place during the seventh century between the adherents of the older Celtic Church and those who supported the Papal claims for supremacy throughout Britain.

On the one hand, the See of Canterbury, founded by the Roman Saint Augustine, claimed jurisdiction in the north as well as in the south of Britain, in opposition to the Celtic Abbot of Iona, who was then the real Metropolitan of the Church in the north of England.

<margin>*Long struggle.*</margin>
Wilfrid of York and Benedict Biscop of Jarrow spent many years in a series of embassies between 670 and 690, traveling back and forth between Northumbria and Rome. They strove to introduce Papal authority by the aid of imported books, relics, and craftsmen skilled in building stone churches to replace the simple wooden structures that, at that time, were the only ecclesiastical buildings in Northumbria<note>2. The points of difference between the Roman and Celtic Churches were very trivial, the chief being the date for the celebration of Easter and the shape of the monastic tonsure.</note>. Very large numbers of illuminated manuscripts were brought to England during the many journeys of Wilfrid and Benedict Biscop; and important libraries were created at York and at Jarrow, which led to these places becoming literary and artistic centers of great European importance.

<margin>*Synod of Whitby.*</margin>
In the end, after many failures, Wilfrid, Archbishop<note>3. See note 2 on page 97.</note> of York, was successful in bringing Northumbria under the supremacy of Canterbury and Rome. In 664, a great Council was held at Whitby in the presence of the Northumbrian King Oswiu. Bishop Colman, the successor of Saint Aidan at Lindisfarne, represented the Celtic Church and the authority of Saint Columba, while Wilfrid appeared to support the authority

<summary>This page details the seventh-century conflict between the Celtic and Roman Churches for religious supremacy in Britain, specifically in Northumbria. It describes how figures like Wilfrid and Benedict Biscop imported Roman art, architecture, and manuscripts, leading toward the Synod of Whitby.</summary>

<keywords>Celtic Church, Papal supremacy, See of Canterbury, Wilfrid of York, Benedict Biscop, Northumbria, Synod of Whitby, King Oswiu, Bishop Colman, Lindisfarne, Saint Columba, illuminated manuscripts</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the account of the religious transition in Northumbria, following the established libraries at York and other centers, focusing on the resolution of the conflict between Celtic and Roman traditions at the Synod of Whitby.</meta>

# THE ROMAN VICTORY. [CHAP. VI.]

<margin>
Defeat of the Celtic party.
</margin>
...of Saint Peter and the Bishop of Rome. After hearing that Saint Peter possessed the keys of Heaven and Hell, while Saint Columba could claim no such marvelous power, King Oswiu decided in favor of the Roman Supremacy. This decision, though based on such fanciful grounds, was a fortunate one for the English Church, since, in the main, learning, culture, and established order generally were on the side of the Italian Church.

The practical result of this Roman victory at the Synod of Whitby in 664 was that a classical influence gradually extended itself in all the English centers for the production of illuminated manuscripts. It has already been noted that the splendid manuscripts of Lindisfarne and other Northumbrian monasteries, though of Celtic origin, show a distinct Roman influence in the improvement of the drawing of their figures of saints. By degrees, the Irish element in the illuminations grew less and less; though the interlaced patterns and fantastic dragon and serpent forms lasted for many centuries in all the chief countries of western Europe and formed an important decorative element until the thirteenth century<note>1. This very decorative class of ornament not only survived until the thirteenth century but was again revived in Italy at the close of the fifteenth century; see below, page 193.</note>.

<margin>
Bede of Durham.
</margin>
One of the chief schools of English manuscript illumination, that of the Benedictine Abbey at Durham, was raised to a position of European importance by the Northumbrian monk Bede (Baeda), afterward called the Venerable Bede, who was born in 673, a few years after the Synod of Whitby.

As the author of a great *Ecclesiastical History of the English Nation*, Bede ranks as the Father of English History; he did much to foster the study of ancient classical authors, was himself a skillful writer of manuscripts, and made the Abbey of Jarrow, where he lived until his death in 735, an active center for the production of richly illuminated manuscripts of many different literary classes.

<margin>
Northumbrian school.
</margin>
In the eighth century, the schools of illumination in the Abbeys of Jarrow, Wearmouth, and York in Northumbria, and of Canterbury and Winchester in the south were among

<summary>Following the Synod of Whitby, King Oswiu’s preference for Roman authority introduced classical artistic influences into English manuscript production. The Venerable Bede later elevated the Abbey of Jarrow into a major European center for learning and illuminated manuscripts.</summary>

### CHAP. VI.] ENGLISH SCHOOL OF ILLUMINATION. 97

<margin>*Celtic and Classic styles.*</margin>
...the most active and artistically important in the world$^1$. In these schools of miniature painting, a unique English style of illumination was gradually created, partially formed from a combination of two very different styles: that of the Irish Celtic illuminators and that of the Italian classical scribes.

This English school of illumination, which had been partially developed before the close of the tenth century, became the first and most important in all of Europe for its genuine artistic merit, and it continued to hold this leading position for a considerable period$^2$.

# CHAPTER VII.

## THE ANGLO-SAXON SCHOOL OF MANUSCRIPTS<note>1. The word "Anglo-Saxon" is a convenient one to use and is supported by various ancient authorities; for example, in a manuscript *Benedictional* (in the library of Corpus College, Cambridge) England is called "Regnum Anglo-Saxonum," and the English king is entitled "Rex Anglorum vel Saxonum."</note>.

<margin>*Danish invasions.*</margin>
The ninth century in England was one of great turmoil and misery, because of the fearful havoc wrought by the Danish Northmen throughout the whole length and breadth of the land. In Northumbria, the thriving literary and artistic school which had been raised to such preeminence by Bede was utterly blotted out of existence by the invading Danes; and when at last King Alfred, who reigned from 871 to 901, secured an interval of peace, he was obliged to seek instructors in the art of manuscript illumination from the Frankish kings.

<margin>*Time of King Alfred.*</margin>
In this way, the wave of influence flowed back again from France to England. In Charles the Great's time, the Carolingian school of manuscripts had been largely influenced by the Celtic style, which Alcuin of York introduced from Northumbria, and now the later art of Anglo-Saxon England received back from France the forms of ornament and the technical skill which in Northumbria itself had become extinct.

Alfred was an enthusiastic patron of literature and art, especially the art of manuscript illumination, and before long a new school of manuscript art was created in many of the

<summary>This page describes how Danish invasions destroyed the Northumbrian artistic traditions established by Bede, forcing King Alfred to re-import artistic knowledge from the Frankish kings. This created a cultural exchange where Anglo-Saxon England regained the artistic styles and skills that Northumbrian scholars had originally brought to the continent during the time of Charlemagne.</summary>
<keywords>Danish invasions, Northumbria, Bede, King Alfred, Frankish influence, Carolingian school, Alcuin of York, Anglo-Saxon manuscripts, manuscript illumination</keywords>

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the revival of manuscript illumination in English Benedictine monasteries, specifically focusing on the Winchester school and the Benedictional of Aethelwold.</meta>

Benedictine monasteries of England and especially among the monks of the royal city of Winchester, which in the tenth century produced works of extraordinary beauty and decorative force.

<margin>*Benedictional of Aethelwold.*</margin>
As an example of this, we may mention the famous *Benedictional* of Aethelwold, who was Bishop of Winchester from 963 to 984<note>1. This splendid manuscript is in the possession of the Duke of Devonshire; a good description of it, with engravings of all its miniatures, is published in *Archaeologia*, Vol. xxiv. 1832, pp. 1 to 117, and a colored copy of one of the miniatures is given by Westwood, *Irish Manuscripts*, Plate 45.</note>. The writer of this sumptuously decorated manuscript was Bishop Aethelwold’s chaplain, a monk named Godemann, who afterwards, about the year 970, became Abbot of Thorney. Unlike the manuscripts of earlier date in which the illuminated pictures are usually few in number, this *Benedictional* contains no less than thirty full-page miniatures, mostly consisting of scenes from the life of Christ. Each picture is framed by an elaborate border, richly decorated in gold and brilliant colors, with conventional leaf-work of classical style. The drawing of the figures is dignified, and the drapery is usually well conceived and treated in a bold, decorative way, showing much artistic skill on the part of the illuminator.

Fig. 21 shows one of the miniatures, representing the Ascension; the coloring is extremely beautiful and harmonious, enhanced by a skillful use of burnished gold.

<margin>*Foreign influence.*</margin>
Though the figures and especially the delicately modeled faces have a character of their own, peculiarly English in feeling, yet in the general style of the miniatures and in their elaborate borders, there are very distinct signs of a strong Carolingian influence. This was owing, no doubt, to the introduction of Frankish illuminators and the purchase of Carolingian manuscripts during the reign of Alfred the Great, more than half a century before the date of this manuscript.

There is, for example, much similarity of style in the miniatures of this *Benedictional* and those in a Carolingian

<header>CHAP. VII.] THE WINCHESTER SCHOOL.</header>

manuscript of *the Gospels* written for King Lothaire in the monastery of St. Martin at Metz soon after 843<note>1. The Gospels of Lothaire are in Paris, *Bibl. Nat. Lat.* 266.</note>; see figure 14 above, page 71.

<margin>Winchester Charter.</margin>
Another very fine example of the Winchester school of illumination is the manuscript *Charter* which King Edgar granted to the New Minster at Winchester in 966. The first page consists of a large miniature, painted in gold and brilliant colors on a purple-stained leaf of vellum<note>2. This is one of the latest examples of the use of vellum dyed with the *murex* purple; the purple grounds occasionally used in fifteenth-century manuscripts are usually produced by laying on a coat of opaque purple pigment.</note>, with Christ in Majesty supported by four angels in the upper part of the picture. Below are standing figures of the Blessed Virgin Mary and Saint Peter, with King Edgar in the middle offering his charter to Christ. The entire picture is very skillfully designed so as to fill the whole page in the most decorative way, and it is framed in a border with richly devised conventional leaf-forms.

In artistic power, this tenth-century Winchester school of illuminators appears, for a while at least, to have been foremost in the world. Both in delicacy of touch and in richness of decorative effect, the productions of this school are superior to those of any contemporary continental country.

<margin>Saint Dunstan as an artist.</margin>
Saint Dunstan, the great ecclesiastical statesman of the ninth century, created another school of illumination in the Benedictine Abbey of Glastonbury. Dunstan himself was no mean artist, as may be seen from a fine drawing of Christ which he executed<note>3. Now preserved in the Bodleian Library at Oxford.</note>; the Saint has represented himself as a small monkish figure prostrate at the feet of Christ. At the top of the page is inscribed in a twelfth-century hand, “Pictura et scriptura hujus pagine subtus visa est de propria manu sancti Dunstani” <note>Translated: "The picture and writing of this page seen below is by Saint Dunstan’s own hand."</note>.

During the tenth century, a large number of illuminated manuscripts were executed in the southern parts of England. The miniatures in these are very unlike and, as decoration, very inferior to the manuscripts of the Anglo-Carolingian

<page-num>102</page-num>

### OUTLINE DRAWINGS. [CHAP. VII.

<margin>Colored ink drawings.</margin>
style, as represented by the magnificent *Benedictional* of Aethelwold. This class of illumination consists of drawings, often with a large number of small figures, executed with a pen in red, blue, and brown outlines. The drawing of these figures is very stylized; the heads are small, the poses are awkward, and the draperies are shown in numerous small, fluttering folds, drawn with an apparently shaky line, as if the artist had lacked a steady hand. This, however, is a mere mannerism, as wherever he intended a steady line—for example, in the drawing of the faces—the artist has drawn with the utmost decision and firmness of touch. The costumes of these curious outline drawings, the architectural accessories, and other details all show clearly the influence of the very degraded forms of classical Roman art, which still survived among the manuscript illuminations of Italy <note>1. The famous "Utrecht Psalter" is the best-known example of a fine manuscript of this date with outline drawings of the revived classical style. Some Northern influence is shown in the interlaced ornaments of the large initials. Facsimiles of some pages have been published by W. G. Birch, London, 1876.</note>. This degraded form of classical art was far from being a good model for the Anglo-Saxon illuminator to imitate, and the blue and red outline miniatures are very inferior to the lavish Anglo-Carolingian manuscripts which were being produced at Winchester by contemporary illuminators.

<margin>
MSS. of the 12th century.

Roll of St. Guthlac.
</margin>
In the eleventh century, Anglo-Saxon miniatures in colored outline improved greatly in beauty of form and in gracefulness of pose, until at the beginning of the twelfth century, extremely fine miniatures of this class were produced. A very beautiful example of this is a long vellum roll illuminated with eighteen circular miniatures, mostly drawn with a pen in dark brown ink. These outline miniatures represent scenes from the life of Saint Guthlac, the Hermit of Crowland. The series begins with a drawing of the youthful Guthlac receiving the <term>tonsure</term> from Hedda, Bishop of Winchester (676 to 705), in the presence of the Abbess Ebba and two nuns. The whole composition is very skillfully arranged to fill the circular medallion, and there is great dignity and even delicate beauty in the separate figures. The precision of

<page-num>103</page-num>
<header>CHAP. VII.] LIFE OF SAINT GUTHLAC. </header>

<margin>*Beauty of line.*</margin>
The touch shown in the drawing is most admirable, recalling the perfect purity of line seen in the finest Greek vase paintings, in which, as in these miniatures, the greatest effect is produced with the fewest possible strokes. A few flat washes are used in the backgrounds, but the principal part of the miniatures is executed with this pure outline.

There is no basis for the suggestion that these medallion drawings were intended as designs for stained glass. There is much similarity of style in stained glass paintings and manuscript illuminations during the twelfth to the fourteenth centuries in England, just as in the early Byzantine manuscripts the same design served for both a miniature painting and a massive wall mosaic. The same simplicity of drawing and flatness of composition were preserved in both classes of art, and there is nothing exceptional in the fact that these miniatures of Saint Guthlac might have served as excellent subjects for a glass painter <note>1. This beautiful roll is now in the British Museum, *Harl.*, Roll Y, 6; two of the miniatures are photographically illustrated by Birch and Jenner, *Early Drawings and Illuminations*, London, 1879, p. 142.</note>.

<margin>*Pontifical of St Dunstan.*</margin>
The *Pontifical* of Saint Dunstan (*Brit. Mus. Cott. Claud.* A. 3), created in the early part of the eleventh century, is a magnificent example of decorative art, both in its noble designs and richness of color. Although no gold is used, a very splendid effect is produced, especially in a large miniature representing Saint Gregory enthroned under an elaborate architectural canopy, with figures of Archbishop Dunstan and the Benedictine scribe of this beautiful manuscript lying prostrate at his feet; see Westwood, *Irish Manuscripts*, Plate 50.

<margin>*Byzantine decadence.*</margin>
The beauty of the best English manuscripts of the twelfth century stands in remarkable contrast to the once splendid Byzantine school of illumination, which by this time had sadly declined from its former vigorous splendor, and had become weak in drawing, clumsy in pose, and inharmonious in color. The English school, on the other hand, throughout the twelfth century, was making rapid advances towards a

<page-num>104</page-num>
<header>REIGN OF KING CANUTE. [CHAP. VII.</header>

perfection of both design and technique that culminated in the Anglo-Norman style of the latter part of the thirteenth century, which for beauty of all kinds remained for a long time quite unrivaled in any European country.

<margin>Canute as a patron of art.</margin>
To return to the Anglo-Saxon school of manuscripts in the eleventh century, it should be observed that the Danish King Canute, unlike his destructive predecessors, did everything he could to encourage literature and art in England. With the aim of fostering the production of fine illuminated manuscripts, he introduced into this country—and especially into the royal and monastic libraries of Winchester—a large number of Roman manuscripts with the usual illuminations of the debased classical type. This, no doubt, helped to encourage the production of miniatures in outline such as those in the *Utrecht Psalter* <note>1. This *Psalter*, which is now in the public library at Utrecht, may possibly be one of the actual manuscripts that Canute brought from abroad. It was certainly in England for many centuries before it came into the possession of Sir Robert Cotton, from whose library it must have been stolen; otherwise, it would have passed into the British Museum library along with the rest of the great Cotton collection of manuscripts. The *Utrecht Psalter* was once thought to be the work of an Anglo-Saxon artist, but it is most likely the work of a French scribe. However, the miniatures are mainly in the debased classical style of Rome, and the character of the writing is even more distinctly classical, differing very little, in fact, from that of the fourth-century Virgil of the Vatican written several centuries earlier.</note>. <margin>Weak coloring.</margin> Another variety of Anglo-Saxon manuscript illumination, executed during the first half of the eleventh century, consists first of all of a pen drawing in brown outline, to which the artist later added narrow bands of blue or red with a brush, applied in a thin wash as a kind of edging for the brown outlines, apparently with the goal of giving roundness to the drawing <note>2. Good examples of this curious style of miniature can be seen in a manuscript in the British Museum, *Cotton, Tib. C. vi*.</note>.

This class of illumination is, however, very inferior in beauty and decorative splendor to the finest works of the monks of Winchester and Glastonbury, in which solid color in a great variety of tints is used—for example, in the previously mentioned *Benedictional* of Aethelwold and the *Pontifical* of Saint Dunstan.

<page-num>105</page-num>
# CHAP. VII.] THE NORMAN INVASION.

<margin>The Anglo-Norman school.</margin>
The Norman conquest of England in 1066 soon put an end to the Anglo-Saxon school of illumination, with its weak imitations of the debased classical style of Italy. In place of this, the magnificent Anglo-Norman schools of miniature painting were developed on both sides of the British Channel. England and Normandy became one country, and as long as this union lasted, manuscripts of a precisely similar character were produced both in Normandy and in England, as is described in the following chapter.

<vocab>Norman conquest, 1066, Anglo-Saxon, illumination, Anglo-Norman, miniature painting, Normandy</vocab>

This artistic style was shared between England and Normandy while the two regions were under a single government.</summary>

<page-num>106</page-num>

# CHAPTER VIII.

## THE ANGLO-NORMAN SCHOOL OF MANUSCRIPTS.

THE twelfth century in England and Northern France was a period of rapid artistic development in almost all branches of the arts, from manuscript illumination to a great cathedral or abbey church.

<margin>The Norman invasion.</margin>
With regard, however, to the art of illuminated manuscripts and other branches of art in England, it should be observed that though the conquered English and the Norman conquerors with remarkable rapidity merged with great solidarity into one united people¹, yet for a long period after the Conquest it was distinctly the Norman element that took the lead in all matters of art and literature. The bishops, abbots, and priors of the great English ecclesiastical foundations were for a long period wholly or mainly men of the Norman race; thus (intellectually) the native English took a lower place, and did far less to advance the arts of England than did the Normans, who formed the upper and <margin>Robert of Gloucester.</margin> more cultivated class. As Robert of Gloucester, the Benedictine monastic chronicler of the thirteenth century, says,

> **Of the Normans are these high men, who are of this land,**
> **And the low men are Saxons, as I understand².**

---

<note>¹ Indeed, it was not very long before the tables were turned and Normandy was reconquered by an English army under a king who, though of Norman blood, was distinctly an English king. The victory of Henry I over Robert, Duke of Normandy, at Tenchebray in 1105, went far to wipe out any feeling on the part of the English that they were a nation under the rule of a conqueror.</note>
<note>² *Chronicles of Robert of Gloucester*, Hearne's edition, 1724 (reprinted in 1810), Vol. 1. p. 363.</note>

<vocab>Anglo-Norman, illuminated manuscripts, Norman invasion, Robert of Gloucester, Benedictine, 12th century, Henry I., Tenchebray</vocab>

<summary>This page explains that during the 12th century, the Norman elite dominated English art and literature, with Norman clergy leading the major ecclesiastical foundations. Despite the gradual merging of the two peoples, the artistic advancements of the period were primarily driven by the Norman upper class.</summary>

<keywords>12th century art, Anglo-Norman school, manuscript illumination, Norman Conquest, social hierarchy, Robert of Gloucester, Henry I, Tenchebray</keywords>

--- Page 138 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Anglo-Norman school, moving from the social hierarchy of the Norman conquest to the specific architectural and artistic developments that characterized 12th-century England.</meta>

<page-num>107</page-num>

# CHAP. VIII. THE ANGLO-NORMAN SCHOOL.

<margin>
Architectural
growth.
</margin>
In the eleventh century, stone building on a large scale for military and ecclesiastical purposes was introduced into England by the Normans, replacing the fragile wooden structures of the Anglo-Saxons. Towards the close of the twelfth century, the Gothic style of architecture—with its pointed arches and four-part vaults—was brought to England by the Cistercian monks of northern France and soon spread far and wide throughout the kingdom.

The artists of this century began to study the human form, its pose and movement, and also learned to depict gracefully designed folds in drapery with much truth and with a keen sense of beauty¹.

<margin>
Anglo-
Norman
school.
</margin>
Manuscripts of various classes were now richly illuminated with many varied series of pictorial subjects, and the old formal rules of Byzantine conservatism were soon completely set aside. In the ornaments of the Anglo-Norman manuscripts of the twelfth century, rich foliage is used, made of stylized forms that recall the old acanthus leaf, the half-expanded fronds of various ferns and other plants—all used with great taste in their arrangement and wonderful life and spirit in every line and curve of the design. Older Celtic motifs are also used: ingeniously devised interlaced work of straps and bands, plaited together in complicated knots, and frequently ending in strange forms of serpents, dragons, and other grotesque monsters². These ornaments are strongly decorative in both form and color and, though delicately painted, are treated somewhat broadly, very unlike the microscopic detail of the earlier Irish and Anglo-Celtic school.

<margin>
Illumi-
nated
Psalters.
</margin>
At this time, a large number of magnificently illuminated *Psalters* were produced; and the use of gold leaf, both for the backgrounds of pictures and in combination with brilliant pigments, began to come into more frequent use. A fine typical example of English manuscript art at the

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Anglo-Norman school, focusing on specific examples of 12th-century illumination such as the Huntingfield Psalter and the spread of this artistic style across the Angevin Empire.</meta>

<page-num>108</page-num>
<header>ANGLO-NORMAN MANUSCRIPTS. [CHAP. VIII.</header>

The end of the twelfth century is represented in the so-called *Huntingfield Psalter*, which was created, probably in a monastic house in Yorkshire, shortly before 1200 A.D.<note>This beautiful manuscript is now owned by Mr. Quaritch, who listed it at £800 in his December 1891 catalogue. It seems to have once belonged to Sir Roger Huntingfield, who died around 1337 A.D.</note> It contains 68 miniatures in a very fine style, delicately painted on backgrounds that are partially gold; the subjects are taken from both the Old and the New Testaments, beginning with the Creation of the World. The general style of the illuminations in this *Psalter* is more exclusively English in character and less Norman than is typical for manuscripts of this date.

<margin>*Martyrdom of St. Thomas.*</margin>
The book is interesting because it contains one of the earliest representations of the Martyrdom of Thomas à Becket, who later became such a popular saint in England and Normandy. In this case, the painting is not from exactly the same date as the majority of the manuscript, but it was evidently added not many years after Becket's death, which occurred in 1170; Saint Thomas was canonized only two years later<note>It is noteworthy that even the earliest miniatures of Saint Thomas's death represent him in Mass vestments, officiating at the High Altar, whereas he was actually killed late in the afternoon on the north side of the church.</note>.

One of the earliest representations of this subject is a miniature painted by Matthew Paris on the border of a page of his *Greater Chronicle* in the library of Corpus Christi College, Cambridge, No. xxvi.

<margin>*The Angevin kingdom.*</margin>
Though I have used the phrase "Anglo-Norman" to refer to the school of manuscript illumination which, from the twelfth to the fourteenth century, existed on both sides of the Channel, it should be noted that manuscripts of a type similar to those of Normandy were produced in many places far to the south. Indeed, they were produced throughout almost all the territories of the Angevin kings, including the entire western half of France down to Gascony and the Pyrenees. The fact is that, to a great degree, all forms of Norman art extended throughout the entire Angevin dominions; so that, for example, we find a cathedral as far south as Bayonne (near the Spanish border) which closely resembles both in

<summary>This page describes the Huntingfield Psalter as a primary example of late 12th-century English illumination and discusses early depictions of Thomas à Becket's martyrdom. It also explains that the "Anglo-Norman" artistic style extended across the entire Angevin Empire, reaching as far south as Gascony and Bayonne.</summary>

<page-num>109</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER VIII.] THE PERFECTION OF ENGLISH ART.</header>

general design and details of moldings and carvings in the ecclesiastical architecture of Canterbury and Caen.

<margin>*English art in the 13th century.*</margin>
*English art at its highest period of development.* The thirteenth century was the peak period for all kinds of Anglo-Norman art; and indeed, for a brief time, England occupied the foremost position in the world regarding nearly all the main branches of the fine arts.

<margin>*Henry III as an art patron.*</margin>
The early years of the thirteenth century were a time of war and turmoil, little favorable to artistic progress, but during the long reign of Henry III, which lasted from 1216 to 1272, progress of the most remarkable kind was made. The King himself was an enthusiastic patron of all the arts, ranging from manuscript illumination to the construction of structures such as Westminster Abbey; and the lesser arts of life, such as weaving, embroidery, metalwork, together with stained glass, mural painting, and other forms of decoration, were all brought to a wonderful state of perfection in England between 1250 and 1300.

<margin>*Houses of Henry III.*</margin>
Immense sums were spent by the King on improving and decorating his palaces and manor houses all over the kingdom with a level of refinement and splendor that had been previously unknown. Many interesting contemporary documents still exist that list the expenses of the many works Henry III carried out. He spent large sums on fitting the windows with glass casements, laying down floors of "painted tiles," and paneling the walls with wainscot that was richly decorated with painting in gold and colors. Large mural paintings were executed by a whole army of painters on the walls of the main rooms; and decorative art for both domestic and church purposes was brought to a level of perfection in England far beyond that of any country on the continent.

<margin>*Chief works of Henry III.*</margin>
The chief works of Henry III were the building of a magnificent palace at Westminster in place of the simpler structure of the earlier Norman kings; the reconstruction of Westminster Abbey; and providing a great shrine of pure gold, richly studded with jewels of enormous value, for the body of Edward the Confessor. A long and interest-

His major achievements include the reconstruction of Westminster Abbey and the creation of a golden shrine for Edward the Confessor.</summary>

<page-num>110</page-num>
<header>110 THE PAINTED DECORATIONS [CHAP. VIII.</header>

An interesting series of accounts of these and other lavish expenditures of money still exists in the Record Office¹.

<margin>
Wall-paintings at Westminster.
</margin>
A magnificent series of wall-paintings, with subjects from sacred and secular history and from the Apocryphal books of the Old Testament, was executed by various artists, both monks and laymen, on the walls of the main rooms in the new Palace of Westminster. In style, these paintings were very similar to the miniatures in an illuminated manuscript of the time; they were simply designed, flat in treatment, and executed with the most minute and delicate detail. A great richness of effect was produced by the use of wooden stamps, with which delicate diaper patterns and other designs were stamped over the backgrounds of the pictures on the thin coat of *gesso* which covered the stone wall. These minutely executed reliefs were then thickly gilded, forming rich gold backgrounds, such as those so commonly used in the manuscripts of the Anglo-Norman school; see fig. 23, p. 130.

<margin>
Paintings copied from manuscripts.
</margin>
The close connection between these magnificent wall paintings and the illuminated miniatures in manuscripts is evidenced by an interesting record: in the year 1250, the King ordered Richard de Sanford, Master of the Knights Templars, to lend an illuminated manuscript in French titled "*The Deeds of Antioch* and the *History of the Crusades*" to the painter Edward of Westminster, so that he might copy the miniatures, using the designs to paint the walls of "the Queen's lower room in the new Palace of Westminster" with a series of historical pictures. From these paintings of "the Deeds of Antioch," the Queen's room was thereafter known as "the Antioch chamber.²"

---

<note>¹ See *Vetusta Monumenta*, Vol. VI. pp. 1 to 37, and Plates 26 to 39; illustrations are provided here of "the Painted Chamber" and its decorations before the fire of 1834, and a number of interesting extracts are quoted from the accounts now preserved in the Record Office.</note>

<note>² *The Deeds of Antioch* probably refers to the capture of Antioch in 1098 under the Crusader leaders Tancred and Godfrey of Bouillon. In the same way, the "Jerusalem" and "Jericho chambers" in the house of the Abbot of Westminster were named after the paintings on their walls. The curious "archaism" of these paintings, featuring figures of knights in the armor of the eleventh century, is explained below; see page 128.</note>

# CHAPTER VIII. OF WESTMINSTER PALACE.

<margin>The Painted Chamber.</margin>
The largest of the halls in the Westminster Palace, decorated with a marvelous series of exquisitely finished paintings, was known as "the Painted Chamber" *par excellence* because of its great size and the immense number of pictures which covered its walls. The system of decoration adopted in the thirteenth century was not to paint large pictures in a large hall, but simply to multiply the number of small ones, keeping the figures as delicate in execution and small in scale as if the room had been of the most limited dimensions.

This had the effect of enormously adding to the apparent scale of the room, a great contrast to the method of decoration which was employed in later times of decadence, when large halls were dwarfed and rendered insignificant by covering the walls with figures of colossal size. The sixteenth-century tapestry in the great hall at Hampton Court is a striking example of the way in which gigantic figures may destroy the scale of an interior.

<margin>Existing fragments.</margin>
The great beauty and extreme minuteness of the work can be seen in a few damaged fragments, now in the British Museum, which were not completely destroyed when the Royal Palace of Westminster, the seat of the two Houses of Parliament, was burned in 1834.

In the second half of the thirteenth century, during the reigns of Henry III and Edward I, the painting of England was unrivaled by that of any other country. <note>See, for example, that wonderful frontal, covered with miniature paintings, from the High Altar of Westminster Abbey, which is now preserved in the south ambulatory of the Sanctuary.</note> Even in Italy, Cimabue and his assistants were still laboring in the fetters of Byzantine conventionalism, and produced no works which, for jewel-like beauty of color and grace of form, were quite equal to the paintings of England under Edward I.

<margin>English sculpture.</margin>
In sculpture too, England was no less pre-eminent; no continental works of the time are equal in combined dignity and beauty, both of the heads and of the drapery, to the bronze effigies of Henry III and Queen Eleanor of Castile on the north side of Edward the Confessor's Chapel at Westminster. These noble examples of bronze sculpture

<page-num>112</page-num>
<header>THE ARTS OF ENGLAND. [CHAP. VIII.</header>

<margin>William Torell.</margin>
...were the work of the London citizen and goldsmith William Torell, who executed them using the beautiful *cire perdue* <note>lost wax</note> process with the greatest technical skill¹; see page 232 regarding their gilding, which was performed using the old "mercury process."

<margin>The Fitz-Othos.</margin>
One of the primary English families of the thirteenth century, among whom the practice of various arts was hereditary, was named Otho or Fitz-Otho. Various members of this family were goldsmiths, manuscript illuminators, cutters of dies for coins, and makers of official seals, as well as painters of mural decorations. The elaborate gold shrine of [Edward] the Confessor, one of the most costly works of the Middle Ages, was created by the Otho family. The great royal seals of more than one king were their handiwork, and it should be noted that the seals of England—not only from the thirteenth century but throughout almost the entire medieval period—were by far the most beautiful in the world, both for their splendor and complexity of design and for their exquisite, minute detail.

<margin>English needlework.</margin>
Another minor branch of art in which England far surpassed the rest of the world during the thirteenth century was the art of embroidering delicate pictures in silk, especially for ecclesiastical vestments. The most famous embroidered vestments now preserved in various locations in Italy are the handiwork of English embroiderers between the years 1250 and 1300, though their origin is not usually recognized by their current owners². The embroidered miniatures on these marvelous pieces of needlework closely resemble the style of the illuminations in fine Anglo-Norman manuscripts of the thirteenth century; in many cases, they have clearly been copied from manuscript miniatures.

---

¹ Various attempts have been made to show that Torell was an Italian, and that the painted retable at Westminster was the work of a foreign artist, but there is not the slightest foundation for either of these theories.
² As examples of this, I may mention the famous "Lateran Cope" in Rome, the "Piccolomini Cope" at Pienza, and two others of similar date and style in the museums of Florence and Bologna. On many occasions, we find that the Popes of this period, when sending the pallium to a newly elected English Archbishop, suggested that they would like in return embroidered vestments of English work, *opus Anglicanum*. It should be noted that in almost all published works on the subject, the previously mentioned copes are wrongly described as being of Italian workmanship.
