<page-num>113</page-num>
<header>CHAP. VIII.] MANUSCRIPTS OF THE VULGATE.</header>

<margin>
*Decline of English art.*
</margin>
In short, there is ample evidence to show that Anglo-Norman art of the thirteenth century, in almost all branches—and especially on English soil—had reached a higher level of aesthetic and technical perfection than had then been attained by any other country in the world. In the fourteenth and fifteenth centuries, largely due to the Black Death and the long-lasting Wars of the Roses, the arts of England declined; but it should not be forgotten that there was one period, from about 1260 to 1300 or 1320, when England held the leading position in the artistic history of the world.

Regarding the Anglo-Norman manuscripts of the thirteenth and early fourteenth centuries, the most remarkable category—both for its beauty of execution and the extraordinary number produced—consists of copies of the <term>Vulgate</term>, which are richly decorated with a large number of initial letters containing tiny miniatures of human figures¹.

<margin>
*Manuscript Bibles.*
</margin>
These Bibles vary in size from large quartos or folios down to the tiniest <term>codex</term> with microscopic handwriting. In the latter, it seems to have been the scribe's primary goal to fit the entire <term>Vulgate</term>, including the <term>Apocrypha</term>, the *Prologue of St. Jerome*, and an explanatory *list of Hebrew names*, into the smallest space possible. The thinnest <term>uterine vellum</term> of the finest quality is used², the text is often heavily abbreviated, and the characters are of almost microscopic size³. In these smallest Bibles, the initials are mostly decorated with stylized leaves and grotesque dragon monsters; but in the larger manuscripts, the initials at

---

<note>¹ Both before and after this period, manuscripts of the <term>Vulgate</term> were relatively rare; however, between 1250 and about 1330, many thousands of manuscript Bibles must have been produced, all very similar in style, design, choice of subject, and handwriting. There is no other large category of manuscripts in which such remarkable uniformity of style can be seen.</note>

<note>² As an example of the extraordinary thinness of this <term>uterine vellum</term>, I might mention a Bible from about 1260 in my own possession; it consists of 646 leaves, yet measures barely an inch and a half thick. Despite its extreme thinness, this vellum is opaque enough to prevent the writing on one side from showing through to the other.</note>

<note>³ For example, a Bible of this type in the Cambridge University Library, dating from about 1280, has between thirteen and seventeen lines per inch!</note>

<meta>This page continues the description of Anglo-Norman manuscript illumination, focusing on the artistic detail and technical methods used in 13th-century Bibles.</meta>

<page-num>114</page-num>
<header>MINUTE PAINTINGS. [CHAP. VIII.</header>

<margin>
*Historiated Bibles.*
</margin>
The beginning of every book—about 82 in total—is illuminated with a miniature painting of the most exquisite workmanship, a perfect model of beauty and refined skill. The drawing of the faces and hair is especially beautiful, executed with a fine, crisp line and the most precise and delicate touch, worthy of a Greek artist of the best period. The drawing of the hair and beards of the male figures is most masterly, featuring waving curls full of grace and spirit, despite the extreme minuteness of the scale.

<margin>
*Method of execution.*
</margin>
The miniatures of this school are executed in the following manner: first, a slight outline is lightly sketched with a lead or silver point; the main masses are then filled in with flat, solid color; the internal drawing of the folds of the clothing, the hair, features, and the like, are then added with a delicate pointed brush capable of drawing the finest possible line; and finally, some shading is added to give roundness to the forms, especially of the drapery. A broader touch is used for this shading, unlike the initial drawing of the details, which is executed with a thin, though boldly applied, line. As a rule, the portions in shadow are filled with a pure pigment; the highlights are represented with white, and the mid-tones with a mixture of white and the same pigment used for the dark shadows. Through this somewhat conventional system of coloring, the local color is never lost, and the overall effect is highly decorative—and far more suitable for painting on such a minute scale than a more realistic system of color would have been¹.

<margin>
*Bible of Mainerius.*
</margin>
One of the larger and more magnificent manuscripts of this class, in the library of Ste. Geneviève in Paris, is a historiated *<term>Vulgate</term>* in three large volumes. This is of special interest because it is signed by its scribe, a monk named Mainerius of the Benedictine Abbey of Canterbury.

Most of these Bibles and other sacred manuscripts of this

---

<note>¹ This method of painting shadows in pure color, and using the same pigment mixed with white for the rest, was used on a large scale by many Sienese painters in the fourteenth century, and by the Florentine Fra Angelico in the fifteenth. Fra Angelico's earliest works were manuscript illuminations, executed around the year 1407 in the Dominican Convent at Fiesole.</note>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the production of illuminated manuscripts by monastic orders, specifically the Benedictines, and the resulting unity of style across Western Europe.</meta>

<page-num>115</page-num>
<header>CHAP. VIII.] UNITY OF STYLE.</header>

<margin>*Benedictine scribes.*</margin>
The manuscripts of this period appear to have been written and illuminated in the great Benedictine Abbeys of England and Normandy. On this side of the English Channel, York, Norwich, Bury St Edmunds, Winchester, St Albans, and Canterbury were especially famous for their schools of illumination¹. And some work of this kind was probably done in every Benedictine house².

The unity of a great monastic Order like that of <term>St Benedict</term>, and the fact that monks were often transferred from a monastery in one country to another of the same Order in a different country, had an important influence on the artistic development of medieval Europe.

<margin>*Monastic unity.*</margin>
This unity of feeling was, of course, encouraged by the existence of a common language (Latin) among all the clergy of Western Europe; and to a great extent, the old traditions of a great Western Empire, uniting various races under one system of government, survived in the organization of the Catholic Church.

This unity of life, custom, and thought, which was such a striking feature of the monastic system, was largely the reason why we find a simultaneous change of artistic style occurring at several widely distant centers of production³. Consequently, it is also usually impossible, based on the style of illumination in an Anglo-Norman manuscript of the thirteenth century, to judge whether it was produced in Normandy or in England.

<margin>*Backgrounds of sheet gold.*</margin>
One extremely magnificent class of illumination from this date and school—specially used for *<term>Psalters</term>*, *<term>Missals</term>*, and other service books—has the background behind the figures formed from an unbroken sheet of burnished gold, creating a most sumptuously decorative effect.

---

¹ The Bodleian Library (*Douce*, 366) possesses an especially beautiful manuscript *<term>Psalter</term>*, which belonged to Robert of Ormsby, a monk of Norwich Abbey.

² In all periods, the Benedictines were the primary monastic scribes and miniaturists; the Mother House at <term>Monte Cassino</term> was one of the chief centers in Italy for the production of manuscripts, and wherever the Benedictines settled, they brought the art of manuscript illumination with them; see page 211.

³ This is especially noticeable in the development of architectural styles; not only general forms, but details such as moldings and the like seem to appear all over England almost simultaneously.

<summary>This page discusses how the organizational unity of the Benedictine Order and the use of Latin fostered a consistent artistic style in manuscript illumination across England and Normandy. It also highlights the use of burnished gold backgrounds in luxury service books like Psalters and Missals.</summary>

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the varieties of backgrounds used in illuminated manuscripts, following the description of plain gold backgrounds mentioned on the previous page.</meta>

<page-num>116</page-num>
<header>VARIETIES OF BACKGROUND. [CHAP. VIII]</header>

<margin>*Chequered backgrounds.*</margin>
In the fourteenth century, the plain gold background was mostly replaced by delicate <term>diapers</term> <note>Repetitive geometric patterns</note> of diamond and chessboard forms, with alternating squares of gold and blue or red. These were very rich and beautiful in effect, and sometimes of an extremely small scale, so that each diamond or square of the pattern is no larger than an ordinary pin’s head. In France, these patterned backgrounds were used very frequently, and their use survived in some cases until the early part of the fifteenth century.

<margin>*Scroll patterns.*</margin>
Another form of background, used in Anglo-Norman miniatures, consists of delicate scroll patterns or outlined patterns applied with a fine brush and with fluid gold over a base of flat opaque color. Gold scroll-work of this kind on a *pink* ground is especially characteristic of miniatures painted in England during the fourteenth and first half of the fifteenth century.

<margin>*Architectural backgrounds.*</margin>
A fourth style of background, used in miniature pictures of this period, consists of architectural forms which frequently enclose the whole miniature, combining the background, frame, and canopy into one rich architectural composition. This is often painted in gold, with details in firm, dark lines; although treated conventionally, it frequently provides a representation of an exquisitely beautiful Gothic structure <note>See below, figure 25, page 133</note>.

<margin>*Realistic backgrounds.*</margin>
Last of all come the realistic backgrounds, with pictorial effects of distance and aerial perspective. These are often very skillful and even beautiful in effect, but they are not as strongly decorative or as perfectly suited to manuscript illumination as the more conventional backgrounds of an earlier date.

These realistic surroundings began to be introduced in the fourteenth century, but are more especially characteristic of the fifteenth century. In the sixteenth century, when the illumination of manuscripts had ceased to be a real, living art—though still painfully and skillfully practiced by such masters of technique as <term>Giulio Clovio</term> and various Italian and French painters—the pictorial character of the backgrounds was carried to an excessive degree of elaboration and decline.

<summary>This page outlines four primary styles of backgrounds in medieval miniatures: chequered patterns, gold scroll-work on opaque colors, architectural frames, and the eventual shift toward realistic, three-dimensional landscapes.</summary>
<keywords>manuscript illumination, diaper patterns, Anglo-Norman miniatures, architectural backgrounds, Gothic style, realistic backgrounds, aerial perspective, Giulio Clovio</keywords>

--- Page 148 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of illuminated manuscripts, moving from general background styles to specific examples of magnificent Anglo-Norman Psalters from the thirteenth century.</meta>

<page-num>117</page-num>
<header>CHAP. VIII.] ILLUMINATED PSALTERS.</header>

<margin>
Psalter at Burlington House.
</margin>
Among the most magnificent of the Anglo-Norman manuscripts of the thirteenth century are copies of the *Psalter*. One in the library of the Society of Antiquaries at Burlington House is of extraordinary beauty for the delicate and complicated patterns of interlaced scroll-work which fill its large initials. The first letter *B* of the beginning of the Psalms (*Beatus vir* etc.) is, in this and some other illuminated *Psalters* of the same class, of such size and elaboration that it occupies most of the first page. Among its ingeniously devised interlaced ornaments, various little animals—rabbits, squirrels, and others—are playing, which are marvels of minute and delicate painting. Round the border which frames the whole are ten minute medallion pictures, some of them representing musicians playing on various instruments; one of these is a kind of barrel organ, called an *organistrum*, worked by two players. This magnificent manuscript dates from about the middle of the thirteenth century.

<margin>
The Tenison Psalter.
</margin>
Another even more beautiful *Psalter* in the British Museum, called *Archbishop Tenison's Psalter* after its former owner, was illuminated for Queen Eleanor of Castile, the wife of Edward I, about the year 1284. It was intended as a marriage gift for their third son Alphonso, who, however, died in August 1284, a few days after the signing of his marriage contract. For this reason, the manuscript was unfortunately left unfinished, and was later completed by a very inferior illuminator. The letter *B* on the first page is filled by an exquisite miniature of the Royal Psalmist; and in the lower part of the border is the slaying of Goliath by a youthful David, with Goliath represented as a gigantic knight in chain armor. At intervals around the border are minute but very accurately painted birds of various kinds, including the gull, kingfisher, woodpecker, linnet, crane, and goldfinch. In places where the text does not reach the end of the line, the space is filled by a narrow band of ornament in gold and colors, occupying the same space that a complete line of words would have done. This method of avoiding any blank spaces on the page, and making the whole surface one unbroken mass of beauty, was employed in the finest manuscripts.

<summary>This page describes two notable 13th-century Anglo-Norman Psalters: the intricately decorated Burlington House Psalter and the Tenison Psalter, the latter of which was commissioned for Queen Eleanor of Castile but left unfinished upon the death of Prince Alphonso.</summary>
<keywords>Anglo-Norman manuscripts, Psalter, Burlington House, Beatus vir, organistrum, Tenison Psalter, Eleanor of Castile, Alphonso, King David, Goliath, medieval illumination</keywords>

<page-num>118</page-num>
<header>MANUSCRIPTS OF THE APOCALYPSE. [CHAP. VIII.</header>

scripts of this and of other classes, especially the manuscripts of France and Flanders.

<margin>**Tenison Psalter.**</margin>
The *<term>Tenison Psalter</term>* appears to have been written and illuminated in the <term>Monastic House</term> of the Blackfriars in London; it is truly one of the noblest existing examples of English art during the thirteenth century, and is unsurpassed in beauty and skillful technique by the manuscripts of any age or country¹.

<margin>**MSS. of the Apocalypse.**</margin>
**Manuscripts of the Apocalypse.** The <term>Anglo-Norman</term> and French manuscripts of the <term>Apocalypse</term>, produced during the fourteenth century, are on the whole the most beautiful class of illuminated manuscripts that the world has ever produced².

For combined decorative splendor, exquisite grace of drawing, and poetry of sentiment, they are quite unrivaled. During several years before and after 1300, a considerable number of these copiously illustrated manuscripts of the <term>Apocalypse</term> seem to have been produced with a certain uniformity of style and design, which shows that, as in the case of the <term>historiated Bibles</term>, one model must have been copied and passed on from hand to hand through the <term>Scriptoria</term> of many different <term>Monastic Houses</term>.

<margin>**Perfect beauty.**</margin>
No words can adequately express the refined and poetic beauty of these <term>miniatures</term> of Apocalyptic scenes, glowing with the utmost splendor of burnished gold, ultramarine, and other brilliant pigments. The full figures of the angels—their beautiful serene faces, their exquisitely penciled wings with feathers of bright colors, and the simple, dignified folds of their drapery—are all executed with the most wonderful certainty of touch and the highest possible sense of romantic beauty.

The accessories are hardly less beautiful; the <term>Gothic arches</term> and pinnacles of the New Jerusalem, the vine plants and other trees and flowers, are designed with a perfect balance between decorative conventionalism and realistic truth, and

---

¹ The first pages of the two last-mentioned *Psalters* are illustrated by Shaw, *The Art of Illumination*, London, 1870, pp. 17 to 23.
² An example of the most marvelous beauty and perfection was presented by Lady Sadleir to the Trinity College library in Cambridge.

<summary>This page describes the Tenison Psalter as a masterpiece of 13th-century English art and extols the 14th-century Anglo-Norman and French Apocalypse manuscripts as the finest illuminated works ever produced. The author notes that these manuscripts shared a uniform style across various monastic scriptoria and praises the exquisite detail of their angelic figures and architectural settings.</summary>

<page-num>119</page-num>
<header>CHAP. VIII.] THE ENGLISH SCHOOL.</header>

Last of all, the sumptuous backgrounds covered with delicate diaper patterns or scroll-work in gold, blue, and crimson, all unite the whole composition into one perfect harmony, like a mosaic of gleaming gems, fixed in a matrix of pure, shining gold.

<margin>Machine-made art.</margin>
Perhaps nothing could better exemplify the gulf that separates the artistic productions of this feverish, steam-driven nineteenth century from the serene glories of the art of bygone days than a comparison of such a book as the Trinity *Apocalypse* with that masterpiece of commercial art called "the Victoria Psalter." The latter, printed on a steam-press on machine-made paper, illuminated by chromolithography, and bound in a machine-embossed leather cover, produces a total effect which cannot be adequately described in polite language<note>The *Victoria Psalter* is, however, frequently described in booksellers' catalogues, not only in polite, but in enthusiastic language. As an example, I may quote the following:

->THE BEAUTIFUL VICTORIA PSALTER:<-

> **PSALMS of David illuminated by OWEN JONES**, *beautifully printed in large type, on thin cardboards, on 104 pages, each of which is surrounded by* SUMPTUOUS BORDERS IN GOLD AND COLORS, *with the* CAPITALS ILLUMINATED, *and some of the pages consisting of large and most beautifully illuminated texts, columbier 4to. elegantly bound in morocco, the sides elaborately carved, leather joints, and gilt edges* (A VERY HANDSOME VOLUME), £4. 10s. n. d.</note>.

<margin>English Monasteries.</margin>
*The later English manuscripts.* In the fourteenth century, a more distinctly English style of illumination began to branch off from the Anglo-Norman style. Something like separate schools of painting gradually grew up in the great Benedictine Monasteries, such as those at St Albans, Norwich, Glastonbury, and Bury Saint Edmunds.

The type of face represented in English miniatures from about the middle of the fourteenth century onwards is rather different from the French type with its long oval face and pointed nose<note>These same facial characteristics are very noticeable in the beautiful carved ivory diptychs and statuettes of the Virgin and Child made during the fourteenth century in France and England.</note>. In English manuscripts, the faces are rounder and plumper, and the backgrounds are very frequently formed

<header>THE CHARACTERISTICS OF [CHAP. VIII.</header>

by gold scroll-work over a peculiar pink, made by a mixture of red lead with a large proportion of white.

<margin>The Black Death.</margin>
On the whole, the style of figure painting in English manuscripts deteriorated very distinctly after the ravages caused by the Black Death in the middle of the fourteenth century; that is to say, the average quality of excellence declined. Especially in the fifteenth century, a great deal of very coarse and inferior manuscript illumination was produced. On the other hand, there were some illuminators in England whose work is not surpassed by that of any contemporary French or Flemish artist.

<margin>Outline drawings.</margin>
One very beautiful class of English illumination, executed about the middle of the fourteenth century, has very small and delicate figures drawn in firm outline with a pen and brown ink. Relief is then given to the figures by the partial application of transparent washes of delicate color, producing an effect of great beauty and refinement. *The Poyntz Book of Hours* in the Fitzwilliam Library has no fewer than 292 miniature paintings in this very beautiful style. The book was written for a friend and companion of the Black Prince around the year 1350. Its delicate paintings have unfortunately, in many places, been coarsely touched up with gold and colors by a later hand.

<margin>Lectionary of Sifer Was.</margin>
A very fine, characteristic example of English art towards the close of the fourteenth century is preserved in the British Museum (*Harley Manuscripts 7026*). This is a noble folio manuscript *Lectionary*<note>A *lectionary* contained the *Gospels* and *Epistles* arranged for use during the celebration of Mass.</note>, unfortunately incomplete, which was written and illuminated by a monk named Sifer Was for Lord Lovel of Tichmersh, who died in 1408. It was presented by him to the Cathedral church of Salisbury, as is recorded by a note that asks for prayers for the donor’s soul. The text is written in a magnificent large Gothic hand, of the kind imitated by the printers of early *Missals*<note>Especially for the Canon of the Mass. The famous *Mentz Psalter* of 1459 is printed in characters of this size and style; see below, page 149.</note> and *Psalters*. On the first page is a large, beautifully painted miniature representing the scribe—

<header>CHAP. VIII.] ENGLISH ILLUMINATIONS.</header>

Sifer is shown presenting the manuscript to Lord Lovel. The figures are large in scale, and the heads are carefully executed portraits, evidently painted with great iconic skill. Each page of the text has a richly decorative border with conventional foliage of the characteristically bold English type. Figures of angels are introduced at the sides, and an exquisitely minute little painting is placed at the top, by the initial letter of the page.

<margin>English foliage.</margin>
The English foliated borders and capitals in manuscripts of this type are very bold and decorative in effect, with a simple form of leaf with few serrations, twining in most graceful curves and broadly painted in blue and red with very good effect, even in many manuscripts where the execution is not of the most refined kind. A variety of what is commonly known as "the pineapple design"<sup>1</sup> is frequently introduced into these very effective pieces of ornament.

<margin>Portrait figures.</margin>
It should be noted that the first growth of portrait painting in Western Europe seems to have arisen out of this custom of introducing portrait figures of patrons and donors at the beginning of important manuscripts. In French and Burgundian manuscripts especially, we find many very interesting portraits of kings and princes, together with those of the authors or the illuminators of richly decorated manuscripts.

<margin>Altarpieces.</margin>
Donors' portraits are also commonly introduced into votive altarpieces, usually in the form of small kneeling figures. As time went on, these figures of donors gradually became more important in scale and position. Thus, for example, the magnificent altarpiece in the Brera Gallery in Milan, painted by Piero della Francesca about the year 1480<sup>2</sup>, has, in the most conspicuous place in the foreground, a kneeling figure of the donor, Duke Federigo da Montefeltro

<sup>2</sup> <note>In the Brera Catalogue, this very beautiful painting is wrongly attributed to Fra Carnovale, a pupil of Piero della Francesca.</note>

<header>FINE EXAMPLES OF [CHAP. VIII.</header>

of Urbino, which is actually larger in scale than the chief figures of the picture—the Madonna and attendant angels. During the fourteenth century, in both altar paintings and manuscript illuminations, the portraits of living people were treated in a more subordinate way.

<margin>Portrait of Richard II</margin>
A fine example of portraiture in a manuscript can be seen in the *Epistre au Roy Richard II d'Angleterre* (Brit. Mus. *Royal Manuscripts* 20 B. vi), written by a hermit of the Celestine Order in Paris. The upper half of the first page is occupied by an exquisite miniature of Richard II on his throne, surrounded by courtiers, accepting the bound copy of the manuscript from the monastic author. The author kneels on one knee, presenting his book with one hand, while in the other he holds a sacred banner embroidered with the *Agnus Dei*. The background features a sumptuous chessboard pattern in gold, blue, and red, and the whole page is surrounded with a so-called ivy-leaf border.

<margin>Portraits of Henry VI and his Queen</margin>
The *Shrewsbury Manuscript*, containing a collection of chivalric *romances* (Brit. Mus. *Royal Manuscripts* 15 E vi), has another beautiful example of miniature portraiture. The first painting represents John Talbot, Earl of Shrewsbury—for whom this interesting manuscript was illuminated—kneeling to present the book to Queen Margaret of Anjou on the occasion of her marriage to Henry VI. The King and Queen are represented side by side on a double throne, surrounded by a group of court attendants. The kneeling figure of Earl Talbot is interesting for its costume; the mantle which the Earl wears is powdered (*semée*) with small garters embroidered in gold, an early but now obsolete form of state robe worn by Knights of the Order of the Garter. Both these manuscripts, though executed for English patrons, are of French workmanship.

Some of the most magnificent manuscripts of the fifteenth century and earlier were, like Lord Lovel's *Lectionary*, illuminated at the expense of some wealthy layman for the purpose of presentation to a cathedral or abbey church. In return for the gift, the church often agreed to observe a yearly <term>obiit</term> or annual Mass for the donor's soul, which in England was called

<header>CHAP. VIII.] ENGLISH MANUSCRIPTS.</header>
<meta>Digitized by Google watermark at bottom of page</meta>

“the year’s mind”; and this kind of gift thus often served to provide a “Chantry” of a limited kind.

<margin>*Queen Mary’s Prayer-book.*</margin>
One of the finest examples of English manuscript art from the fourteenth century is a *Psalter* commonly known as “Queen Mary’s Prayer-book.” This exquisite manuscript, which is kept in the British Museum, contains a large number of miniatures of Biblical scenes before the *Psalter* itself. These are executed in outline and treated with delicate washes of transparent color. The *Psalter* is illuminated in a completely different style, with brilliant gold and colors in all the miniatures and borders, which are painted with a wonderful delicacy of touch that is unsurpassed by the best French work. A *Bestiary* is included in the margins of the *Psalter*; and at the end, there are beautiful paintings of New Testament scenes. The date of this book is circa 1330; in 1553, it was given to Queen Mary.

<margin>*MSS. of Dan Lydgate.*</margin>
Another English manuscript of special interest, for both its text and its beautiful illuminations, is a copy in the British Museum of Dan Lydgate’s *Life of Saint Edmund*. This was written and illuminated in 1433 by a monk in the Benedictine Monastery at Bury Saint Edmunds; it is an early and very beautiful example of a manuscript written in the common tongue. In style, the illuminated borders are not unlike those in “Queen Mary’s Prayer-book.”

Another very similar manuscript, in both date and style, was sold at the Perkins sale in June 1873 for £1,320¹. This is a magnificently illuminated folio of “The Siege of Troy, compiled by Dan John Lydgate, Monk of Bury”; it contains seventy miniature paintings, primarily of battle scenes, in which the combatants wear armor from the first half of the fifteenth century. The illuminated borders are of the boldly decorative English type mentioned above, and the miniatures are large in scale, in many cases extending across the whole width of the page with its double column of text.

# ENGLISH SUBJECTS [CHAP. VIII.

<margin>*Woodcut initials.*</margin>
The use of woodcut initials seems to have brought the illuminator’s art to an end more quickly in England than was the case in continental countries. Caxton’s later books have printed initials<note>Caxton appears to have begun using woodcut initials in 1484 or 1485, but most continental printers continued to use hand-painted capitals many years later than that.</note> instead of blank spaces left for the illuminator, as seen in most of the early printed books of Germany, France, and Italy. English book-buyers appear to have been soon satisfied with simple illustrations in the form of rather roughly executed woodcuts.

The subjects represented in English miniatures are for the most part the same as those in contemporary French manuscripts; however, the martyrdom of Saint Thomas of Canterbury occurs more frequently in English than in any continental manuscripts<note>This scene and the name of Saint Thomas are frequently erased wherever they appear in English manuscripts. This was done by the special order of Henry VIII, who, after his quarrel with the Pope, appears to have regarded Thomas à Becket as a sort of personal enemy.</note>. Almost immediately after the event in 1170, this scene began to be represented; see above, page 108.

<margin>*St George and the Dragon.*</margin>
Another specifically English subject is Saint George, who was at first the Crusaders’ Patron and then the national Saint of England. He is usually represented as a Knight on horseback slaying the dragon with a lance. This subject did not come into popular use until the fourteenth century<note>See page 187 for a fine Italian example of this subject. It is interesting to note that the popular legend of Saint George and the dragon is simply a medieval version of the old classical myth of Perseus and Andromeda. In the more authentic Oriental lives of Saint George, this episode is not included.</note>.

Both in England and in France, during the fourteenth and fifteenth centuries, manuscript *Chronicles* and *Histories* of both ancient and modern times formed a large and important class of manuscripts; these were usually abundantly illustrated with miniatures. The *Chronicles* of Sir John Froissart was rightly a very favorite book on both sides of the Channel<note>It should be remembered that Norman-French continued to be the Court language of England until late in the fifteenth century, and for certain legal purposes even later. Its use still survives in the Law-Courts of Quebec and Montreal.</note>, and many richly illuminated manuscripts of it still exist; see below, page 139.

<summary>This page discusses the transition from hand-painted illuminations to woodcut initials in English books, specifically noting the work of William Caxton. It also identifies popular themes in English manuscripts, such as the martyrdom of Thomas à Becket and the legend of Saint George.</summary>
<keywords>Caxton, woodcut initials, Saint Thomas of Canterbury, Saint George, Froissart's Chronicles, manuscript illumination, Henry VIII, medieval art history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of English illuminated manuscripts, specifically focusing on the historical *Chronicles of England* and the format of parchment rolls used for world histories.</meta>

<page-num>125</page-num>
<header>CHAP. VIII.] IN ILLUMINATED MANUSCRIPTS.</header>

<margin>*Manuscript Chronicles.*</margin>
The British Museum possesses a magnificent manuscript of the *Chronicles of England* in seven large folio volumes, which were compiled and written at the command of Edward IV. The miniatures which decorate this sumptuous work are partly Anglo-Norman and partly Flemish, in the style of the school of the Van Eycks at Bruges.

One favorite form of *Chronicle*, giving an abstract of the whole world's history, was in the shape of a long parchment roll, illuminated with miniatures in the form of circular medallions. Some of these great rolls were written and illuminated by English miniaturists, but they appear not to have been as common in England as they were in France; see below, page 139. On these rolls the writing usually continues down the strip, not at right angles to the long sides, as on classical papyrus rolls.

<page-num>126</page-num>
<header>THE TIME OF ST. LOUIS.</header>

## CHAPTER IX.

### FRENCH MANUSCRIPTS.

<margin>Psalter of St. Louis.</margin>
DURING the thirteenth century, "the art of illumination, as it is called in Paris,"<note>Dante, *Purgatory* xi. 80; see above, page 31.</note> flourished under the Saintly King Louis IX (1215—1270) as much as it did in England under Henry III. Manuscripts of most exquisite beauty and refinement were produced in Paris, in a style little different from those of the Anglo-Norman school. One of the most beautiful and historically interesting is a *Psalter* (*Paris, Bibliothèque Nationale*) which is said to have been written for St. Louis about 1260. This is a large folio, copiously illustrated with sacred subjects minutely painted on a ground of burnished gold enriched by tooling. Many of the miniatures are framed in a beautiful architectural composition of cusped arches, with delicate open tracery supported by slender columns.

<margin>Perfect finish.</margin>
Fig. 22 gives the bare design of one of the <term>historiated initials</term> in this lovely manuscript, the capital *B* at the beginning of the Psalms. In the upper part is the scene of David watching Bathsheba bathing; and below is a kneeling figure of the king adoring Christ in Majesty. No reproduction can give any notion of the exquisitely delicate painting, or of the splendor of its burnished gold and colors. The historical scenes from the Old Testament have, after the usual fashion of the time, the Hebrew warriors and their enemies represented as medieval knights in armor.

It should, however, be observed that in this and many

<meta>This page continues the discussion on French manuscript illumination, moving from the 13th-century style of the Psalter of Saint Louis to the broader trends of archaism and the transition to Books of Hours in later centuries.</meta>

# ARCHAISM IN ILLUMINATIONS. [CHAP. IX.

<margin>Archaism of detail.</margin>
In other French and English miniatures of the time, ancient warriors are represented not in the armor of the actual date of the manuscript's execution, but with the dress and arms of a couple of generations earlier. The monastic artists were not skilled archaeologists, but they wished to suggest that the scene they were painting was one that had happened long ago, and therefore they introduced what was probably the oldest armor they were acquainted with—that of their grandfathers’ or great-grandfathers’ time. This is an important point, as in many cases a wrong judgment has been formed regarding the date of a manuscript from the mistaken supposition that contemporary dress and armor were represented in it.

It is just the same with the thirteenth-century art of England. Paintings executed for Henry III in his Palace at Westminster had representations of knights in the armor of William the Conqueror’s time or a little later. In later times, especially in the fifteenth century, this *naïve* form of archaeology was given up, and the heroes of ancient and sacred history are represented exactly like kings and warriors of the artist’s own time.

<margin>Manuscript Bibles.</margin>
The historiated Bibles of Paris in the thirteenth century were equal in beauty and very similar in style to those of the Anglo-Norman miniaturists, but they do not appear to have been produced in such immense quantities as they were in the more northern monasteries.

In the fifteenth century, the influence of the Church tended to check the study of the Bible on the part of the laity, and very few manuscripts of the Bible were then written. Their place was to some extent taken by the *Books of Hours*, enormous numbers of which were produced in France and the Netherlands, all through the fifteenth century; see page 141.

*French illuminated Manuscripts of the XIVth and XVth centuries.* To this class belong a great many of the magnificent manuscripts of *the Apocalypse* which have been described under the head of Anglo-Norman manuscripts. No hard and fast line can be drawn between the manuscript styles of Normandy and the northern provinces of France.

<summary>This page explains the practice of archaism in medieval miniatures, where artists used outdated armor to signify the past, and notes the 15th-century shift from Bible production to Books of Hours. It also highlights the stylistic similarities between Anglo-Norman and Northern French manuscript traditions.</summary>
<keywords>archaism, manuscript illumination, armor, Henry III, William the Conqueror, historiated Bibles, Books of Hours, 15th century, Anglo-Norman style, Apocalypse manuscripts</keywords>

--- Page 160 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of French manuscript illumination, specifically focusing on the 14th-century styles in Paris and Saint-Denis, the persistence of older motifs, and the introduction of the "ivy pattern."</meta>

## CHAP. IX. BEAUTY OF FRENCH MANUSCRIPTS.
<sig>M. C. M. 9</sig>
<meta>Digitized by Google watermark at bottom.</meta>

<margin>Archaism of style.</margin>
In the fourteenth century, Paris and Saint-Denis were important centers for the production of manuscripts of the most highly finished kind. Historiated Bibles, in both Latin and French, continued to be produced in great numbers until past the middle of the fourteenth century. Some of these French translations, executed as late as 1370, are what may be called archaistic in style; that is to say, the subjects selected and the method of their treatment and execution continued to be almost the same as that of the historiated *Vulgates* of France and Normandy at the beginning of the century. The miniatures are very small in scale and are often painted on backgrounds of brilliant checkered and other diapered patterns in red, blue, and gold. Though extremely decorative and beautiful, the miniatures of this class are not quite equal to those of the thirteenth-century Bibles, either in vigor of drawing or in delicacy of touch.

<margin>The ivy pattern.</margin>
On the whole, in the fourteenth century, the French schools of illumination were the finest in the world, and the manuscripts of Northern France were the most sumptuously decorated of all. One especially beautiful style of ornament was introduced early in the century and lasted with little modification for more than a hundred years. This was the method of writing on a wide-margined page and then covering the broad marginal space with delicate flowing scrolls or curves of foliage. Leaves and small blossoms of various shapes were used, but more especially one form of triple-pointed leaf which is known commonly as the “ivy” or “thorn-leaf pattern.” A brilliant effect is given to these rich borders by forming some of the leaves in burnished gold; and variety is given to the foliage by the introduction of minutely painted birds of many kinds—songbirds, game birds, and others—treated with much graceful realism <note>In the magnificent English embroideries of the thirteenth century, such as the Lateran and Pienza copes mentioned on page 112, we see birds of exactly similar style and kinds introduced among the scroll-work of the grounds and borders.</note>.

Fig. 28 shows part of a border from a manuscript of this class, a *Book of Hours* executed for the Duke de Berry; the

### CHAP. IX.] THE FINEST PERIOD.

->9—2<-
<meta>Digitized by Google</meta>

Typical pointed "ivy-leaves" grow from each of the quatrefoils which are introduced to hold the arms and initials of the owner. It comes from the same manuscript as the illumination shown in figure 25.

<margin>*Decorative unity.*</margin>
These elaborate borders are usually made to grow out of the ornaments of the illuminated initials in the text, and thus a sense of unity is given to the whole page. The decorations of the page thus become not just an addition, but an essential part of the text itself.

Figure 24 shows a miniature from a French manuscript of this magnificent class, the *Treasure-Book* of the Abbey of Origny in Picardy, executed about 1312 for the Abbess Héloise. It contains fifty-four large miniatures of scenes from the life and martyrdom of Saint Benedicta. The shaded part of the border is of the richest burnished gold, and the whole effect is magnificently decorative.

The scene represented is the murder of the Saint, whose soul is being carried up to Heaven by two Angels, held in the usual conventional loop of drapery.

<margin>*Horae of the Duke of Anjou.*</margin>
As an example of this class of illumination, we may mention the famous *Book of Hours* of the Duke of Anjou (Paris, *Bibl. Nat.*) illuminated about the year 1380. Every page has a rich and delicate border covered with ivy foliage, and enlivened by exquisitely painted birds, such as the goldfinch, the thrush, the linnet, the jay, the quail, the sparrow-hawk, and many others; and at the top of the page, at the beginning of each division of the <term>Horae</term>, is a miniature picture of most perfect grace and beauty. The decorative value of these pictures is enhanced by a background, either of <term>gold diaper</term>, or else of delicate scroll-work in light blue painted over a ground of deep ultramarine.

Enormous prices were frequently paid by wealthy patrons for sumptuously illuminated manuscripts, especially in the fifteenth century for *Books of Hours*.

---

<note>The phrase *ivy pattern* is a convenient one to use, as it expresses a very common and well-defined type of ornament, but the leaf is too conventionally treated to be recognized as that of the ivy or any other plant; the pattern is also varied with blossoms of different forms and colors.</note>

<page-num>133</page-num>
### CHAP. IX.] COSTLY MANUSCRIPT HORAE.

<margin>Book of Hours of the Duke of Berri.</margin>
The Paris Library possesses (*Bibl. Nat. Lat. 919*) a very magnificent manuscript *Book of Hours* (Horae), which was painted for the Duke of Berri at the beginning of the century by a French miniaturist named Jaquemart de Odin. At the Duke's death, this *Book of Hours* was valued at no less than four thousand *livres Tournois*, equal in modern value to at least two thousand pounds. It is mentioned as follows in the inventory of the Duke's personal property: *item, unes tres belles heures tres richement enluminées et historiées de la main de Jaquemart de Odin*.... Like all books of this class, specifically painted for a distinguished person, the arms and badges of the owner are introduced among the leaf-like ornaments of the borders of many pages; as the inventory states, *par les quarrefors des feuilles en plusieurs lieux faictes des armes et devises*<note>See Laborde, *Les Ducs de Bourgogne*, Vol. II. p. 1, and note to p. 121.</note>.

Fig. 25 shows part of a page from this lovely book, with a miniature of the Birth of the Virgin, painted by Jaquemart de Odin, within a beautiful architectural framing of the finest style.

Space does not allow any attempt to describe, even in outline, the many splendid classes of illuminated manuscripts which were produced by French artists of the 14th and 15th centuries. Only a few notable points can be briefly mentioned.

<margin>Architectural framing.</margin>
One special beauty of French illumination of this date is due to the exquisite treatment of architectural frames and backgrounds which are used to enshrine the whole picture. The loveliest Gothic forms are introduced, with the most delicate detail of tracery, pinnacles, canopy-work, shafts, and arches, all being frequently executed in gold with subtle transparent shading to give an effect of relief. From a technical point of view, these manuscripts reach the highest pitch of perfection; the burnished gold is thick and solid in appearance, and is convex in surface so as to catch highlights, and look, not like gold leaf, but like actual plates of the purest and most polished gold<note>The manner in which this splendid effect is produced is described below; see page 234.</note>. The pigments are of the

The author highlights the technical perfection of French illumination, particularly its architectural frames and the use of thick, burnished gold.</summary>

<header>CHAP. IX.] COPIOUS ILLUSTRATION.</header>
<page-num>135</page-num>

most brilliant colors, so skillfully prepared and applied that they are able to defy the power of time to change their hue or even dim their splendor.

<margin>*Survival of style*</margin>
Another noticeable point about French and Franco-Flemish illumination is the manner in which certain modes of decoration survived with very little alteration for more than a century. Thus, we find the blue, red, and gold diapers used for backgrounds, and the ivy-leaf pattern and its varieties <note>Shown, for example, in figure 25, page 134.</note>, which had been fully developed before the middle of the fourteenth century, still surviving in manuscripts of the second half of the fifteenth century, and continuing in use until the growing decline of taste caused them to be superseded by borders and backgrounds painted in a naturalistic rather than a decorative manner <note>The border from the Grimani Breviary shown on page 168 is an example, though a very beautiful one, of this decline in taste.</note>.

<margin>*Costly Horae*</margin>
The Franco-Flemish manuscripts of the fifteenth century were in some cases remarkable for the amazing amount of laborious illumination and the enormous number of miniatures which they contain. Some of these, which were executed for royal or princely patrons and generous paymasters, engaged the incessant labor of the illuminator for many years. In these cases, he was usually paid a regular salary, and so was relieved from the incentive for hasty work which caused so much inferior illumination to be produced in the fifteenth century.

<margin>*The Bedford Breviary*</margin>
One of the most famous examples of this lavish expenditure of time on one book is the *Breviary* of the Duke of Bedford, who was Regent of France from 1422 to 1435 <note>Now in Paris, *Bibl. Nat. Lat.* 17, 294. John, Duke of Bedford, was a son of Henry IV; he married Anne, daughter of the Duke of Burgundy, in 1423. Very fine portraits of the Duke and Duchess of Bedford appear in the *Bedford Missal* mentioned below.</note>. This wonderful manuscript, in addition to countless elaborate initials and borders around every page, contains more than 2,500 miniature paintings, all delicately and richly executed in burnished gold and brilliant colors, with backgrounds, in many cases, of the fourteenth-century type, with chess-board

<summary>This page describes the persistence of decorative styles like the ivy-leaf pattern in Franco-Flemish manuscripts and highlights the extensive, high-quality work found in royal commissions like the Bedford Breviary.</summary>
<keywords>Franco-Flemish illumination, ivy-leaf pattern, Duke of Bedford, Bedford Breviary, manuscript production, naturalistic style, miniature painting, patronage</keywords>

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Franco-Flemish manuscript illumination, transitioning from the general style of the period to specific famous examples like the Bedford Missal.</meta>

<header>THE BEDFORD MISSAL. [CHAP. IX.</header>
<page-num>136</page-num>

patterns and other diaper patterns of the most elaborate and sumptuous kind. The figures are of the finest Franco-Flemish style, showing the influence of the Van Eycks, who were then becoming the most skillful painters, technically at least, in the world.

<margin>*The Bedford Missal.*</margin>
Another no less famous manuscript is the *Bedford Missal* in the British Museum, which was painted for the Duke of Bedford and was presented by his wife to Henry VI of England when he was crowned King of France in Paris in the year 1430. The *Bedford Missal* contains no fewer than fifty-nine large miniatures and about a thousand smaller ones, not counting initials and borders. One point of special interest about this gorgeous manuscript is that the illuminations have evidently been executed by at least three different miniaturists, who represent three different schools: the Parisian-French, the Franco-Flemish, and the English.

<margin>*Manuscripts by various hands.*</margin>
It is by no means uncommon to find the work of several different illuminators in one manuscript. Naturally, when a wealthy patron ordered a magnificent book, he was not always willing to wait several years for its completion, as must have been necessary when the whole of a sumptuous manuscript was the work of one man.

Again, it was not an uncommon thing for unfinished manuscripts to be sent to Bruges, Ghent, and other centers of the illuminator's art from various distant towns and countries—especially from France, Italy, and Spain—in order that they might be decorated with borders and miniatures by one of the Flemish miniaturists.

In some cases, it was only the miniature subjects which were left blank, so that we have the text with the illuminated borders and initials executed in the style of one country, while the miniatures are of another quite different school.

Moreover, we find from the Guild records of Bruges that a certain number of Italian and Spanish scribes had taken up their residence in Bruges and become members of the Guild of Saint John and Saint Luke, so that some manuscripts actually written in Flanders have a text which in style is Italian or Spanish.

<summary>This page describes the Bedford Missal, a highly decorated 15th-century manuscript, and explains how multiple artists from different regions often collaborated on a single book. It also notes that manuscripts were frequently sent to Flemish centers like Bruges for specialized decoration, resulting in a blend of international artistic styles.</summary>

<keywords>Bedford Missal, Duke of Bedford, Henry VI, Van Eyck, Franco-Flemish style, miniatures, illuminators, Bruges, Ghent, Guild of Saint John and Saint Luke, manuscript production</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of stylistic variations in manuscript illumination, specifically introducing the monochromatic French technique known as grisaille.</meta>

<header>CHAP. IX.] ILLUMINATIONS IN GRISAILLE.</header>
<page-num>137</page-num>

Various other combinations of style occur not infrequently. Many English manuscripts, for example, have miniature paintings which are French or Flemish in style, united with bold decorative borders of the most thoroughly English type.

<margin>Manuscripts in Grisaille.</margin>
*Manuscripts in Grisaille.* In addition to the illuminations glowing with gold and color of jewel-like brilliance, a peculiar class of miniature painting came into use in France during the fourteenth century and, to some extent, lasted until the close of the fifteenth. This was a system of almost monochromatic painting in delicate bluish-grey tints with highlights touched in with white or fluid gold; this is called painting in *<term>grisaille</term>* or *<term>camaieu-gris</term>* <note>The Italians call it <term>chiaro-scuro</term> or "light and shade" painting; its use in manuscripts may have been suggested by the <term>grisaille</term> stained glass windows which were introduced by the Cistercian monks, whose rule prohibited the use of brightly colored figure subjects either in their windows, on their walls, or in their books.</note>; it frequently suggests the appearance of an onyx *cameo* or other delicate relief.

The earliest examples of *<term>grisaille</term>*, dating from the first half of the fourteenth century, sometimes have backgrounds of the brilliant gold, red, and blue <term>diapers</term>, while the figures themselves are painted in *<term>grisaille</term>*; but in its fully developed form, no accessories of color are used, and no burnished gold is introduced, only the *matte*, glossless fluid gold being used in some cases for the highlights.

<margin>Delicacy of Grisaille.</margin>
Some of the miniatures of this class are extremely beautiful for the delicacy of their modeling and the great refinement of the design, and are evidently the work of artists of the highest class. This system of illumination, being unaided by the splendor of shining gold and bright colors, requires a rather special delicacy of treatment, and was, of course, quite unsuited for the cheap and gaudy manuscripts which were mere commercial products. In some cases, the *<term>grisaille</term>* pictures are clearly the work of a different hand than the rest of the book, and thus we sometimes see them combined with richly illuminated initials and ivy-leaf borders of the usual gorgeously colored type.

In some late manuscripts, the *<term>grisaille</term>* miniatures are distinctly intended to imitate actual <term>bas-reliefs</term>, and are

<vocab>Grisaille, camaieu-gris, chiaro-scuro, Cistercian, illumination, miniature painting, bas-reliefs</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the "grisaille" technique of manuscript illumination, a monochromatic style using grey tints and gold highlights that emerged in 14th-century France. It notes the exceptional artistic skill required for this refined style and its occasional combination with more colorful decorative elements.</summary>
<keywords>grisaille, camaieu-gris, chiaro-scuro, Cistercian monks, miniature painting, 14th century, 15th century, manuscript illumination, bas-relief, diaper patterns</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of manuscript ornamentation, specifically describing the influence of classical sculpture and the rise of secular manuscripts in the 14th century.</meta>

<page-num>138</page-num>
<header>ILLUMINATED MANUSCRIPTS [CHAP. IX.</header>

...painted with deceptive effects of roundness. This led to the introduction of imitations of classical reliefs in gilt bronze or veined marble into manuscript ornaments, such as those that occur so often in the very sculpturesque paintings of the great Paduan, Andrea Mantegna.

<margin>Secular Manuscripts</margin>
Until the early fourteenth century, the art of the illuminator had been mostly devoted to books on sacred subjects. At this time, however, manuscripts of *Chronicles*, accounts of *travel*, *Romances*, and other secular works—often in the vernacular—were extensively written and illuminated in the most sumptuous manner, especially for the royalty of France and Burgundy.

Philip the Bold of Burgundy, who died in 1404, was an enthusiastic patron of literature and the miniaturist’s art, as was Charles V of France (1337–1380). A typical example of this school of manuscripts is a magnificent folio, formerly in the Perkins collection¹, of *Les cent Histoires de Troye*, a composition in prose and verse written by Christina of Pisa² around 1390. This magnificent volume contains one hundred and fifteen delicately executed miniatures, the first of which depicts Christina presenting her book to Philip of Burgundy.

<margin>Interesting details</margin>
These miniatures and others of the same class are very interesting for their accurate representations of contemporary life and customs. The costumes, the interior fittings and furniture of rooms, views of streets and the countryside, feasts, tournaments, the king among his courtiers, scenes in the Court of Justice, and countless other subjects are represented with minute detail and great realistic truth. In fact, in the miniatures of this class of manuscripts, we see the very beginnings of an early school of *<term>genre</term>* painting; in its poetic feeling and sense of real beauty, it ranks far higher than the ignoble realism of the later Dutch painters.

---

<note>1. It was sold for £650 at the Perkins sale in June 1873.</note>
<note>2. Christina was one of the most famous authors of her time; she produced thirteen different works. One of these, *The Feats of Arms and Chivalry*, was translated and printed by Caxton in 1489, about a century after it was written.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of secular manuscripts, focusing on the rise of historical chronicles, travel narratives, and romantic poetry during the 14th and 15th centuries.</meta>

<page-num>139</page-num>
# CHAP. IX.] OF SECULAR WORKS.

<margin>Manuscript Chronicles.</margin>
One rather unusual class of manuscript, which belongs both to this period and the following (the fifteenth) century, consists of French or Latin *Chronicles of the World*. These begin with the Creation and reach down to recent times, written and illuminated with numerous miniature paintings on great rolls of parchment, often measuring from fifty to sixty feet in length. These are usually rather coarse in their execution.

Sir John Froissart’s *Chronicles*, and their continuation from the year 1400 by Enguerrand de Monstrelet, were favorite manuscripts for sumptuous illumination among the courtier class of both France and England.

<margin>Manuscript travels.</margin>
Among the many illuminated books of travel produced during the latter part of the fourteenth and the fifteenth centuries, one noble example in the Paris library may be selected as a typical example. This is a large folio manuscript entitled *Les Merveilles du Monde*, containing accounts in French of the travels of Sir John Mandeville, Marco Polo, and others. This manuscript was written about the year 1412 for the Duke of Burgundy and was given by him to his uncle, the Duke of Berry. Its numerous miniatures are very delicate and graceful, featuring an elaborate pictorial style with views of landscapes and carefully painted buildings, street scenes, and other realistic backgrounds for the figure subjects, all executed with great patience and much artistic feeling. The richly illuminated borders of the text are filled with elaborate foliage, in which real and conventional forms are mingled with fine decorative results.

<margin>Manuscript poems.</margin>
In the fourteenth century, the growing love for national poetry and the more widely spread ability to read and write—which in previous centuries had been mostly confined to the clergy—led to the production of a large number of illuminated manuscripts. These included works such as the *Quest of the Holy Grail*, including the whole series of the *Chansons de Geste* with the Lancelot and Arthurian romances, the *Roman de la Rose* (one of the most popular productions of the fourteenth century), and a whole class of *Fabliaux*, or short stories in verse dealing with subjects of a chivalrous and romantic character.

<summary>This page describes various types of secular illuminated manuscripts from the 14th and 15th centuries, including massive parchment rolls of world chronicles, famous travelogues like those of Marco Polo, and popular romantic and chivalric poetry.</summary>

--- Page 171 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the transition from monastic to secular manuscript production, focusing on the popularity of historical romances and the development of professional illumination guilds in France and Flanders.</meta>

# SECULAR SCRIBES AND [CHAP. IX.

Romances based on ancient history and mythology, such as *Les cent Histoires de Troye* written by Christina of Pisa¹ about 1390—1395, became very popular among the knightly courtiers of the rulers of France and Burgundy².

In manuscripts of this class, the miniature illuminations play a very important part and give great scope to the imagination and skill of the illuminator.

<margin>Italian influence.</margin>
In southern France, the style of manuscript illumination differed a good deal from that of the northern provinces. During the fourteenth century, there was a significant element of Italian influence, partly due to the establishment of the Papal Court at Avignon and the introduction there of Simone Martini (or Memmi) and other painters from Florence and Siena to decorate the walls of the Papal Palace³.

On the whole, however, manuscripts were not produced in such abundance or with such skill in southern France as they were in the north. Paris, Burgundy, and the French districts of Flanders were the chief homes of the illuminator's art.

<margin>Secular miniaturists.</margin>
By this time, the production of illuminated manuscripts had ceased to be almost entirely in the hands of monastic scribes, as it had been in earlier days when manuscripts dealing with secular subjects were rarely known.

In Paris, Brussels, Antwerp, Bruges, Ghent, Arras, and other French and Flemish cities, large classes of secular writers and illuminators of manuscripts grew up, and special guilds of illuminators were formed, exactly like the guilds of other arts and crafts⁴.

Before long, this great expansion of the art of illumination, and the fact that it became a trade—a method of earning a

---

<note>² These chivalrous romances were no less popular in England; Dan Lydgate's *Book of the Siege of Troy*, adapted and translated from Guido de' Colonna's romance, was one of the most popular English books in the fifteenth century; see page 123.</note>
<note>³ See Muntz, *Les Peintres d'Avignon*, 1342—1352, Tours, 1885; and *Les peintures de Simone Martini à Avignon*, Paris, 1885. Many of these paintings still exist in a well-preserved state, especially those on the vault of the Popes' small private chapel.</note>

--- Page 172 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the shift from monastic to secular manuscript production, noting how the commercialization of the craft in the 15th century affected the quality of the art.</meta>

<page-num>141</page-num>
# CHAP. IX.] THE DECADENCE OF ILLUMINATION.

livelihood, like any other craft, led to a serious decline in the art. Although wealthy patrons were able to pay high prices for richly illuminated manuscripts, thus maintaining the production of very elaborate and artistically valuable works of miniature art, the practical result was a growing decadence in style and workmanship.

<margin>Decay of the art.</margin>
No illuminator working primarily for a financial reward could possibly rival the marvelous productions of the earlier monastic scribes. Laboring for the glory of God and the credit to be won for themselves and for their monasteries, they could devote years of patient toil to the illumination of one book, free from all sense of hurry, and finding in their work the chief joy and relaxation of their lives¹.

In most even of the best productions of the guild-scribes of the fifteenth century, one sees occasional signs of weariness and haste; and in the cheap manuscripts, which were produced by the thousand in France and Flanders during the latter part of the fifteenth century, there is a coarseness of touch and a mechanical monotony of style that reminds one of the artistic results of the triumphant commercialism of the nineteenth century.

<margin>Cheap manuscripts.</margin>
It is especially in the cheap *Books of Hours* of the second half of the fifteenth century that the lowest artistic level is reached in France, Flanders, and Holland. Education had gradually been extended among various classes of laymen, and by the middle of the fifteenth century it appears to have been usual not only for all men above the rank of artisans to be able to read, but even women of the wealthy middle class could use prayer books. Hence arose a great demand for illustrated *Books of Hours*², which appear to have been produced in enormous quantities by the professional scribes of towns such as Bruges, Paris, and many others. These common manuscript *Horae* are monotonous in form and detail; they nearly always have the same set of miniatures, which are

---

¹ See page 206 regarding the favorable conditions under which the monastic illuminators did their work.
² *Books of Hours* were the prayer books of the laity, just as the <term>breviary</term>, <term>portiforium</term>, or <term>portoos</term> was the prayer book of the priest.

It must not, however, be supposed that all the later French manuscripts, even from the latter half of the fifteenth century, were of this inferior class. Although the best figure painting was far inferior to the glorious miniatures in the *Apocalypses*

<page-num>143</page-num>
### CHAP. IX.] OF THE PICTORIAL TYPE.

this date are often very remarkable for their beauty, their refinement, and their interesting and very elaborate details.

<margin>King René's romance.</margin>
Some very fine manuscript illuminations of the highly pictorial type were executed for King René of Anjou, who died in 1480. Figure 26 shows a good example of this, with a carefully painted landscape background; it is one of sixteen fine miniatures in a manuscript of the *Roman de la très douce Mercy du Cueur damour épris*, one of the poetical and allegorical romances which were then so popular in France. This miniature represents the meeting of the Knight *Humble Requeste* with the Squire *Vif Désir*. This manuscript is now in Vienna, in the Imperial library, No. 2597.

<margin>Beauty of fruit and flowers.</margin>
The illuminated borders are also frequently of very great merit and high decorative value; they are formed of rich and fanciful combinations of various plants and flowers, treated at first with just the right amount of conventional style, but tending, toward the end of the fifteenth century, to an excessive and too pictorial realism. As late as the middle of the fifteenth century, the "ivy pattern" of the previous century survived with little modification, and very beautiful borders occur with branches of the vine, the oak, the maple, and other trees, together with a great variety of flowers, such as the rose, the daisy, the columbine, the clove-pink or carnation, the pansy, the lily, the iris or blue flag, the corn-flower, the anemone, the violet, and the thistle; and with many kinds of fruit, especially the grape, the strawberry, the pomegranate, and the mulberry. Among this wealth of fruit and foliage, variety is provided by the introduction of birds, insects, animals, and grotesque monsters that are half-beast and half-human, or else living figures growing out of flower blossoms—all designed with much graceful fancy and decorative beauty.

<margin>Later style.</margin>
Toward the close of the fifteenth century, one skillfully treated but less meritorious style of illuminated border became very common in France and Flanders. This consisted of isolated objects, such as sprigs of various kinds of flowers and fruits, especially strawberries, together with butterflies and other insects, shells, reptiles, and the like, scattered over the margin of the page, very frequently on

<page-num>144</page-num>
### PERIOD OF DECADENCE. [CHAP. IX.

<margin>*Imitation of relief.*</margin> a background of dull fluid gold<note>See below, page 230, for an explanation of the difference between "matte" gold applied as a fluid pigment with a brush and burnished gold leaf laid over a raised "mordant" or enamel-like ground.</note>. A deceptive effect of relief is commonly attempted by the painting of strong shadows, as if each object were lying on the gold ground and casting its shadow on the flat surface. This attempt at relief, of course, marks a great decline in taste, and yet it occurs in manuscripts that show much artistic feeling and great technical skill; as, for example, in the magnificent *Grimani Breviary*, mentioned below on p. 167 (see fig. 38).

<margin>*Use of fluid gold.*</margin> In French and Flemish miniatures of this period, gold, applied with a brush, is often used to touch in the highlights, not only in the *grisaille* miniatures but also in paintings with brilliant pigments, much in the same way as in the Umbrian and Florentine pictures of the same date.

Many manuscripts of the early part of the sixteenth century have elaborate architectural borders consisting of tiers of canopied niches containing statuettes, all executed in fluid, *matte gold*.

<margin>*Harsh colors.*</margin> The use of a very harsh emerald green is characteristic of this period of decline in France and in Flanders; and generally, there is a lack of harmony in color in the miniatures of this time, in which gaudiness rather than real splendor gradually becomes the main characteristic.

<margin>*Renaissance style.*</margin> At the end of the fifteenth century, the influence of the classical Renaissance of art in Italy began to affect French manuscript illuminations, and especially those by Parisian miniaturists. The introduction of architectural forms of the Italian classical style into the backgrounds of miniatures was the first sign of this, examples of which occur as early as the year 1475 or 1480. Fig. 27 shows a characteristic example of a French miniature executed under Italian influence. This is a scene of the marriage of the Blessed Virgin Mary to the elderly Joseph, who holds in his hand the dry rod which had blossomed. One of the unsuccessful suitors is breaking his rod across his knee, as in Raphael’s early *Sposalizio* in the Brera Gallery at Milan.

<summary>This page discusses the decline of manuscript illumination during the late 15th and early 16th centuries, noting the use of deceptive relief, fluid gold highlights, and the emerging influence of Italian Renaissance architectural styles on French and Flemish miniatures.</summary>

<page-num>146</page-num>

# MINIATURE BY JEHAN FOUCQUET. [CHAP. IX.

<margin>
*Horae <note>A Book of Hours.</note> of Jehan Foucquet.*
</margin>

The painting depicted in Fig. 27 is from a manuscript *Book of Hours* illuminated by the famous miniaturist Jehan Foucquet of Tours, who was employed by Louis XI from 1470 to 1475. This manuscript *Horae*, which has been severely damaged—as the miniatures were cut out from the text—was originally created for Master Etienne Chevalier. Foucquet and other French illuminators of his time were heavily influenced not only by Italian art, but also by the Flemish school of miniaturists who followed Memlinc and Rogier van der Weyden; however, by the end of the fifteenth century, Italian influence became dominant and soon eliminated all remaining traces of the older medieval or Gothic style.

Fig. 28 shows part of a border from the same manuscript that is illustrated in Fig. 25 on page 134.

<page-num>147</page-num>

# CHAPTER X.

## PRINTED BOOKS WITH PAINTED ILLUMINATIONS.

DURING the last few years of the fifteenth century and the first twenty or thirty years of the sixteenth century, Paris was remarkable for the production of a beautiful class of books which form a link between printed books and illuminated manuscripts.

<margin>Paris *Horae* <note>Books of Hours</note> on vellum.</margin>
These are the numerous *Books of Hours* printed on vellum, richly decorated with woodcut¹ borders and pictures, and frequently illuminated by painting in gold and opaque colors over the engravings. One of the earliest of these vellum-printed *Horae* was produced by Pigouchet for the bookseller Simon Vostre in 1487²; the pictures and borders are very simply treated in broad outline, which the illuminator was meant to fill in with color, aided only in the general design by the woodcut³. In 1498, Pigouchet began to produce for Simon Vostre *Books of Hours* of quite a different and still finer style, with engravings of the most exquisite beauty of design and delicacy of detail, perfect masterpieces of the engraver’s art. The decorative borders in these lovely books have

² <note>Perhaps the earliest was one issued in 1486 by Antoine Verard.</note>
³ <note>In these earliest Parisian printed *Horae*, the backgrounds of the borders are left plain white—unlike the later ones, in which the borders have dotted or <term>criblée</term> backgrounds.</note>

<page-num>148</page-num>
<header>PARIS HORAE ON VELLUM. [CHAP. X.</header>

<margin>Effect of coloring.</margin>
dotted (<term>criblée</term>) backgrounds, and the whole effect, though merely in black and white, is rich and decorative in the highest degree. The relatively coarse touch of the illuminator ruins the beauty of these <term>Horae</term>; but luckily, many copies have escaped this tasteless treatment, which must have appealed only to a very ignorant love of gold and gaudy color on the part of the purchasers.

<margin>Decline of style.</margin>
In the early part of the sixteenth century, vast numbers and varieties of these vellum-printed <term>Horae</term>¹ were issued by Pigouchet and Vostre, Antoine Verard², Thielman Kerver and his widow, the Hardouyn brothers, and other Paris printers and publishers. The cuts from the earlier fifteenth-century editions³ were reproduced, and a great number of new ones were cut; but after the year 1500, there was a very rapid deterioration of style. Even between the cuts of 1498 and those of 1503, a very marked change for the worse is apparent, as the fine medieval French style was replaced by somewhat weak imitations of the works of the Italian Renaissance.

These Parisian prayer books gradually replaced the coarse manuscript <term>Horae</term> which were still produced in the early part of the sixteenth century; and the latest examples of these vellum-printed books—the work of Geoffroi Tory and others as late as 1546—eventually were sold without any assistance from the hand (one can hardly say the art) of the illuminator in his extreme decline.

<margin>Latest decline.</margin>
In a weak way, the art of writing and illuminating manuscripts, as a sort of plaything for the wealthy, lingered on in Paris until the seventeenth century. An illuminated *Book of Hours* (*Office de la Sainte Vierge*), with four miniatures and many floral head-pieces of very minute

Fresh arrangements and combinations of a large stock of engraved blocks were made for the printing of almost every copy, and thus the long list given by Brunet is very incomplete; see the last volume of Brunet's *Manuel du libraire*, Paris, 1865.</note>

<header>CHAP. X.] THE EARLIEST PRINTED BOOKS.</header>

workmanship, which was in the Perkins collection¹, is signed *N. Jarry Parisinus Scribebat, 1660*. Other elaborate examples of Nicholas Jarry’s work exist in the Paris library, mostly painted in *grisaille*.

<margin>Early printing.</margin>
A few words on the connection between early printing and the art of manuscript illumination may not be out of place here. The inventors of printing—Gutenberg, Fust, and Schoeffer—appear to have had no idea of producing cheap books through their new art; rather, they believed that for a fixed sum, they could produce a more magnificent and beautiful book than a scribe could for the same price. Such a finished masterpiece of art as the *Mazarine Bible*, issued by Gutenberg in the year 1455, was not sold at a lower rate than the price of a manuscript Bible; but it was cheaper than a manuscript of equal splendor. So also, very few scribes of the fifteenth century could, with the utmost labor, have produced such a marvel of beauty as the *Mentz Psalter* of 1459, printed on the finest vellum and illuminated with 280 large initials printed in blue and red—perfect marvels of technical skill in the perfect fit of the two colors, or <term>registration</term> as it is now called².
<margin>The Mentz Psalter.</margin>

It is not known at what price this magnificent Psalter was originally sold, but existing records show that copies of the *Vulgate* produced in 1462 at Mentz <note>Mentz is an archaic name for the city of Mainz.</note> by the same printers, Fust and Schoeffer, were sold in Paris for no less than sixty gold crowns, equal in modern value to double that number of sovereigns.

<margin>Illumination and printing.</margin>
For this reason, as beauty rather than cheapness was aimed at by the inventors of printing, they left spaces for the introduction of richly illuminated and <term>historiated initials</term>, which were frequently inserted by the most skillful miniaturists of the time. Thus the art of printing and illumination walked hand in hand for more than half a century. Some of the earliest printers had originally been illuminators of manuscripts, as, for example, Peter Schoeffer de Gernsheim³, Mentelin of

---

¹ Sold in June, 1873, for £181, with the rest of the Perkins library.
² A copy of this glory of the printer’s art in Mr. Quaritch’s possession is priced in his catalogue of 1891 at £5250; only eight copies are known to exist.
³ In 1449 Schoeffer was a young illuminator of manuscripts residing in Paris.

<summary>This page explores the early history of printing, noting that pioneers like Gutenberg and Schoeffer prioritized artistic excellence and splendor over low production costs. It explains how early printed masterpieces, such as the Mentz Psalter, utilized advanced techniques like color registration and left space for hand-painted illumination by professional miniaturists.</summary>

<header>THE GREAT BEAUTY OF [CHAP. X.</header>

<margin>
The various arts of the printer.
</margin>
Strasburg, Bämler of Augsburg, and many others¹. The workshop of an early printer included not only compositors and printers, but also cutters and founders of type, illuminators of borders and initials, and skillful binders who could cover books with various qualities and kinds of binding². A purchaser in Gutenberg’s shop who had bought, for example, his magnificent Bible³ in loose sheets would then have been asked what style of illumination or rubrication he was prepared to pay for, and then what kind of binding and how many brass bosses and clasps he wished to have⁴.

<margin>
Early Italian printing.
</margin>
In Central and Northern Italy especially, the printed books of the fifteenth century and the first decade of the sixteenth century were decorated with illuminations of the most beautiful kind. Books printed in Venice about 1470–1475 by Nicolas Jenson of Paris and Vendelin of Spires, and Florentine books, even from a few years later, frequently contain masterpieces of the illuminator's art. The Magnificent Lorenzo de' Medici and others of his family were generous patrons of this class of work; as were also many of the Venetian Doges and prelates, especially various members of the Grimani family.

There are no grounds whatever for the belief that the early

In 1471 he was elected Warden or *Doyen* of his guild.</note>
