<meta>This page continues the discussion of early printing history, specifically refuting the idea that printed books were intended to be fraudulent copies of manuscripts. It details the transition from large, expensive liturgical works to the portable and more affordable Aldine classics.</meta>

<page-num>151</page-num>
<header>CHAP. X.] THE EARLY PRINTED BOOKS. 151</header>

<margin>Early colophons.</margin>
printed books were passed off as manuscripts, or that Fust was accused of having multiplied books by magical arts. The early printers usually inserted a statement in their *colophon* to the effect that the book was produced "without the aid of a pen (either of reed, quill, or bronze), by a new and complicated invention of printing characters." Many different varieties of this statement occur.

In the Mentz *Psalter* printed by Fust and Schoeffer in 1459, the printer's statement at the end is, *Presens Psalmorum codex venustate capitalium decoratus, rubricationibusque sufficienter distinctus, adinvencione artificiosa imprimendi ac characterizandi; absque ulla calami exaracione sic effigiatus et ad laudem Dei...* In the Mentz *Catholicon* of 1460, the phrase used is, *Non calami, stili aut penne suffragio......*

It was not until about half a century after the invention of printing that the new art grew into an important means for the increase of knowledge through the copious production of cheap books.

<margin>Aldine books.</margin>
No other typographer did as much for the advancement of learning as Aldus Manutius, a Venetian scholar and printer, who, in the year 1501, initiated a new and cheaper form of book by printing his Virgil in a small 12mo size, with a new and more compact form of character, now commonly known as *italic type*¹. As Aldus records in three verses at the beginning of the Virgil, the new italic font of type was designed and cut by Francesco Francia, the famous Bolognese painter, goldsmith, and die-cutter.

These small *italic* books by Aldus were not all intended for sale at a low rate; many copies exist which are magnificently illuminated, and some are even printed on vellum.

The publication of the cheaper Aldine classics dealt a death blow to the illuminator's art, which the early, large, and costly printed folios had done little or nothing to replace.

It should also be noticed that half a century before the invention of printing with movable types, quite at the

---

<note>¹ The first, or almost the first, book printed by Aldus was the *Hero and Leander* of Musaeus in 1494 in a small 4to size. The Virgil of 1501 was followed rapidly by a Juvenal and a Martial, issued in the same year.</note>

<summary>This page describes how early printers used colophons to credit their new technology rather than hide it, and it highlights Aldus Manutius's revolutionary introduction of small, portable books using italic type. These "Aldine classics" made literature more accessible and eventually ended the tradition of hand-illuminating printed books.</summary>

<page-num>152</page-num>
<header>WOOD-CUTS USED TO [CHAP. X.</header>

<margin>*Wood-cuts in manuscripts*</margin>
beginning of the fifteenth or toward the close of the fourteenth century, some few manuscripts of a cheap and inferior sort had their miniature illustrations not drawn by hand, but printed from roughly cut wood-blocks. These prints were afterwards colored by hand. Manuscripts of this class are very rare, and are now chiefly of value as supplying the earliest known European examples of wood engraving¹.

One of the most notable examples of these manuscripts illustrated with wood-cuts is described by Mr. Quaritch in his catalogue No. 291 of 1873². This is a South-German manuscript of about the year 1400, containing certain pious *Weekly Meditations* written on 17 leaves of coarse vellum; throughout the manuscript text are scattered 69 wood-cuts of Saints and Prophets, along with Biblical and other sacred scenes, averaging in size three inches by two inches and a quarter. These miniature designs are all richly illuminated with gold and colors; some of them have names and other inscriptions forming part of the engraved block.

<margin>*Block-books.*</margin>
This method of combining printing and manuscript very soon led to the next stage, that of <term>Xylographic</term> printing or "block-books"; in which not only the illustrations but the text itself was cut on blocks of wood and printed like the wood-cut pictures; each page occupying a separate plank of wood³.

These block-book illustrations were colored by hand in a very decorative and effective way, far superior to the coarse, gaudy painting in opaque pigments with which the Parisian illuminators so often spoiled the exquisite miniatures and the borders in the vellum-printed *Horae*. The block-books are not painted over with *opaque* pigment, but delicately washed in with *transparent* tints, without obscuring the outlines of the printed pictures, which, though simple and even unrefined in

---

<note>¹ Chinese wood engravings of a considerably earlier date do exist.</note>
<note>² See page 1373; this remarkable manuscript was then (in 1873) priced at £650.</note>
<note>³ Early wood-cuts were not cut on the cross ends of the grain, but on the "plank side" of a wooden board.</note>

<summary>This page describes the transition from hand-drawn manuscript illustrations to wood-cut prints and the eventual development of "block-books" (xylography), where both text and images were engraved into wood. It highlights the superior, transparent coloring techniques used in block-books compared to the opaque pigments common in Parisian illuminated books.</summary>
<keywords>wood-cuts, manuscripts, Xylographic printing, block-books, Mr. Quaritch, Weekly Meditations, vellum, Horae, illuminators, printing history</keywords>

<page-num>153</page-num>
<header>CHAP. X.] ILLUSTRATED MANUSCRIPTS.</header>

treatment, are often full of real beauty and great decorative charm¹.

<margin>*Illumination and printing.*</margin>
Thus we see that as early as about the year 1400, the printer's art had begun to supplement that of the manuscript illuminator²; and the two arts continued to work, as it were, hand in hand until after the close of the fifteenth century, when the illumination of manuscripts ceased to be a real living art and gradually degenerated into a mere appendage to individual pomp and luxury.

---

<note>¹ The *Cantica Canticorum* of about 1435 has most lovely designs, and the *Apocalypse*, the *Ars Moriendi*, the *Speculum Humanae Salvationis*, and the *Biblia Pauperum* all have woodcut illustrations of great vigor and spirit, produced between about 1420 and 1450.</note>
<note>² Even before 1400, initial letters in manuscripts had been occasionally printed from wooden stamps covered with red or blue pigment.</note>

<summary>This page describes how the arts of printing and manuscript illumination coexisted from approximately 1400 until the late 15th century. It notes that while woodcuts initially supported manuscript production, illumination eventually declined into a decorative luxury as printing became dominant.</summary>

<keywords>manuscript illumination, printing history, woodcuts, Cantica Canticorum, Apocalypse, Ars Moriendi, Speculum Humanae Salvationis, Biblia Pauperum, 15th-century art</keywords>

## CHAPTER XI.

### ILLUMINATED MANUSCRIPTS OF THE TEUTONIC SCHOOL AFTER THE TENTH CENTURY.

<margin>German manuscripts of the 11th century.</margin>
Although the eleventh century was mostly a period of artistic decadence—mentioned above as having followed the brilliant Carolingian period (see page 78)—yet we find that in certain places in Germany there was a very distinct beginning of an artistic revival, especially in the illumination of manuscripts, around the middle of the eleventh century and even earlier. A school of magnificently decorative art began to develop then; and although the drawing of the human figure was still weak, manuscript illuminators produced effects of the noblest decorative character, foreshadowing that marvelous climax of manuscript art which was reached in the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries.

<margin>Missal of Henry II.</margin>
Figure 29 shows a sumptuously decorative page from an eleventh-century manuscript *Missal* which was produced for the Emperor Henry II (now in the Munich library). On a brilliant diapered background in gold, red, and blue, a standing figure of the Emperor is crowned by Christ, who sits within a *vesica* aureole. The Emperor receives from two angels the great Cross Standard of the Empire and a sword. His arms are supported by a saint on each side, Saint Ulrich and Saint Emmeram. The whole page is a superb piece of decoration and is especially interesting because illuminations of this type were evidently used by the earliest painters of stained glass windows to provide them with designs.

<meta>Digitized by Google watermark at bottom</meta>

<summary>This page describes the 11th-century revival of German manuscript illumination following the Carolingian decline, highlighting the decorative "Missal of Henry II" as a precursor to later masterpieces and early stained glass design.</summary>
<keywords>Teutonic School, German manuscripts, 11th century, Carolingian period, Missal, Emperor Henry II, vesica aureole, Saint Ulrich, Saint Emmeram, stained glass windows, illumination</keywords>

# GERMAN MSS. OF THE TWELFTH CENTURY. [CHAP. XI.

<margin>*Gospels of the 11th century.*</margin>
A decorative composition; in the center is a Crucifixion with figures of Life and Death at the foot of the cross. In the lower angles are minute paintings of the torn Veil of the Temple and the opened sepulchers; above, at the sides, are symbolic figures of the Church and the Synagogue, or Grace and Law. At the upper angles are the Sun and Moon veiling their faces before the Passion of Christ. Graceful scroll foliage, of the Oriental textile type, fills in the spandrels.

<margin>*Revival of art.*</margin>
In the 12th century, the revival of manuscript art in Germany progressed very rapidly, and an immense number of magnificently illuminated manuscripts were produced, especially in the major Benedictine monasteries, which had always been the primary homes of learning and the chief centers of the illuminator's art in Germany, as in other European countries <note>Much of the German bronze work of this period is extremely fine and skillful in execution, such as the fonts and doors of churches at Hildesheim, Augsburg, and other places. The bronze font at Liége, cast around 1112 by a sculptor of the German school, is a work of most wonderful grace and beauty.</note>.

<margin>*Grotesque forms.*</margin>
Frederick I (Barbarossa), born 1121, died 1190, imitated the example of Charlemagne in his patronage of art and especially of the art of the illuminator. The manuscripts of his time are remarkable for the richness and imagination of their twining masses of conventional foliage, mingled with dragons, monkeys, human forms, and monsters of all kinds, designed with extreme beauty in their strong sweeping curves and colored with brilliant and yet harmonious tints in a superbly decorative way. Although the figure drawing of the illuminators had not reached the perfection that was attained a century later, in terms of decorative ornament nothing could surpass the best German manuscripts of the 12th century <note>Until the 13th century, the art of the Netherlands and Flanders was German in character; after that, Flanders was, artistically as well as politically, partly Teutonic and partly French.</note>. Figures 32 and 33 give good examples of the illuminations of this date.

Figure 32 shows a fine initial S formed out of a winged dragon and ornamented with conventional foliage of the

It also notes the significance of Benedictine monasteries and contemporary German bronze work.</summary>

GERMAN ART OF THE TWELFTH [CHAP. XI.

noblest type. This initial shows the surviving Celtic or rather Northumbrian influence, which in the time of Charlemagne had been so important in the German Empire.

<margin>Painting of the Annunciation.</margin>
Fig. 33 illustrates a miniature of the Annunciation from a fine manuscript *Evangeliarium* or Book of the *Gospels*, which is now in the library at Karlsruhe. The drawing, though stiff in pose, is noble in style; and the whole miniature, with its graceful scroll-work background, is of high decorative value, a prototype of the perfect style of the French and Anglo-Norman illuminations of the second half of the thirteenth century. In this painting, as in many other manuscripts of early date, the Blessed Virgin Mary is represented as occupied in spinning with a distaff while the angel Gabriel approaches to announce the birth of the Messiah.

<margin>Page of a Calendar.</margin>
Fig. 34 shows a very beautifully designed page of the Calendar at the beginning of a *Psalter* executed about the year 1200 for the Landgrave of Thüringen. On the left is the space in which the scribe inserted the days of the months, and on the right is a noble and gracefully drawn figure of Saint Matthew. The interlaced foliage of the initial K is of characteristic German type.

Fig. 35 shows a very elaborate and graceful initial Y, from another manuscript of the same date, decorated by a vine-plant from which a youth is gathering grapes, while a monkey, sitting in the branches, is eating some of the fruit. The whole design is a masterpiece of decorative beauty, elaborately worked out in gold and colors.

<margin>Mural paintings.</margin>
The fine *mural paintings* of this date are frequently identical in style and design with pages from illuminated manuscripts. This is most remarkably the case with the late twelfth-century paintings on the walls and vault of the church of St. Michael at Hildesheim; in which the figures, the conventional foliage, and the general arrangement of the whole have evidently been copied from manuscript illuminations¹.

A complex reproduction of a decorative scheme representing a portion of the 12th-century painted wooden ceiling of the Church of St. Michael in Hildesheim, Germany. The style mirrors that of Romanesque manuscript illumination.

Central Mandorla:
An oval frame (<term>vesica piscis</term>) contains a scene of the Fall of Man. Adam and Eve are depicted standing on either side of the Tree of Knowledge, with the serpent coiled around the trunk. Adam is reaching for or holding the fruit. The interior of the mandorla is filled with a pattern of small decorative circles.

Architectural and Prophetic Grid:
The central scene is surrounded by an intricate grid of scrollwork and geometric patterns.
- Corner Panels: The four corners contain the symbols of the Evangelists (the Winged Man for Matthew, the Winged Ox for Luke, the Winged Eagle for John, and the Winged Lion for Mark).
- Roundels: Nine circular medallions contain busts of biblical prophets and kings, each identified by a Latin inscription:
    - Top (left to right): ISAIA (Isaiah), IEREMIAS (Jeremiah).
    - Left side (top to bottom): EZECHIEL (Ezekiel), DANIEL.
    - Right side (top to bottom): SALOMON (Solomon), DAVID, IESSE (Jesse).
    - Bottom (left to right): OZEE (Hosea), IONAS (Jonah).
- Side Panels: Additional rectangular panels between the roundels and corners feature seated figures, likely further prophets or ancestors.

The entire composition is characterized by elaborate foliate scrollwork and multi-banded borders.
</image-desc>

<meta>The previous page discussed the Romanesque ceiling paintings of St. Michael's Church in Hildesheim as being stylistically identical to contemporary manuscript illuminations. This page moves forward in time to discuss German and Flemish manuscripts of the 13th through 15th centuries.</meta>

### CHAP. XI.] LATER GERMAN MANUSCRIPTS.

The German manuscripts of the 13th and 14th centuries are less purely national in style. The finest illuminations of this date show, in some cases, a marked French influence, and a strong Italian influence was prevalent, especially during the 14th century.

<margin>Manuscript of the 14th century.</margin>
Figure 37 gives a good example of this from a manuscript *Passionale*, written in 1312 for the Abbess of the Convent of St. George at Prague. The figures in this manuscript resemble those in some of the Florentine illuminated manuscripts of Dante’s *Divina Commedia*, executed between 1360 and 1390. The subject of the miniatures shown in Figure 37 is a romantic story of a bride who was carried off by brigands and flung into a blazing furnace; she was rescued unhurt by her husband, a knight, with the aid of the Blessed Virgin Mary.

<margin>School of the van Eycks.</margin>
In the 15th century, an important development of Teutonic art took place under the van Eycks and their pupils. In Flanders, especially in Bruges, Antwerp, and Ghent, a very elaborate and beautiful class of illumination was produced, which was in some respects different in style from the Franco-Flemish school of art.

<margin>School of Memlinc.</margin>
In the latter part of the century, magnificent manuscripts were produced by illuminators of the Memlinc and van der Weyden school. One such example is the famous Grimani *Breviary* in the Venetian Ducal library, so named because it was bought from a Sicilian dealer in 1521 for 500 gold ducats by Cardinal Grimani. He was a member of the Venetian Grimani family, who were liberal patrons of this class of art. This sum was equal to about £2,000 in modern value <note>This "modern value" refers to the time of the book's publication in the late 19th century.</note>. The dealer attributed the miniatures in this manuscript to Hans Memlinc, Gérard of Bruges, and Lieven of Antwerp; they were probably by the latter two illuminators, and not by Memlinc, who died in 1494 or 1495.

<margin>Gérard David.</margin>
Gérard (or Gheeraert) of Bruges was a native of Oudewater in Holland. He was born around the middle of the 15th century and settled in Bruges in 1483. At that time, he became a member of the Guild of Saint John and Saint Luke, a guild to which all painters and manuscript illuminators were required to belong. Gérard took the surname David and became a famous painter of triptychs and altar-pieces, as well

### CHAPTER XI.] LATER GERMAN MANUSCRIPTS.

The German manuscripts of the 13th and 14th centuries are less purely national in style. The finest illuminations of this date show, in some cases, a marked French influence, and a strong Italian influence was prevalent, especially during the 14th century.

<margin>Manuscript of the 14th century.</margin>
Figure 37 gives a good example of this from a manuscript <term>Passionale</term> <note>A book containing the stories of the sufferings of saints or martyrs</note>, written in 1312 for the Abbess of the Convent of St. George at Prague. The figures in this manuscript resemble those in some of the Florentine illuminated manuscripts of Dante’s *Divina Commedia*, produced between 1360 and 1390. The subject of the miniatures shown in Figure 37 is a romantic story of a bride who was carried off by brigands and flung into a blazing furnace; she was rescued unhurt by her husband, a knight, with the aid of the Blessed Virgin Mary.

<margin>School of the van Eycks.</margin>
In the 15th century, an important development of <term>Teutonic art</term> <note>Germanic art</note> took place under the van Eycks and their pupils. In Flanders, especially in Bruges, Antwerp, and Ghent, a very elaborate and beautiful class of illumination was produced, which was in some respects different in style from the Franco-Flemish school of art.

<margin>School of Memlinc.</margin>
In the latter part of the century, magnificent manuscripts were produced by illuminators of the Memlinc and van der Weyden school. One such example is the famous Grimani *Breviary* in the Venetian Ducal library, so named because it was bought from a Sicilian dealer in 1521 for 500 gold ducats by Cardinal Grimani. He was a member of the Venetian Grimani family, who were generous patrons of this class of art. This sum ...

It specifically references a 1312 Passionale and the famous Grimani Breviary.</summary>

### CHAP. XI.] THE BRANDENBURG AND GRIMANI MSS. 167

<margin>The *Horae* of Prince Albert.</margin>
...as a skillful illuminator of manuscripts. Many fine panel paintings by him still exist in Bruges and elsewhere. There are also several fine manuscripts with miniatures by his hand in addition to those in the <term>Grimani Breviary</term>. Among these are two <term>Books of Hours</term> in the collection of the late Baron Anselm Rothschild of Vienna, and another manuscript <term>Horae</term>, which was written and illuminated for the Cardinal Prince Albert, Elector of Brandenburg, who was consecrated Archbishop of Magdeburg in the year 1513 at the age of twenty-three. An interesting monograph, with photographic reproductions of the miniatures, was written by Mr. W. H. J. Weale for Mr. F. S. Ellis, the owner of the manuscript. This lovely manuscript is almost equal in beauty to the <term>Grimani Breviary</term>; it is somewhat later in date, having been illuminated between 1514 and 1523.

<margin>The *Grimani Breviary*.</margin>
The miniatures in the sumptuous <term>Grimani Breviary</term>, which dates from the later years of the 15th century (probably about 1496), are very pictorial in style, with figures that are larger than usual in proportion to the size of the page. In some of the miniatures, the figures are shown only in half-length, so that the elaborately finished heads are painted on a large scale. The borders that surround the pages, enclosing both text and miniatures, are in the Franco-Flemish style, with realistic flowers, fruit, insects, and the like, scattered over a flat gold ground, as is described earlier on page 143. The butterflies, dragonflies, strawberries, irises, and lilies are perfect marvels of naturalistic skill and beauty.

<margin>The month of April.</margin>
Fig. 38 illustrates one of the miniatures in the <term>Grimani Breviary</term>; it is one of the lovely series representing the characteristic occupations of the twelve months in the <term>Calendar</term>, which commonly occur as small pictures at the tops of pages in manuscript <term>Calendars</term> of the 15th century, but in this exceptionally magnificent book, they are full-page miniatures. The one copied in Fig. 38 represents the month of April, a time for

<header>CHAP. XI.] LATE FLEMISH SCHOOL. 169</header>

<margin>The Grimani Breviary.</margin>love-making and outdoor parties of pleasure; here illustrated by a most beautiful and dignified group of ladies and gentlemen, enlivened by the humor of the scene in the left-hand corner, with a little dog barking jealously at another pet dog which is being petted on a lady's lap.

The background, with trees and Cathedral spires like those of Antwerp or Malines, is especially beautiful and highly finished.

Though marvels of minute and beautiful workmanship, these late Teutonic manuscripts belong to a period of decadence. As has already been remarked, neither in poetic feeling nor in decorative value do they approach the masterpieces of French art during the fourteenth century.

<margin>Horae of King René.</margin>Fig. 39 shows a page from a *Book of Hours* (Paris, *Bibl. Nat. Lat.* 10, 532) which was illuminated for King René II of Lorraine (1473 to 1508). The figure of the Virgin shows the influence of Italian art, which about this time, 1490, was largely modifying and adding grace to the paintings of Flanders.

The border, with lupines or vetch-plant realistically painted on a gold ground, is a good typical specimen of the style.

<margin>Horae of Anne of Brittany.</margin>The famous *Prayer-book of Anne of Brittany*, painted about 1500, after her second marriage to Louis XII, is a work of the same magnificent style, with an immense variety of the most exquisitely painted fruits and flowers treated with the most minute realism. It is now in the Paris library. <note>1. The whole of this gorgeous manuscript was published in a fairly good "facsimile" by Curmer, *Le livre d'Heures de la Reine Anne de Bretagne*, 2 Vols. Imperial 4to., Paris, 1861; see also Laborde, *Ducs de Bourgogne*, Vol. I. p. xxiv.</note>

Fig. 40 gives a page from a magnificent *Book of Hours* in the Imperial Library of Vienna (no. 1857); the miniatures in which are of the finest Teutonic type, in some cases suggesting the school of Van der Weyden, and in others that of Hans Memlinc. The conventional scroll-work of foliage with long serrated leaves in the border is very characteristic of the German and Dutch manuscripts of the fifteenth century.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Teutonic School of manuscript illumination, focusing on technical methods and decorative motifs such as grotesque figures and the use of gold.</meta>

# THE TEUTONIC SCHOOL. [CHAP. XI.

<margin>Technical methods.</margin>
Highlights are touched in with white, and the shadows with a *grisaille* blue. Another beautiful style of decoration in manuscripts of this class has conventional flower forms painted in transparent lake with white highlights over a sheet of burnished gold. The skillful use of gold, both in the pigment form and in leaf on a raised enamel-like ground, is especially characteristic of German and Dutch manuscripts of the fifteenth century. In some manuscripts, very beautiful borders are executed in delicate scroll-work with fine lines and dots, all of burnished gold, the effect of which is very magnificent.

The borders and long marginal ornaments, which grow out of the large illuminated initials, are often diversified with figures of a naturalistic or grotesque type, devised with greater fancy and variety than the similar figures of the same sort which occur in so many French manuscripts.

<margin>Manuscript of the Emperor Wenzel.</margin>
Fig. 41 shows a beautiful example of this, which dates from the last years of the fourteenth century, circa 1390. It is an ornament at the foot of one of the pages in a manuscript which was illuminated for the Emperor Wenzel of Bohemia. Two scenes—a prisoner in the stocks and a man being bathed by two attendant girls—are placed in the center of the grand sweeping lines of foliage. The backgrounds, with their delicate scroll-work and diaper patterns, are imitated from those in the fine French and Anglo-Norman manuscripts of the earlier part of the fourteenth century.

In some marginal illuminations, miniature figures of knights jousting are introduced, charging through the scrolls of foliage; and angels, gracefully drawn, are very frequently introduced into the elaborate borders, as is shown on fig. 40.

<margin>Grotesque figures.</margin>
Grotesque figures were great favorites with the Teutonic illuminators; devils and monkeys, pygmies fighting cranes, or strange monsters made up (like the Roman *grylli*) of several animals and birds united, are of frequent occurrence in German and Dutch illuminated manuscripts, more especially in *Books of Hours*, where such fancies were probably a relief from the gravity of the text for both the illuminator and the owner of the book: see below, page 208.

The finest Teutonic manuscripts of the fifteenth century

### CHAP. XI.] THE FLEMISH SCHOOL.

show in their miniatures the influence of the Van Eycks; this is also the case with many of the manuscripts that fall under the category of the Franco-Flemish rather than the Teutonic school¹.

<margin>School of the Van Eycks.</margin>
Fig. 42 gives a fine example of a miniature by an illuminator who must have been an actual pupil of the Van Eycks. It is taken from a fragment of a manuscript of the *Chroniques de Jherusalem*, now in the Imperial Library of Vienna (no. 2533). It represents Duke Baudouin (or Baldwin), who was crowned King of Jerusalem, in the guise of a fifteenth-century German knight, under a graceful Gothic canopy of characteristically German style. The date of this sumptuous manuscript is about 1450.

<margin>Influence on painting generally.</margin>
As noted below regarding Italian art, it is interesting to observe the strong influence that manuscript miniature painting had upon the larger pictures of Teutonic artists. In many cases, the German and Flemish painters of altarpieces were also illuminators of manuscripts, such as Liberale of Verona and Girolamo dai Libri, who are mentioned below; see page 197².

And even without this reason for similarity, it was not uncommon for the painter of a retable to borrow his composition and general decorative scheme from an illuminated <term>manuscript</term> by some skillful artist.

<margin>The Cologne School.</margin>
Fig. 43 shows a good example of this: the central panel of a <term>retable</term> dated 1473, in the church of St. Martin at Colmar, which is almost certainly the work of Martin Schoën or Schöngauer.

In the art of the Cologne School especially, the relationship between panel paintings and manuscript miniature illuminations is very close, both in the general

<note>² Gérard David of Bruges was a notable example of skill in both branches of art; see above, page 165. Gérard's wife also practiced both these arts, and produced manuscript illuminations and panel paintings of almost equal beauty to those of her husband; see below, page 218.</note>

# THE LATER TEUTONIC SCHOOL. [CHAP. XI.

decorative schemes and also in the extreme minuteness and delicacy of the larger paintings.

<margin>*Retable at Cologne.*</margin>
Fig. 44 shows a beautiful example of this: a small panel, now in the Archiepiscopal Museum at Cologne, representing the Virgin and Child seated on a flowery turf with a trellis covered with roses as a background, surrounded by lovely child-angels playing musical instruments. The whole panel is a perfect gem of brilliantly decorative art of the purest and most perfect kind, quite free from the excessive pictorial realism which was growing rapidly among the miniaturists of France and the Netherlands around 1460.

Half a century later, in the early part of the sixteenth century, the same tendency to paint pictures like a magnified manuscript illumination is frequently observed.

<margin>*Triptych by the elder Holbein.*</margin>
Fig. 45 represents one wing of an altar triptych by Hans Holbein the elder, painted about the year 1514. This beautiful figure of Saint Elizabeth of Hungary is interesting for showing the influence of Italian art, which at that time was widely spread throughout Germany and France; also, in its minutely delicate touch and in the grotesque ornaments at the top and bottom, it shows a strong tendency to use the forms and methods of the manuscript illuminator.

<margin>*Illuminations by A. Dürer.*</margin>
Manuscripts of the Teutonic school known to be by the hand of a famous painter are rare; there is, therefore, special interest in the book whose border-illuminations are illustrated in fig. 46. The text itself (a book of prayers) is *printed* on vellum, but forty-five of the pages are decorated with borders drawn by the masterly hand of Albert Dürer in red, green, and violet ink—a method possibly suggested to Dürer by seeing tenth or eleventh-century manuscripts that were illuminated with outline drawings in inks of these three colors. This beautiful prayer-book was decorated by Albert Dürer in 1515 for the Emperor Maximilian; it is now in the Munich Library. <note>Maximilian's Prayer-book has been described (with copies of the borders) by Stoeger, *Vignettes of Albert Dürer*, Munich, 1850.</note> There is much that is grotesque and humorous introduced among the finely designed scroll-work of these borders; and

*Dutch fifteenth-century manuscripts.* For the most part, the manuscripts of Holland resemble those of either other contemporary Germanic schools or the Franco-Flemish schools.

In the fifteenth century, an enormous number of *Books of Hours* and other works for private devotion—such as "The Book of Christian Belief" (*Den Boeck van den Kersten Ghelove*) and others of that type—were produced in Holland. Many of these are written in the vernacular (the common language).

### CHAP. XI.] DUTCH MANUSCRIPTS.

<margin>Dutch methods of ornament.</margin>
Flemish manuscripts; but they are usually very decorative in treatment, of a simple, homely style, which is not without charm. The decorative initials are often very large and beautiful, in some cases occupying a large proportion of the page; and the borders, which grow gracefully out of these large capitals, are magnificently rich both in design and execution. Gold is used profusely and with remarkable taste and skill in these Dutch illuminations, which frequently have a combination of *matte*, fluid gold applied with the brush over a ground of brilliantly burnished gold leaf. Very beautiful initials are also formed by painting with a transparent lake red over a ground of burnished gold, which shines through the red pigment, thus producing a brilliantly decorative effect.

<margin>Realistic details.</margin>
The miniatures of the fifteenth-century Dutch manuscripts are noticeable for their realistic architectural details, with interiors of rooms full of elaborate furniture, bookshelves, sideboards covered with silver plate, or the humbler jugs and dishes of pewter, with countless other kinds of fittings and furniture.

Dutch miniatures with ecclesiastical scenes frequently have elaborately rendered interior views of churches, which are usually very interesting from their illustration of the choir and altar fittings, the retables, the "riddles" or altar-curtains, the tabernacles for the Reserved Host, and many other valuable records of medieval church furniture and ritual <note>These minutely rendered ecclesiastical scenes occur frequently in other classes of Teutonic illumination.</note>.

One very delicate and beautiful kind of illumination, which occurs in many of the best Dutch manuscripts, is by no means peculiar to Holland, but is also found in many English, French, Flemish and Italian manuscripts.

<margin>Skillful use of the pen.</margin>
This consists of capitals, often of large size, decorated with rich ornamentation executed wholly with thin lines of blue and red drawn with a very fine pen. The firmness of touch and spirited quality of this pen illumination is often very remarkable, showing the most perfect training of hand and eye on the part of the illuminator. Though not as

<summary>This page describes the decorative techniques of 15th-century Dutch manuscripts, highlighting their elaborate initials, the skillful use of matte and burnished gold, and the realistic depiction of domestic and ecclesiastical interiors. It also notes the fine pen-drawn ornamentation common in high-quality manuscripts across Europe.</summary>

<keywords>Dutch manuscripts, illumination, gold leaf, miniatures, ecclesiastical scenes, retables, riddles, pen illumination, Teutonic illumination, medieval furniture</keywords>

--- Page 213 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of delicate and beautiful types of illumination, specifically focusing on the transition from manuscript techniques to pen-work in early printed books.</meta>

<header>PEN ILLUMINATIONS. [CHAP. XI.</header>

<margin>Pen-work.</margin>
As gorgeous as the usual initials painted with gold and colors, this line ornament is sometimes of the richest and most delicate quality that can be imagined. In some cases, a purple or violet ink is used, as well as the brighter blue and red, especially in Italian manuscripts.

The form of the pen ornaments used in this class of illumination is very much the same in all the major European classes of manuscripts—a somewhat exceptional circumstance, since, as a rule, each country has its own peculiar types of decoration.

<margin>Illumination in printed books.</margin>
This beautiful pen-work reached its highest point of perfection in the first half of the fifteenth century. It is frequently used for the illuminated initials in the early printed books of Germany. Books printed at Strasbourg by Mentelin, from about 1460 to 1468, are often decorated with very elaborate and skillfully drawn ornaments of this type; in many cases, these were probably by Mentelin’s own hand, since he was a skilled manuscript illuminator before he began to practice the art of printing <note>The Fitzwilliam Library contains a beautiful example of this style of pen illumination in a large folio volume of the *Summa* of Aquinas, printed by Mentelin around 1465 or 1466. In his youth, Mentelin was a manuscript illuminator in Paris while also studying at the university; see page 150.</note>.

The printed books of Koburger of Nuremberg are also remarkable for the beauty of their illuminations, featuring both blue and red pen-work as well as painted ornaments in gold and color.

<summary>This page describes the intricate pen-work illumination used in 15th-century manuscripts and early printed books, noting its unusual stylistic consistency across Europe. It specifically highlights the work of early printers like Mentelin and Koburger, who applied their skills as manuscript illuminators to the decoration of printed volumes.</summary>
<keywords>pen-work, illumination, Strasbourg, Mentelin, Koburger, Nuremberg, Fitzwilliam Library, Thomas Aquinas, Summa, early printing</keywords>

# CHAPTER XII.

## THE ILLUMINATED MANUSCRIPTS OF ITALY AND SPAIN.

<margin>Classical survival.</margin>
As has already been mentioned, the old classical forms survived in the manuscript miniatures of Italy for many centuries with little alteration.

A slow but steady degradation in the forms of classical art began to occur about the fifth or sixth century; the fact is that no art can remain stationary for long. There must be either progress or decay, and once the habit of copying older forms becomes the established rule, artistic degradation soon becomes inevitable.

<margin>Italian decline.</margin>
Just as the manuscript art of the Byzantine illuminators first lost its vitality and then rapidly deteriorated, so in Italy the late-surviving classical style of miniature became weaker and weaker in drawing, feebler in touch, and duller in composition, until in the eleventh and twelfth centuries a very low stage of degradation was reached. This occurred at the very period when the illuminator’s art in more northern countries was growing into its most vigorous development of power and decorative beauty.

The great Renaissance of art in Italy, which led to such magnificent results from the fourteenth to the sixteenth centuries, lagged behind the artistic movement in the north in its early beginnings. Consequently, during the thirteenth century, when England, France, and Germany had almost reached

<summary>This chapter introduces the history of Italian and Spanish illuminated manuscripts, noting that while classical forms persisted in Italy for centuries, they eventually declined into a period of stagnation and poor quality that lasted until the early Renaissance.</summary>
<keywords>illuminated manuscripts, Italy, Spain, classical art, Byzantine art, artistic degradation, Renaissance, miniatures</keywords>

--- Page 215 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relative delay of the Italian artistic renaissance compared to the developments in Northern Europe during the thirteenth century.</meta>

reached their climax of artistic growth, Italy had hardly begun to advance<note>Such work as the Pisan Baptistery pulpit by Niccola Pisano, executed in about 1260, was an almost isolated phenomenon, and it was not until about half a century later that Giotto and his pupils produced paintings of equal merit to those of France and England during the second half of the thirteenth century.</note>.

<margin>Manuscript of Donizo.</margin>
As an example of the degraded state of Italian art during the twelfth century, I may mention a manuscript in the Vatican library (*Vat. 4922*)<note>See *Mon. Germ. Hist.* xii. p. 348 seq.; and Agincourt, *Hist. d’Art*, Pl. 66.</note> of a poem in honor of the Countess Matilda written by a monk of Canossa named Donizo, which has a number of miniature illustrations. These are of the lowest type, utterly feeble in the drawing of the human form and quite without any feeling for the folds of drapery; the figures are mere shapeless masses without any decorative beauty of color to make up for the helpless ignorance of the illustrator; see fig. 47.

Later on in the twelfth century, and during the first half of the thirteenth century, art in Italy was mainly a feeble reflection of the then-degraded art of the Byzantines. This was partly due to the introduction into Italy of mosaic-workers from Constantinople, such as those who decorated the vault of the old Cathedral of Florence (now the Baptistery) with badly drawn but grandly decorative mosaics of the Day of Judgment<note>Partly owing to the inherently decorative beauty of the glass *tesserae*, Byzantine mosaics, even from a period of decline, are usually fine and rich in effect.</note>.

<margin>Oderisi of Gubbio.</margin>
Little is known of the two illuminators of manuscripts who are immortalized by Dante (*Purg. xi. 79—83*). Oderisi of Gubbio, whom Dante calls the “Honor of the art that in Paris is called *alluminare*,” is said to have been employed by Pope Boniface VIII to illuminate manuscripts in Rome about the time of the great Jubilee of 1300, when Dante visited Rome as an envoy from Florence.

<margin>Franco of Bologna.</margin>
Franco (Francesco) of Bologna is the other miniaturist mentioned by Dante as an artist of great merit; nothing is known of him or of his works. During the thirteenth and

# ITALIAN MANUSCRIPTS [CHAP. XII.]

In the fourteenth century, Bologna was one of the primary Italian centers for the production of manuscripts, partly because it was the site of one of the oldest and most important universities in Europe.

<margin>Manuscript of Giotto's school.</margin>
One of the finest manuscripts of the Florentine school, created by an unknown *miniatore* of the school of Giotto, is a *Missal* in the Chapter library of the Canons of Saint Peter's in Rome. The arms of the donor, repeated several times among the floral borders, show that the manuscript was illuminated for Giotto's patron, Cardinal Gaetano Stefaneschi, probably between 1330 and 1340. The same volume contains, by the same illuminator's hand, a richly illuminated *Life of Saint George*, with large historiated capitals of great beauty and finely decorative coloring. Fig. 48 shows one of the initials with Saint George slaying the dragon, and the Princess Saba kneeling at the side.

<margin>Italian art in France.</margin>
In some cases, especially during the fourteenth century, skillful Italian illuminators appear to have worked in France. Many French and even Flemish manuscripts, such as some of those created for Philip of Burgundy and the Duc de Berri toward the end of the century, distinctly show two styles of painting, French and Italian; the book was evidently the work of two different artists. Some of these Italian paintings in French manuscripts suggest the hand of a disciple of Simone Martini (Memmi), or some artist of the highly decorative Sienese school; this was probably in many cases due to the introduction of Italian painters into Avignon when the Papal court resided there; see page 140.

<margin>Late artistic revival.</margin>
It was, however, not until nearly the middle of the fourteenth century that Italy produced many illuminated manuscripts of any remarkable beauty. Those created under the immediate influence of Giotto, between 1300 and about 1340, were not as a rule comparable to the illuminations of Northern Europe either for decorative value or for minute beauty of detail.

By the middle of the fourteenth century, however, the illuminator's art in Italy, and especially in Florence, had reached a very high level of excellence.

<meta>Digitized by Google watermark at bottom of page</meta>

It also describes the cross-cultural exchange between Italian and French artists during the Papal residency in Avignon.</summary>

<header>THE ILLUMINATIONS OF DON SILVESTRO [CHAP. XII.</header>

dead monk's piety and skill <note>This enshrined hand, and another, said to be that of a later miniaturist of the same Monastery, Don Lorenzo, still exist in the Sacristy of the church of Santa Maria degli Angeli.</note>. Some of Don Silvestro's manuscripts are now preserved in the Laurentian library in Florence, and a number of miniatures cut out of his choir-books were acquired by W. Young Ottley <note>These magnificent miniatures were sold with the rest of the Hailstone Collection in 1891; one of them, in the possession of the present writer, is a magnificent initial O, measuring eight by nine inches, enclosing a very beautiful seated figure of Saint Stephen in a violet dalmatic with richly decorated gold apparels.</note>.

<margin>Manuscripts of Don Silvestro.</margin>
The existing works of Don Silvestro show that the enthusiasm of his fellow monks was not exaggerated. The miniatures are noble in style, finished with the most exquisitely minute touch, splendidly brilliant in color, and in every way masterpieces of the illuminator's art. These choir-books are of enormous size, being intended to be placed on the central choir lectern so that the whole body of monks standing around could chant the *antiphonalia* from the same book, and the initials are proportionately large to the size of the page. Thus some of the figures of Saints which fill the central spaces of the large initials are as much as six to seven inches in height, and yet they are painted with the minute detail of an ordinary sized miniature.

<margin>Methods of decoration.</margin>
The backgrounds of these splendid figures are usually of burnished gold, decorated by incised tooling of <term>diapers</term> or scroll-work; and the floral borders, which surround the letters and form marginal ornaments to the pages, consist of nobly designed conventional foliage in vermilion, ultramarine, and other fine pigments, relieved and lighted up by bosses of burnished gold thickly sprinkled among the sumptuous colored foliage. Tooled and burnished gold is also used largely for the decoration of the dresses of the figures, their crowns, jeweled ornaments, and the <term>apparels</term> and <term>orphreys</term> of their vestments. The whole effect is magnificent in the extreme, and yet, in spite of the dazzling brilliance of the gold and colors, the whole effect is perfectly harmonious and free from the harsh gaudiness which dis-

It also notes the current locations of some of his works, including the Laurentian library and private collections.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of ornamental styles in Italian illumination and introduces the monk Don Lorenzo of the Camaldolese Monastery.</meta>

<header>CHAP. XII.] AND DON LORENZO.</header>

figures so much of the late fifteenth-century work of the French and Flemish manuscript painters.

<margin>Italian ornament.</margin>
The specific style of ornament used by Don Silvestro survived in Italian illumination for nearly a century and a half. In Italy, realistic forms of fruit and flowers—which were painted with such taste and skill by the northern miniaturists—were almost never used. Throughout the fifteenth century, across the manuscripts of the Florentine, Sienese, and Venetian schools, the same purely conventional forms of foliage were used, featuring large curling leaves of alternating blue and red, lit up by jewel-like studs and bosses of burnished gold.

<margin>The monk Don Lorenzo.</margin>
According to Vasari, the same Camaldolese Monastery produced another manuscript illuminator whose skill was nearly equal to that of Don Silvestro. This was Don Lorenzo, who appears to have been born around 1370 and died around 1425 <note>See Vasari, Milanesi Ed. Vol. II. p. 15. Vasari also mentions a monk of the same monastery named Don Jacopo, a contemporary of Don Silvestro, who illuminated twenty large choir-books of extraordinary beauty.</note>. Examples of his skill, also in the form of large choir-books, are preserved in the Laurentian Library in Florence. They are rich with miniatures of great beauty and, like Don Silvestro’s paintings, show a lavish investment of time and patience in the exquisite detail with which they are finished. Vasari tells us that his hand was also preserved as a holy relic in the treasury of Santa Maria degli Angeli.

<margin>Visit of Leo X.</margin>
In later times, Pope Leo X—who, like other members of the Medici family, was an enthusiastic lover of illuminated manuscripts—visited the Monastery and wished to take some of these choir-books by Don Lorenzo to the Basilica of St. Peter in Rome <note>He appears to have decided against purchasing these choir-books because they were of the special Camaldolese <term>Use</term>, and therefore could not be used in the Vatican Basilica.</note>.

The Dominican Convent of San Marco in Florence, where the famous Florentine painter Fra Beato Angelico <note>Fra Angelico’s works were executed throughout the first half of the fifteenth century...</note> was a

# FRA ANGELICO AS AN ILLUMINATOR. [CHAP. XII.

<margin>Dominican painters.</margin>
...friar, possesses—or until quite recently did possess—a magnificent collection of choir-books richly illuminated with miniatures by various members of the Convent. Some of these are said to have been painted by Fra Angelico himself, others by one of his brothers who was a friar in the same Convent <note>This is very doubtful. Fra Angelico's brother, Fra Benedetto da Fiesole, was a scribe rather than a miniaturist, and probably only wrote the fine, large script; the illuminations were likely added by a pupil of Fra Angelico named Zanobi Strozzi, who died in 1468.</note>.

The records of the Dominican Convent at Fiesole, where Fra Angelico was born, show that he was working there as a painter of illuminated manuscripts in the year 1407 and for some time afterward.

<margin>Fra Angelico's style.</margin>
It is noteworthy that Fra Angelico's style, even when painting a large-scale mural fresco, was essentially that of the manuscript illuminator. He is completely unrealistic in drawing and even more so in color; he does not use any realistic effects of light and shadow, but instead paints all his figures glowing with the most brilliant effects of gold and color. His style is far earlier than that of his own time, and it features certain technical peculiarities which, as a rule, are found only in manuscript illuminations <note>As an example of this, I may mention Fra Angelico's system of painting the shadows of drapery in pure color, using the same color mixed with white for the rest of the folds. To some extent, this method was used by the Sienese school of painting, which in other respects resembles the style of miniatures in illuminated manuscripts; see above, p. 114.</note>.

<margin>MSS. of northern Italy.</margin>
In the fifteenth century, the manuscript art of central and northern Italy—especially Siena, Florence, Venice, and Milan—rose to a height of beauty and perfection that left it quite without rival in any country in the world. As was the case when writing of the glories of manuscripts like the French *Apocalypses* of the fourteenth century, words are inadequate to describe the refined beauty of the best Italian manuscripts of this period. As has been already pointed out, Italy was late in beginning its artistic Renaissance; and now, just when the rest of Europe was sinking into a more or less...

---

...century. Vasari mentions some magnificent manuscripts illuminated by him for the Cathedral of Florence, but they are not currently known to exist.

<summary>This page explores Fra Angelico's background as a Dominican monk and manuscript illuminator, noting how his decorative, non-realistic style influenced even his large-scale frescos. It also praises the 15th-century Italian manuscript tradition as the finest in the world.</summary>

<keywords>Fra Angelico, manuscript illumination, Dominican Convent, Fiesole, Fra Benedetto, Zanobi Strozzi, Sienese school, Italian Renaissance, Vasari, choir-books, miniatures</keywords>

--- Page 222 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Italian manuscripts in the 15th century, moving from the specific work of individual monks like Fra Angelico to the broader flourishing of the Renaissance and the production of both religious and secular texts.</meta>

# CHAP. XII.] ITALIAN MANUSCRIPTS IN THE FIFTEENTH CENTURY. 191

<margin>*The Renaissance in Italy.*</margin>
Despite a rapid and complete state of decadence elsewhere, Italy blossomed into one of the most magnificent artistic periods that the world has ever seen <note>Taking it as a whole—in painting, sculpture, the medallist's art, and other branches of the fine arts—no country and no period, except for Athens in the time of Pericles, has ever quite equaled the artistic glories of Florence under Cosimo the Elder and Lorenzo de' Medici.</note>. The manuscripts of this period are not unworthy of the general artistic glories of the time, and in some cases, their technical qualities bear witness to an almost superhuman amount of dexterity and patience.

During the first half of the century, by far the greater proportion of the manuscripts written in Italy were for ecclesiastical purposes. Among the most magnificent, but at the same time also the rarest, are folio manuscript *Pontificals* <note>Pontificals contain those services that only bishops or archbishops could celebrate; therefore, comparatively few were required.</note>, executed for wealthy ecclesiastics of episcopal rank.

<margin>*The Fitzwilliam Pontifical.*</margin>
An Italian folio *Pontifical*, dating from early in the fifteenth century and now in the library of the Fitzwilliam Museum, is, of its kind, one of the most beautiful manuscripts in the world. The delicacy of execution in the figures, and especially the faces, is little short of miraculous. The numerous historiated initials, each representing some episcopal act of consecration or benediction and scattered thickly all through the volume, are a remarkable proof of the patient, unwearied skill which, through years of labor, must have been devoted to this one superb volume.

<margin>*Italian poems.*</margin>
Among the illuminated manuscripts with secular texts, the most important are copies of Dante’s *Divine Comedy*, the works of Boccaccio, and the poems of Petrarch. The first page of such works as these is usually richly decorated with a wide border of scroll foliage, studded with the usual gold bosses. Frequently, small miniatures in medallion frames are set at intervals among the conventional leafage; at the bottom is a shield to receive the owner's coat of arms, surrounded by a delicately painted leafy wreath, which is supported on each side by a graceful figure of a flying angel.

---

It emphasizes the extraordinary artistic skill and detailed decoration, such as historiated initials and heraldic borders, characteristic of the Italian Renaissance.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the examination of 15th-century Italian manuscript illumination, focusing on the heraldic decorations and the transition toward classical and sculptural influences during the Renaissance.</meta>

<page-num>192</page-num>
<header>ITALY IN THE FIFTEENTH CENTURY. [CHAP. XII.</header>

<margin>*The owner's arms.*</margin>
or Cupid¹. In many cases, the shield is still left blank; the book was not written for any specific purchaser, and the owner neglected to insert his coat of arms².

The painting of the wreath which surrounds the shield is usually very beautiful, and the two flying angels or *amorini* are models of grace. This motif of the wreath held by two flying figures was widely used by the Florentine sculptors of the fifteenth century, such as Ghiberti and Luca della Robbia; it was suggested by a similar design, of much lower quality, which occurs on so many ancient Roman sarcophagi.

<margin>*Classical influence.*</margin>
Some of the most elaborate Italian manuscripts of the second half of the fifteenth century are decorated with very detailed and skillfully painted copies of ancient classical gems, cameos, coins and medals, or reliefs in marble and bronze. Remarkable skill is often shown by the way in which the illuminator has given the appearance of relief and the actual texture of the metal or stone³. Beautiful as the borders of this class are, they belong to a period of declining taste, though not of skill, and they paved the way for the elaborate, trivial works of Giulio Clovio and other miniaturists of the sixteenth-century period of decline.

<margin>*Capture of Constantinople.*</margin>
The influx of Greek exiles into Florence, after the conquest of Constantinople by the Ottoman Turks in 1453, led to the famous revival of classical learning, and for a while made Florence not only the artistic but the intellectual center of the world. Many of these fugitive Greeks brought with them both Greek and Latin manuscripts of ancient date, and a new development of manuscript art took place as a result of this.

---

¹ A beautiful manuscript from around 1460 in the Fitzwilliam Museum has its first page surrounded with a border of this design style, the interest of which is much increased by the finely written signature, “Jacopo da Fabriano,” introduced among the leafy ornaments of the margin.

² This type of design, with a blank space for the owner's coat of arms, is used for many of the beautiful woodcut borders in the early printed books of Florence and Venice.

³ Decorative accessories of this sculptural kind are widely used in the paintings of Andrea Mantegna of Padua.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of 15th-century Italian illumination, transitioning from the classical influences mentioned previously to the revival of archaistic styles and the development of the "Roman" script used in both manuscripts and early printing.</meta>

<page-num>193</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XII.] ARCHAISTIC STYLE.</header>

<margin>*Copying of early writing.*</margin>
Though manuscripts of service books and other sacred works continued to be written in the medieval “Gothic” form of character, for secular manuscripts <note>And to some extent for manuscripts of religious works as well. This archaic form of letter was also used by Sweynheim, Pannartz, and other early printers at Subiaco and in Rome; hence it was called the *Roman* letter, as opposed to the *Gothic* letter.</note> a very beautiful kind of “Roman” hand was widely used by the scribes of Florence, Venice, and other Italian centers of the illuminator’s art. This newly developed style of writing was based on the beautiful, clear form of character that had been used by the most skillful northern scribes of the ninth and tenth centuries. At the same time, a style of illumination for borders and initials was imitated—or rather adapted—with the greatest taste and skill from the characteristic interlaced patterns of England, France, and Germany during the twelfth century.

<margin>*Celtic style of ornament.*</margin>
This beautiful kind of ornament consists of delicately interlaced and plaited bands of white or gold, brought into relief by filling in the background, or the spaces between the laced bands, with alternating colors of blue, red, and green.

This style of initial was also used extensively for the early printed books of Rome, Florence, and Venice <note>One of the finest examples of this style of illumination is in a volume of the Italian translation of Pliny’s *Natural History*, printed on vellum by Nicolas Jenson in Venice in 1476; it is now in the Bodleian Library at Oxford.</note>, many copies of which were illuminated in the most magnificent way, quite equal to the ornaments of the finest vellum manuscripts.

Some of the Italian manuscripts of the second half of the fifteenth century are unrivaled by the illuminated manuscripts of any other country or age for their delicate beauty and exquisite refinement of detail.

<margin>*Italian Books of Hours.*</margin>
Among the greatest marvels of human skill ever produced are some of the very small *Books of Hours* that were executed for the merchant princes of Florence and Venice and for other wealthy Italian patrons. The borders in these frequently have minute figures of Cupid-like angels (*amorini*) playing among decorative foliage, or birds and animals, such as fawns, cheetahs, and the like,

---

<meta>This page continues the discussion of late 15th-century Italian illumination, transitioning from the aesthetic styles of borders and initials to the overall physical and artistic quality of the manuscripts, as well as the prominent centers and patrons of the craft.</meta>

<page-num>194</page-num>
<header>ITALIAN MANUSCRIPTS OF [CHAP. XII.</header>

designed with a degree of grace and modeled with a microscopic refinement of touch that no words can adequately describe.

<margin>Beauty of the text.</margin>
And it is not only the unequaled beauty of the painted decorations and miniatures for which these late Italian manuscripts are so remarkable; the mere writing of the text in the most brilliant black and red ink is of striking beauty in the form of the letters and the perfect regularity of the whole. Last of all, the vellum used by the Italian scribes of this period is far more beautiful—due to its ivory-like perfection of tint and surface—than that of any other class of manuscripts. Though not, of course, as exquisitely thin as the uterine vellum <note>A very fine, high-quality parchment supposedly made from the skin of stillborn or neonatal calves.</note> of the Anglo-Norman thirteenth-century scribes, it is more beautiful in texture and does much to complete the artistic perfection of the manuscripts of fifteenth-century Italy through its exquisitely polished surface and perfect purity of tint.

<margin>Manuscripts of Northern Italy.</margin>
The provinces of Florence, Pisa, Siena, Bologna, and Venice (including Verona) were all important centers for the production of fine illuminated manuscripts. On the whole, Florence was the most famous in this as in other branches of art, and it was especially to Florence that wealthy foreign princes sent their commissions when they desired to possess exceptionally beautiful manuscripts.

<margin>Corvinus as a patron of art.</margin>
One of the most enthusiastic art patrons of Europe, Matthias Corvinus, King of Hungary from 1458 to 1490, had a large number of magnificent manuscripts written and illuminated for him by various *miniatori* <note>Italian term for manuscript illuminators or miniaturists.</note> of Florence; some of these are now in the Imperial Library of Vienna.

Likewise, Federigo da Montefeltro, Duke of Urbino around the same time, purchased from a Florentine that most superbly illuminated Bible, in two large folio volumes dated 1478, which is now in the Vatican Library¹.

Among the miniaturists who worked for King Corvinus, the most famous was a Florentine named Attavante di

---

¹ See Wattenbach, *Schriftwesen*, 2nd Edition, pp. 411 and 469; and Romer, *The Manuscripts of the Corvinian Library*, in *l'Art*, Vol. x. 1877.

It also identifies Florence as the leading center for illumination, commissioned by great patrons such as Matthias Corvinus and Federigo da Montefeltro.</summary>

<page-num>195</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XII.] THE FIFTEENTH CENTURY.</header>

<margin>*Attavante the miniaturist.*</margin>
Gabriello, who was born in 1452. Vasari mentions him as a pupil and friend of Fra Angelico <note>See Vasari’s life of Fra Giovanni da Fiesole, Milanesi edition, Vol. II, p. 522 onward.</note>, and describes at great length and with much enthusiasm a lavish manuscript of Silius Italicus—belonging to the Dominican Monastery of San Giovanni e Paolo in Venice—as being the work of Attavante.

This once magnificent manuscript still exists, but in a very damaged state, in the Venetian Biblioteca Marciana (Cl. XII. Cod. LXVIII.); all the large miniatures have been cut out, but the borders with winged Cupids, birds, and animals among decorative scroll-work are marvels of beauty and minute delicacy of touch. While quite worthy of Attavante's fame, this manuscript cannot be his work, as it was produced many years too early, in the time of Pope Nicholas V, who reigned from 1447 to 1455.

<margin>*Manuscripts at Venice.*</margin>
The same library does, however, possess authentic examples of Attavante's wonderful illuminations. The borders are especially remarkable for the tiny medallion heads which are introduced among the conventional foliage. These minute pictures occur in many of the finest manuscripts of this class; and other <term>miniatori</term> painted them with a microscopic refinement of detail, fully equal to the best illuminations of Attavante. Fig. 49 gives a good typical example of this style of border, with two Cupid-like angels and busts of saints in quatrefoil medallions.

Some of the borders of this class, especially in Venetian and Florentine manuscripts, are decorated with very cleverly painted representations of jewels, such as emeralds and rubies, set at intervals along each margin. These are often wonderful examples of skillful realism; the transparency of the gem and its bright reflected lights are rendered with an almost deceptive appearance of reality.

<margin>*The miniaturists called dai Libri.*</margin>
In the fifteenth century, Verona was one of the primary Italian centers for the production of magnificent manuscripts. Various members of one family, known from their occupation as "dai Libri," were especially famous as miniaturists. Stefano, the eldest, was born about 1420; he and his younger brother

<meta>This page continues the history of 15th-century Italian manuscript illumination, transitioning from Attavante to the Veronese school, specifically the work of the Dai Libri family and Liberale of Verona, while also noting the artistic centers of Padua and Ferrara.</meta>

<page-num>197</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XII.] DAI LIBRI AND LIBERALE OF VERONA.</header>

Francesco were both skilled miniaturists, and Francesco’s son Girolamo dai Libri (1474 to 1556) was famous not only as a <term>miniatore</term>, but also as a painter of altar-pieces and other large-scale sacred pictures¹.

<margin>Liberale of Verona.</margin>
Another Veronese painter, Liberale di Giacomo, who was born in 1451, was a very skillful miniaturist in his youth.

He spent some years illuminating large choir-books for the Benedictine monastery of Monte Oliveto near Siena, and then after 1469 he was long occupied in the illumination of similar choir-books for the Cathedral of Siena².

The miniatures in these great <term>Antiphonals</term> are most exquisitely finished, rich in imagination, and brilliant in color, but they lack decorative breadth of style. Despite a much greater expenditure of labor and eyesight, these wonderful illuminations are far inferior to the works of the fourteenth-century French miniaturists; they show signs of that decadence of taste which, in the sixteenth century, led to the destruction of the true illuminator’s art³.

<margin>Manuscripts of Northern Italy.</margin>
In addition to Venice, Padua and Ferrara were both important centers of manuscript illumination of a very high order during the fifteenth century. The Paduan miniatures show strongly the influence of Andrea Mantegna and Gian Bellini, whose styles also appear in the contemporary manuscripts of Venice. The British Museum possesses a magnificent example of the work of one of the most capable <term>miniatori</term> of Padua, a <term>Missal</term> by Benedetto Bordone, who also illuminated the great choir-books of the Convent of Santa Justina in Padua.

---

The Bodleian contains an exquisite *Book of Hours* illuminated by Girolamo dai Libri for the Duke of Urbino.</note>

<note>² The *Antiphonals* which Liberale illuminated at Monte Oliveto are now preserved in the Chapter Library at Chiusi. Those which he painted at Siena are now in the Cathedral Library. <page-num>199</page-num>

# CHAP. XII. MANUSCRIPTS OF NORTHERN ITALY.

<margin>School of Ferrara.</margin>
Ferrara also produced many very beautiful manuscripts, especially under the patronage of Duke Borso d’Este. It was for this Duke of Ferrara that the magnificent choir-books, now held in the Municipal Library at Ferrara, were created.

Figure 50 shows a miniature from a very splendid Bible that was illuminated for Duke Borso d’Este between 1455 and 1461 by Taddeo di Crivelli and Franco di Messer Giovanni da Russi, two very talented miniaturists of the Ferrarese school, though they were natives of the neighboring city of Mantua.

<margin>Parma and Modena.</margin>
Parma, Modena, and Cremona were also thriving centers of the illuminator’s art; in fact, wherever there was a school of painting in Italy, a secondary school of manuscript miniaturists seems also to have existed. The two types of painting influenced one another; and in some cases, as indicated below<note>See page 200, and compare pages 163 and 175 for examples of similar influence from the manuscript illuminators of Germany and Italy.</note>, the more important art of large-scale painting owed more to the manuscript illuminators than has commonly been acknowledged.

<margin>School of Milan.</margin>
Milan, especially under Duke Ludovico and other members of the Sforza family, was an active center of manuscript illumination. Some very beautiful late manuscripts exist with miniatures that show the influence of Leonardo da Vinci and his pupil Bernardino Luini; a *Book of Hours* in the Fitzwilliam Museum is a good example of this.

<margin>Illuminated documents.</margin>
One rather exceptional class of richly illuminated manuscripts was produced in large numbers in Italy during the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries; these were state documents, university diplomas and licenses, patents of nobility, and various kinds of legal instruments, often very elaborately decorated with illuminations and miniatures in gold and colors.

In Venice especially, immense numbers of these were produced; the most elaborate are appointments of governors, commissions of high-ranking officials, and patriarchal briefs, along with state records and documents of the most varied kinds. Bologna, Padua, Pisa, and others of the major universities of Italy issued diplomas for doctoral degrees and licenses to

<summary>This page explores the prominent schools of manuscript illumination in Northern Italy, specifically in Ferrara, Milan, and Venice. It highlights the patronage of the d’Este and Sforza families and notes the use of illumination in official state documents and university diplomas.</summary>

<header>VENETIAN ART. [CHAP. XII.</header>

<page-num>200</page-num>

give instructions, which were frequently very magnificently decorated with gold letters and richly illuminated capitals and borders.

<margin>*Retables like manuscripts.*</margin>
Before moving on to the Italian *miniatori* of the final period, it is worthwhile to note the strong influence that the art of manuscript illumination had on the painters of large retables and other sacred pictures in Italy, and especially in Venice; this was also the case with the contemporary painters of Germany and Flanders¹. Many Venetian altarpieces, because of their minute detail, their use of burnished gold enriched with tooled patterns, their decorative treatment of flowers, and their architectural backgrounds and frames, look exactly like a page from an illuminated manuscript.

<margin>*Retable in Venice.*</margin>
Figure 51 shows a characteristic example of this: a magnificent retable glowing with brilliant colors and burnished gold, now in the Accademia of Venice, which was painted in 1446 on the small island of Murano by two painters named Johannes and Antonius de Murano².

The same strongly marked influence of the decorative style of illuminated manuscripts can be seen in nearly all the works of Carlo Crivelli, another Venetian painter from the late 15th century, and in the gorgeous retables of Gentile da Fabriano³, a follower of Fra Angelico’s richly decorative and brilliantly colored method of painting.

<margin>*The 16th century.*</margin>
*Italian manuscripts of the 16th century.* By about the end of the first decade of the 16th century, the art of manuscript illumination had ceased to be a true living art in Italy. Although it continued to be practiced with great technical skill for more than half a century longer, the art—which had once been one of the most beautiful and dignified of all branches of art—declined into the production of costly toys to please a few Popes and luxurious princes who were willing to pay very high prices for manuscripts illumi-

---

¹ For examples of this, see page 175 above.
² In some pictures, each of these painters also signs himself *Alamanus*, which does not necessarily mean they were German, but possibly natives of Lombardy, who were often called *Alamani* by their Italian neighbors.
³ Especially in his magnificently decorative altarpiece of the *Adoration of the Magi* in the Florentine Academy, dated 1423.

<summary>This page discusses the significant influence of manuscript illumination on Venetian altarpieces and identifies the early 16th century as the period when the craft began to decline into a luxury trade for the elite.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the stylistic evolution of manuscripts, moving from the decorative Venetian style of the previous page to the highly technical but arguably decadent work of Giulio Clovio and the history of the Vatican Library.</meta>

<page-num>202</page-num>
<header>GIULIO CLOVIO AND EXTREME DECADENCE. [CHAP. XII.</header>

illuminated by the skillful hands of Giulio Clovio and other miniaturists, whose patience, eyesight, and technical skill were superior to their sense of what was fitting and beautiful in an illuminated manuscript.

<margin>Giulio Clovio.</margin>
Of all the illuminators of this class, the Dalmatian Giulio Clovio¹ (1498–1578) was the most famous and technically the most skillful. He found many wealthy patrons in Italy and was employed by Charles V of France.

The Soane Museum in London possesses a characteristic example of his style, a *Commentary on the Epistles of Saint Paul*, executed for Giulio's early patron, Cardinal Marino Grimani of Venice, the brother of the owner of the Grimani *Breviary* mentioned above. Clovio's miniatures are marvels of minute execution, but not truly decorative in style, and in design usually quite unsuited to their purpose. In most cases they resemble large oil paintings reduced to a microscopic scale; the figures are commonly feeble imitations either of large pieces of contemporary tapestry or else of painting in Michelangelo's grandiose style, both of which of course were utterly unsuited for miniatures in a manuscript².

<margin>The Vatican MSS.</margin>
### *The Manuscripts in the Vatican Library.* 
The Archives of the Vatican library contain a number of records of the development of the library during the sixteenth century and later³.

In medieval times manuscripts were rare and costly, so that even Kings, Popes, and Universities possessed libraries

---

<note>¹ Clovio is the Italianized form of a harsh Croatian name; the artist adopted the name Giulio as a compliment to his friend and teacher Giulio Romano, Raphael's favorite pupil. J. W. Bradley, *Life of Giulio Clovio*, London, 1891, gives an interesting account of him and of his times; see also Vasari, Ed. Milanesi, Vol. VII. p. 557.</note>

<note>² The ex-king of Naples' library possesses a *Book of Hours*, on the illuminations of which (Vasari tells us) Giulio Clovio spent nine years. It certainly is a marvel of human patience and misdirected skill; the text was written by a famous scribe named Monterchi, who was specially renowned for the beauty of his writing.</note>

<note>³ An interesting little volume on this subject has been published by Eug. Müntz, *La Bibliothèque du Vatican*, Paris, 1886; it deals chiefly with the growth of the library during the sixteenth century.</note>

<summary>This page details the career and style of the famous illuminator Giulio Clovio, critiquing his technically brilliant but stylistically "decadent" miniatures, and introduces the historical growth of the Vatican Library's manuscript collection.</summary>
<keywords>Giulio Clovio, manuscript illumination, Renaissance art, Soane Museum, Cardinal Marino Grimani, Vatican Library, Michelangelo, Monterchi</keywords>
