<meta>This page continues the discussion on the Vatican Library’s archives and the records of development during the sixteenth century, specifically looking at library size and the compensation of manuscript illuminators.</meta>

<page-num>203</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XII.] THE VATICAN LIBRARY. 203</header>

which in size were very insignificant compared to those of ancient Alexandria, Rome, and Byzantium.

<margin>The Vatican library.</margin>
Even in Leo X’s time (1513–1522), the Vatican library, which was probably the largest in the world, contained only 4,070 manuscripts and printed books. A century earlier, before the invention of printing, two or three hundred volumes would have constituted an enormous library.

As a rule, even Royal and Public libraries were contained in a few iron-bound chests or *armaria*; and borrowers had to deposit a pledge—a gold ring, a silver cup, or some other valuable article, which was retained by the librarian until the manuscript had been restored. In the Vatican, this practice survived until the sixteenth century, and books exist among the Archives in which were recorded the date, the title of the book, the borrower’s name, and a short description of the deposited pledge. When the book was returned, the word “restituit” was written in the margin.

<margin>Payments to scribes.</margin>
The same Archives contain a number of accounts giving the sums paid to various illuminators of manuscripts, especially in the time of Pope Paul III (Alessandro Farnese, 1534 to 1553), who was a great patron of Giulio Clovio and other miniaturists. In 1540, a number of <term>scriptores et miniatores</term> employed in the Vatican library received as pay 4 gold ducats each monthly, at a rate of 10 <term>Julii</term> to the ducat, equal to about £20 in modern value.

In 1541, Messer Paolo received 30 gold ducats for writing and illuminating four volumes.

<margin>Del Piombo as an illuminator.</margin>
It is interesting to note that the famous painter Sebastiano del Piombo¹ (“Fra Bastiano piombator”) received payment <term>pro libris miniatis</term> in the year 1546 from Pope Paul III.

In 1549, Federigo Mario di Perugia received 4½ ducats a month for his labor <term>in scribendis et ornandis seu pingendis libris</term>. This is the same miniaturist who illuminated some choir-books for the Roman Monastery of Sant’ Agostino².

---

<note>¹ Fra Sebastiano was called “del Piombo” from his office as superintendent of the pendant lead seals, *piombi* or *bullae*, which were attached to Papal Briefs and other documents; one class of these documents were called *Bulls* because of their lead *bullae*.</note>
<note>² See Montault, *Livres de chœur des églises de Rome*, Arras, 1874, p. 9.</note>

<summary>This page details the relatively small scale of the Vatican Library in the 16th century and describes the administrative practices of the time, such as requiring valuable pledges from book borrowers and the specific wages paid to manuscript scribes and illuminators.</summary>
<keywords>Vatican Library, Leo X, Pope Paul III, manuscript illumination, book borrowing, armaria, Sebastiano del Piombo, scriptores et miniatores, historical wages</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of manuscript production, shifting from Vatican library records to the survival of the scribe's craft in large-scale choir-books and the stylistic influences on Spanish and Oriental manuscripts.</meta>

<page-num>204</page-num>
<header>SPANISH MANUSCRIPTS. [CHAP. XII.</header>

It was especially for the large choir-books that the art of the scribe and illuminator survived, the reason being that no printer’s set of type had characters of sufficient size to be read by a whole circle of singers. Thus we find Italian and Spanish manuscript *Antiphonals*¹ and the like, which have the grand Gothic writing of the fifteenth century executed as late as the year 1620 or even later².

<margin>Spanish MSS.</margin> *The Manuscripts of Spain, Portugal, and the East.* Little needs to be said about the manuscript illuminations of the Spanish peninsula, since they contain little that is native or original.

In the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries, many magnificent illuminations were produced in Spain and Portugal, but they are mainly imitations of either Italian or Flemish miniatures. In earlier times in Northern Spain, the influence of France was paramount, and in Southern Spain, the beautiful “Saracenic” art of the Moorish conquerors influenced all branches of the fine arts, including that of manuscript illumination.

<margin>Muslim influence.</margin> To some extent, the same Muslim influence is apparent in the decorative borders of Sicilian and Venetian manuscripts, especially during the fifteenth century.

The illuminations of Oriental manuscripts do not fall within the limits of this brief treatise, but it should be noted that during the medieval period, and down to the present century, Persian and Arabic manuscripts with decorative illuminations of extraordinary beauty and skillful execution have been largely produced in Syria, Persia, and India under the Muslim conquerors.

For delicacy of touch, for intricate beauty of ornament,

---

<note>¹ The Fitzwilliam Museum possesses two noble vellum choir-books of this class dated 1604 and 1605. Although the miniatures are poor, the writing of the text and the music might well pass for the work of a fifteenth-century scribe.</note>

<note>² A valuable but by no means exhaustive list of manuscript illuminators is given by J. W. Bradley, *Dictionary of Miniaturists, Illuminators and Calligraphers*, London, 1887. The names of Italian miniaturists are especially numerous, partly because Italian manuscripts are more frequently signed by their illuminators than the manuscripts of other countries. See also Bernasconi, *Studj sopra la storia della pittura Italiana dei secoli xiv e xv*, Verona, 1864.</note>

<page-num>205</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XII.] ORIENTAL MANUSCRIPTS.</header>

and for decorative splendor in the use of gold and color, these Oriental manuscripts are, in their own way, unsurpassed.

<margin>*Persian MSS.*</margin>
In orthodox Sunni manuscripts, miniatures with figure subjects do not occur, but they are lavishly used in the manuscripts of the Persians and other members of the Sufi sect. The drawing of the human form is without the dignity and grace that is seen in Western manuscripts, but as pieces of decoration, Oriental miniatures are of high merit. Copies of the *Koran* and the works of the favorite Persian poets are among the most common kinds of Oriental manuscripts. It is the latter that are so often sumptuously decorated with figure subjects.

<page-num>206</page-num>

# CHAPTER XIII.

## THE WRITERS OF ILLUMINATED MANUSCRIPTS.

<margin>
The beauty of manuscripts.
</margin>
***The Monastic Scribes.*** It may be interesting to consider the causes that made the illuminated manuscripts of the medieval period among the most perfect and beautiful works of art that the world has ever produced. No one can examine the manuscripts of any of the chief European countries down to the fourteenth century without a feeling of amazement at their almost unvarying perfection of execution, the immense fertility of imagination in their design, and the utterly unsparing labor that was lavished on their production. Moreover, the manuscripts of this earlier period, before their production became a commercial art in the hands of secular scribes, are especially remarkable for their uniform excellence of workmanship, and their complete freedom from any signs of haste or weariness on the part of their scribes and illuminators.

<margin>
Conditions of life.
</margin>
Now the fact is that the countless illuminated manuscripts which were produced in so many of the Benedictine and other monastic houses of Europe were executed under very exceptionally favorable circumstances <note>J. R. Green, in his *Short History of the English People*, chapter III, gives an interesting sketch of the development of literature and the art of the scribe in the great monasteries of England, especially from the eleventh to the fourteenth century.</note>. In the first place, the monastic scribe lived in a haven of safety and rest in the middle of a tumultuous and war-harassed world. While at work in the <term>scriptorium</term> he was troubled with no thoughts of

<meta>Digitized by Google watermark at bottom</meta>

<summary>This page begins a discussion on the exceptional quality of medieval illuminated manuscripts, attributing their beauty and perfection to the peaceful, focused environment of monastic scriptoriums. It notes that before manuscript production became a commercial trade, monastic scribes worked with unhurried dedication and immense creativity.</summary>
<keywords>Monastic Scribes, Illuminated Manuscripts, Benedictine, scriptorium, J. R. Green, Short History of the English People, medieval art, manuscript production</keywords>

--- Page 238 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the favorable conditions of monastic scribes, emphasizing their lack of time pressure and the personal joy they found in their craft.</meta>

<page-num>207</page-num>
# CHAP. XIII.] MONASTIC ILLUMINATORS.

<margin>Absence of hurry.</margin>
any necessity to complete his task within a limited time in order to earn his daily bread. Food and clothing of a simple though sufficient kind were secured to him, whether he finished his manuscript in a year or in twenty years. He worked for no payment, but for the glory of God and the honor of his monastic foundation, and last, but not least, for the intense pleasure which the varying processes of his work gave him.

<margin>Pleasant work.</margin>
No one who examines a fine medieval manuscript can help seeing in it the strongest marks of the delight which the illuminator had in his work; and this sort of retrospective sympathy with the pleasure of the workman in his work is an important element in the beauty of ancient works of art of many different kinds and dates, from the simple but beautiful wheel-turned vase of the Greek potter, down to the carved foliage in a Gothic church, or the complicated ornamentation of an illuminated initial.

<margin>Relief from monotony.</margin>
Again, it should be remembered that the life of a medieval monk was a very uneventful and monotonous one, and even the most pious soul must at times have felt a weariness in the oft-repeated and lengthy *Offices* which made him spend so large a portion of each day within the Choir of his monastic church. Thus it was that his work as an illuminator of manuscripts provided the one great relief from his otherwise gray and monotonous life, from which he turned to revel in every variety of fanciful shape and of varied arrangement of gleaming gold and brilliant pigments. Here at least was no monotony, but the fullest scope for imaginative fancy and the love of variety which is inborn in the human mind.

<margin>Scope for humor.</margin>
In the illumination of his manuscript the monastic scribe, even when decorating a sacred book, could lay aside for a moment the solemn religious thoughts to which his vows had bound him; he could sport with every variety of grotesque monster and of pagan imagery, and could find vent for his repressed sense of fun and humor by the introduction of caricatures and pictorial jokes of all kinds among the foliage of his borders and initials without any fear of reproof on the

<summary>This page describes how monastic illuminators worked without time pressure or financial need, finding creative relief and personal joy in their craft amidst an otherwise monotonous religious life. Their work allowed for artistic freedom, including the inclusion of humor and pagan imagery even within sacred texts.</summary>
<keywords>monastic scribes, medieval manuscripts, illumination, artistic freedom, religious life, scriptorium, creative relief, grotesque imagery</keywords>

<page-num>208</page-num>
# HUMOROUS ILLUMINATIONS. [CHAP. XIII.

<margin>*Grotesque figures.*</margin> part of his superiors¹. Figure 52 from a French fourteenth-century manuscript shows a characteristic example of an illuminator's humorous fancy: a grotesque Bishop with a mitre made out of a pair of bellows.

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration (labeled Fig. 52) depicting a grotesque ecclesiastical figure. The figure is a cleric or bishop with a large mitre shaped like a set of bellows. He holds a crozier in his left hand and raises his right hand in a gesture of blessing or speech. He has bird-like legs and feet. The caption to the right reads: Fig. 52. Grotesque figure from a French manuscript of the fourteenth century.</image-desc>

Very frequently, the jealousy that existed between the Regular and the Secular Clergy is expressed in the pictorial sarcasms of the monastic illuminators. This feeling, from the Secular side, is vividly set forth in the amusing Latin poems of Walter Map², who, toward the close of the twelfth century, was the parish priest of a small church in the Forest of Dean³. Walter Map's satire is mainly directed against the Cistercian order of monks, with whom he was specifically brought into contact because his parish was situated near the Cistercian Abbey of Flaxley.

<margin>*Humorous scene.*</margin> Figure 53, from a German manuscript of the end of the twelfth century, now in the Chapter library of Prague Cathedral, gives an interesting example of the introduction of a humorous scene into a serious work, Saint Augustine's *De civitate Dei*. The illuminator, whose name was Hildebert, had been annoyed by a mouse that stole his food; and here, on the last leaf of the manuscript, he represents himself interrupted in his work and throwing something at the mouse that is nibbling at his food. These explanatory words are written on the open page of his book:

<margin>*A wicked mouse.*</margin>
> **Pessime mus, sepius me provocas ad iram, ut te deus perdat.**
> "You wicked mouse, you provoke me to anger all too often; may God destroy you."

<note>2. The poems of Walter Map were edited by Thomas Wright for the Camden Society in 1841.</note>
<note>3. Walter Map subsequently obtained various degrees of promotion, and in 1197 became the Archdeacon of Oxford.</note>

<page-num>210</page-num>
<header>REASONS WHY ILLUMINATIONS [CHAP. XIII.</header>

<margin>
*Portrait of the scribe.*
</margin>
At the feet of the scribe, a lad named Everwinus—possibly a monastic novice—is seated on a low stool, drawing a piece of ornamental scroll-work. The monk Hildebert’s desk is in the form of a lectern supported by a carved lion; in it are holes to hold the black and red inkhorns, and two pens or brushes. In his left hand, the scribe holds the usual penknife, and another pen is tucked behind his ear.

<margin>
*Short hours of labor.*
</margin>
There is yet another condition under which the monastic scribe worked that had an important effect on the consistent excellence of his work: he could never remain at work long enough at any one time for his hand or eye to grow weary. Owing to the constantly recurring choir services—the *Seven Hours* <note>The canonical hours or daily offices of prayer in a monastery.</note> which he had to attend—the monastic scribe could probably never continue laboring at his illumination for more than about two hours at a time.

<margin>
*No weariness.*
</margin>
The importance of this fact is very clearly seen when we compare one of the earlier monastic manuscripts with a fifteenth-century French or Flemish *Book of Hours* executed by a professional secular scribe. In the older manuscripts, the firmness of the line and the delicate, crisp touch never waver; the artist’s evident sense of power and joy in his manual dexterity lasts without diminishing from the first to the last page of his book.

<margin>
*Variety of labor.*
</margin>
Additional beauty is given to medieval manuscripts by the fact that each scribe commonly performed much of the important work in preparing his own inks and pigments; in some cases, this even included beating out the gold leaf he was about to use in his miniatures and borders¹. No colors bought from a dealer in a commercial age could ever equal the beauty or durability of the pigments that an illuminator made, or at least prepared, for his own use. Furthermore, his command over the materials of his art would greatly enhance his pleasure in using them, to say nothing of the relief provided by the variety of his labors.

All these influences, combined with others which it might—

---

¹ Theophilus, in *Schedula Diversarum Artium* (List of Various Arts), I. 30–33, writes as if every illuminator had to beat out or grind his own gold.

<page-num>211</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XIII.] MANUSCRIPTS ARE SO BEAUTIFUL.</header>

<margin>Varied schemes of ornament.</margin>
[The processes involved, which would] be wearisome to dwell upon, combined to make the manuscripts of the pre-commercial period works of the most unvarying technical perfection, unspeakably rich in the varied wealth of imagination shown in their decorative schemes, as well as in the minute detail of each part. The illuminated ornament in one place is concentrated into a gem-like miniature within the narrow limit of a small initial letter. At another place, it spreads out into the splendor of a full-page picture, which swallows up most of the text and covers the whole page with one mass of burnished gold and brilliant color. Or again, springing from its roots in an illuminated capital, it grows over the margin and frames the text with a mass of richly designed and exquisitely graceful foliage.

Every possible scheme of decoration is to be found in these manuscripts; but in all cases, the illuminator is careful to make his painted ornament grow out of and form, as it were, an integral part of the written text. Thus, the work becomes not merely a book decorated with pictures, but a close combination of writing and illumination, forming one harmonious whole in a united scheme of decorative beauty <note>1</note>.

<margin>Monastic Scriptoria.</margin>
*The Scriptoria of Monasteries.* As I have previously mentioned, it was more especially the Benedictine monasteries <note>2</note> that were the centers for the production of medieval manuscripts <note>3</note>. I will therefore describe the usual arrangements of the *Scriptorium* in a Benedictine House.

In early times—in the eighth and ninth centuries, for example—the Scriptorium and library appear usually to have been a separate room, near or over the Sacristy, and adjoining the Choir of the church <note>4</note>.

<column-break/>

<note>1 In this respect, as is noted above on page 33, classical manuscripts appear to have been inferior to those of the medieval period.</note>
<note>2 Monte Cassino, the first and chief of the Benedictine monasteries, founded by Saint Benedict himself, was for many centuries one of the chief centers in Italy for the writing and illumination of manuscripts.</note>
<note>3 According to the severe Cistercian Rule, richly illuminated manuscripts were not allowed to be written or even used in Houses of that Order, which in England, from the end of the twelfth century, came next in size and importance to the monasteries of the parent Benedictine Order.</note>
<note>4 See the plan of the Abbey of St. Gallen, published by Prof. Willis, *Arch. Jour.*, Vol. v. page 85 seq.</note>

<page-num>212</page-num>
<header>SCRIPTORIA IN THE [CHAP. XIII.</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

<margin>Scriptoria in cloisters.</margin>
During most of the medieval period, however, and in England until the suppression of the Abbeys by Henry VIII, the system was to devote one entire walk or alley of the cloister—the one nearest the church—to the double purpose of a Scriptorium and a library. This was naturally the warmest and driest portion of the cloister, at least in most cases when the usual arrangement was followed of placing the cloister on the south side of the nave of the Abbey church <note>1 The Abbey of Westminster is a well-preserved example of the typical Benedictine plan.</note>.

<margin>Monastic library.</margin>
This north walk (as it commonly was) of the cloister faced south and so received plenty of sun; at each end of it, a screen was placed to shut it off from the rest of the cloister, which formed a sort of common living room for the monks <note>2 One walk of the Benedictine cloister, usually the western one, was used as the schoolroom where the novices repeated their "Donats" and other lessons. Consequently, in many cloisters, one sees the stone benches carved with marks for numerous "go-bang" boards—a favorite monastic game.</note>. Along one side of this alley of the cloister, oak cupboards (*armaria*) with strong locks and hinges were fixed against the wall of the church to receive the manuscripts which formed the library of the monastery <note>3 No monk could borrow a book to read without the express permission of his superiors, given in the Chapter House.</note>. At Westminster and in other Benedictine monasteries, the marks showing where these *armaria* were fixed are visible on the cloister wall, or rather along the wall of the church, which forms one side of this walk of the cloister.

<margin>Scribes' carrels.</margin>
Down the middle of the alley, a clear passage was left, and the other side of the passage—the side opposite the bookcases—was occupied, at least in the fourteenth century and probably much earlier, by a row of little wooden box-like rooms called *carrels* <note>4 The word *carrel* is probably a corruption of the French *carré*, derived from the *square* form of these little rooms.</note>, each of which was devoted to the use of one scribe. As a rule, there were either two or three of these carrels to each bay or compartment of the cloister. They were commonly made of wainscot oak, about six by eight feet in

<header>CARRELS AT GLOUCESTER    [CHAP. XIII.</header>

space for his black and red inkhorns, his colors and other implements; see fig. 53 on p. 209.

These little rooms were provided with wooden floors and ceilings, so as to be warm and dry; they were set close against the traceried windows, which in most cloisters ran all along the internal sides of the four alleys.

<margin>Cloister at Gloucester.</margin>
The cloister of Gloucester Abbey¹ has a slightly different arrangement. Here a series of stone recesses, each intended to hold a carrel, extends all along the side of this walk² of the cloister. There are two of these recesses to each bay, and the lower part of the outer wall, instead of consisting of open tracery, is of solid masonry, pierced only by a small glazed window to give light to the scribe; above the carrel recess there is the usual large arch filled in with tracery; see fig. 54³.

When provided with these and other wooden fittings, the cloister of a Benedictine Abbey would not have been either in appearance or fact as cold and comfortless as such places usually look now. With a small portable brazier the monastic scribe in his little wooden cell was safe from damp and probably fairly warm even in cold weather.

*The Rites and Monuments of Durham*⁴ (Cap. XLI.) give the following very interesting description of the *carrels* with which the Durham cloister was fitted up;

And in every window three PEWS or CARRELS, where every one of the old Monks had his carrel, separate by himself, that,

<page-num>215</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XIII.] AND AT DURHAM.</header>

<margin>*The Durham carrels.*</margin>
> "when they had dined, they did resort to that place of the cloister and there studied their books, every one in his carrel, all the afternoon, until evensong time. This was their exercise every day. All their pews or carrels were all finely wainscoted (with oak) and very close, all except the front which had carved work that gave light in at their carrel doors of wainscot. And in every carrel was a desk to lay their books on. And the carrels were no larger than from one stanchion (mullion) of the window to another.

<margin>*The Durham armaria.*</margin>
> And over against the carrels against the church wall did stand certain great almeries (*armaria* or cupboards) of wainscot all full of BOOKS, with a great store of ancient manuscripts to help them in their study, wherein did lie as well the old ancient Doctors of the Church as other profane authors, with diverse other holy men’s works, so that every one studied what Doctor pleased them best, having the Library at all times to go study in besides their carrels."

In the sixteenth century, owing to the introduction of printed works, the books in the Benedictine monastery of Durham had become too numerous for the row of *almeries* along the north walk of the cloister to hold them; and so a separate room was provided as a second library. The present library at Durham is the old Dormitory or *Dorter* of the Monks with all its "cubicles" or *sleeping-carrels* removed.

<margin>*Other monastic Scriptoria.*</margin>
In the Houses of other religious foundations the arrangements for the writing of manuscripts were different from those of the Benedictines. In a Convent of Dominican Friars, for example, each friar worked in his own cell where he slept, and in a Carthusian monastery each monk had a complete little house and garden with a small study and oratory and a larger room, where his labors, literary or mechanical, were carried on.

The Dominican House of San Marco in Florence, of which Fra Beato Angelico was a member, throughout the fourteenth and fifteenth centuries was famous for the magnificent manuscripts that were illuminated there; see above,

<summary>This page concludes a historical description of the wainscoted carrels and book cupboards at Durham, where monks studied religious and secular texts. It further explains how the rise of printed books led to the creation of larger libraries and compares the study spaces of Benedictine, Dominican, and Carthusian orders.</summary>

<page-num>216</page-num>
<header>216 THE LATER CLASS [CHAP. XIII.</header>

Page 190. Various other convents of Dominican Friars in Italy were also important centers of manuscript illumination. Some of the Regular Canons were also famous as illuminators, especially the Austin Canons.

### THE SECULAR SCRIBES AND ILLUMINATORS.

<margin>Growth of Guilds.</margin>
Towards the latter part of the 13th and throughout the 14th century, secular artisans across all varieties of arts and crafts were gradually throwing off the bonds of the old feudal serfdom that had long bound them. The growth in number and importance of the trade guilds—which developed so rapidly in England under Henry III—was one of the chief signs of the growing importance of the artisans in the major towns of this and other European countries.

<margin>Importance of the Trade-Guilds.</margin>
At the end of the 13th century, in London, Florence, and many other cities, no one could possess the rights of a citizen or a share in the municipal government without becoming a member of one of the established trade guilds. Edward I, Edward III, and other English kings set an example by enrolling themselves as members of one of the London guilds¹; and in Florence, it was necessary for Dante to become a member of a guild² before he could serve the Republic as one of the *Priori*.

Initially, the scribes and illuminators (<term>librorum scriptores et illuminatores</term>³) were members of one general guild that included craftsmen in all the decorative arts and their subsidiary processes, such as leather tanning, vellum making, and even saddlery⁴.

---

<note>¹ Frequently in the Linen-armourers' Guild—those who made defensive armor of padded and quilted linen, which was a very important protection against assassination and was used until the 17th century.</note>
<note>² Dante selected the Apothecaries' and Physicians' Guild.</note>
<note>³ This phrase was used in the 12th century by Ordericus Vitalis, *Hist. Eccles.* Book III, page 77, Edited by Le Prevost.</note>
<note>⁴ Medieval saddlery—with its cut, gilt, and stamped leather (<term>cuir bouillé</term>), rich and elaborate in design—was a decorative art of significant character; in technique, it was similar to that of the bookbinder, which in most places was included in the same guild.</note>

It highlights how membership in these guilds became a prerequisite for citizenship and political office in cities like London and Florence.</summary>

--- Page 248 ---
<meta>This page continues the history of secular guilds, focusing on the 15th-century Guild of Saint John and Saint Luke in Bruges and the eventual commercialization of manuscript production.</meta>

<page-num>217</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XIII.] OF SECULAR ILLUMINATORS.</header>

<margin>Guilds in the 15th century.</margin>
Gradually, the Guilds became more numerous and more specialized in character, until their fullest development was reached in the first half or middle of the fifteenth century. Much interesting information about the miniaturists' Guild in Bruges during the second half of the century has been published by Mr. Weale¹.

This was the Guild of Saint John and Saint Luke; and every painter, miniaturist, illuminator, rubricator, copyist, maker of vellum, binder, or seller of books who lived and worked in Bruges was required to belong to this Guild. This rule, which existed in Ghent, Antwerp, and most artistic centers, served a dual purpose: on the one hand, it protected the individual illuminator from any kind of injustice or oppression; and, on the other hand, it helped maintain a high standard of excellence in the work produced by the Guild members.

<margin>Rules of the Guilds.</margin>
No miniaturist could be admitted until he had presented a sufficiently good sample of his skill to the Dean of the Guild. Furthermore, all members were subject to fines if they used inferior materials of any kind, such as impure gold, adulterated ultramarine, vermilion, or similar substances. In this way, the officers of the Guild acted as mediators between the artisan and his patrons, ensuring reasonable pay for the artist and, in exchange, reasonably high-quality workmanship for the employer or customer. The Guilds also prevented any form of commercial exploitation by strictly limiting the number of apprentices or workmen each master could employ.

<margin>Decline of manuscript art.</margin>
Thus, although fine manuscripts were still written and illuminated in many of the major monasteries of Europe, a large class of secular illuminators arose, especially in Paris and the principal towns of Flanders and northern Germany. In this way, the production of manuscripts, particularly illuminated *Books of Hours*, became a regular commercial process. This led to the inevitable result that much work of a very inferior quality was produced to meet the rapidly growing demand for inexpensive and flashy books.

---

<note>¹ See *Le Beffroi*, Bruges, Vol. IV. 1873.</note>

<summary>This page discusses the peak of artist guilds in the 15th century, focusing on the regulations of the Guild of Saint John and Saint Luke in Bruges. While these guilds protected artists and maintained quality, the increasing commercial demand for manuscripts eventually led to the mass production of lower-quality works.</summary>

<page-num>218</page-num>
# COSTLY MANUSCRIPTS OF [CHAP. XIII.

An immense number of these cheap manuscript <term>*Horae*</term> were produced according to a few fixed patterns, with a mechanical dullness of repetition in every border and miniature with which they were decorated.

<margin>Costly Horae.</margin>
At the same time, manuscripts were still produced, mostly at the special order of some royal patron or wealthy merchant, which—in elaborate beauty and in unsparing labor of execution—are hardly surpassed by the work of the earlier monastic scribes<note>In poetic beauty, however, they cannot be compared to the glory of the French <term>*Apocalypses*</term>, such as the one in the library of Trinity College, Cambridge.</note>. Examples of this are mentioned above on pages 135 and 169.

The Dukes of Burgundy and the Kings of France, toward the close of the fourteenth and the first half of the fifteenth century, numbered many <term>illuminators</term> among their regular paid adherents. In some cases, the artist was permanently engaged and passed his whole life in the service of one prince; while in other cases, famous <term>illuminators</term> were hired for a few months or years when the patron wanted a specially magnificent manuscript either for his own use or as a royal gift on the occasion of a marriage, a coronation, or another great event.

<margin>Women artists.</margin>
In some cases, we find that women learned to be manuscript <term>illuminators</term> of great skill and artistic taste. For example, Cornelia, the wife of Gerard David of Bruges<note>Gerard David is mentioned above as one of the <term>illuminators</term> of the famous Grimani <term>*Breviary*</term>; see page 165.</note>, was, like her husband, both an <term>illuminator</term> of manuscripts and a painter of altar <term>retables</term>. A fine <term>triptych</term> painted by Cornelia, in the possession of Mr. H. Willett of Brighton, is a work of great beauty and refinement, which would be difficult to distinguish from a painting by Gerard David himself.

<margin>Costly gifts.</margin>
In the fifteenth century, the commercial value of sumptuously illuminated manuscripts rose to its highest point. No object was thought more suited for a magnificent wedding present to a royal personage than a costly manuscript<note>See pages 117 and 122 for examples of this.</note>.

>Item: he charges himself with 50s. received from the said Edmund for his office, namely that of Precentor.

>Item: paid for one bottle to put ink in: 10d.
>Item: paid for ink: 13d.
>Item: for vermilion: 9d.
>Item: for the scribe’s commons for 18 weeks, paid at 10d. per week: 15s.
>Item: for the said scribe’s stipend for the same period: 13s. 4d.
>Item: paid to Adam Acton for noting “Liber generationis and the Passions” in the said book: 7d.
>Item: for examination and for making grey capital letters: 3s.
>Item: for the illumination of the said book: 3s. 3d.

<page-num>224</page-num>

# CHAPTER XIV.

## THE MATERIALS AND TECHNICAL PROCESSES OF THE ILLUMINATOR.

<margin>Finest vellum.</margin>
*Vellum for scribes*¹. The most remarkable skill is shown by the perfection to which the art of preparing vellum² for the scribe was brought. The exquisitely thin uterine vellum, which was specially used for the minutely written Anglo-Norman *Vulgates* of the thirteenth century, has already been described; see page 113. For ivory-like beauty of color and texture, nothing could surpass the best Italian vellum of the fifteenth century.

One occasional use of the very thin uterine vellum should be noted. For example, in a twelfth-century German copy of the *Vulgate* now in the Corpus library in Cambridge, some of the miniature pictures have been painted on separate pieces of uterine vellum and then pasted into their place on the thicker vellum pages of the manuscript. This, however, is an exceptional thing.

<margin>High price of vellum.</margin>
The vellum used for illuminated manuscripts appears to have been costly, partly because of the skill and labor that were required for its production and, in the case of uterine vellum, because of the great number of animal skins that were required to provide enough material for the writing of a single manuscript such as a copy of the *Vulgate*.

---

¹ <note>See Peignot, *Essai sur l'histoire du parchemin et du vélin*, Paris, 1812.</note>
² <note>Strictly speaking, the word *vellum* should denote parchment made from calfskin, but the word is commonly used for any of the finer qualities of parchment which were used for manuscripts.</note>

<meta>Digitized by Google watermark at page footer</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the high quality and specialized preparation of vellum used in medieval manuscripts, specifically highlighting the thin "uterine vellum" and the fine Italian varieties. It also notes the high cost of these materials due to the intensive labor and the large number of skins required for a single volume.</summary>

<keywords>vellum, uterine vellum, Anglo-Norman Vulgates, Italian vellum, manuscript materials, parchment, book production, Peignot</keywords>

--- Page 256 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of manuscript materials, moving from the general description of vellum quality to its historical costs, physical properties, and the decline in preparation techniques in the modern era, before transitioning to the history of paper.</meta>

<page-num>225</page-num>

<header>CHAP. XIV.] VELLUM FOR MANUSCRIPTS. 225</header>

<margin>Cost of vellum.</margin>
Even the more common kind of parchment used for official documents was quite a costly item. The roll containing the visitation expenses of Bishop Swinfield, Bishop of Hereford from 1282 to 1317, shows that 150 sheets of parchment cost 3s. 4d., which is about £4 in modern value¹.

The vellum used for manuscripts has a different texture on its two sides. One side, upon which the hair grew, has a *matte*, unglossy surface; the other (interior) side of the skin is perfectly smooth and, in the case of the finest vellum, has a beautifully glossy texture like that of polished ivory.

In writing a manuscript, the scribe was careful to arrange his pages so that two glossy and two dull pages faced each other².

<margin>Poor quality modern vellum.</margin>
The art of preparing vellum of the finest kind is now lost; the vellum made in England is usually spoiled first by rubbing down the surface to make it unnaturally even, and then by loading it with a sort of priming of plaster and white lead, very much like the paper of a cheap memorandum book.

The best modern *vellum* is still made in Italy, especially in Rome. Good, thick, untreated vellum of a fine, pure color can be obtained in Rome, though in limited quantities and at a high price³, but nothing is now made which resembles either the finest ivory-textured vellum of fifteenth-century Italian manuscripts, or the exquisitely thin uterine vellum of the Anglo-Norman Bibles.

<margin>Use of paper.</margin>
*Paper*⁴. Although by far the majority of the illuminated manuscripts of the Middle Ages are written on vellum, paper was occasionally used long before the fifteenth century,

---

² <note>The same arrangement is to be seen in books printed on vellum.</note>
³ <note>For example, the vellum alone required to print a small, thick folio, such as Caxton's *Golden Legend*, would now cost about £40.</note>

Maunde Thompson. See also E. Egger, *Le papier dans l'antiquité et dans les temps modernes* (Paper in Antiquity and Modern Times), Paris, 1866.</note>

<page-num>226</page-num>
<header>PAPER MANUFACTURE. [CHAP. XIV.</header>

...when its manufacture was greatly expanded to meet the demand created by the invention of printing.

Paper made from papyrus pith has already been described; see Chapter II, page 22.

<margin>Method of making paper.</margin>
A very different process was used for the various kinds of paper made in medieval and modern times. The essence of the process consists of making a fine pulp from cotton or linen rags by pounding them for a long time with enough water to give the mixture the consistency of thick cream. A handful of this fluid pulp is then spread evenly and thinly over the bottom of a fine wire sieve, through which the excess water drains away. This leaves a thin, soft mass that is then removed from the sieve, pressed, dried, and finally soaked with size <note>A gelatinous substance used to glaze paper or vellum</note> to make the paper suitable for writing. This process leaves the wire marks of the sieve indelibly printed onto the paper.

These marks are of two kinds: *first*, those of the thicker wires that run lengthwise along the sieve at intervals of about an inch or slightly more; and *secondly*, very fine cross-wires, placed close together and woven at right angles to the thicker wires mentioned first.

<margin>Watermarks.</margin>
In the fourteenth century, what are called *watermarks* came into use along with the invention of linen paper. A simple device indicating the city or province where the paper was made was woven with fine wire into the bottom of the sieve, and this mark was impressed upon the paper, just like the other parallel wires of the sieve. A double-headed eagle, a vase, a letter, or a bull’s head are among the earliest paper marks found in manuscripts and books of the fifteenth century¹. Later, during the sixteenth century, each manufacturer adopted his own mark²; and even more recently, the year has been added³.

---

<note>¹ A good illustrated account of early watermarks is provided by Sotheby, *Principia Typographia*, London, 1858.</note>
<note>² Some fifteenth-century paper has a specific maker’s mark, but more often a general town or district mark was used, such as cross-keys, a Cardinal’s hat, an Imperial crown, or a double eagle.</note>
<note>³ What is now called "foolscap paper" originally took its name from a watermark in the shape of a *fool’s cap and bells*, a device that was frequently used in the sixteenth and seventeenth centuries.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the history of paper manufacturing, transitioning from the technical process of creating pulp and watermarks to the historical timeline and geographical origins of different paper types.</meta>

<page-num>227</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XIV.] THE DATES OF ITS USE.</header>

<margin>Evidence of date.</margin>
These paper-marks in some cases provide useful evidence regarding the origin and date of a manuscript or printed book; however, too much reliance should not be placed on such evidence, since paper often remained in stock for a long time, and the products of one manufactory were frequently exported for use by the scribes and printers of more than one distant country <note>Some of Caxton's books, printed in Westminster, bear many different paper-marks from Germany and Flanders, even in the same volume.</note>.

Paper of Oriental make has no watermark, but the earliest linen paper of the fourteenth century made in Christian Europe always has a watermark of some kind, which is very clearly visible.

<margin>Earliest cotton paper.</margin>
*The dates of paper manufacture.* The earliest paper appears to have been made in China at a date even before the Christian Era. Its manufacture was next extended to Syria, and especially to Damascus <note>Paper was also made at an early date in Constantinople, through its intimate relationship with the East. Hence the monk Theophilus, writing in the eleventh century, calls linen paper "Greek vellum," *pergamena Graeca*; see I. 24.</note>. This early paper was made from the cotton plant, the "tree-wool" of Herodotus. Hence it was called *charta bombycina* or *Damascena*, or, because of its silky texture, *charta serica*. Paper of this class, almost as beautiful in texture as vellum, is still made in the East and used for the fine illuminated manuscripts of India, Persia, and other Muslim countries.

<margin>Arab MSS. on paper.</margin>
Many Arab manuscripts written on cotton paper from as early as the ninth century still exist. The Muslim conquerors of Spain and Sicily introduced the manufacture of this *charta bombycina* into Western Europe, and it was used to a small extent for Greek and Latin manuscripts during the tenth and eleventh centuries. It was, however, rarely used in Christian Europe until the thirteenth century.

<margin>Wool-paper.</margin>
At first, only cotton was used in the manufacture of paper, but gradually a mixture of wool and then flax or linen was introduced.

Peter, who was Abbot of Cluny from 1122 to 1150, mentions in his treatise *Adversus Judaeos* manuscripts written on wool paper, made "from the scrapings of old cloths" <note>ex rasuris veterum pannorum</note>.

---

<sig>15—2</sig>
<meta>Digitized by Google</meta>

<meta>This page continues the history of paper manufacturing, moving from the general origins and watermarks discussed previously to the specific emergence of linen paper and its spread across Europe and into England.</meta>

<page-num>228</page-num>
<header>THE MANUFACTURE AND [CHAP. XIV.</header>

<margin>Linen paper.</margin>
In the fourteenth century, linen paper began to be made—at first mixed with wool, and then made of pure linen. This fourteenth-century paper is distinguished by its strength, its dense texture, and its thick wire marks; the watermark is especially clear and transparent. Paper was frequently used for official documents, charters, and similar records before it came into use for manuscript books <note>This old paper is almost as strong, tough, and durable as parchment—very unlike modern machine-made paper.</note>.

<margin>Early manuscripts on paper.</margin>
The British Museum holds one of the oldest known books on paper (*Arundel Manuscripts*, 268); this is a collection of *astronomical treatises* written by an Italian scribe early in the thirteenth century.

In the fourteenth century, the Spanish factories of *cotton paper* were in decline, and the first factory for *linen paper* was started at Fabriano in northern Italy. In 1340, another factory was set up in Padua, and before the close of the fourteenth century, paper was made in nearly all the major cities of northern Italy, especially in Milan and Venice, and as far south as Florence and Siena.

In Germany, papermaking began in Mentz <note>Mainz</note> around 1320; and in 1390, a factory was started at Nuremberg with the assistance of Italian workmen. South Germany, however, was supplied with paper from northern Italy until the fifteenth century.

In Paris and other places in France, paper began to be made soon after the first factories in Italy were established.

<margin>Paper in England.</margin>
In England, cotton paper was used extensively in the fourteenth century, especially for legal documents. In Oxford, in the year 1355, a quire <note>A quantity of paper, typically 24 or 25 sheets.</note> of paper, small folio size, cost five pence, equal in modern value to eight or nine shillings. In the fifteenth century, its value had decreased to three or four pence per quire.

Paper does not appear to have been made in England until the reign of Henry VII; before that time, it was mainly imported from Germany and the Netherlands.

All of Caxton’s books are printed on foreign paper, and the first book printed on paper which was made in England was

<meta>This page continues the history of paper manufacturing, specifically focusing on the first paper mill in England and the superior quality of early Venetian paper compared to later machine-made varieties.</meta>

<page-num>229</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XIV.] USE OF PAPER.</header>

<margin>Earliest English paper.</margin>
Wynkyn de Worde’s Bartholomaeus, *De proprietatibus rerum*, printed about the year 1495, four years after Caxton’s death, includes the following interesting *colophon*, which alludes to the first paper manufactory in England, set up by John Tate at Hertford.

This *colophon*, which does not do credit to Wynkyn de Worde’s literary style, runs thus:

> And also, out of your charity, call to remembrance
> The soul of William Caxton, the first printer of this book
> In the Latin tongue at Cologne, to advance himself,
> So that every well-disposed man may look upon it;
> And may John Tate the younger enjoy happiness,
> Who lately has caused this thin paper to be made in England,
> In which this book is now printed in our English.

During the fifteenth century, the making of paper reached its highest degree of perfection, and in the following century, its excellence began to decline.

<margin>Beauty of Venetian paper.</margin>
The Venetian paper of about 1470, used, for example, in the printed books of Nicolas Jenson and other printers in Venice, is a substance of very great beauty and durability, inferior only in appearance to the very best sort of vellum. It is very strong, of a fine creamy tint, and sized<gloss>1</gloss> with great skill, so as to have a beautiful glossy texture. For the illuminator’s purpose, it appears to have been almost as good as vellum. It even receives the raised mordant for burnished gold of the highest beauty and brilliance.

The very small quantity of good paper that is now manufactured, mainly for artistic purposes, is made by hand in exactly the same way that was employed in the fourteenth or fifteenth century.

Most paper is now made by machinery, and as a rule contains more esparto grass than pure linen fibre.

---

<gloss>1</gloss> The size was made by boiling down shreds of vellum. Blotting paper is paper that has not been sized. A coarse grey variety was used as early as the fifteenth century, though, as a rule, fine sand was used for this purpose until about the middle of the present century, especially on the Continent.

<summary>This page describes the establishment of the first English paper mill by John Tate and discusses the exceptional quality of 15th-century Venetian paper. It contrasts the durable, hand-made linen papers of the past with modern machine-made papers containing esparto grass.</summary>
<keywords>Wynkyn de Worde, William Caxton, John Tate, papermaking, Venice, Nicolas Jenson, vellum, sizing, esparto grass, colophon</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of materials used in book production, transitioning from the history of paper to the specific metals and pigments—namely gold, silver, and tin—used by illuminators to decorate manuscripts.</meta>

<page-num>230</page-num>
<header>FLUID GOLD [CHAP. XIV.</header>

### THE METALS AND PIGMENTS USED IN ILLUMINATED MANUSCRIPTS¹.

<margin>Fluid and leaf gold.</margin>
**Gold and silver or tin.** The splendor of illuminated manuscripts of almost all classes, except manuscripts of the Irish school such as the *Book of Kells*, is largely due to the very skillful use of gold and silver. These metals were applied by the illuminator in two ways: *first*, as a fluid pigment, and *secondly* in the form of leaf.

The fluid method appears to have been the older one. It is easier to apply, but is not comparable in splendor of effect to the highly burnished leaf gold, which was used with such perfection of skill by the illuminators of the fourteenth century.

<margin>Method of grinding.</margin>
*Fluid gold* was made by laboriously grinding the pure metal on a porphyry slab into the finest possible powder. This powdered gold, mixed with water and a little size, was applied with a brush like any other pigment; see Theophilus, I. 30 to 33². When dry, it could be polished to some extent by burnishing, but as it was laid directly onto the comparatively uneven and yielding surface of the vellum, it never received a very high polish. As a rule, therefore, fluid gold was left unburnished, and its surface remained dull or *matte* in appearance.

<margin>Dull and burnished gold.</margin>
For this reason, it was not infrequently used in conjunction with burnished leaf gold, with a fine decorative effect being produced by the contrast of the *matte* and polished surfaces. Thus, for example, in fourteenth- and fifteenth-century manuscripts, a delicate diaper of scroll patterns is sometimes painted with a fine brush over a ground of burnished gold leaf.

---

<note>¹ Modern "shell gold" is practically the same thing as the fluid gold of the medieval illuminators, except that it is not made with the pure, unalloyed metal.</note>
<note>² The following are the most useful and easily accessible books on the technical processes of the illuminator: Theophilus, *Schedula diversarum Artium* (Schedule of Various Arts), Hendrie's edition with a translation, London, 1847; Cennino Cennini, *Trattato della pittura* (Treatise on Painting), 1437, edited, with a translation, by Mrs. Merrifield, London, 1844; and a large and valuable collection of early manuscripts on the same subject, edited and translated by Mrs. Merrifield under the title of *Original Treatises on the Arts of Painting*, 2 Vols., London, 1849.</note>

<summary>This page describes the two primary methods medieval illuminators used to apply gold and silver to manuscripts: as a fluid pigment and as leaf gold. It details the process of creating fluid gold by grinding metal into powder and explains why it typically had a matte finish compared to the highly polished burnished leaf.</summary>
<keywords>illuminated manuscripts, fluid gold, leaf gold, burnishing, vellum, pigments, Theophilus, Cennino Cennini, Mrs. Merrifield, shell gold, medieval art techniques</keywords>

--- Page 262 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of gold as a pigment, detailing its revival in the fifteenth century, the Cistercian order's opposition to its use, the application of fluid silver, and the technical preparation required for burnished gold leaf.</meta>

<page-num>231</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XIV.] USED AS A PIGMENT.</header>

In the fifteenth century, during the decline of the illuminator’s art, the use of fluid gold, which had previously greatly diminished, was much revived, especially for the background of the realistic borders in Flemish manuscripts¹, for touching in the highlights of miniatures, and for many other purposes. When used to cover large surfaces, it is always unsatisfactory in effect and has little decorative value.

<margin>Cistercian severity.</margin>
The preparation of this gold pigment was a very slow and laborious matter. The severe Cistercian rule regarded this process as a waste of precious time; and indeed the use of gold in any form was prohibited in the manuscripts used in Cistercian Abbeys. In the dialogue between a Cistercian and a Cluniac monk, *De diversis utriusque ordinis observantiis* (*Thesaur. Nov. Anecdot.* Vol. v. 1623), the Cistercian asks “what use there can be in grinding gold and painting large capitals with it”; *aurum molere et cum illo molito magnas capitales pingere litteras, quid est nisi inutile et otiosum opus?* St. Bernard himself had an even stronger objection, not only to gold in manuscripts, but to any ornaments with grotesque dragons and monsters, on the ground that they did not tend to edification.

<margin>Fluid silver.</margin>
*Fluid silver* was prepared and applied in the same way, but it was much less used than gold pigment. A very beautiful effect is produced in some of the gorgeous Carolingian manuscripts by using in the same ornament both gold and silver, which mutually enhance each other’s effect by contrast of color.

<margin>Leaf gold.</margin>
*Burnished Gold leaf.* The extraordinary splendor of effect produced by skillfully applied gold leaf depends mainly on the fact that it was laid, not directly onto the vellum, but onto a thick bed of a hard enamel-like substance, which gradually set (as it got dry) and formed a ground nearly as hard and smooth as glass; this enabled the gold leaf laid upon it to be burnished to the highest possible polish, until in fact the gold gave a reflection like that of a mirror. This

---

<note>¹ See page 144.</note>

<summary>This page discusses the 15th-century revival of fluid gold in Flemish manuscripts and the Cistercian order's religious objections to such luxury. It also contrasts fluid silver with gold and explains how gold leaf was burnished on a hard ground to create a mirror-like finish.</summary>
<keywords>fluid gold, Cistercian rule, St. Bernard, fluid silver, Carolingian manuscripts, burnished gold leaf, vellum, manuscript illumination</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical description of applying gold leaf, specifically focusing on the use of a thick mordant ground to create a raised, solid appearance and the necessity of using high-purity gold.</meta>

<page-num>232</page-num>
<header>THE USE OF GOLD LEAF [CHAP. XIV.]</header>

<margin>Mordant ground.</margin>
The enamel-like ground, or *mordant* as it was called, was commonly as thick as stout cardboard, and its edges were rounded off. This had the dual result of making the gold leaf applied to it look not like a thin leaf, but like a thick plate of gold¹, and at the same time the rounded edges catch the light and so greatly increase the decorative splendor of the metal.

<margin>Convex surface.</margin>
Thus, for example, the small bosses and studs of gold, which are scattered so densely throughout the foliage in the illuminated borders of Italian manuscripts of the fourteenth and fifteenth centuries, are convex in shape, like an old-fashioned watch-glass, and each boss reflects a brilliant speck of light regardless of the direction from which the light falls upon the page. Perhaps the most sumptuous use of gold leaf is to be seen in some of the early fourteenth-century French manuscripts, in which large miniatures are painted with an unbroken background of solid-looking burnished gold, with a mirror-like power of reflection.

It was only by slow degrees that the illuminators reached the perfect technical skill of the fourteenth century in their application of gold leaf.

<margin>Purity of the gold.</margin>
In the first place, the purest gold had to be beaten out, not the alloy of gold, silver, and copper which is used today for making the gold leaf of what is called "the finest quality." The English illuminators at the close of the thirteenth and in the fourteenth century frequently obtained their gold in the form of the beautiful florins of Florence, Lucca², or Pisa, which were minted from absolutely pure gold³. In England, there was no gold coinage until the series of *nobles* was begun by Edward—

---

<note>¹ That is to say, it looks as if the whole substance, mordant and all, were one solid mass of gold, nearly as thick as a modern half-sovereign; see Theophilus, I. 24 and 25.</note>
<note>² Thus, when William Torell was about to gild the bronze effigy of Queen Eleanor in Westminster Abbey, he obtained a large number of gold florins from Lucca.</note>
<note>³ Not even the smallest mixture of alloy was permitted in the gold coinages of the Middle Ages. Dante (*Inferno* xxx. 73) mentions the coiner Maestro Adamo who had been burned at Romena in 1280 for issuing florins which had scarcely more alloy than a modern sovereign.</note>

<page-num>233</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XIV.] IN ILLUMINATIONS. 233</header>

III.¹, but these were made of quite pure gold, like the Italian florins, and so suited the purpose of the illuminator.

Another important point was that the gold leaf was not beaten to even one-twentieth the extreme thinness of modern leaf. The leaves were very small, about three by four inches at most, and no more than fifty to a hundred of these were produced from an Italian gold ducat, which weighed nearly as much as a modern sovereign².

We find that in many cases the illuminator prepared his own gold leaf, and it was not uncommon for the crafts of the goldsmith and the illuminator to be practiced by the same man. For example, the Fitz-Othos, mentioned on page 112 as a distinguished Anglo-Norman family of artists in the thirteenth century, were skilled both as makers of gold shrines and as illuminators of manuscripts. Many interesting notes about the Fitz-Othos and other artists employed at Westminster during the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries can be found among the royal accounts now preserved in the Record Office: see *Vetusta Monumenta*, Vol. VI., p. 1 seq.

Among the accounts of the expenses for decorating the royal chapel of Saint Stephen at Westminster with painting during Edward III’s reign, we find that John Lightgrave paid for six hundred leaves of gold at the rate of five shillings per hundred, equal to about £5 or £6 in modern value. And John "Tynbeter" received six shillings for six dozen leaves of tin used instead of silver—not because it was cheap, but because tin was not as liable to tarnish.

These accounts are in Latin, which is not always of

The famous Erechtheum inscription of 404 B.C. records one drachma as the cost of each leaf (πέταλον) used for gilding the marble decorations; see *Cor. Ins. Att.* I. 324, fragment C, col. ii. lines 35 and 42. Eighteen pence will now buy 100 leaves of gold.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the materials and craftsmen involved in medieval illumination, moving from the procurement of gold leaf to the specific technical processes and the professional overlap between goldsmiths and painters.</meta>

<page-num>234</page-num>
<header>METHOD OF APPLYING [CHAP. XIV.</header>

...Ciceronian purity; a classical purist might perhaps complain about such phrases as these:
*Item. Pro reparatione brushorum, 8d.*, under the date 1307; and, in the following year,
*Item. Unum scarletum blanketum, 2s. 7d.*
The scarlet blanket was not bought to keep the artist warm, but to produce a red pigment, as is described below on page 246.

<margin>Goldsmith artists.</margin>
This close connection between the arts of the goldsmith and the illuminator had its parallel in other branches of the arts, and with results of very considerable importance. Many of the leading painters and sculptors of Italy, during the period of highest artistic development, were also skilled goldsmiths—as, for example, Ghiberti, Verrocchio, Antonio Pollaiuolo, Francesco Francia, and many others.
This habit of working with precious metals gave a neatness and precision of touch to the painter, and in the art of illuminating manuscripts, it taught the artist to use his gold so as to produce the richest and most decorative effect.

<margin>The gold mordant.</margin>
*The mordant.* We now come to the most difficult part of the illuminator's art: creating a base for his gold leaf of the greatest hardness and smoothness of surface. It is a subject dealt with at great length by all the major writers on the technique of the illuminator, from Theophilus in the eleventh century down to Cennino Cennini at the beginning of the fourteenth¹.

Though differing in details, the general principle of the process is much the same in all: the finest possible sort of *gesso*, plaster, gypsum, or whitening was very finely ground to an extremely fine powder and then worked up with egg white or glue size to the consistency of cream, so that it could be applied with a brush. After the first coat was dry, a second and a third coat were added to build the mordant to the necessary thickness, so that it stood out in visible relief upon the surface of the vellum.

---
<note>¹ The best account of how to make the mordant was given around 1398 by a Lombard illuminator named Johannes Archerius; see Mrs. Merrifield's interesting collection of *Treatises on Painting*, Vol. I, page 259 and following.</note>

<summary>This page explains the historical connection between goldsmithing and illumination, noting how the precision of metalworking influenced great Italian artists. It also describes the technical preparation of "mordant," a gesso-based ground used to create a raised surface for gold leaf on manuscripts.</summary>

<keywords>goldsmiths, illuminators, Italian art, Renaissance artists, mordant, gesso, manuscript technique, Johannes Archerius, pigments</keywords>

--- Page 266 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of preparing the mordant base, specifically the addition of color for visibility and the subsequent application and burnishing of gold leaf.</meta>

<page-num>235</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XIV.] THE GOLD LEAF.</header>

In order that the illuminator might see clearly where his brush was going, and keep his mordant accurately within the required outline, it was common to add some coloring matter, such as bole Armeniac (red ochre), to the white *gesso*, which otherwise would not have shown up very clearly on the cream-white vellum. In many cases, however, this coloring matter is omitted.

<margin>Application of leaf.</margin>
When the last coat of the *gesso-mordant* was dry and hard, its surface was carefully polished with the burnisher and it was then ready to receive the gold leaf; several days of waiting would often be required before the whole body of the mordant had set perfectly hard. White of egg was then lightly brushed over the whole of the raised mordant, and while the albumen was still moist and sticky, the illuminator gently slid onto it the piece of gold leaf, which he had previously cut approximately to the required shape. He then softly dabbed the gold leaf with a pad or bunch of wool, until it had completely adhered to the sticky mordant, working it with special care so as to completely cover the rounded edges. After the albumen was quite dry, and the gold leaf firmly fixed in its place, the artist brushed away all the excess gold leaf with a stiff brush; that is, all the leaf under which there was no mordant-ground to hold it fast.

<margin>Burnishing process.</margin>
The gold was then ready to be polished. For this purpose various forms of burnisher were used, the best being a hard, highly polished rounded pencil of crystal or stone, such as hematite, agate, chalcedony and the like; or in the absence of those, the highly polished enamel tooth of a dog, cat, rat or other carnivorous animal was nearly as good <note>See Theophilus, I. 25.</note>. In fact, patience and labor were the chief requisites; one recipe, in Jehan le Begue’s manuscript, § 192 <note>See Mrs. Merrifield, *op. cit.* Vol. I. p. 154.</note>, directs the illuminator to burnish and to go on burnishing until the sweat runs down his forehead. But caution, as well as labor, was required; it was very easy to scratch holes in the gold leaf, so that the mordant showed through, unless great care was used in the rubbing. In that case the illuminator had to apply another

<summary>This page describes the technical process of applying gold leaf to a manuscript, including the use of red ochre for visibility, the application of egg white as an adhesive, and the intensive labor required for burnishing the gold to a high shine.</summary>
<keywords>gold leaf, gesso, mordant, bole Armeniac, vellum, burnishing, illumination, Jehan le Begue, Theophilus</keywords>

--- Page 267 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of gold leaf application, focusing on the difficulties of the burnishing process and specific historical recipes for the mordant ground.</meta>

<page-num>236</page-num>
<header>USE OF GOLD [CHAP. XIV.</header>

piece of leaf to cover up the scratches, and do his burnishing over again. To secure the highest polish, illuminators burnished the hard mordant as described above before laying on the gold leaf. In most cases two layers of gold leaf were applied, and sometimes even more, in order to ensure a perfect and unbroken surface.

<margin>**Application of gold.**</margin>
All writers speak of this burnishing as being a very difficult and uncertain process even for a skilled hand, requiring exactly the right temperature and amount of moisture in the air, otherwise it was liable to go wrong. If the gold was to be applied in minute or intricate patterns, the illuminator did not attempt to cut his leaf to fit the mordant-ground, but laid it in little patches so as to cover a portion of the ornament. The superfluous gold between the lines of the pattern was then brushed away, as the leaf only remained where it was held by the mordant. With all possible care and skill, it was hardly possible always to ensure a sharp clean outline to each patch of gold; and so one commonly finds that the illuminator has added a black outline around the edge of each patch of gold, in order to conceal any little raggedness of the edge.

As examples of medieval recipes for making the mordant I may mention the following:—

<margin>**Recipes for the mordant.**</margin>
> “Mix gypsum, white marble, and eggshells finely powdered and colored with red ochre or *terra verde*; to be mixed with white of egg and applied in thin coats, and to be burnished before the application of the gold.”

When dry, this mixture slowly set into a beautiful, hard and yet not brittle substance, capable of receiving a polish like that of marble, and forming the best possible ground to receive the gold leaf. Much of its excellence depended on the patience of the illuminator in applying it in very thin coats; each of which was allowed to dry completely before the next was laid on. When ready to receive the gold leaf, after the burnishing of the mordant was finished, some purified white of egg was brushed over to make the gold leaf adhere firmly so as not to work loose or tear under the friction of the burnisher.

In some cases white lead (*ceruse*) was added to the *gesso*,

<meta>This page continues the discussion of gold-mordant recipes, specifically comparing the egg-based methods of Cennino Cennini with the oil-based methods of Giovanni da Modena, and introduces the technique of tooling patterns into gold leaf.</meta>

<page-num>237</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XIV.] IN ILLUMINATIONS</header>

<margin>Recipes for the mordant.</margin>
as, for example, in a recipe given by Cennino Cennini (§ 131 to 139, and 157) for a mordant made of fine gypsum, ceruse (white lead), and sugar of Candia—that is, ordinary pure white sugar¹. This is to be ground up with white of egg, applied in thin coats, and burnished. To color the mordant, Cennino adds *bole Armeniac*, *terra verde*, or verdigris green.

Giovanni da Modena, a Bolognese illuminator, gives the following recipe for a different gold-mordant to be used with oil instead of egg white or size². Instead of *gesso*, it is to be made of a mixture of white and red lead, red ochre, bole Armeniac, and verdigris; the whole is to be ground first with water, then thoroughly dried, and ground again with a mixture of linseed oil and amber or mastic varnish.

This variety of mordant appears to have been used in many fifteenth-century Italian manuscripts. It is not as good a mixture as the *gesso* and white of egg, as the oil used to mix it is liable to stain the vellum through to the other side of the page, and even to print a mark on the opposite page, especially when the book has been severely pressed by the binder.

<margin>Tooled patterns.</margin>
*Tooled patterns on gold leaf.* In many Italian and French manuscripts, especially of the fourteenth and fifteenth centuries, a very rich and brilliant effect is produced with tooled lines impressed into the surface of the flat gold. Diapered and scroll-work backgrounds, the <term>nimbi</term> <note>halos</note> of Saints, the <term>orphreys</term> and <term>apparels</term> <note>ornamental bands and embroidered decorations</note> on vestments, and many other kinds of decoration were skillfully executed with a pointed bone or ivory tool. This was impressed upon the gold leaf after it was burnished, and through the gold into the slightly elastic body of the

---

<note>¹ In Cennino's time, the early part of the fourteenth century, sugar was sold in Europe by the ounce as a costly drug. Apothecaries, not grocers, dealt in it. In Persia, Syria, and some other Muslim countries, cane sugar was made and used in comparatively large quantities throughout the medieval period; but in Europe, it did not come into use as an article of food until the 16th century, and even then it was very expensive.</note>

<note>² The date of this recipe is about 1410; it is quoted in Jehan de Begue's manuscript published by Mrs. Merrifield, Vol. i. pp. 9, 95, and 154; see also Theophilus, 1. 31, who speaks of burnishing fluid gold laid on a mordant of red lead and cinnabar.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of decorative techniques for gold leaf, specifically focusing on tooling and stamped patterns, before transitioning to the use of silver and tin as alternatives in medieval illumination.</meta>

<page-num>238</page-num>
<header>USE OF METALS. [CHAP. XIV.</header>

<margin>Stamped patterns.</margin>
*Gesso-mordant.* Patterns were also produced with the help of tiny punches, which stamped dots or circles; these, when grouped together, formed little rosettes or powderings, like those used in the panel paintings of the same time. Gold treated in this way had to be quite thick, and in some cases, when a large flat surface of mordant was to be gilded, as many as three layers of thick gold leaf were used to provide the necessary body of metal.

<margin>Silver leaf.</margin>
Burnished *silver leaf* was occasionally used by medieval illuminators, though not very often, as it was very liable to tarnish and blacken. For this reason, *leaf tin* was often used instead of silver, as tin does not oxidize in such a noticeable way; see above, p. 233.

The use of all three metals—gold, silver, and tin—is described by Theophilus in *Schedula Diversarum Artium*, I. 24, 25, and 26. Theophilus speaks of laying the gold leaf directly onto the vellum with only the help of egg white. This method was not uncommon in early times, and it was not until the end of the thirteenth century that the full splendor of effect was reached with the help of the thick, hard mordant-ground.

<margin>Cheap methods.</margin>
Inferior processes were sometimes used for the cheap manuscripts of later times. Thus, burnished tin leaf covered with a transparent yellow lacquer or varnish made from saffron was used instead of gold.

Cennino and other writers describe a curious method of applying gold easily and cheaply. The illuminator was first to paint his design with a mixture of <term>size</term> <note>a type of glue</note> and crushed glass or crystal; when dry, this left a surface like modern sandpaper or glass-paper. The artist was then to rub a piece of pure gold over the rough surface, which ground off and held a sufficient amount of gold to produce the effect of gilding. Only a very coarse effect, worthy of the nineteenth century, could have been produced by this process.

<vocab>Stamped patterns, gesso-mordant, silver leaf, leaf tin, Theophilus, Schedula diversarum Artium, vellum, mordant-ground, Cennino, gilding</vocab>

<summary>This page describes techniques for decorating gold leaf with stamped patterns and the use of silver and tin as alternatives to gold in manuscript illumination. It also mentions cheaper, inferior methods of gilding using saffron-colored tin or abrasive surfaces to grind gold directly onto the page.</summary>

<keywords>gilding techniques, gesso-mordant, silver leaf, leaf tin, Theophilus, Cennino Cennini, vellum, medieval illumination, saffron lacquer, gold application</keywords>

<page-num>239</page-num>

## CHAPTER XV.

### THE MATERIALS AND TECHNICAL PROCESSES OF THE ILLUMINATOR (*continued*).

<margin>Vehicles used.</margin>The colored **pigments of the illuminators.** Although medieval manuscripts are splendid and varied in color to the highest possible degree, this rich decorative effect was produced by using only a few pigments and the simplest of *mediums*, such as <term>size</term> <note>a type of glue</note> made by boiling down scraps of vellum or fish bones¹, or else gum arabic, or occasionally egg white or a mixture of both the yolk and the white². Generally, the technique of manuscript illumination is the same as that used for panel paintings executed in distemper (*tempera*). An oil medium was unsuitable for manuscript work because the oil ruined the beautiful opaque whiteness of the vellum and caused the painting to show through to the other side³.

---

<note>¹ See Theophilus, I. 33 and 34; he recommends egg white as a medium for <term>ceruse</term> <note>white lead</note>, <term>minium</term> <note>red lead</note>, and carmine, and ordinary vellum <term>size</term> for most other pigments. Jehan le Begue's manuscript gives the same advice regarding the use of egg white, but suggests using gum arabic with other pigments; see section 197.</note>

<note>² The British Museum holds an interesting manuscript on pigments entitled *On the Colors of Illuminators* (*De coloribus Illuminatorum*, Sloane manuscripts, 1754); see also Eraclius, *On the Arts of the Romans* (*De artibus Romanorum*), published by Raspe, London, 1783 and 1801; and the twelfth-century *Mappae Clavicula* printed in *Archaeologia*, Vol. 32, pp. 183 to 244. The first book of Theophilus, *Schedule of Diverse Arts* (*Diversarum artium schedula*), written in the eleventh century, contains much interesting information on this subject; see also the works mentioned above on page 230.</note>

<note>³ *The Journal of the Society of Arts* (Dec. 25, 1891, and Jan. 8 and 15, 1892) contains a valuable series of papers on "The Pigments and Mediums of the Old Masters" by Mr. A. P. Laurie, who provides new insights into the treatises edited by Mrs. Merrifield through his own chemical investigations.</note>

<summary>This page discusses the pigments and binding mediums used in medieval manuscript illumination, noting that a limited palette and simple binders like size, gum arabic, and egg were used. It explains that oil was avoided because it damaged the vellum, making the technique similar to tempera panel painting.</summary>
