<meta>This page continues the discussion on the materials of the illuminator, focusing specifically on the origin, manufacture, and significant value of ultramarine blue pigment.</meta>

<page-num>240</page-num>
<header>ULTRAMARINE BLUE. [CHAP. XV.</header>

<margin>
Ultrama-
rine blue.
</margin>
*Blue pigments.* The most important blue pigment, during both classical and medieval times, was the costly and very beautiful *ultramarine (azzurrum¹ transmarinum)*, which was made from *lapis lazuli*, a mineral chiefly imported from Persia. This *ultramarine* blue was the *cyanus* or *coeruleum* of Theophrastus and Pliny. It is not only the most magnificent of all blue pigments, but it is also the most durable, even when exposed to light for a very long period.

<margin>
Its manu-
facture.
</margin>
The general principle of the manufacture of ultramarine is very simple; it consists merely of grinding the *lapis lazuli* to powder, and then separating the deep blue particles from the rest of the stone through repeated washing². The process of extracting the blue was made easier if the *lapis lazuli* was first <term>calcined</term> <note>heated to a high temperature</note> by heat. This is the modern method and was occasionally done in medieval times, but it injures the depth and brilliance of the pigment. In the finest manuscripts, ultramarine was used that had been prepared by the better though more laborious process without the aid of heat.

<margin>
Its great
value.
</margin>
The proportion of pure blue in a lump of *lapis lazuli* is much smaller than it looks; the stone was and is rare and costly, and thus the finest ultramarine of the medieval painters was often worth considerably more than double its weight in gold³.

Both in classical and medieval times, it was usual for the patron who had ordered a picture to supply the necessary *ultramarine* to the artist, who was only expected to provide the less costly pigments in return for the sum for which he had contracted to execute the work.

Pliny (*Hist. Nat.* XXXIII. 120) tells a story of a trick played by a painter on his employer, who suspiciously

---

<note>¹ This word is spelled in many different ways.</note>
<note>² In medieval times, this was done by first embedding the powdered stone in a lump of wax and resin, from which the blue particles were laboriously extracted by long-continued kneading and washing. The theory of this apparently was that the wax held the colorless particles and allowed the blue to be washed out; see Mrs. Merrifield, *Treatises on Painting*, Vol. I. pp. 49, and 97 to 111.</note>
<note>³ The modern value of ultramarine is about equal to its weight in gold. Sir Peter Lely, in the time of Charles II, paid £4. 10s. an ounce for it.</note>

<summary>This page discusses the origin, production, and immense value of ultramarine blue pigment, which was derived from lapis lazuli and prized for its beauty and durability. It explains that the pigment was so expensive that patrons typically provided it to artists separately from their standard contract fees.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the value of ultramarine, transitioning from its high cost to historical anecdotes about its theft and recovery.</meta>

<page-num>241</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XV.] THEFT OF ULTRAMARINE.</header>
<sig>M. C. M. 16</sig>

<margin>Method of theft.</margin>
watched the artist to see that he did not steal any of the precious *ultramarine* which had been doled out to him. At frequent intervals the painter washed his brush, dipped in the ultramarine, in a vessel of water; the heavy pigment sank to the bottom, and at the end of the day the artist poured off the water and secured the mass of powdered ultramarine at the bottom.

It is interesting to note that Vasari, in his *life of Perugino*, tells precisely this story about Pietro, who was annoyed at the suspicions expressed by a certain Prior for whom he was painting a fresco <note>The Prior in question was the Superior of the Convent of the Frati Gesuati in Florence.</note>. The Prior was in despair at the enormous amount of pigment that the thirsty wall sucked in, and he was pleasantly surprised when, at the conclusion of the work, Perugino returned to him a large quantity of *ultramarine*, as a lesson that he should not suspect a gentleman of being a thief.

<margin>Ultramarine scraped off.</margin>
The library of Corpus Christi College, Cambridge, possesses a manuscript which provides interesting proof of the great value of ultramarine to the medieval illuminator. This is a magnificent copy of the *Vulgate* by a German scribe of the twelfth century, extensively illustrated with miniature pictures, many of which had backgrounds, either partially or wholly, covered with ultramarine. All through the book, the ultramarine has in medieval times been very carefully and completely scraped off, no doubt for use in another manuscript. This theft has been accomplished with such skill that wonderfully little injury has been done to the beautiful illuminations, except, of course, the loss of splendor caused by the removal of the ultramarine.

<margin>Impasto.</margin>
In illuminated manuscripts *ultramarine* is very freely used. It is especially noticeable for the thick body (*impasto*) in which it is applied, so as very often to stand out in visible relief. The reason for this is that this, and some other blue pigments, lose much of their depth of color if they are ground into very fine powder. Hence both the ultramarine

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of blue pigments, moving from the use and value of ultramarine to the composition and history of smalto blues and German blue.</meta>

<page-num>242</page-num>
<header>SMALTO BLUES. [CHAP. XV.</header>

and *smalto* blues are always applied in a comparatively coarse-grained powder; and this, of course, necessitates the application of a thick body of color.

<margin>*Ancient cyanus.*</margin>
*Smalto blues.* Next in importance to real ultramarine come the artificial *smalto* or "enamel" blues, which were used extensively in Egypt at a very early date under the name of artificial *cyanus*; see Pliny, *Natural History* XXXVII. 119. Among the Greeks and Romans as well, this was a pigment of great importance, and when skillfully made, it is only slightly inferior in beauty to natural ultramarine.

<margin>*Vitreous pigment.*</margin>
*Smalto blue* is simply a powdered blue glass or vitreous enamel, colored with an oxide or carbonate of copper. Vitruvius (VII. xi. 1) describes the method of making it by fusing in a crucible the materials for ordinary bottle glass, mixed with a quantity of copper filings. The alkaline silicate of the glass frit acts upon the copper, which slowly combines with the glass, giving it a deep blue color. The addition of a little oxide of tin turns it into an opaque blue enamel, which, when cold, was broken up with a hammer and then powdered, but not too finely, in a mortar.

Smalto blue is widely used for the simple blue initial letters which alternate with red ones in a great number of manuscripts. The glittering particles of the powdered glass can easily be distinguished by a close examination. Like ultramarine, smalto blue is always applied in a thick layer.

The monk Theophilus (II. 12), who wrote during the eleventh century—a period of some artistic and technical decline—advises the glass painter who wants a good blue to search among ancient Roman ruins for the finely colored *tesserae* of glass mosaics, which were so widely used by the Romans to decorate their walls and vaults, and then to grind them for use.

<margin>*German blue.*</margin>
*Azzurro Tedesco* or *Azzurro della Magna*, German blue, was much used by illuminators as a cheap substitute for ultramarine. This appears to have been a natural compound of carbonate of copper with a brilliant blue color. It was occasionally used to adulterate the costly ultramarine, but

<summary>This page explains the production and historical use of smalto blues, which are made from ground blue glass colored with copper, and introduces German blue as a cheaper natural copper carbonate alternative.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of blue pigments, describing how to detect adulteration in pigments and transitioning into the use of vegetable dyes like indigo and earthy greens like terra verde.</meta>

<page-num>243</page-num>
# CHAP. XV.] DYES USED AS PIGMENTS.
<sig>16—2</sig>

This fraud was easily discovered by heating a small quantity of the pigment on the blade of a knife; it underwent no change if it was pure, but if adulterated with *Azzurro della Magna*, it showed signs of blackening¹.

<margin>Indigo.</margin>
*Indigo blue.* The above-mentioned blues are all mineral in character and are durable under almost any circumstances. To some extent, however, the vegetable *indigo* blue was also used for manuscript illuminations, both alone and also to create a compound purple color.

<margin>Method of using dyes.</margin>
Colors of all kinds prepared from vegetable or animal substances required special treatment to make them fit for use as pigments in solid or *tempera* painting. Although indigo and other colors of a similar class are the best and simplest dyes for woven fabrics, they are too thin in body to be used alone as pigments. Thus, in both classical and medieval times, these dye-pigments were prepared by having a small quantity of white earth, powdered chalk, or similar materials absorb a large quantity of the thin dye. This brought the dye into a concentrated, solid, and opaque form, rather than leaving it as a mere stain as it would otherwise have been.

These kinds of pigments are described by Pliny in *Natural History* (xxxv. 44 and 46) and by Vitruvius (vii. xiv). Eraclius, in his work on technique, *De Artibus Romanorum*, calls them *colores infectivi*, or "dyed colors"—an accurately expressive phrase.

One method, occasionally used for cheaper manuscripts, was to paint onto the vellum with white lead and then color it by repeatedly applying a brush dipped in the thin dye-pigment. Many of the colors mentioned below belong to this class.

<margin>Terra verde.</margin>
*Green pigments.* A fine, soft green much used in early manuscripts is a natural earthy pigment called *terra verde* or green *Verona earth*. This needs little preparation, except

---

<note>¹ German blue was also liable to turn into a bright emerald green if exposed to damp air. This change has occurred in a large part of the painted ceilings of the Villa Madama, which Raphael designed for Cardinal de' Medici (later Clement VII) on the slopes of Monte Mario, a short distance outside the walls of Rome.</note>

<page-num>244</page-num>

# CHAP. XV.] GREEN PIGMENTS.

washing, and is of the most durable kind; it is a type of ochre, colored not with iron, but by the natural presence of copper.

<margin>*Verdigris green.*</margin>
A much more brilliant green pigment was made of *verdigris* (*verderame*) or carbonate of copper, produced very easily by moistening metallic copper with vinegar or by exposing it to the fumes of acetic acid in a closed earthen vessel; see Theophilus, book I, chapter 37.

*Verdigris green* was much used by manuscript illuminators, especially during the fifteenth century, when a very unpleasant, harsh, and gaudy green appears to have been popular. When softened by mixing it with white pigment, verdigris gives a more pleasant and softer color.

<margin>*Chrysocolla.*</margin>
A native carbonate of copper, which was called by the Romans *chrysocolla*<note>This was because it was used by goldsmiths for *soldering gold*.</note>, was also used for medieval manuscripts. It is, however, harsh in tint if not tempered with white. Both the last-named pigments were specially used with egg yolk as a medium.

*Prasinum*, a vegetable green made by staining powdered chalk with the green of a leek, was sometimes used.

Cennino Cennini also recommends a grass green made by mixing orpiment (sulfide of arsenic) and indigo.

One of the best and most commonly used greens was made by a mixture of smalto blue and yellow ochre; other mixtures were also used.

***Red pigments.*** Red and blue are by far the most important of the colors used in illuminated manuscripts, and it is wonderful to see what variety of effect is often produced by the use of these two colors alone.

<margin>*Vermilion and minium.*</margin>
The chief red pigments used by illuminators are vermilion (*cinnabar* or sulfide of mercury) and red lead (*minium*), from which the words *miniator* and *miniature* were derived, as is explained above on page 31<note>*Minium* was widely used in the manuscripts of classical times; this is mentioned by Pliny (*Natural History* XXXIII. 122), who says *minium in voluminum quoque scriptura usurpatur* (minium is also used in the writing of books).</note>.

Both these pigments are very brilliant and durable reds,

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of pigments used in manuscript illumination, moving from the preparation of greens to a detailed examination of red pigments, including mineral-based colors like vermilion and red lead, as well as organic reds like murex and kermes.</meta>

<page-num>245</page-num>
# CHAP. XV.] RED PIGMENTS.

the more costly vermilion is the more beautiful of the two; it has a slightly orange tint.

<margin>Mixed reds.</margin>
Illuminators commonly used the two colors mixed. One recipe recommends one-third of red lead combined with two-thirds of vermilion; Jehan le Begue's manuscript, § 177 (Mrs. Merrifield's edition, Vol. I, page 141). Vermilion was prepared by slowly heating together metallic mercury with sulfur. Red lead (a protoxide of lead) was made by roasting white lead or else *litharge* (ordinary lead oxide) until it absorbed a larger proportion of oxygen.

<margin>Ochre reds.</margin>
*Rubrica* or *Indian red* was a less brilliant pigment, which also was largely used in illuminated manuscripts, especially for headings, notes, and the like, which were hence called *rubrics*. *Rubrica* is a fine variety of *red ochre*, an earth naturally colored by oxide of iron<note>All natural earthy pigments owe their colors to the various metals, which in combinations with different substances give a great variety of tints. Thus, iron gives red, brown, yellow, and black; copper gives many shades of brilliant blues and greens; and manganese gives a quiet purple, especially in combination with an alkaline silicate.</note>; another variety was called bole Armeniac. In classical times, the *rubrica* of *Sinope* was specially valued for its fine color.

In addition to these mineral and very permanent reds, there were some more fugitive vegetable and animal scarlets and reds which were used in illuminated manuscripts.

<margin>Murex.</margin>
*Murex*. One of these, the *murex* shellfish, has already been mentioned for its use as a dye for the vellum of the magnificent Byzantine and Carolingian gold-written manuscripts. The *murex* was also used as a *color infectivus* by concentrating it on powdered chalk<note>Plutarch (*De defec. Or.* § 41) mentions flour made from beans as being used with *murex* purple and *kermes* crimson to give them sufficient body for the painter's purpose.</note>.

<margin>Kermes.</margin>
*Kermes*. Another very beautiful and important carmine-red pigment was made from the little *kermes*<note>Kermes is the Arabic name for this insect.</note> beetle (*coccus*) which lives on the ilex oaks of Syria and the Peloponnese. It is rather like the *cochineal* beetle of Mexico, but produces a finer and more durable color, especially when used as a dye.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of red pigments, transitioning from mineral-based reds to those derived from organic sources such as insects and plants, detailing their preparation and application in medieval arts.</meta>

<page-num>246</page-num>
<header>KERMES AND LAKE. [CHAP. XV.</header>

<margin>*Kermes red.*</margin>
For the woven fabrics of classical and medieval times, and in the East even at the present day, kermes is one of the most beautiful and important of all the colors used for dyeing. The medieval name for kermes red was *rubeum de grana*; when required for use as a pigment, it appears to have been common not to extract the color directly from the beetle, but to obtain it from clippings of red cloth which had been dyed with kermes. This was done by boiling the cloth in a weak alkaline solution and precipitating the red pigment from the water with the help of alum.

The reason for this method is not obvious. Possibly it was first done as a means of utilizing waste clippings of the costly red cloth, and then, once the habit was established, no other method was known to the color-makers, who in some cases bought pieces of cloth specifically to cut them up and use them in this way<note>¹ It should be remembered that a large number of the medieval recipes and processes were not based on any logical principle, and endless complications were often introduced quite needlessly; this is well shown in a very interesting paper by Prof. John Ferguson of Glasgow on *Some Early Treatises on Technological Chemistry*, read before the Philosophical Society of Glasgow, January 6, 1886.</note>. The *scarletum blanketum* mentioned on page 234 was purchased for this purpose.

<margin>*Madder.*</margin>
*Madder-red* was also used as a pigment by boiling the root of the madder plant (*rubia-tinctorum*), and then using the concentrated extract to dye powdered chalk. Various red and purple flowers, such as the violet, were used in the same way as *colores infectivi*.

<margin>*Lac.*</margin>
*Lake-red* (*lacca* or *lac*) was produced and named after a natural gum or resin, the *lach* of India; see Cennino Cennini, § 44.

This is a beautiful transparent color which, in some fine manuscripts of the fifteenth century, is used as a transparent glaze over burnished gold. The effect of this is very magnificent, as the metallic gleam of the gold shines through the deep transparent red of the over-painting. Lake was also used as an opaque, solid pigment by mixing it with white, which immediately gave it "body" and destroyed its transparency.

*Purple* of a very magnificent tint was occasionally made by a mixture of *ultramarine* with the carmine-red of the

<page-num>247</page-num>
<header>CHAPTER XV.] YELLOWS AND WHITES.</header>

kermes beetle; this was especially used by the illuminators of the fourteenth and fifteenth centuries.

<margin>Yellows.</margin>
*Yellow ochre*, a fine earth pigment colored by iron, was the principal yellow of the illuminators.

In late manuscripts, *orpiment* (sulfide of arsenic), which is a more brilliant lemon-yellow, was occasionally used; see Cennino Cennini, § 47.

*Litharge yellow*, an oxide of lead, was another important color, but more especially for the oil painter, who used it very extensively as a drier¹.

Another fine ochreous earth of a rich brown color was the *terra di Siena* or “raw Siena”; the color of this was made warmer in tint by roasting, thus producing “burnt Siena.”

<margin>Use of white.</margin>
*White pigments* were perhaps the most important of all to the illuminator, who usually only used pure colors for his deepest shadows; all lights and half tints, both in miniature pictures and in decorative foliage, being painted with a large admixture of white. The use of this system of coloring by Fra Angelico and many painters of the Sienese school has already been referred to; see page 190.

For this reason, it was very important to use a pure and durable white pigment which would combine well with other colors.

<margin>Lime white.</margin>
*Bianco di San Giovanni* was in all respects one of the best of the whites used by illuminators.

This was simply pure *lime-white*, made by burning the finest white marble; the lime was then washed in an abundance of pure water, then very finely ground, and finally dried in cakes of a convenient size; see Cennino Cennini, § 58; and

---

<note>¹ The use of litharge as a drier was one of the most important improvements made in the technique of oil painting by the Van Eycks of Bruges in the first half of the fifteenth century. Before then, oil paintings on walls had often been laboriously dried by holding charcoal braziers close to the surface of the picture. Among the accounts of the expenses of painting the Royal Palace of Westminster in the thirteenth century (see above, page 110), charcoal for this purpose is an important item in the cost. Paintings on panel, being movable, were usually dried by being placed in the sun; but, in every way, a good drier like litharge works better than heat, whether from fire or sunshine.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of pigments and materials used in medieval illumination, specifically detailing the composition and manufacture of white pigments and the different types of black ink.</meta>

<page-num>248</page-num>
<header>WHITE PIGMENTS. [CHAP. XV.</header>

Theophilus, I. 19. The medium used with it was the purest size or <term>gum Arabic</term> of the most colorless kind.

Another white pigment was made of powdered chalk and finely ground eggshells; this was a less cold white than the <term>bianco di San Giovanni</term>.

<margin>White lead.</margin>
White lead (*cerusa* or *biacca*) was also used¹, especially by the later illuminators, but with very unfortunate results, since white lead is liable to turn to a metallic gray or even black if exposed to any impure sulfurous atmosphere.

Many beautiful manuscripts have suffered much owing to the blackening of their highlights which had been touched in with white lead; especially manuscripts exposed to the gas- and smoke-poisoned air of London or other large cities.

<margin>Process of manufacture.</margin>
The *biacca* of the medieval illuminator was made in exactly the same way that Vitruvius and Pliny describe; see Vitruvius VII. xii.; and Pliny, *Hist. Nat.* XXXIV. 175.

A roll of lead was placed in a clay *dolium* or large vase, which had a little vinegar at the bottom. The top was then sealed down, and the jar was left in a warm place for a week or so, until the fumes of the acetic acid had converted the surface of the lead into a crust of carbonate. This carbonate of lead was then flaked off and purified by repeated grinding and washing.

In order to keep the white pigments perfectly pure, some illuminators used to keep them under water, so that no dust could reach them.

**Black inks.** Two inks of quite different kinds were used for the ordinary text of medieval manuscripts.

<margin>Carbon ink.</margin>
One of these was a pure carbon-black (modern Indian or Chinese ink); this has been described under the classical name *atramentum librarium*²; see above, page 27. The great advantage of this carbon ink is that it never fades; it is not a dye or stain, but it consists of very minute particles of carbon which rest on the surface of the vellum.

The other variety was like modern black writing ink, only

---

<note>¹ See Theophilus, I. 39.</note>
<note>² See Vitruvius, VII. 10; and Pliny, *Hist. Nat.* XXXV. 41; and Dioscorides, v. 183.</note>

<meta>This page continues the description of the two main types of black ink used in medieval manuscripts, specifically focusing on iron-based ink and its chemical properties.</meta>

<page-num>249</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XV.] BLACK INKS.</header>

<margin>Iron ink.</margin>
of very superior quality. This acts as a dye, staining the vellum a little below the surface. Unfortunately, it is liable to fade, though when kept from the light (as in most manuscripts) it has stood the test of time very well.
    Sometimes the medieval illuminators distinguished these two kinds of black ink, calling the first *atramentum* and the second *encaustum*; but frequently the names are used indifferently for either: see Theophilus, I. 40. The *encaustum* was made by boiling oak bark or gallnuts, which are rich in tannin, in acid wine with some iron filings or vitriol (sulfate of iron). The combination of the iron and the tannin gives the inky black¹. Both these black inks were used with gum Arabic.

<margin>Beauty of the plain text.</margin>
A great part of the beauty of medieval manuscripts is quite unconnected with their illumination. The plain portion of the text, from the exquisite forms of its letters and the beautiful glossy black of the ink on the creamy ivory-like vellum page, lighted up here and there by the crisp touch of the rubricator’s red, is a thing of extraordinary beauty and charm. This perfection of technique in the writing and beauty of the letters lasted considerably longer than the illuminator’s art did. Hence, in some of the manuscripts of the period of decadence, executed during the fifteenth century, the plain black and red text is very superior in style to the painted ornament; and one cannot, in some cases, help regretting that the manuscript has not escaped the disfigurement of a coarse or gaudy scheme of illumination.
    *Red inks* were of three chief kinds, namely *vermilion*, *red lead*, and *rubrica* or red ochre, which have been already mentioned.

<margin>Purple ink.</margin>
    *Purple ink* was used largely, not often for writing, but for the delicate pen ornaments of the initials in certain classes of late Italian and German manuscripts. A vegetable pigment seems to have been used for this; the lines appear to be stained and do not consist of a body color resting on the surface of the vellum.

---

<note>¹ Sometimes accidentally produced in domestic life by some overdrawn tea remaining on a steel knife.</note>

--- Page 281 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of materials used in medieval manuscript production, moving from inks to the tools used for gold writing and sketching, specifically lead, silver, and stone pencils.</meta>

<page-num>250</page-num>
<header>LEAD AND CHALK PENCILS. [CHAP. XV.</header>

*Gold writing* is usually done with fluid gold pigment, but in later manuscripts, very grand titles and headings are sometimes done with burnished leaf gold applied on a raised mordant, with the writing first done with a pen dipped in the fluid mordant.

*The pencils and pens of the Illuminator.* Two quite different classes of pencils were used for lightly sketching the outline of the future floral design or miniature.

<margin>Lead point.</margin>
One of these was the silver-point or lead-point¹, very much like the metallic pencil of a modern pocketbook. The use of the silver-point was known in classical times; Pliny (*Natural History* XXXIII. 98) notes it as a strange thing that a white metal like silver should make a black line when used for drawing. However, it is a faint grey rather than a black line that a point of pure silver makes, especially on vellum, and so it was more common for illuminators to use a softer pencil of mixed lead and tin; Cennino recommends two parts of lead to one of tin² for making the lead point, *piombino*.

<margin>Red pencil.</margin>
Another kind of pencil was made of a soft red stone, which owed its colour to iron oxide. Because of its fine blood-red tint, the illuminators called it *haematita*, *lapis amatista*, or *amatito*; hence, an ordinary lead pencil is now called either *lapis* or *matita* in Italy. This stone is quite different from the hard *haematite* which was used in classical times for the early cylinder-seals of Assyria.

<margin>Burnisher.</margin>
The harder varieties of the *amatista* or *haematite* were used to burnish the gold leaf in manuscripts, with small pieces being polished and fixed in a convenient handle. They were also used as a red pigment, with the stone being calcined, quenched in water, and finely ground; see Cennino Cennini, § 42.

---

<note>¹ The modern "lead-pencil" is incorrectly named, being made of *graphite*, which is pure carbon. This does not appear to have been used in medieval times.</note>
<note>² The vellum was not prepared in any way to receive the silver-point drawing; but when an artist wanted to make a finished study in silver-point, he covered his vellum or paper with a priming of fine *gesso*, powdered marble, or wood-ashes; this gave a more abrasive surface to the paper, and made the silver rub off more easily and mark much more strongly. In the case of manuscript illuminations, a strongly marked line was not needed, as the outline was only intended as a guide to the painter.</note>

<page-num>252</page-num>
<header>SCRIBES' IMPLEMENTS. [CHAP. XV.]</header>

<margin>List of tools.</margin>
*The implements of scribes and illuminators.* The following is a list of the principal tools and materials required by the illuminator of manuscripts, including those which have already been described <note>See above, pages 29 and 30, regarding the pens and inkstands used by classical scribes.</note>.

Pens, pencils, and chalk of various sorts, as described above.
Brushes made of <term>miniver</term>, badger, and other kinds of hair.
Grinding-slabs and rubbers made of <term>porphyry</term> or other hard stone, and a bronze mortar.
Sharp penknife and palette knife.
Rulers and a metal ruling-pen.
Dividers to prick out the guiding lines of the text.
Scissors for shaping the gold leaf.
Burnishers, stamps, and *<term>stili</term>* for decorating the gold.
Small horns to hold black and red ink; see fig. 53 on page 209.
Color box, palette, pigments, gold leaf, and <term>media</term> of various kinds.
Sponge and pumice stone for erasures.

<margin>Paintings of scribes.</margin>
Miniatures representing a scribe writing a manuscript are the most common of all subjects in several classes of illuminated manuscripts. For example, the first capital letter of Saint Jerome's *Prologue* in the <term>historiated</term> Anglo-Norman *Vulgates* almost always contains a very tiny painting of a monastic scribe <note>This is usually intended to represent Saint Jerome translating or revising the Latin edition of the Bible.</note>, seated and writing at a sloping desk, with his pen in one hand and his penknife in the other <note>Additionally, the first miniature in the French and Flemish *<term>Horae</term>* usually depicts *Saint John on Patmos* writing his Gospel. The eagle stands by patiently holding the Evangelist's inkhorn. In some manuscripts, the Devil, clearly intimidated by the eagle's beak, makes a feeble attempt to tip over the ink. In the latest manuscript *<term>Horae</term>*, this scene is replaced by the scene of *Saint John at the Latin Gate*.</note>.

In one respect, such scenes are always treated in a conventional way; that is, the scribe is represented writing in a

<page-num>253</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XV.] TECHNICAL METHODS.</header>

...complete and bound book, whereas both the writing of the text and the illuminations were done on loose sheets of <term>vellum</term>, which could be conveniently pinned down flat on the desk or drawing-board.

<margin>*The scribes' processes.*</margin>
The processes used in the execution of an illuminated manuscript of the fourteenth or fifteenth century were as follows:

*First*, if the text were to be in one column, four lines were ruled marking the boundaries of the block of text and the margin. These four lines usually cross at the corners and are carried to the extreme edge of the <term>vellum</term> <note>A two-columned page of text had, of course, two sets of framing lines, one for each block of writing.</note>.

<margin>*Ruled lines.*</margin>
Next, the scribe, with a pair of dividers or compasses, pricked out at even distances the number of lines which were to be ruled to serve as a guide in writing the text. These pricked holes were, as a rule, set at the extreme edge of the <term>vellum</term> and were intended to be cut off by the binder, but in many manuscripts they still remain. The scribe then filled the space within the first four marginal lines with parallel ruled lines at the intervals indicated by the pricks at the edge.

<margin>*Stylus lines.*</margin>
In early manuscripts, the guiding lines to keep the text even are usually ruled, not with color or ink, but simply traced with a pointed <term>stylus</term>, which made a sufficiently clear impressed line on the <term>vellum</term>, showing through from one side to the other.

<margin>*Lead lines.*</margin>
In the twelfth and thirteenth centuries, the practice began of ruling the lines with a *lead point*;

<margin>*Red lines.*</margin>
and then, from the fourteenth century onwards, they were usually ruled with bright red pigment <note>In some manuscripts, lines are ruled in blue or purple, but much less frequently than in the more decorative vermilion.</note>; this has a very decorative effect in brightening the mass of black text, and thus we find in many early printed books <note>In certain classes of books, such as large Bibles and Prayer-books, the custom of ruling red lines lasted until the nineteenth century.</note> these red guiding lines have been ruled in merely for the sake of their ornamental appearance.

---

<summary>This page describes the technical preparation of vellum for manuscripts, detailing the transition from using blind-impressed stylus lines to lead points and eventually decorative red vermilion lines.</summary>
<keywords>vellum preparation, ruling lines, stylus, lead point, vermilion, scribal tools, bookbinding, manuscript illumination</keywords>

--- Page 285 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of manuscript production, specifically detailing the writing of the text and the subsequent stages of decoration and illumination, following the preparation of ruled lines described on the previous page.</meta>

<page-num>254</page-num>
<header>THE PROCESSES OF THE [CHAP. XV.</header>

This ruling was nearly always done with special metal <term>ruling-pens</term>, very like those now used for architectural drawing; and thus the lines are perfectly even in thickness throughout.

<margin>The plain text.</margin>
The next stage in the work was the writing of the plain black text. In early times it appears to have been usual, or at least not uncommon, for the same hand to write the text and add the painted <term>illuminations</term>; but when the production of illuminated manuscripts came mostly into secular hands, the trades of the <term>scribe</term> and the <term>illuminator</term> were usually practiced by different people; and in later times a further division still took place, and the <term>miniaturist</term> frequently became separated from the decorative <term>illuminator</term>.

<margin>Guiding letters.</margin>
Thus we find that in many manuscripts the <term>scribe</term> has introduced in the blank spaces minute <term>guiding letters</term> <note>These guiding letters were used in all early printed books that had initials painted in by an illuminator.</note> to tell the <term>illuminator</term> what each initial was to be; and, especially in fifteenth-century Italian manuscripts, instructions are added for the <term>miniaturist</term>, telling him what the subject of each picture was to be. These instructions were commonly written on the edge of the page so that they were cut off by the binder, but in many cases they still exist, not obliterated by the subsequent painting.

But to return to the progress of the page, when the <term>scribe</term> had finished the plain text, leaving the necessary blank spaces for the illuminated capitals and miniatures, the work of decoration then began.

<margin>Decoration.</margin>
As a rule the decorative foliage and the like was finished before the separate miniatures, if there were any, were begun. First the <term>illuminator</term> lightly sketched in outline the design of the ornament, using a lead point. Next, wherever burnished gold was to be introduced, the thick <term>mordant-ground</term> was laid on; the <term>gold leaf</term> was then applied and finished with <term>tooling</term> and <term>burnishing</term>.

The reason why the gold was applied before any of the painting was begun was this: the long rubbing with the

<summary>This page describes the transition from ruling lines to writing the text and the subsequent division of labor between scribes, illuminators, and miniaturists. It also details the initial steps of decoration, specifically the application and burnishing of gold leaf.</summary>
<keywords>manuscript production, scribe, illuminator, miniaturist, guiding letters, gold leaf, mordant-ground, burnishing, illumination, ruling-pens</keywords>

--- Page 286 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of manuscript production, detailing the application and burnishing of gold leaf, the sequence of painting, the use of transfer patterns for borders, and the final preparation for binding.</meta>

<page-num>255</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XV.] MEDIAEVAL SCRIBE.</header>
<meta>Digitized by Google watermark at bottom of page</meta>

<margin>Gold leaf.</margin>
The <term>burnisher</term> acted not only on the <term>gold leaf</term>, but also naturally rubbed the <term>vellum</term> a little distance all around it. This would have smudged the painting around the gold if it had been applied first. Moreover, the burnisher was liable to carry small particles of gold onto the surrounding vellum, which would have given a ragged look to the design if the adjacent surfaces had not been subsequently covered with <term>pigment</term>. In cases where there is an isolated <term>gold boss</term>, there is usually a slight disfigurement from the burnisher rubbing the vellum all around the gold. In these cases, the outline of the gold was made clean and definite by the addition of a strong black outline, as is mentioned above.

<margin>The painting.</margin>
When the whole of the burnished gold was finished, the painting was then executed. If any fluid gold pigment was used, that was usually added last of all.

<margin>Transferred patterns.</margin>
In some cases, in the later and cheaper French and Flemish manuscripts, the ornaments in the borders were not specially designed and sketched in for the manuscripts; instead, previously used outline patterns were transferred onto the vellum by a bone <term>stilus</term> and ordinary transfer paper, made by rubbing red chalk all over its surface.

In some of the better-quality manuscripts with the “ivy-leaf” border, the illuminator made the general design of one page serve for the next one in this way: when he had drawn in the main lines of the scroll-pattern on the borders of one page, he held the vellum up to the light and so was able to trace the pattern through from the other side of the leaf. To prevent monotony, he varied the design by introducing different little blossoms among the repeated scroll-work which formed the main pattern.

<margin>Preparation for binding.</margin>
When the <term>scribe</term>, the <term>rubricator</term>, the <term>illuminator</term>, and the <term>miniaturist</term> (either as one person or as several different people) had completed the manuscript, it was ready for the <term>binder</term>. As an indication of the order in which the leaves of the manuscript were to be bound, the scribe usually placed a letter or number on the lower margin of the last page of each <term>gathering</term> of leaves.

<summary>This page describes the technical sequence of manuscript illumination, explaining why gold leaf was burnished before painting and how border patterns were sometimes transferred or traced. <meta>This page continues the technical description of manuscript production, transitioning from the artistic application of gold and pigment to the organizational markings used by scribes and binders.</meta>

<page-num>256</page-num>
<header>SCRIBES' SIGNATURES. [CHAP. XV.</header>

In the earliest printed books, these guiding letters, or *signatures* as they are called, were added by hand in the same way¹; but within a few years, the regular and more developed system of printed *signatures* was introduced².

<margin>Scribes' signatures.</margin>
Scribes' signatures at the end of manuscripts are comparatively rare, but they do occasionally occur in various interesting forms. My friend Mr. W. J. Loftie kindly sends me the following:

In a *Sarum Missal* of the fifteenth century at Alnwick Castle,

> “ Librum scribendo Jon Whas³ monachus laborabat,
> Et mane surgendo multum corpus macerabat.”
<note>The monk Jon Whas labored at writing the book, and by rising early in the morning, he greatly exhausted his body.</note>

More commonly, manuscripts terminate with a vague phrase invoking a blessing on the scribe, such as this from a Bible in the Bodleian (No. 50),

> “ Qui scripsit hunc librum
> Fiat collectum in paradisum.”
<note>He who wrote this book, may he be gathered into paradise.</note>

Or this, which occurs in several manuscripts,

> “ Qui scripsit scribat,
> Semper cum Deo vivat.”
<note>Let him who wrote it write on; may he live forever with God.</note>

<margin>Owner's name.</margin>
In another manuscript *Vulgate* in the Bodleian (No. 75), the owner of the book, who was named *Gerardus*, has recorded the fact in this fanciful way:

> “ Ge ponatur et rar simul associatur
> Et dus reddatur cui pertinet ita vocatur.”
<note>Let "Ge" be placed, and "rar" be joined with it, and "dus" be returned; he to whom it belongs is called thus.</note>

---

<note>¹ As a rule, these manuscript signatures in printed books were written close to the edge of the page and so have been cut off by the binder; in some tall copies, however, they still exist.</note>
<note>² The next stage was the numbering of each *folio* or leaf, and the latest system was to number each page. Folios appear to have been first numbered in books printed at Cologne around the year 1470. A further modification has recently been introduced: in two-column pages, each column is numbered separately.</note>
<note>³ The *Lectionary* mentioned on page 120 was written and signed by a monastic scribe called Sifer Was.</note>

<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of scribes' signatures and colophons to the physical binding of manuscripts, detailing both luxurious and common methods.</meta>

<page-num>257</page-num>
<sig>17</sig>

# CHAPTER XVI.

## THE BINDINGS OF MANUSCRIPTS.

<margin>Costly bindings.</margin>
For the more magnificent classes of manuscripts, such as the *Textus* (*Gospels*) used as altar ornaments, every costly and elaborate artistic process was employed. In addition to the sumptuous gold and jeweled covers mentioned above on page 55, manuscripts were bound in plates of carved ivory set in gold frames, or in plaques of Limoges enamel—especially the *champlevé* enamels with the heads of the figures attached in relief, such as were produced with great skill at Limoges during the eleventh to the thirteenth centuries. Some *Evangeliaria* were bound in covers made of ancient Roman or Byzantine ivory diptychs, a custom to which we owe the preservation of the most important existing examples of these¹. Such costly methods of binding were, of course, exceptional, and most manuscripts were covered in a much simpler manner.

<margin>Common bindings.</margin>
The most common form of binding was to make the covers of stout oak boards, which were covered with parchment, calfskin, pigskin, or some other leather. Five brass or bronze bosses were fixed on each cover, arranged in this pattern :·:, and two or four stout clasps made of leather straps with brass catches were firmly nailed onto the oak. The corners of the covers were often strengthened by brass or *latten* <note>a brass-like alloy</note> corner pieces, and in some cases metal edgings were nailed all along

---

<note>¹ Some fine examples of magnificently bound manuscripts are illustrated by Libri, *Monumens inédits*; *Hist. Ornam.* Paris, 1862–1864.</note>

M. C. M.

<summary>This page describes the various methods of binding manuscripts, ranging from luxurious gold, ivory, and enamel covers for altar books to common leather-wrapped oak boards with metal bosses. It notes how the reuse of ancient ivory diptychs as book covers helped preserve important historical artifacts.</summary>
<keywords>manuscript binding, Textus, Gospels, Limoges enamel, champlevé, Evangeliaria, ivory diptychs, oak boards, metal bosses, latten, Libri</keywords>

<page-num>258</page-num>
<header>VARIOUS METHODS OF [CHAP. XVI.</header>

the edges of the oak, making a very strong, massive, and heavy volume. Large pieces of rock crystal, amethyst, or other common gems were frequently set in the five bosses of the covers. These were always cut in a rounded form *en cabochon*, not faceted as is modern custom.

The small amount of decoration usually employed on early bindings was often limited to tooled lines joining the five bosses on the covers <note>In Geyler’s *Fatuorum Navicula*, many editions of which, abundantly illustrated with woodcuts, were published shortly before and after the year 1500, the woodcut showing the first fool of the series, the *Bibliomaniac*, represents him surrounded by books, all of which are bound according to this design.</note>.

<margin>Titles of Manuscripts</margin>
If the title of the manuscript was placed on the binding—a practice that was not very common—it was usually written on the upper part of one of the covers. In some cases, the title was written on a separate slip of vellum and was protected by a transparent slice of horn fixed with small brass nails.

This appears to have been the usual system as long as books were kept in chests or *armaria*; but when open bookshelves with chained books came into use, around the time when printing was invented, the title of a book was usually written on the front edges of the leaves.

At that time, books were placed on the shelves in the opposite way from how they are kept now, so that the front edge of the volume, rather than the spine, was visible.

<margin>Painted edges</margin>
Towards the close of the fifteenth century and throughout the sixteenth, the front edges of printed books and manuscripts were sometimes decorated with painted illumination, usually a portrait of the author of the work or some object illustrating its subject <note>A complete sixteenth-century Venetian library, consisting of one hundred and seventy volumes—all with painted illuminations on their edges—is now in the library of Mr. Thomas Brooke at Armitage Bridge, near Huddersfield. The whole collection forms a beautiful array of delicately painted miniatures, mostly the work of Cesare Vecellio, a Venetian illuminator of the latter part of the sixteenth century; see the *Catalogue of Mr. Brooke’s library*, London, 1891, Vol. II., pp. 663 to 681.</note>.

The parchment used to cover the oak bindings of manuscripts was often colored by staining or painting; red and purple were the favorite colors. Chaucer, in the

<summary>This page describes the physical finishing of manuscript bindings, including the use of gemstones, the placement of titles on covers or edges, and the decoration of page edges. It highlights how the transition from storage in chests to open shelves changed the way books were labeled and displayed.</summary>
<keywords>bookbinding, bosses, gemstones, cabochon, armaria, vellum, horn, chained books, painted edges, Geyler, Fatuorum Navicula, Bibliomaniac, Cesare Vecellio</keywords>

<page-num>259</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XVI.] BINDING MANUSCRIPTS. 259</header>

The Prologue to the *Canterbury Tales*, in describing the Clerk of Oxford, says:

> **For he would rather have at his bed’s head,**
> **Twenty books, clothed in black and red,**
> **Of Aristotle and of his philosophy,**
> **Than rich robes, or a fiddle or a psaltery.**

<margin>*Painted bindings.*</margin>
In some cases, the oak covers of manuscripts were not hidden by leather, but were decorated with elaborate paintings. A very interesting series of folio account books of the Cathedral of Siena, now preserved in the *Opera del Duomo*, is especially remarkable for its pictured bindings. These manuscripts date from about 1380 to 1410, with one volume being devoted to the expenses and records of each year. On one cover of each is a large painting on the oak, frequently showing a view of some part of Siena or the interior of the Cathedral. Very interesting evidence regarding the old fittings of the high altar—including Duccio di Buoninsegna’s great retable—and the original position of the magnificent pulpit is provided by some of these pictured covers. One volume of this Sienese series is now in the South Kensington Museum.

<margin>*Stamped leather.*</margin>
In the fourteenth century, book bindings began to be decorated by stamping patterns with dies or punches on the vellum or pigskin covering the oak board—a method of decoration that was greatly elaborated and developed in the sixteenth century, especially by German and Dutch bookbinders.

The earlier stamped designs were of a much simpler character, usually consisting of patterns scattered all over the surface of the cover, featuring small flowers or animals such as lions, eagles, swans, and dragons of a heraldic character. In many cases, these punches, or at least their designs, continued to be used for a long time; thus, one occasionally encounters a fifteenth-century book whose binding is decorated with stamps in a fourteenth- or even thirteenth-century style.

The later class of stamped bindings, belonging more to printed books than to manuscripts, is often very beautiful and

<page-num>260</page-num>
<header>STAMPED BINDINGS. [CHAP. XVI.</header>

<margin>Stamped bindings.</margin>
These bindings were decorative in character, with the whole surface of the cover being completely embossed in relief by the skillful application of a great number of punches used in various combinations to form one large and perfectly united design. In these later times, from about the middle of the sixteenth century, the tendency was to cut larger designs on a single punch or die; and the leather or parchment was softened by boiling so that a large surface could be embossed in one operation. This process was greatly aided by the invention of the screw-press, which enabled the workman to apply a steady and long-continued pressure. However, in the older stamped bindings, small punches were generally used, and the force was simply applied by the blow of a hammer¹.

<margin>English bindings.</margin>
In England, very fine stamped bindings of this class were made even in the first half of the fifteenth century. And, just as in earlier times the work of the binder and the manuscript illuminator had been carried out by the same man, or at least in the same workshop, we find that some of the earliest English printers, such as Julian Notary, were also skillful binders of their own printed books. The very fine stamped bindings of Julian Notary and other English craftsmen are commonly decorated on one side with the Tudor arms and badges supported by angels, and on the other side with a pictorial scene of the Annunciation of the Blessed Virgin Mary featuring I. N. or other maker’s initials.

<margin>Wallet bindings.</margin>
Returning now to the earlier bindings of manuscripts, we should mention one system which was frequently applied to *Books of Hours*, *Breviaries* (<term>portiforia</term>), and other *portable* books. This system involved extending the leather covering far beyond the edges of the wooden boards that formed the main covers of the manuscripts, so that the book, its edges and all, were protected, very much as if it were kept in a bag. In fact, this sort of binding was essentially a leather bag to the inside of which the book was attached.

->The mouth of the bag was closed by a running thong,<-

---

<note>¹ A similar change took place during the reign of Elizabeth I in England when coins, which up to that time had always been made by hammering, were first struck by the "mill and screw."</note>

<page-num>261</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XVI.] COLOURED BINDINGS.</header>

a loop or some other fastening, and the book was thus carried about, hanging from its owner’s girdle¹.

In bindings of this class, the leather covering was frequently dressed with the hair still on it. Corpus Christi College at Oxford possesses a very well-preserved example of this: a manuscript from the thirteenth century in a contemporary bag-covering made of deer skin, with its soft brown fur in a perfect state of preservation.

<margin>Velvet.</margin>
Bindings of red or violet velvet were also frequently used for manuscripts. Plain red velvet, with elaborate clasps and corner-pieces of chased gold or silver, was perhaps the most common form of binding for costly manuscripts of the fourteenth and fifteenth centuries. Fine gems, especially the carbuncle and turquoise, were set in the gold mounts of some of these princely books.

<margin>Dyed vellum.</margin>
Vellum dyed with the *murex* <note>a genus of sea snails used to produce purple dye</note> was used to cover the oak boards of manuscripts at a time when purple-stained vellum was no longer used for the pages of manuscripts. A fine green dye and other colors were also used for vellum bindings. The Vatican records of books borrowed (and returned) usually mention how each volume was bound. Among the earliest of these records, dating from the pontificate of Leo X (1513 to 1522), the most common descriptions of bindings are *in tabulis*, *in rubio*, *in albo*, *in nigro*, and *in gilbo*, indicating the color of the skin or velvet in which the manuscript was bound.

<margin>Later bindings.</margin>
In the sixteenth century, when private luxury and pomp were taking the place of the earlier religious feelings and beliefs which had so greatly fostered the decorative arts, bindings as costly as those of the *Altar-textus* <note>a formal copy of the Gospels used on the altar</note> of the great cathedral and abbey churches were again made at the command of wealthy patrons. Thus, for example, Cardinal Grimani had his famous *Breviary*² bound in crimson velvet, the greater part of which is concealed by the most elaborate

---

¹ In the miniature paintings in manuscripts of the fourteenth and fifteenth centuries, one often sees ladies represented with their *Horae* <note>Books of Hours</note> suspended in this way from their girdles.
² See page 167.

<page-num>262</page-num>
<header>LATER BINDINGS. [CHAP. XVI.</header>

<margin>Gold mounts.</margin>
mounts, clasps, corner-pieces, and borders of solid gold, of the most exquisite workmanship, decorated with a medallion portrait head of the Cardinal himself.

So also the very similar *Horae* of Albert of Brandenburg¹ is decorated with clasps and other mounts of pure gold; and an immense number of other sumptuous bindings, rich with embossed and chased gold and studded with precious gems, were made to enshrine the costly manuscripts of Giulio Clovio and other famous miniaturists of the sixteenth-century period of decadence.

<margin>Bindings of needlework.</margin>
At the close of the fifteenth century, or rather earlier, the custom became popular of having *Horae* and other manuscripts owned by wealthy secular personages bound in velvet, richly decorated with embroidery in gold and silver thread and silk mixed with a great number of seed pearls. The arms, badges, and initials of the owner are the most common designs for these embroideries.

Some of the German examples of this class of binding are especially elaborate and magnificent; but on the whole, this method of decoration is not at all suited for covering books.

<margin>Works on bindings.</margin>
With regard to books on the subject of early bindings, it is much to be regretted that existing works—of which there are a great many, especially in French—all begin just about the period when bindings of the greatest interest and the truest artistic value were no longer made. Plenty has been written about the costly bindings in which Grolier, Maioli, and other wealthy book-buyers had their purchases encased, but no work exists on the bindings of the medieval period, when, frequently, the covers of a manuscript were as much a labor of love as the illuminated pages within. The sixteenth-century binders, who worked for Grolier and other rich patrons of art, lived at the verge of a commercial epoch, and though their works are often very pretty and technically of high merit, yet they cannot be compared, as true works of art, with the bindings of the period before printing was invented.

---

<note>¹ See page 167.</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the cost and appreciation of manuscripts and their bindings, contrasting medieval works with those from the later period of decline.</meta>

<page-num>263</page-num>
<header>CHAP. XVI.] VALUE OF MANUSCRIPTS.</header>

<margin>Low cost of manuscripts.</margin>
*The present value of illuminated manuscripts.* Overall, a fine manuscript may be regarded as one of the cheapest works of art from bygone days that can currently be purchased by an appreciative collector. Many of the finest and most perfectly preserved manuscripts appearing on the market today are actually sold for smaller sums than they would have cost when they were new, despite the great additional value and interest they have gained from their antiquity and comparative rarity.

For example, a beautiful and perfectly preserved historiated Anglo-Norman *Vulgate* from the thirteenth century—with its full count of eighty-two pictured initials, written on between six and seven hundred leaves of the finest uterine vellum—can now commonly be purchased for between £30 and £40. This hardly represents the original value of the vellum on which the manuscript is written.

Manuscripts of a simpler nature, however beautifully written, if they are merely decorated with blue and red initials, commonly sell for considerably less than the original cost of their vellum¹.

Again, the more costly manuscripts of fine style, which now fetch several hundred pounds, usually contain a wealth of pictorial decoration and painstaking craftsmanship far in excess of what could be purchased for a similar sum in any other branch of art.

<margin>Lack of taste.</margin>
Another noticeable point is that the modern monetary values of manuscripts, even those bought only as works of art, are by no means in proportion to their real artistic merits. Manuscripts from the finest period of the illuminator’s art, the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries, are now sold for much smaller sums than the immeasurably inferior but more showy and over-elaborate manuscripts from the period of decline in the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries.

---

<note>¹ This same lack of appreciation extends to bindings. As a rule, a book in a fine medieval binding sells for no more than if it were in a modern binding by Bedford. It is only the sixteenth-century bindings in the so-called “Grolier style” and the like which add significantly to the value of a book.</note>

<summary>The author explains that illuminated manuscripts are often sold for less than their original production costs and argues that modern collectors mistakenly value showy, later manuscripts over the superior artistry of the 13th and 14th centuries.</summary>
<keywords>manuscript market, illumination value, Anglo-Norman Vulgate, uterine vellum, artistic merit, medieval bindings, Grolier style, Francis Bedford</keywords>

--- Page 295 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion regarding the market value of illuminated manuscripts, contrasting the higher prices paid for later, technically inferior works with the lower prices of earlier, more artistically significant masterpieces.</meta>

<page-num>264</page-num>
<header>MODERN PRICES. [CHAP. XVI.</header>

<margin>*Modern lack of taste.*</margin>
A sad example of the current lack of taste and appreciation for beauty in art is shown by the fact that a manuscript signed and illuminated by Giulio Clovio would sell for a much larger sum than a masterpiece as perfect as a fine example of a fourteenth-century Anglo-Norman *Apocalypse*.

Likewise, the late and inferior *Horae* <note>Books of Hours</note> from about 1480 to 1510 often sell for much higher prices than simpler but far more beautiful manuscripts of an earlier date. Of course, I am speaking here of the value of manuscripts regarded purely as works of art, not those that are primarily important for the interest of their text.

The result of this is that a collector with genuine knowledge and an appreciation for artistic quality can perhaps spend his money more effectively by purchasing manuscripts than by buying any other class of art, whether medieval or modern.

<summary>The author laments that modern collectors often pay more for later, inferior manuscripts than for superior early works, suggesting that knowledgeable collectors can find great value in the current market.</summary>
<keywords>Giulio Clovio, Anglo-Norman Apocalypse, Horae, manuscript values, art market, book collecting, illumination, 14th century art</keywords>

# APPENDIX.

Mr. Jenkinson, the Librarian of the University of Cambridge, has kindly supplied me with the following interesting extracts from a manuscript of the thirteenth century in the Parish Library of St. James’ at Bury St. Edmunds (M 27 + B 357)¹, which gives instructions to scribes and illuminators of manuscripts as to the various tools they are to use.

> “A scribe should have a scraper or razor for scraping dirt from the parchment or membrane. He should also have abrasive pumice and a plane to smooth and level the surface of the parchment. He should have lead and a small line with which he may rule the parchment, with a margin remaining free on all sides, both on the hair side and the flesh side . . . . . .
> The scribe should sit in a chair with arms raised on both sides <gloss>i.e., a board</gloss> supporting a desk or lectern, with a footstool suitably placed for his feet.
> The scribe should have a heater covered with a blanket. He should have a knife <note>Latin: *arcanum*</note> with which to shape the pen so that it is handy and suitable for writing . . . . . . He should have a dog's (?) or boar's tooth for polishing the parchment . . . . . . And he should have a viewing aid <note>likely spectacles</note> so that he does not waste time through error. He should have coals in the heater so that the ink can dry more quickly in cloudy or rainy weather . . . . . . And he should also have <term>minium</term> <note>red lead</note> for forming scarlet, red, or crimson letters and capitals. He should also have dark powder; and the azure discovered by Solomon².”

---

¹ This library is now deposited in the Guildhall; the shelf-mark is probably that of an old monastic library.
² Probably a mistaken version of Pliny’s statement (*Natural History* XXXVII. 119) that azure blue (*cyanus*) was invented by a king of Egypt.

<header>DIRECTIONS TO SCRIBES. [APP.</header>

*Translation.*

> “The scribe should have a sharp scraper or knife to rub down the rough spots of his parchment or vellum. He should also have a piece of ‘biting’ pumice stone and a flat tool to smooth down and make the surface of his parchment even.
>
> He should have a lead pencil and a ruler with which to draw lines on the parchment, leaving a margin free from lines on both sides of the parchment, on the back of the leaf as well as on the flesh side......
>
> The scribe should sit in an armchair, with arms raised on each side to support a desk or <unclear>?</unclear>; a footstool should be conveniently placed under his feet. The scribe should have an <term>epicaustorium</term>¹ covered with leather; he should have an <term>arcanum</term> <note>pen-knife?</note> with which to shape his pen, so that it may be well formed and suitable for writing......
>
> He should have the tooth of a dog (?) or of a wild boar for the polishing of his parchment...... And he should have spectacles, lest a troublesome delay be caused through blunders. He should have hot coals in a brazier so that [his ink] may dry quickly [even] in cloudy or rainy weather...... He should also have <term>minium</term> <note>red lead</note> for the painting of red, crimson, or purple letters and initials. He should also have a dark powder (pigment), and the azure which was invented by Solomon.”

The following excellent description of the chief kinds of service books which were used during the later medieval period was originally written in 1881 by Henry Bradshaw, the Cambridge University Librarian, for *The Chronicles of All Saints’ Church, Derby*, by the Rev. J. C. Cox and Mr. W. H. St. John Hope. It is by the kind permission of Mr. Cox and Mr. Hope that I am able to reprint Mr. Bradshaw’s valuable note, which, with admirable clarity and conciseness, explains the character of each of the principal classes of service books used in English churches and the manner in which these books became differentiated and multiplied down to the time of the Reformation.

<page-num>267</page-num>
<header>APP.] SERVICE BOOKS.</header>

### NOTE BY HENRY BRADSHAW.

In the old Church of England, the Services were either—
<margin>*The Hours.*</margin>
**1.** For the different hours (Matins, Lauds, Prime, Terce, Sext, Nones, Vespers, and Compline), said in the Choir,
**2.** For processions, in the church or churchyard,
**3.** For the Mass, said at the altar, or
**4.** For occasions, such as Marriage, Visitation of the Sick, Burial, etc., said as the occasion required.

Of these four, all have their counterparts, more or less, in the English Service of modern times, as follows:
**1.** The Hour Services, of which the principal ones were Matins and Vespers, correspond to our Morning and Evening Prayer.
<margin>*Processions.*</margin>
**2.** The Procession Services correspond to our hymns or anthems sung before the Litany that precedes the Communion Service in the morning, and after the third Collect in the evening; however, they are no longer sung during a procession to the Churchyard Cross or a secondary altar in the church. The only relic (in common use) of the actual procession is that used on occasions such as the Consecration of a Church, etc.
**3.** The Mass answers to our Communion Service.
<margin>*Occasional Services.*</margin>
**4.** The Occasional Services are either those used by a priest—such as Baptism, Marriage, Visitation and Communion of the Sick, Burial of the Dead, etc.—or those reserved for a bishop, such as Confirmation, Ordination, Consecration of Churches, etc.

All of these services except the last mentioned are contained in our “Prayer Book” with all their details, except for the lessons at Matins and Evensong, which are read from the Bible, and the hymns and anthems, which have been at the discretion of the authorities since the sixteenth century. This concentration or compression of the services into one book is the natural result of time; the further back we go, the more numerous are the books that our old inventories show. To take the four classes of services and service books mentioned above:
<margin>*The Breviary.*</margin>
**1.** The Hour Services were latterly contained, as far as the text was concerned, in the *Breviarium*, or *Portiforium*, as it was preferably called in England¹. The musical portions of this book were

---

¹ An explanation of the nature and constitution of the Breviary will be found in the preface to the Psalter volume of the Cambridge University Press edition of the Sarum Breviary, recently published.

<summary>Henry Bradshaw categorizes medieval Church of England services into four groups—the Hours, Processions, the Mass, and Occasional Services—and explains their modern Anglican equivalents. He begins to describe the specific books used for these services, starting with the Breviary or Portiforium.</summary>

<keywords>Henry Bradshaw, Service Books, Matins, Nones, Vespers, Processions, Mass, Occasional Services, Prayer Book, Breviary, Portiforium, Sarum Breviary</keywords>

--- Page 299 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of medieval liturgical books by Henry Bradshaw, detailing the components that make up the Breviary and the history of the Processionale.</meta>

<page-num>268</page-num>
<header>268 VARIOUS KINDS OF [APP.</header>

contained in the *Antiphonarium*. But the Breviary itself was the result of a gradual amalgamation of many different books:

<margin>*The Breviary.*</margin>
(a) The *Antiphonarium*, properly so called, containing the anthems (*Antiphonae*) to the Psalms, the responds (*Responsoria*) to the lessons (*Lectiones*), and the other miscellaneous verses and responds (*Versiculi et Responsoria*) throughout the service;

(b) The *Psalterium*, containing the Psalms arranged for use at the different Hours, together with the Litany as used on certain occasions;

(c) The *Hymnarium*, or collection of hymns used in the various Hour services;

(d) The *legenda*, containing the long lessons used at Matins, drawn from the Bible, the *Sermologus*, and the *Homiliarius* (used respectively at the first, second, and third Nocturns at Matins on Sundays and some other days), as well as from the *Passionale*, which contains the acts of the saints read on their feast days; and

(e) The *Collectarium*, containing the *Capitula*, or short lessons used at all the Hour services except Matins, and the *Collectae* or *Orationes* used in them.

<margin>*Procession Services.*</margin>
2. The Procession Services were contained in the *Processionale* or *Processionarium*. It will be remembered that the rubric in our "Prayer Book" concerning the Anthem ("In Quires and places where they sing, here followeth the Anthem") is *indicative* rather than *imperative*, and that it was first added in 1662. It states a fact; and, no doubt, when processions were abolished, along with the altars to which they were made, cathedral choirs would have found themselves in considerable danger of being swept away also, had they not taken a stand and been content to sing the processional anthem without moving from their position in the choir. This alone sufficed to carry on the tradition; and looked upon in this way, the modern anthem book of our cathedral and collegiate churches, and the hymn book of our parish churches, are the only legitimate successors of the old *Processionale*. It must also be kept in mind that the morning and evening anthems in our "Prayer Book" do not correspond to each other as closely as might at first sight appear to be the case. The morning anthem comes immediately before the Litany which precedes the Communion Service, and corresponds to the processional anthem or respond sung at the

<meta>Digitized by Google watermark at bottom center</meta>

<summary>This page details the five constituent parts of the Breviary and explains how the medieval Processionale evolved into the modern anthem and hymn books used in English churches. It highlights how the 1662 Prayer Book rubric helped preserve cathedral choir traditions after liturgical processions were abolished.</summary>

<keywords>Breviary, Antiphonarium, Psalterium, Hymnarium, Legenda, Collectarium, Processionale, Prayer Book 1662, Cathedral Choirs, Liturgy, Hour Services, Matins</keywords>

--- Page 300 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of medieval liturgical books, moving from the Breviary and Processionale to the books used for the Mass and Occasional Services.</meta>

<page-num>269</page-num>
<header>APP.] SERVICE BOOKS.</header>

churchyard procession before Mass. The Evening Anthem, on the other hand, follows the third Collect, and corresponds to the Processional Anthem or Respond sung “*eundo et redeundo*,” in going to and returning from some subordinate altar in the church at the close of Vespers.

<margin>The Mass.</margin>
3. The Mass, which we call the Communion Service, was contained in the *Missale*, as far as the text was concerned. The Epistles and Gospels, being read at separate lecterns, would often be written in separate books called *Epistolaria* and *Evangeliaria*. The musical portions of the Altar Service were latterly all contained in the *Graduale* or Grayle, so called because one of the principal elements was the *Responsorium Graduale* or Respond to the *Lectio Epistolae*. In earlier times, these musical portions of the Missal Service were commonly contained in two separate books, the *Graduale* and the *Troparium*. The *Graduale*, being in fact the *Antiphonarium* of the Altar Service (as indeed it was called in the earliest times), contained all the passages of Scripture, varying according to the season and day, which served as *Introits* (*Antiphonae et Psalmi ad Introitum*) before the Collects, as Gradual Responds or Graduals to the Epistle, as *Alleluia* versicles before the Gospel, as *Offertoria* at the time of the first oblation, and as *Communiones* at the time of the reception of the consecrated elements. The *Troparium* contained the *Tropi*, or preliminary tags to the *Introits*; the *Kyries*; the *Gloria in excelsis*; the *Sequences* or *Prosae ad Sequentiam* before the Gospel; the *Credo in unum*; the *Sanctus* and *Benedictus*; and the *Agnus Dei*; all, in early times, liable to have insertions or *farsuræ* of their own, according to the season or day, which, however, were almost wholly swept away (except those of the *Kyrie*) by the beginning of the thirteenth century. Even in Lyndewode’s time (1433 AD), the *Troparium* was explained to be a book containing merely the Sequences before the Gospel at Mass, so completely had the other elements then disappeared or become incorporated in the *Graduale*. This definition of the *Troparium* is the more necessary, because so many old church inventories still remain which contain books that were long since disused even at the time the inventory was written, so that the lists would be unintelligible without some such explanation.

<margin>Occasional Services.</margin>
4. The Occasional Services, so far as they concerned a priest, were of course more numerous in the old days than now, and included the ceremonies for *Candlemas*, *Ash Wednesday*, *Palm Sunday*, etc.

<summary>This page describes the liturgical books used for the Mass and Occasional Services, detailing the roles of the Missale, Graduale, and Troparium. It explains how musical elements and specific service readings were organized and how these books evolved or were consolidated by the 15th century.</summary>
<keywords>Service books, Mass, Missale, Epistolaria, Evangeliaria, Graduale, Troparium, Antiphonarium, Lyndewode, Occasional Services, Candlemas, Ash Wednesday, Palm Sunday, liturgy, church history</keywords>

--- Page 301 ---
<meta>This page concludes the discussion on medieval liturgical books, specifically focusing on those used for occasional services and the rules governing the liturgical calendar.</meta>

<page-num>270</page-num>
<header>SERVICE BOOKS. [APP.</header>

besides these books of actual services. The book which contained these was called the *Manuale* in England, while on the Continent the name *Rituale* is more common. No church would likely be without one of these. The purely episcopal offices were contained in the *Liber pontificalis* or Pontifical, for which an ordinary church would have no need.

<margin>The Ordinale.</margin>
5. Besides these books of actual services, there was another that was absolutely necessary for the correct understanding and definite use of those already mentioned. This was the *Ordinale*, or the book containing the general rules relating to the *Ordo divini servitii* [Order of Divine Service]. It is the <term>Ordinarius</term> or <term>Breviarius</term> of many Continental churches. Its method was to go through the year and show what was to be done; which days were to take precedence over others; and how, under such circumstances, the details of the conflicting services were to be handled. The basis of such a book would be either the well-known <term>Sarum Consuetudinarium</term> (named after St. Osmund, but actually drawn up in the first quarter of the thirteenth century), the Lincoln <term>Consuetudinarium</term> belonging to the middle of the same century, or another such book. By the end of the fifteenth century, Clement Maydeston's <term>Directorium Sacerdotum</term>, or Priests' Guide, had superseded all such books and came itself to be called the <term>Sarum Ordinale</term>. This continued until about 1508, when the shorter <term>Ordinale</term>, under the name of <term>Pica Sarum</term> ("the rules called the Pie"), having been divided and redistributed according to the seasons, came to be incorporated into the text of all the editions of the <term>Sarum Breviary</term>.

->CAMBRIDGE,<-
->March 17, 1881.<-

->H. B.<-

Mr. Micklethwaite has kindly pointed out to me the following passage from the <term>Cistercian Consuetudines</term> (Guignard, *Documents inédits*, Dijon, 1878, p. 174), chapter LXXII, "*Nullus ingrediatur coquinam excepto cantore et scriptoribus ad planandam tabulam, ad liquefaciendum incaustum, ad exsiccandum pergamenum....*" That is, the kitchen fire might be used for melting the wax on the tablets so that a fresh list of names could be written (see above, p. 8), for liquefying frozen ink, and for drying the vellum skins to prepare them for writing.

->CAMBRIDGE: PRINTED BY C. J. CLAY, M.A. AND SONS, AT THE UNIVERSITY PRESS.<-
